## M: M-Day–marker bull

### M-Day
 
 <i>n.</i> Metrication Day

>  <b>LELAND</b> <i>metrication:</i> the process of shifting from measuring in imperial units ... to metric units... New Zealand built up to this slowly, but M-day came it was drastic. <i>(1980,Personal Kiwi-Yankee Dictionary, A,64</i>)

### m.a.
 
 <i>abbr.</i> (Rural) [stock sales] mixed age animals.

>  17 m.a.ewes $15-$27.50 <i>(Mar 9, 2000,Taranaki Daily News</i>)

### maania
 
 <i>n.</i> red tussock

>  Maori names for tussock varied from region to region. In one southern dialect, <i>wi</i> was used for silver tussock (furher north the same word was applied to rushes) and <i>patiti</i> seemed to be a general term for tall tussocks, with <i>maania</i> an alternative or the word for red tussock in particular. The Maniatoto (<i>maania</i> = blood, <i>toto</i> = plain) was formerly covered in red tussock. <i>(Nov 2003,NZ Geographic,No. 66/40</i>)

### MAANZ
 
 <i>abbr.</i> Maritime Archaeological Association of New Zealand

>  MAANZ, the Maritime Archaeological Association of New Zealand, will be showing off its brand new conservation laboratory... On Saturday the conservation lab will have an exhibition of the work of MAANZ volunteers. <i>(Jun 26, 2001,Contact (Wgtn),5</i>)

### Mabel
 
 <i>n.</i> a nickname for, and often a term for, an effeminate person [ca1952 Waiouru Military Camp, F.M. O'Brien eg He's a real Mabel, fair go. Old Mabel Jones, at it again.]

### Mabel Island lily
 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Arthropodium cirratum</i>, a local name for rengarenga

>  <b>COCKAYNE</b> <i>Arthropodium cirratum</i> Mabel Island lily <i>(1919,NZ Plants & Their Story</i>) [NOTES: (And)]

>  <b>R MASON</b> <i>Mabel Island lily (Arthropodium cirrhatum).</i> Name used sometimes in Wellington and Marlborough. The plant grows on Mabel Island near Picton but is not known to grow elsewhere near Picton. It is the <i>renga-lily, rock lily </i>etc. of Auckland. [cf ? 1830 <i>Lycopodium ramulosum </i>T. Kirk] <i>(1954,Personal Communication</i>)

>  Mr H.G. Clarke's Chatham Island Lily, of only five month's growth - and Mrs Fildes' Mabel Island Lily (which belongs, we believe, to the orchid family), were much remarked upon. <i>(Dec 3, 1885,Marlborough Express,2</i>)

>  A lobelia, growing snugly in the trunk of a tree fern; an orchid in flower, better known as "The Mabel Island Lily," but greatly improved by domestication; and a new Zealand fuchsia were all horticultural gems. <i>(Dec 5, 1884,Marlborough Express,2</i>)

>  On it [Mabel Island] I found over 20 species of native flora, the chief of which is the celebrated lily, best known as the Mabel Island lily; but let the botanist go there and see for himself. <i>(Jan 12, 1899,Otago Witness,31</i>)

### mac/macca
 
 <i>n.</i> macrocarpa firewood [see also macrocarpa]

>  Dry Macca, this years [sic] burning. $75m3, or $260 4m3 truck load. <i>(Oct 23, 2010,Wairarapa Times-Age,17</i>)

### macaroni
 
 <i>n.</i> a nickname for clergy [1984 17 F E48 Pakuranga Coll. 24D MACARONI (PASTOR)]

### MACCAH
 
 <i>abbr.</i> Ministerial Advisory Committee on Complementary and Alternative Health [ORIGIN: acronym]

>  Two years ago, at the instigation of the Greens, Health Minister Annette King announced the establishment of a Ministerial Advisory Committee on Complementary and Alternative Health (acronym: Maccah). <i>(Aug 17, 2002,Listener (New Zealand Listener),36</i>)

### Maccas
 
 <i>n.</i> (informal) McDonalds Fast Food [ORIGIN: abbreviation, Mackers, Maccers var. hypocoristics forms]

>  I refer to the current television ad where a car with fogged windows pulls up at Maccas. <i>(Sep 28, 2004,Independent Herald (Wellington),13</i>)

>  A night at the Opera with a candlelit dinner on the harbour is just as likely to turn someone off as suggesting a combo at Mackers. <i>(Oct 5, 1998,Salient,7</i>)

>  Then I wanted him to have a coffee with me, or maybe go get some Macca's [sic], because he loved cheeseburgers. <i>(Nov 14, 1999,Sunday Star Times,C10</i>)

>  Like when someone spills a thickshake in Macca's. <i>(Mar 31, 2003,Salient,No. 5/54</i>)

>  I told Phil I felt like some Mackers [McDonald's] and he said he would try and get me out by 10 pm before they closed. <i>(Mar 26, 1997,Evening Post,1</i>)

>  I can't pass by Maccers. <i>(May 19, 1997,Salient,24</i>)

>  Stopping in for a "Macca's", as Australians like to say, takes on a new meaning when you have to shoulder Mr Bell's responsibilities. <i>(May 28, 2003,Dominion Post,C6</i>)

>  Is this the only place you can eat your Maccas inside an old plane? <i>(Jul 8, 2008,Dominion Post,C1</i>)

### macchiato
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Macchiato... In New Zealand, it is usually a long black with a tiny amount of frothed milk added. In Italy and Melbourne, and sometimes here, it is demitasse-sized. <i>(Jan 22, 1994,Listener (New Zealand Listener),57</i>)

### machine
 
 <i>n.</i> the mechanical or electric totalisator (See [totalisator](../T#totalisator))

>  It was a win for racing clubs in general to know there was a "scaler" amongst the bookmakers at Pahiatua, as it will have the effect of driving money into the machine where the punters know they will get paid. <i>(Mar 8, 1908,NZ Truth,2</i>) [NOTES: Check whether February or March]

>  <b>WOODHOUSE</b> What a lot [of money] you left behind in the`machine' <i>(1950,Farm & Station Verse,22</i>) [NOTES: [1889] Reeves in 1950 A.E. Woodhouse Farm and Station Verse 22]

>  <b>WILLIAMS & REEVES</b> When racing was developed by the aid of the `machine'. <i>(1891,Double Harness,8</i>)

>  <b>SCOTT</b> The bookmakers would not pay 30-to-1 as the machine is doing. <i>(1900,Colonial Turf,218</i>)

>  Each paid more than GBP25 on the win machine. <i>(Aug 15, 1949,Southern Cross (Wgton),9</i>)

>  An electric machine has a tremendous advantage over the old manual totalisator <i>(Aug 23, 1939,Taranaki Herald,3</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> shearing-machine

>  In sheds where machines are used.. the shearers to pay for combs and cutters at cost price. <i>(1906,Awards etc.,VII/625</i>)

>  We notice from the intercolonial papers that a patent has been granted to Ephraim Chaquette for an improved machine for holding, turning, and shearing sheep, including also improvements in the sheep-shearing implement adapted to this and other sheep-shearing machines. <i>(Oct 23, 1875,Otago Witness,18</i>)

>  With machines the average good shearer has 180 to 200 out at the end of the day. <i>(1945,Korero (AEWS Background Bulletin),3(6) : 24</i>)

>  What about that sheep-shearing machine you spoke of just now? Is there such a machine? <i>(Aug 20, 1887,Auckland Weekly News,20</i>)

>  <b>CARR</b> The boss Maori undertakes to supply all the rouseabouts (as shed hands are termed) i.e., fleece pickers, wool rollers and `Sheep oh!' the [sic] owner or manager generally gets his own pressers and expert (where machines are run). <i>(1913,Country Work,14</i>)

>  The majority of the larger sheepowners ... have come to recognize the advantages of `machines' over `blades'. <i>(Sep 20, 1917,NZ Journal of Agriculture,134</i>)

>  <b>BOWEN</b> Relative quality of workmanship between blades and machines is a debatable point. <i>(1955,Wool Away!,9</i>)

>  <b>MILLS</b> This establishes the date [Nov. 17, 1900] of the change-over from blades to machines ... <i>(1960,Sheep-O,19</i>)

>  <b>DICK</b> `Blades or machines?' I asked. `Oh, we use the snow-comb - know it?' `Yes, yes I do. Where are you going? It's rather early, isn't it?' <i>(1964,High Country Family,144</i>)

>  `There's nothing spooking the sheep. They're quiet enough. It could be one of the boys setting up machines.' `None of them's got a ute like that,' I said. <i>(Dec 1986,Marist Messenger,34</i>) [NOTES: (Joy Cowley)]

>  Generally speaking, machines are used on the plains and blades in the back country. <i>(1945,Korero (AEWS Background Bulletin),III/6 : 23</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> the milking machine

>  <b>COWLEY</b> When Dave wasn't late for milking, he was early getting away, cold rinse through the machines, yards left half-cleaned, short cuts everwhere. <i>(1979,Growing Season, The,37</i>)

>  [Advt.] Boiling Water for cleaning Milking Machines. <i>(Jul 31, 1926,NZ Dairy Produce Exporter,54</i>)

>  The cows were all in the bails, teats washed, ready for the machines. <i>(May 28, 1927,NZ Dairy Produce Exporter,52</i>)

>  <b>CARR</b> Owing to the difficulty in getting efficient `teat pullers' (as milkers are familiarly known) machines are being largely brought into use. <i>(1913,Country Work,38</i>)

>  <b>TURNER</b> ... the<i> milking machines</i>, normally simply called the <i>machines</i>, always in the plural. The parts of the machines are named, the <i>cups</i> which attach to the cow's teats ... . <i>(1966,English Language in Aust. & NZ, The,149</i>)

### machine man
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a shearer who uses machine-driven combs and cutters to shear sheep.

>  <b>BOWEN</b> Machine men only stop a few times a run to change cutters, compared with blade men giving their shears several wipes in a run. <i>(1955,Wool Away!,9</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> It [the woolshed] holds 1,800 woolly sheep - barely sufficient for eight fast machine-men ... [machine-man] <i>(1975,Sixty Thousand on the Hoof,213</i>)

### machine milk
 
<b>1</b> <i>v.</i> (Rural) to milk cows by machine.

>  During this once a day milking period I machine milk all the cows, treating them individually ... <i>(May 1, 1945,NZ Dairy Exporter,42</i>)

### machine shearer
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  ... Shearing Classes have been well attended by students ... in order to qualify as machine shearers. <i>(1912,Sheepowner's Handbook, The,11</i>)

>  <b>BOWEN</b> Grinding does not take long with the machine, ... Machine men only stop a few times a run to change cutters, compared with blade men giving their shears several wipes in a run. <i>(1955,Wool Away!,9</i>)

>  <b>DARE</b> At Woodfield there was no provision for machine shearing ... <i>(1956,Rouseabout Jane,91</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a shearer who uses machine-driven combs and cutters to shear sheep.

>  <b>LOUGHNAN</b> A machine shearer 'goes up the neck' with his comb flat on the skin. <i>(1981,Loughnan Glossary,10</i>)

>  <b>LOUGHNAN</b> Whereas a machine shearer breaks out up the neck, a blade shearer opens up. <i>(1981,Loughnan Glossary,57</i>)

>  WoolPro, it seems to me, doesn't recognise the existence of blade shearers and wish they would all just go away (or become machine shearers). <i>(2000,Wool Grower,Spring 5</i>)

### machine shearing
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>RICHARDS</b> It had been built for hand or blade shearing, so was fairly cramped when converted to a three-stand machine shearing shed. <i>(1986,Off the Sheep's Back,65</i>)

>  ... satisfactory arrangements were made for ... classes in both Machine-Shearing and Wool-Classing at Glenmark Station ... <i>(1912,Sheepowner's Handbook, The,11</i>)

>  Until the outbreak of the Second World War all shearing at Glenaray was done with blades, but owing to the acute shortage of blade shearers ... the owners were forced to change to machine shearing. <i>(Oct 16, 1950,NZ Journal of Agriculture,351</i>)

### machine shears
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [contrast blades](../C#contrast-blades))

>  <b>ACTON-ADAMS</b> <i>Shearing</i>: I have arranged with the Smoko Shearing Company of Wellington, to erect 10 machine shears on the western board of the shed. <i>(May 22, 1900,Letter</i>) [NOTES: 1900 May 22 Acton-adams Letter in 1969 McCaskill Molesworth 83]

### machine shed
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>CARR</b> We were not sorry when the whistle blew (it is a machine shed) all the same. <i>(1913,Country Work,16</i>)

### machine-stripping
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Machine stripping does get milk and may be advisable... If machine stripping - holding the cups down by hand - is to be practised, the time spent on milking will be increased considerably. <i>(Jul 10, 1959,NZ Dairy Exporter,37</i>)

>  The cows are taken in for machine-stripping once a day. <i>(Nov 1971,NZ Dairy Exporter,11</i>)

### Mackenzie Country
 
 <i>n.</i> a region of inland South Canterbury

>  Then my father would say: "They're copping it in the Mackenzie country tonight." <i>(Sep 2000,North & South,138</i>)

>  The crispness of a Mackenzie Country dawn can never be forgotten. <i>(Mar 13, 2004,Listener (New Zealand Listener),46</i>)

>  What is known as the Mackenzie Country, though somewhat undefined as to limits, is now comprised in a new county called Mackenzie County, lately cut off the large original County of Geraldine. <i>(1884,TrNZI,7/178</i>) [NOTES: (Chapman)]



 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) a large pastoral basin of inland South Canterbury, named after the legendary James Mackenzie, purported to have been caught there with stolen sheep. [check spelling of quotes (all cap K?); official name has small k]

>  Would it be suitable in the MacKenzie country to prevent shalerock faces from slipping? <i>(May 20, 1921,NZ Journal of Agriculture,313</i>)

>  <b>MACASKILL</b> On depleted tussock pastures of the MacKenzie country, frost lift followed by wind erosion became a major source of soil loss. <i>(1973,Hold This Land,70</i>)

>  <b>CRESSWELL</b> When the MacKenzie Country was discovered and all available land taken up ... <i>(1951,Canterbury Tales,143</i>)

### mackerel
 
 <i>n.</i> a common name for fishes resembling the northern hemisphere mackerel, esp. <i>Pneumatophorus</i> sp., the southern mackerel; and <i>Trachurus novae-zelandicae,</i> horse mackerel [early spellings mackarel, mackrel, etc.]

>  Another of our food fishes.. is the true mackerel (Scomber astralasicus) . . . The Maoris call the fish tawatawa. <i>(Jan 30, 1890,Otago Witness,18</i>)

>  The natives ... sold us ... as much fish as served all hands, they were of the Mackerel kind and as good as ever was eat. [f@n1] The Southern Mackerel, <i>Pneumatophorus colias. </i> <i>(Nov 8, 1769,Cook's Journals,1/195</i>)

>  ... a vast number of boats were on board almost loaded with macarel of 2 sorts, one exactly the same as is caught in England. [fn.] The Southern Mackerel, <i>Pneumatophorus colias</i> (Gm), is rather similar to the English species. The other sort was perhaps <i>Trachurus novae-zelandicae</i> Richardson. <i>(Nov 9, 1769,Banks Journal,2/428,1962 ed.</i>)

>  <b>YATE</b> ... in the mackerel-season, when several tribes go together to the little creeks where these fish frequent, and always succeed in capturing some hundreds of thousands before they return ibid 109 Their method of preparing the mackarel is different ... It is gutted, throughly washed with sea-water, and hung up to drain ... <i>(1835,New Zealand (various authors/titles),71</i>) [NOTES: (Griffiths Collection)]

>  [mackarel] We got only fish, ... and these of various sorts, such as Shirks [check], Sting-rays, Breams, Mullet, Mackarel ... <i>(Dec 5, 1769,Cook's Journals,1/219,1955 ed.</i>)

>  <b>PARKINSON</b> [mackrel: attrib] ... native ... brought us a large quantity of fish, mostly of the mackrel kind, with a few John Dories. <i>(Nov 9, 1769,Journal,104</i>)

>  <b>POLACK</b> The<i> patiki</i> [a-acute], between the large flounder and the sole, is equally excellent with the European fish, as are also the <i>mackarel,</i> of which there are several varieties. <i>(1838,New Zealand  (Polack),1/322</i>)

>  <b>YATE</b> [mackarel] Those saltwater fish] most plentiful, and of greatest note, are, soles, mackarel, cod-fish, a species of salmon, whiting, snapper, mullet, bream, skate, gurnards, and a few smaller kinds, some not near so large as a sprat; with an abundance of crayfish, oysters, shrimps, prawns, muscles, and cockles. <i>Ibid.</i> 85 In their great fishing-expeditions for mackarel, all concerned in making or mending the nets, the ground upon which those nets are made, the river upon the banks of which the work goes on, are all in a state of sacredness; ... <i>(1835,New Zealand (various authors/titles),71</i>)

>  <b>HEAPHY</b> The bream, ling, gurnard, herring, sole, rock-cod, and a species of mackarel, are all in abundance, as are also many fish unknown elsewhere [than Wellington]. <i>(1842,New Zealand (Heaphy),49</i>)

>  <b>SHORTLAND</b> Mackerel were playing round us in large shoals, and were taken in great numbers by the crew. ... This fish was much larger than its namesake known on the shores of the West of England, and had not its delicate flavour. <i>(1851,Southern Districts of NZ,143</i>)

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> The <i>tamure</i>, or snapper, and the <i>kahawai</i>, mackeral, were taken with a hook ... <i>(1855,Te Ika a Maui,500</i>)

>  <b>HURSTHOUSE</b> ... the best [sea fish] are the Hapuka ..., the Moki, the Wharehou[ao-acute], the Patiki (a small plaice), the Kawai (a coarse common fish rising at the fly in tidal waters and sometimes dubbed salmon) a sort of mackerel, and some mullet, rock-cod, bastard-skate, and snapper. <i>(1857,New Zealand (various authors/titles),1/122</i>)

>  <b>HAY</b> We have, indeed, a wealth of fish in all the tidal waters. Sharks, schnapper, rock-cod, mackerel, mullet, herring, sole, halibut, albacore, barracouta, king-fish, and others. <i>(1882,Brighter Britain,2/230</i>)

>  When we see the mackerel splashing along we know they are after squid; the mullet, latris, and parrot-fish are always after them, and even the moki and butter-fish join the hunt. <i>(1896,TrNZI,28/53</i>) [NOTES: (Henry)]

>  <b>TREGEAR</b> Mackerel were prepared by being cleaned inside and washed with salt water ... <i>(1904,Maori Race, The,107</i>)

>  <b>HYDE</b> Or in the evening ... a one-armed man solemnly proffers a large and gleaming mackerel, caught off the edge of the Auckland wharves. <i>(1936,Passport to Hell,15</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> BONITO Offshore anglers use bonito fillets for snapper bait and deep-sea anglers claim its dark-red flesh attracts almost every sporting fish up to shark and marlin. It is known as Mackerel, Horse Mackerel, Skippy, Skipper and Skipjack because of its habit of breaking the surface when feeding. The name `Skipjack' has also been applied however to at least half-a-dozen other species. <i>(1981,Fisherman's Bible,267</i>)

>  <b>SPEIGHT</b> The only true mackerels recorded from Canterbury are the Pacific Bonito <i>Sarda chiliensis</i>) and the large-scaled Tunny (<i>Gasterochisma melampus</i>) which may reach a length of 6 feet; but a related family includes the well-known Barracouta (<i>Thyrsites atun</i>), the King Barracouta, <i>Jordanidia solandrii</i> (often known locally as Kingfish, but not to be confused with the North Island Kingfish which is a form or Yellowtail) and the elongated, silvery-hued frost fish (<i>Lepidopus caudatus</i>) whose curious stranding habits can hardly yet be said to be satisfactorily explained. tentatively divided into 1. <i>Trachurus</i> spp. JACK MACKEREL HORSE MACKEREL a. <i>T novaezelandiae</i> of south-east Asian distribution; and b. <i>T. declivis</i> restricted to Australasia 2. <i>Scomber australasicus</i> and Indo-Pacific species, (COMMON) MACKEREL, BLUE MACKEREL, ENGLISH MACKEREL (from its similarity to the N. hemisphere species), FRIGATE MACKEREL, PACIFIC MACKEREL, SOUTHERN MACKEREL; 3. of uncertain attribution <i>(1927,Natural History of Canterbury,199</i>)

>  (Queen Charlotte Sound) ... the natives now and then brought Hake, ed. fn <i>Merluccius australis</i>.]Paracutas, [ed. fn <i>Thyrsites atun</i>.] a small sort of Mackarel, ed. fn <i>Scomber japonicus?.</i>] ... <i>(Feb 1777,Cook's Journals,3/808</i>) [NOTES: 1777 Feb Anderson's Journal in Cook Journals (1967) 3/808]

>  There are plenty of mackerel here, but no nets to catch them, and there are pilchards; please to bring one good pilchard net. <i>(Feb 10, 1843,Letters from New Plymouth,3,1968 ed.</i>)

>  <b>HECTOR</b> 32. MACKEREL. ... This valuable fish (<i>Scomber australasicus</i>) is only known to me from two large hauls ... made in Wellington Harbour about the 15th December, 1870, but it appears to be the fish ... called by the natives [113] Tawa-tawa. ... [It] is very superior in delicacy to the Scad or Horse Mackerel, which is commonly called the Mackerel in New Zealand. <i>(1872,Edible Fishes,112</i>)

>  <b>SHERRIN</b> MACKEREL (<i>Scomber Australasicus</i>). ... This valuable fish is well known as an occasional visitant in Wellington Harbour, being called by the Natives tawatawa. In colour, size, and form it closely approaches the common mackerel of England, and is very superior in delicacy to the scad or horse-mackerel, which is commonly called the mackerel in New Zealand. <i>(1886,Handbook Fishes of NZ,61</i>)

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> <i>Tawatawa, n. mackerel; scomber australasicus. </i>[so 1917/474] <i>(1892,Dictionary of the NZ Language,190</i>)

>  <b>WAITE</b> <i>Scomber Pneumatophorus</i> ... Mackerel <i>(1911,Records of the Canterbury Museum,1/233</i>)

>  <i>Scomber australasicus</i>, Mackerel. <i>(1928,TrNZI,60/383</i>) [NOTES: (Griffin)]

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> <i>tawatawa, 1. Pneumatophorus australasicies, mackerel. </i> <i>(1957,Dictionary of the Maori Language,405</i>)

>  <b>GRAHAM</b> Mackerel <i>Pneumatophorus australasicus</i> (Cuv. and Val.) <i>(1956,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,410</i>) [NOTES: 1955 Whitley Namelist in 1956 Graham NZ Fishes 410]

>  <b>POLACK</b> Deep sand-banks within a short distance of the shore, are extremely favourable for the congregating of shoals of mullet, cod, mackerel, a bastard salmon, and shark. <i>(1840,Manners & Customs of the NZers,1/203</i>)

>  Rurima is famous for its fish; hapuka (<i>Oligorus gigas</i>), ... moki (<i>Latris ciliaris</i>), king-fish (Seriola lalandii),<i> wharehou (Neptomenus brama)</i>, barracoota (<i>Thyrsites atun</i>), mackerel (<i>Scomber australasicus</i>), and the delicious little maomao ... <i>(1873,TrNZI,5/153</i>)

>  <b>PAUL et al.</b> [index to common names 283-293] mackerel 1379-81, 2074-87, 2109-11 macarel 2110 MACKERELS 2047-113 <i>(1987,Fishes: A Bibliography</i>)

>  <i>Trachurus novae-zelandicae</i> (Richardson). Horse mackerel. <i>(1938,TrRSNZ,68/410</i>) [NOTES: Graham Fishes of Otago Harbour]

>  <b>GRAHAM</b> Horse Mackerel <i>Trachurus novae-zelandicae</i> Richardson (Syn. <i>Scomber dimidiatus</i> Richardson, <i>S. clupeoides</i> Richardson, and <i>Caranx sinus-obscuri</i> Richardson) <i>(1956,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,406</i>) [NOTES: 1955 Whitley Namelist in 1956 Graham NZ Fishes 406]

>  <b>GRAHAM</b> HORSE MACKEREL (HATURE) <i>Trachurus novae-zelandicae</i> Richardson <i>(1956,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,237</i>)

>  <b>PARROTT</b> THE HORSE MACKEREL <i>Trachurus novae-zelandicae(Richardson)</i> <i>Ibid.</i> 169 Horse Mackerel <i>Trachurus novae-zelandicae</i> Mackerel; Scad <i>(1957,Sea Anglers' Fishes of NZ,83</i>)

>  <b>DOOGUE & MORELAND</b> HORSE MACKEREL ... <i>Other names: Trachurus novaezelandiae;</i> mackerel; hauture (Maori). <i>(1966,Sea Anglers' Guide,232</i>)

>  <b>McLINTOCK</b> <i>mackerel, horse</i> hature, hauture <i>Trachurus novae-zelandicae</i> <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,3/705-712</i>)

>  <b>NATUSCH</b> Family CARANGIDAE. This family contains ... the strongly-scuted herring-scad and horse mackerel ... . <i>(1967,Animals of NZ, The,221</i>)

>  <b>MORELAND</b> HORSE MACKEREL [<i>Trachurus declivis</i>] ... Mackerel and yellowtail are alternative names. <i>(1967,Marine Fishes,48</i>)

>  Luke was still out of work ... when the mackerel began to come in. He caught a few of the smaller type known locally as `English mackerel' ... One day, the other [fisherman] ... hauled in a fine `horse mackerel'. <i>(1970,Some Other Country,150</i>) [NOTES: 1970 Middleton `The Loners' in 1984 Some Other Country 150]

>  <b>SORENSON</b> <i>MACKEREL</i> (two species) (a) Scientific name: (i) <i>Scomber australasicus</i> (ii)<i> Trachurus declivis</i>. (b) Suggested commercial name[s]: (i) Blue Mackerel (ii) Jack mackerel. (c) Other common names: (i) Common Mackerel, Frigate mackerel, English mackerel (ii) Horse Mackerel. (d) Principal localities where taken: (both) Gisborne, Nelson, Picton ... <i>(1970,Nomenclature of NZ Fish,34</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> MACKEREL ... The common Mackerel is also known as the English Mackerel, Frigate Mackerel or Southern Mackerel. The Horse Mackerel is also known as the Yellowtail. The Koheru is also sometimes known as Yellowtail and is often misnamed Horse Mackerel and Scad. <i>(1981,Fisherman's Bible,162</i>)

>  <b>HECTOR</b> MACKEREL ... This is the Hauture of the natives, and the Scad of the northern hemisphere (<i>Trachurus trachurus</i>). <i>(1872,Edible Fishes,110</i>)

### mackerel gull
 
 <i>n.</i> = red-billed gull, tarapunga [only in 1873 edition; nf 1888, =RED-BILLED GULL [Buller?]]

>  <b>HUTTON</b> 109. LARUS SCOPULINUS. Forst. Mackerel Gull. Tarapunga. <i>(1871,Birds of New Zealand,40</i>)

>  LARUS SCOPULINUS, Forst. - Mackerel Gull. <i>(1886,TrNZI,18/128</i>) [NOTES: (Hamilton)]

>  Among the other birds seen [on Auckland Islands] were the skua gull, the black-backed gull, the mackerel gull, the yellow-billed albatross, the nelly, the Cape pigeon, and the white-headed petrel (<i>Procellaria lessoni</i>). <i>(1889,TrNZI,21/384</i>)

>  The mackerel gull is that dainty, cheerful little slate-backed sea-bird that congregates in such numbers on the ... breakwater's end. <i>(1901,TrNZI,33/218</i>)

>  Small gulls, usually known as mackerel-gulls - they have white breasts, pearly grey backs, black marked wings, and crimson bills, legs and feet - were very plentiful. on the banks abreast of the vessel. <i>(Aug 17, 1922,Auckland Weekly News,17</i>)

>  The red-billed or mackerell gull - .. the Maoris' tarapunga - is a smaller and prettier bird. <i>(Aug 13, 1908,Auckland Weekly News,38</i>)

>  <b>COCKAYNE</b> ... and accompanying [the Auckland Island Shag on Auckland Islands] is the pretty little mackerel-gull, with dove-coloured back, white head and breast, and brilliant red legs and beak. <i>(1910,NZ Plants,117</i>)

>  <b>FALLA et al.</b> RED-BILLED GULL <i>Larus scopulinus Other names</i>: Tarapunga, Mackerel Gull, Jackie (Chathams). <i>(1966,Birds of New Zealand,154</i>)

### mackerel ripple
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>GRAHAM</b> The ripple caused by a shoal of these fast-swimming fish, [horse mackerel] together with the delicate colourings, was a pretty sight from a moving launch. This ripple on the water is the well-known Mackerel Ripple. <i>(1956,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,237</i>)

 <i>n.</i>

### mackerel season
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>YATE</b> ... fishing is now not much regarded, except in the mackarel-season, when several [North Auckland] tribes go together to the little creeks where these fish frequent, and always succeed in capturing some hundreds of thousands before they return; ... <i>(1835,New Zealand (various authors/titles),71</i>)

### mackerel tuna
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>WILSON</b> MACKEREL TUNA <i>Euthynnus alleteratus</i>: A sub-tropical species are known as Little Tunny and in America as False Albacore. ... They were once called Mackerel Tuna because they are so common on Mackerel grounds where they are a constant nuisance to professional Mackerel fishermen. <i>(1981,Fisherman's Bible,268</i>)

### Maconachie
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> There was plenty of food [on Gallipoli], consisting of `bully' and biscuit, bacon, cheese and `Maconarchie' sugar and tea; ... <i>(May 9, 1915,New Zealander's Diary,113</i>) [NOTES: 1915 May 9-11 Williams New Zealander's Diary (c1922) 113]

>  <i>Tickler, Maconochie, Hindenburg</i>. - Three bad men. <i>(1918,New Zealand At the Front,32</i>)

>  <b>AITKEN</b> Midday passed in this way; we made a meal of bread, tinned Maconochie rations and pork-and-beans, which for attack fare ... was wonderful to have. <i>(1963,Gallipoli to the Somme,160</i>)

>  <b>WAITE</b> <i>(1919,New Zealanders at Gallipoli</i>)

### Macquarie cabbage
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> Stilbocarpa polaris [more fully Macquarie Island cabbage]

>  <b>CROWE</b> Macquarie Island cabbage <i>Stilbocarpa polaris </i> Punui... The early whalers and sealers called the plant Macquarie Island cabbage, eating it as a remedy for, and as prevention against, scurvy. <i>(1997,Field Guide to Native Edible Plants of NZ,84</i>)

>  So did Macquarie Island, an Australian possession that features in New Zealand English, as Orsman documented, at least in the case of the plant Macquarie cabbage <i>Stilbocarpa polaris</i>. Although the Macquarie cabbage takes its name from an island now belonging to Australia, the earliest quotations I have found are in the <i>Transactions of the New Zealand Institute</i> (1870 and 1883... This plant occurs on the Auckland, Campbell and Antipodes Islands, as well as on Macquarie, and has been reported from the Snares. <i>(Aug 2000,NZWords,No. 4 : 7</i>)

>  <i>Stilbocarpa polaris</i>, `Macquarie Island Cabbage' of the sealers, in flower and fruit [on Macquarie Island]. <i>(1883,TrNZI,15/448</i>)

>  <b>MOORE & IRWIN</b> <i>STILBOCARPA POLARIS. </i>... Early whalers and sealers used the plant as an antiscorbutic, hence the old name `Macquarie Island cabbage'. According to experience in 1950-51, `The petioles taste like celery when cooked; pickled rhizomes taste like turnips; and leaves when cooked like wet blotting paper' (Taylor 1955). <i>(1978,Oxford Book of NZ Plants,96</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> an old name for the Chatham Island lily.

>  The Snares are] almost entirely covered with scrub and trees of stunted growth, the Tupari, Akeake, and Kokomuka. Of M'Quarrie cabbage there is abundance, and of fine growth, some of the leaves measuring two feet in diameter.Patches clear of scrub are clothed with Lutaki tussock. <i>Myosotidium hortensia</i> (formerly <i>M. nobile</i>) = CHATHAM ISLAND LILY <i>(1870,TrNZI,2/176</i>) [NOTES: (Hector Outlying Islands)]

>  <b>LAING & BLACKWELL</b> <i>Myosotidium hortensia (The Chatham Island Lily).] </i> <i>(1940,Plants of NZ,368</i>)

>  <b>COCKAYNE</b> The most famous of all the Chatham Island plants is the giant forget-me-not (<i>Myosotidium nobile</i>) ..., frequently called by the absurd name of Chatham Island lily, or, what is worse, Macquarie cabbage! <i>(1910,NZ Plants,123</i>)

>  <b>COCKAYNE</b> <i>Myosotidium nobile </i> <i>(1919,NZ Plants & Their Story</i>) [NOTES: (And)]

### Macquarie Island shag
 
 <i>n.</i> (Environment)

>  Additions to the collection in the museum this week include eggs of the king penguin, the rock hopper penguin, the royal penguin, and the Macquarie Island shag, collected by Mr. Waite when he was with the Aurora on her recent cruise to the sub-Antarctic Islands. <i>(Jul 30, 1912,Evening Post,3</i>)

>  <b>FALLA et al.</b> MACQUARIE ISLAND SHAG <i>Phalacrocorax albiventer purpurascens </i> <i>(1966,Birds of New Zealand,71</i>)

### macrocarpa
 
 <i>n.</i> . [as fire wood; aka mac and macca - see also]

>  FOR SALE FIREWOOD Makrakapa, good loads, $55, delivered. <i>(Apr 1982,Karori and Western Suburbs News</i>)



 
 <i>adj.</i> [attrib]

>  They have taken to stripping the bark from macrocarpa-trees. <i>(1926,NZ Journal of Science,8/333</i>) [NOTES: (Oliver)]

>  <b>HYDE</b> Against the walls grew macrocarpa trees; ... <i>(1938,Nor the Years Condemn,39</i>)

>  <b>FRAME</b> ("Child") Oh how lovely ... to have a macrocarpa hedge instead of African Thorn. <i>(1951,Lagoon, The,60,1961 ed.</i>)

>  <b>SLATTER</b> Juvenile delinquency. What about us? ... [10] ... Bombarding the old widow's roof with indirect fire of stones from behind the high macrocarpa hedge. <i>(1959,Gun in My Hand, A,9</i>)

>  <b>SCRYMGEOUR</b> Originally ... sheltered by thick microcarpa hedges ... was a beautiful site of a previous homestead, ... built by a Scotchman of the `Clan McLean'. <i>(1960,Memories of Maoriland,113</i>)

>  <b>HYDE</b> The great macrocarpa trees have a dark serious pungency of their own. <i>(1937,Wednesday's Children,93</i>)

>  <b>OWEN & PERKINS</b> [Caption] The original Webber homestead..is hidden amongst the macrocarpa grove directly behind the wharf. <i>(1986,Speaking for Ourselves,175</i>)

>  Our crib was shielded from the road by a macrocarpa hedge. Actually, the term "hedge" does not convey the reality. A better description might be our crib was shielded from the road by a small forest of enormous macrocarpa trees masquerading as a hedge. <i>(Jan 1, 2004,Dominion Post,C1</i>)

>  I refer to a macrocarpa hedge on a neighbour's property. <i>(1912,Otago Witness,51</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> the common name of <i>Cupressus macrocarpa</i> [the tree; Californian, naturalised in NZ where it grows distinctively, is named distinctively (as opposed to US Monterey Cypress), and where there is a variety of cultivars.]

>  <b>FRAME</b> Jake came to the orchard, its entrance concealed by great trees, firs, macrocarpas, Australian firs, poplars and one native tree that Jake recognised by the now-familiar dark evergreen leaves. <i>(1988,Carpathians, The,194</i>)

>  Evergreens and Flowering Shrubs, &c. 30,000 Coniferae Trees... 4000 Cupressus Macrocarpas.. " Lawsoniana from 12s per doz. to 24s. <i>(Jul 1, 1874,Otago Daily Times,4</i>)

>  <b>HULME</b> There is a clump of macrocarpa shadowing the next bend in the ruts. <i>(1983,Bone People, The,168</i>)

>  And it was as cold as hell because the frost hadn't lifted ..., the stables being in a shelterbelt of macrocarpas and pines that hadn't been cut back ... <i>(Aug 29, 1987,Listener (New Zealand Listener),46</i>) [NOTES: (Davin)]

>  <b>KNOX</b> There was none, only a set of bricked steps cut into the bank and a track winding away under some old macrocarpas. <i>(1987,After Z-Hour,18</i>)

>  Your hands, when I was young they seemed thick and strong, / Lifting me high in the tall Macrocarpa. <i>(Apr 3, 1999,Listener (New Zealand Listener),93</i>)

>  Harry Ricketts has chosen 12 poems as well as the hilarious "Thirteen Ways of Starting a New Zealand Novel called Macrocarpa" for his red and blue framed <i>Thirteen Ways</i>. <i>(Nov 7, 1998,Listener (New Zealand Listener),43</i>)

>  The garden.. is well sheltered by large macrocarpas in the background and taupata, poroporo and cabbage trees to the fore. <i>(Aug 1991,NZ Gardener,29</i>)

>  <b>PAYTON</b> <i>(1888,Round about NZ,65</i>) [NOTES: [check p: 65ff. for use of macrocarpa]]

>  We are familiar with the blue-gum and the <i>Pinus insignis</i>, with the ... <i>macrocarpa</i> ... <i>(1894,TrNZI,26/621</i>)

>  <b>REEVES</b> macro carpa cypress .... <i>(1898,Long White Cloud, The,19</i>)

>  <b>BELL</b> A plantation of pines and macrocarpa had already attained forward growth, and showed that this farm must have been one of the first in the locality to be cleared and improved. <i>(1899,In the Shadow of the Bush,19</i>)

>  The larva, which was feeding on <i>macrocarpa</i>, is very handsome. .... This larva is very inconspicuous amongst the foliage of the <i>Macrocarpo. </i> <i>(1900,TrNZI,32/11</i>) [NOTES: (Hudson)]

>  <b>SATCHELL</b> In the centre of the green plain was a group of white buildings, surrounded by a hedge of macrocarpa. <i>(1905,Toll of the Bush,5</i>)

>  <b>TANNOCK</b> Where ... there is plenty of room, the macrocarpa forms a quick-growing hedge ... <i>(1921,Manual of Gardening,18</i>)

>  The garden, being sheltered from the wind by thick macrocarpa, was warm even though patches of snow remained in shady places. <i>(1945,Korero (AEWS Background Bulletin),3(2) : 8</i>)

>  A large number of currents, pinus insignis, macrocarpas, and roses will also be submitted. <i>(Jun 5, 1885,Wairarapa Daily,2</i>)

>  Much of it was airborne, tangled in creaking macrocarpas where the root systems of the trees were heaving in the ground from the force of the gale blowing through their branches. <i>(Jan 15, 2005,Dominion Post,E15</i>)

>  They [sc. nurserymen] consider the Macrocarpa more suitable for shelter, and a quicker grower. <i>(Mar 1, 1879,Auckland Weekly News,13</i>)

>  The macrocarpa is the best shelter belt they have in Canterbury. <i>(Sep 24, 1887,Auckland Weekly News,32</i>)

>  <b>PERRY</b> <i>Cupressus macrocarpa</i>. - Macrocarpa - A very good shelter tree, but short lived on dry soils. <i>(1922,Sheep Farming in NZ,283</i>)

>  <b>DAVIN</b> They were in a quiet high place on the crown of the gebel, sitting under a broad macrocarpa. <i>(1947,For the Rest of Our Lives,385</i>)

>  <b>SLATTER</b> Bombarding the old widow's roof with indirect fire of stones from behind the high macrocarpa hedge. <i>Ibid.</i> 19 The macrocarpas and willows and bluegums are now giving place to oil tanks and engineering works and tanneries. <i>(1959,Gun in My Hand, A,10</i>)

>  <i>macrocarpa n</i> -s [NL <i>macrocarpa</i> (specific epithet of <i>Cupressus macrocarpa</i>), fr. <i>macr- + -carpa</i> (fr. Gk <i>karpos</i> fruit) - more at HARVEST] : a New Zealand evergreen shrub or tree (<i>Cupressus macrocarpa</i>) that is used for hedges and that sometimes causes abortion in cattle that browse on it. <i>(1961,Merriam-Webster Third International,1355</i>)

>  <b>BACON</b> <i> macrocarpa</i> - An introduced tree, Cupressus family, much used for high, sturdy breakwind hedges. <i>(1963,In the Sticks,181</i>)

>  <i> macrocarpa</i> [s] <i>Cupressus macrocarpa</i> Hartw. <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,45</i>)

>  macrocarpa n. a large New Zealand coniferous tree, used to form shelter belts on farms and for rough timber. [< Maori] [sic] [alleged Maori derivation: possibly based on 1961 Webster] <i>(1982,Collins Concise English Dictionary,676</i>) [NOTES: [cf. 1986 NZPOD 450; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 294]]

>  <b>Dr C. CORNE  Letter (Auckland)</b> <i>microcarpa </i>(= macrocarpa) is a similar deformation. <i>(Oct 14, 1983,Letter</i>)

 <i>n.</i>

>  Or perhaps you'd prefer shiny macropipers, rugged tussocks and sharp-toothed pseudopanax. <i>(Sep 16, 2001,Sunday Star Times,D8</i>)

### Mactown
 
 <i>n.</i> The US base at McMurdo Sound, Antarctica

>  "One of the several blessings of having been at the pole was to realise on my return to McMurdo how temperate the climate was at 'Mactown' - some days it even gets within a degree or two of zero Celsius. Positively balmy." <i>(Feb 13, 2000,NZ Catholic,11</i>)

### mactra
 
 <i>n.</i> the clam <i>Dosinia mactra</i>

>  [Hdg] The secretive surf clams, the larger shellfish showing its foot is mactra; the smaller is the spisula... The huge mactra surf clam, and the smaller triangular spisula are "new" in the sense that there is no tradition of eating them in New Zealand, despite their being indigenous to our islands. <i>(Jul 5, 2003,Dominion Post,F7</i>)

### mad as a cut snake
 
 <i>phr.</i>

>  <b>Geary</b> Mad as a cut snake, but a wonderful actress. <i>(2003,Man of the People, A,69</i>)

>  "Mad as a cut snake" is one of the many compliments showered on him by Mert officials... <i>(Sep 24, 1990,Listener (New Zealand Listener),8</i>)

### mad as a gumdigger's dog
 
 <i>phr.</i> very angry

>  Where do the sayings "Mad as a gum-digger's dog", "mad a a meat axe" and "Mad as a cut snake" come from? asks Barry Wheaton of Hobsonville... In New Zealand, from the mid-19th century, gumdiggers grubbed a living retrieving fossil kauri gum from the land and their dogs were often disparaged as skinny, useless or mad. <i>(Feb 14, 2004,Listener (New Zealand Listener),48</i>)

### mad as a maggot
 
 <i>phr.</i> very angry or mad [ca1935 Havelock] (See [cf. mad as a meat axe; go maggoty](../M#mad-as-a-meat-axe;-go-maggoty))

>  <b>BENNETT</b> The verbs <i>to go snaky, to go maggoty</i> (cf U.S. <i>maggoty</i>, whimsical) [mean to show anger or annoyance]. Phrases like `mad as a maggot', `mad as a meat-axe' are obviously linked with these expressions (some though not all of which may be heard in Australia; cf. Australian <i>snake-headed,</i> annoyed); whether <i>mad</i> in the sense of angry is a survival of an English provincialism, or has been introduced from America, is not clear. <i>(1943,English as it is spoken in NZ,90</i>)

>  <b>SUTHERLAND</b> `Good bloke,' said Bailey, `Mad as a maggot, of course,' ... <i>(1956,Green Kiwi,121,1960 ed.</i>)

>  <b>SLATTER</b> Mad as a maggot. Ya shoulda married her. <i>(1959,Gun in My Hand, A,43</i>)

>  <b>BAXTER</b> Young Jack Skully, mad as a maggot, singing out in the dark. <i>(1982,Collected Plays,24,1968 ed.</i>) [NOTES: (Wide Open Cage)]

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>mad as a maggot </i>very mad, very silly, very angry, very eccentric; Gordon slatter; eg `Old Millie's mad as a maggot, you know, hosing the lawn when it's raining, that sort of thing.' <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,71</i>)

### mad as a meat axe
 
 <i>phr.</i>

>  Where do the sayings "Mad as a gum-digger's dog", "mad a a meat axe" and "Mad as a cut snake" come from? asks Barry Wheaton of Hobsonville... Mad as a meat axe is out-of-your-mind angry. Frank S Anthony used the saying in his classic <i>Gus Tomlins</i> comic sketches of the 1920s. <i>(Feb 14, 2004,Listener (New Zealand Listener),48</i>)

>  <b>ANTHONY</b> Sally and Ann came in again with the tea. They looked as mad as meat-axes, thinking about the cat, but neither had the nerve to say anything. <i>(1927,Gus Tomlins,172,1977 ed. Sturm ed.</i>)

>  <b>DAVIN</b> Her mother's only got a room at the hotel. And she's mad as a meataxe anyway about the whole idea. <i>(1970,Not Here, Not Now,263</i>)

>  <b>Davin</b> We're just about worn out with it ... and the Colonel had come out, mad as a meat-axe, and saying there's your Labour Government for you. <i>(1947,For the Rest of Our Lives,76</i>)

>  <b>BENNETT</b> Equally meaningless, though of older antecedents. [`than mad as a maggot/meat-axe'], are `mad as one thing', `like one thing' (=`with great speed'). <i>(1943,English as it is spoken in NZ,90</i>)

>  <b>SLATTER</b> He went mad and they shot him mad as a meat-axe in Oamaru ... <i>(1959,Gun in My Hand, A,231</i>)

>  <b>MUIR</b> [interior monologue] Blue. The blue of your eyes. Coenhagen china, gentian smoke, sky, Reckitts, ... Henderson, you're as mad as a meat-axe. <i>(1960,Word for Word,55</i>)

>  <b>SUTHERLAND</b> `Mad as a meat-axe - best man Paramount ever had,' said Bailey enthusiastically. <i>(1956,Green Kiwi,120,1960 ed.</i>)

>  `Mr Ewarts Grave Mistake' by Stephen Walker Of course he was mad. Mad as a meataxe. ... died on the way to the [nut house]. <i>(Jan 11, 1985,Radio NZ / National Radio</i>) [NOTES: 2YA Wellington 10.30 a.m]

>  <b>HULME</b> She's as mad as a meat-axe, they say. Why do they say that? A meat-axe is a razor weapon ... <i>(1986,Te Kaihau,177</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>mad as a meataxe</i> very mad, very angry. NZA. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,71</i>)

### mad as a snake
 
 <i>phr.</i>

>  <b>TURNER</b> There is simile: `as mad as a snake', `as bald as a bandicoot' (an embroidery on the English `bald as a coot'), `as happy as larry', `rough as bags' (which I know better in the variant `rough as sacks'), `as wet as a shag'. <i>(1966,English Language in Aust. & NZ, The,115</i>)

>  <b>MASON</b> <i>Roar of rage from DRINKER. RANGI runs off, stands a moment. </i>RANGI: Thomas a[a-grave] Becket! Fuck! <i>He gives the fingers,</i> DRINKER 1: Mad as a bloody snake. They all are! Scratch them - <i>(1974,Hand on the Rail, The,27,1981 ed.</i>)

### mad as a wet hen
 
 <i>phr.</i> very angry or crazy

>  Hunterville ladies are madder than a wet hen with a nasty male thing in their town who announced in the papers his readiness to take unto himself a wife. <i>(Sep 2, 1893,Observer (Auckland),11</i>)

>  A good deal oftener it is necessary, but anyway he does it, she's mad as a wet hen, and she tells him what she thinks of him. <i>(Jun 25, 1901,Wanganui Chronicle,4</i>)

>  "What's the matter, Rastus? You seem as mad as a wet hen." <i>(Mar 21, 1930,Evening Post,21</i>)

>  It was plain enough that they had had a row, and that the wife was mad as a wet hen. <i>(Oct 8, 1885,Daily Telegraph,4</i>)

>  <b>CROSS</b> I really felt as mad as a wet hen, although I didn't quite know why. <i>(1958,God Boy, The,63</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> `What a lot of twaddle!' I said. ... `Mad as a wet hen in a thunderstorm. Poor people, Dick,' said Ted. <i>(1976,Pacific Star,8</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i> mad as a wet hen</i> angry and very silly simultaneously. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,71</i>)

### Mad Dog
 
 <i>n.</i> nickname for Richard Prebble MP

>  [Caption] Richard Prebble was once caricatured as 'Mad Dog Prebble', but colleagues agree he has mellowed over the years... Political cartoonists loved him. That was during the era when he earned the nickname Mad Dog. A few flourishes of the pen and there was Richard Prebble - a crazed canine, confined to a dog collar, mouth bared in a wild snarl. <i>(Apr 28, 2004,Dominion Post,B5</i>)

### mad money
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>PARTRIDGE</b> [The] following [was] employed by those who served in the [Great] War ...<i> mad money,</i> return fare, it being very generally believed by the New Zealand troops (<i>Fernleaves </i>or, as they preferred to call themselves, <i>Diggers</i>) that every English girl infallibly carried her return fare in case her soldier friend became <i>mad</i>, i.e., acted with excessive freedom of manner; <i>(1935,Slang Today,285</i>) [NOTES: 1935 Mitchell and Strong in Partridge Slang Today 285]

>  <b>BAKER</b> Terms originated in the first Great War [include] ... <i>mad money</i>, a return fare carried by a soldier's feminine acquaintance in case she `got mad' because of too-amorous attention. <i>(1941,NZ Slang,50</i>)

>  <b>PARTRIDGE</b> <i>Mad money</i>. A girl's return fare, carried lest her soldier friend got `mad', i.e. too amorous for her: New Zealand soldiers': 1916-18. Mostly a legend, and concerning only English girls. -->[Supplement] Has, since ca1918, meant money that a girl carries as a precaution against any such quarrel with her boy friend as might leave her monetarily stranded, and been widely used also in Australia and Canada. <i>(1938,Dictionary of Slang,503</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>mad money</i> splurge money; originally return ship fare WWI soldiers believed English girls brought with them in case their Kiwi soldier boy went mad, ie acted with excessive freedom. <i>[Baker] <.i> <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,71</i>)

### MAdGE
 
 <i>abbr.</i> Mothers Against Genetic Engineering

### mado
 
 <i>n.</i> a small sea-fish found in northern New Zealand and Australian waters. [Australian Aboriginal]

>  <b>MORRIS</b> <i>Mado, n.</i> A Sydney fish, <i>Therapon cuvieri</i>, Bleek; called also <i>Trumpeter-Perch. Atypus strigatus,</i> Gunth., is also called <i>Mado</i> by the Sydney fishermen, who confound it with the first species. The name is probably aboriginal. <i>(1898,Austral English,277</i>)

>  <b>PHILLIPPS</b> <i>Atypichthys strigatus </i>(Guenther). MADO. <i>(1927,Bibliography of NZ Fishes,37,1971 ed.</i>)

>  <i>mado n</i>. Sydney fish [Prob. Abo]. <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Whitcombe's),619</i>)

>  <b>mor</b> MADO [<i>Atypichthys strigatus </i>] ... No other names are in use. <i>(1967,Maori Place Names,34</i>)

>  <b>DOOGUE & MORELAND</b> MADO ... <i>Other names: Atypichthys strigatus. </i> <i>(1966,Sea Anglers' Guide,245</i>)

>  <b>NATUSCH</b> Family SCORPIDIDAE. This group includes ... <i>Atypichthys strigatus </i>, the mado ... , with its yellow and brown stripes, a fish not seen much south of East Cape. <i>(1967,Animals of NZ, The,221</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> MADO Close relative of the Sweep but easily distinguished by its distinctive yellow colour and brown longitudinal streaks. <i>(1981,Fisherman's Bible,162</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> SWEEP Good-eating, deep-bodied fish that belong to the family <i>Scorpidae</i>. The young are frequently caught in the shallow waters near wharves, ... One member of the family, the Mado, - figures prominently in the catches of saltwater fishermen seeking snapper and blue maomao. <i>(1981,Fisherman's Bible,245</i>)

>  <b>AYLING</b> <i>MADO</i> (Stripy) <i>Atypichthys latus (Atypichthys strigatus)</i> ... The mado is a small fish similar in shape and size to the sweep ... Until recently it was thought to be <i>Atypichthys strigatus </i> but examination of specimens of [231] ths species from Australia showed that this was not so and in fact the New Zealand mado proved to be another similar Australian species <i>Atypichthys latus. </i> <i>(1982,Collins Guide,231,1984 ed.</i>)

>  <i>mado n</i>. a small NZ and Australian fish with a brown and yellow streaked body. [Abor.] <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,450</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.]]

>  <b>PAUL et al.</b> [index to common names 283-293] mado 1492 <i>(1987,Fishes: A Bibliography</i>)

### madoo
 
 <i>n.</i> [Maori <i>matu</i>. <i>Reed's Concise matu</i>: fat <i>Williams matu</i>: 1. fat 2. richness 3. gist, kernel]

>  <b>Duncan Mackay</b> '... Sawing was especially difficult when there was a lot of gum in the tree trunk. The gum became hot from the action of the saw and made the saw blade sticky and hard to drive. The cross-cutters always had a tin of 'madoo', a mixture of kerosene and salt-free fat, which they used to lubricate the blade.' <i>(1991,Working the Kauri,27</i>)

>  <b>SALE</b> On the log, too, are the steel wedges which kept the cut open and prevented the saw jamming, and finally helped the tree on it's way down; the maul for driving the wedges; and the tin of madoo, a special mix of kerosene and salt-free dripping used to keep the saw blade free of gum (and was made up by the bush cooks). <i>(1978,Quest for Kauri,159</i>)

>  <b>HALKETT & SALE</b> Saws themselves were kept free of accumulations of sticky gum by being lubricated with "maddoo", a mixture of kerosene and salt-free dripping made up by the camp cook. <i>(1986,World of the Kauri, The,89</i>) [NOTES: [224] Madoo: A special mix of kerosene and salt-free dripping used to keep a saw blade free of gum. Also used to grease the skids of the "catamaran" used for hauling cut logs.]

### mafeesh
 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  <b>M & N ROSS</b> `Mafeesh', [the dying soldier] said quaintly <i>(1916,Light and Shade,16</i>)

>  Sa-eeda Ahmed, Akers, arrak,/ Bints, Bomonti. backsheech, barrak;/ Centipedes and Cleopatra,/ Dust and desert, djinns and Fatima;/ `Eggs-cook, ana miskeen,'/ Fever, flies, and felaheen;/ Gehenam, Galabeyah, gods,/ Harems, hooshters, heat and hods;/ `Igri wallah, imshi Y'allah.'/ Jackos, jackals, `gala gala';/ Khamseen, kan kan, tala hena,/ Lice and locusts, love and Lena;/ Mirage, Mahomet, mosquistoe,/ Niggers, T.A.B. and C2;/ Olives oranges, askarie./ Peanuts, pyramids, and gharry;/ `Queens' and `Quacks' and `k'weis kateer,'/ Ruins, rum, and Gyppo beer;/ Scorpion, sand, sun, sin, and Scarab,/ Tibbin, taube, and tricky Arab;/ Whizz-bangs, wadis, `yin-al-deenak,'/ Yids, and yesterday, yeh meenak;/ Kestrels, kingfishers, and kites,/ Torrid days and sleepless nights;/ Mosques and minarets--maleesh;/ `O'ariglak,' `feluce--mafeesh'--/ Bookra, badein, ack-ack-emma,/ The soldier's farewell--ma-es salemma; <i>(Jul 10, 1919,Quick March,33</i>)

### mag
 
 <i>v.</i> to `fast-talk'

>  The graft was as follows: The owners of the horse thought their boy could mag pretty well, so they cross-kidded to him, by explaining that it would be folly to back the horse here but better wait for the meeting. <i>(Apr 18, 1908,NZ Truth,148/2/6</i>)

>  We'd boiled the billy together - magged a little about the recent war. <i>(1967,Return to Open Country,195</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i>

 <i>n.</i> a half-penny

 <i>n.</i>

### maggie
 
 <i>n.</i> a (young) woman, esp. one in black stockings (See [cf. crow](../C#crow))



 
 <i>n.</i> a raft (from mokihi)

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> They frequented this track and the waters of the Waiau which they ventured upon in maggies or rafts of flax stalks. <i>(1959,Early Travellers,340</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> the ace of hearts in the card-game of FORTY-FIVES.

>  <b>McGILL</b> Old Calamity was an early one-man band of fiddle, horn, cymbals and kettledrums. Gambling was poker of 45s, a diggers' card game from California involving legs, queer suits and Maggie, cries of `Get Jinx!' and `Go for the Doctor!' <i>(1980,Ghost Towns of NZ,127</i>)

>  In smoky little back parlours [in Westport] friendly little groups meet to play `forty-fives' with uproarious oaths and much heavy thumping of the table. For [100] the uninitiated it may be stated that in this game the <i>five-fingers</i> (the five of the trump suit) beats all other cards. The trump suit order is five, jack, ace, king queen - all other suits begin with the king. Non-trump suits win high in red; low in black. Obviously the ace of diamonds is the lowest card in the pack unless diamonds are trumps, in which case it is the fourth best. The word "obviously' in the preceding sentence is a little optimistic - as the ace of hearts (the Maggy) is always the third best trump. It is credibly reported (a) that the game is of Irish origin (b) that there are no written rules (c) that the game can only be learned by playing it, and (d) it is as charactistic of Westport as whitebait or coal. <i>(1948,Our Own Country,99</i>)

### magistrate
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  New Zealand legal words may have a substantial local history. One instance is magistrate. Initially, the term was a synonym for justice of the peace, as it was and largely still is in England. <i>(Jan 1999,NZWords,1</i>)

### magistrate's court
 
 <i>n.</i> a local court, with powers to try minor cases (summary jurisdiction), and to hold preliminary inquiries into more serious cases which may go to a higher court. In New Zealand now called a District Court. [magistrate's or magistrates'?]

>  Generally known simply as magistrates (or SMs) they were legally qualified and exercised increasingly important judicial functions in magistrates courts These had a general jurisdiction - civil, criminal and domestic - that set them apart from English magistrates courts which mainly dealt with petty crime and were presided over by justices of the peace. Magistrates courts were renamed district courts and their presiding judges district court judges in 1981, but the term magistrate in New Zealand denoted stipendiary magistrates rather than justices of the peace, who were and are usually referred to simply as justices or JPs. <i>(Jan 1999,NZWords,1</i>)

 <i>adj.</i> go crazy, silly, mad

>  Yes, poor Bob Gordon's gone magnune. Last I saw of him, he was in a sand cart, ... and humming in tune with its maddening melody. <i>(Apr 15, 1918,Kia-ora Coo-ee,2/18,1981 ed.</i>)

>  In this land two infections assail all comers and vie with one another for first place. These are known as Malaria and Balkan Tap respectively. On the first we need not dwell, but a little information about the latter may be of interest. A similar infection has been met with elsewhere, and in Gyppo Land is known as `magnune', whilst away in our own sunny island I think `dippy' about covered it. Here we have a more or less insidious form of the disease, and no one with six months' residence to his credit may hope to escape. There are two varieties of the disease, malignant and benign. In the latter form the symptoms are easily recognised, and are amenable to treatment; but with the malignant type the victims, quite unconscious of infection, blindly affirm their condition to be normal, This form of the malady has several unfortunate features and is incurable. The percentage in our unit is very high. <i>(Sep 15, 1918,Kia-ora Coo-ee,Second Series 3/12,1981 ed.</i>)

>  <i>magpie moth n b</i> : any of several Australian and New Zealand moths of the genus <i>Nyctemera</i> having hairy larvae that feed on many plants <i>(1961,Merriam-Webster Third International,1361</i>)

>  <b>FALLA et al.</b> Feeds mainly on insects, especially on the black hairy caterpillars of the Magpie Moth and often killed by cats among the cinerarias. <i>(1966,Birds of New Zealand,181</i>)

>  Magpie Moth (Nyctemera annulata) 1970: 2 1/2c: Definitive issue. (Later overprinted 4c.) The second moth is the common and conspicuous Magpie Moth, a day-flying member of the large family noctuidae. Its members include many destructive species, whose caterpillars are known as `army worms.' They sometimes appear in enormous numbers, causing considerable damage to crops and pastures. <i>(Sep 16, 1972,Press, The,11/7-9</i>)

### mahoe
 
 <i>n.</i> a small tree commonly found on the coasts or in second-growth bush [Maori mahoe[a-macron]]; also called hina-hina, whiteywood

>  <b>LEE & KENDALL</b> M|a e |oe; Name of a certain tree. <i>(1820,Grammar & Vocabulary of the Language of NZ,172</i>)

>  <b>YATE</b> Mahoe (<i>Melicytus ramiflorus</i>) has a thin, spiral and elegant leaf; ... The bark is smooth and light, ... <i>(1835,New Zealand (various authors/titles),49</i>)

>  <b>POLACK</b> The Mahoi [a-acute] is an elegant tree, growing to the height of fifty feet; its wood is light in substance, of a reddish hue, admitting when polished, of being converted into furniture. <i>(1838,New Zealand  (Polack),2/399</i>)

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> On its being remarked that he was going gray he said, yes, you don't see the kowarawara (an epihpyte) growing on the ngaio or mahoe, a tree which is of no value and never attains any size, but on the large and valuable timber trees, only such a the totara and rimu. I am a totara and pointing to a young man, he is a ngaio <i>(Jun 16, 1849,Journal,6/113</i>) [NOTES: [Of Mamaku of Otaki]]

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Melicytus<i> ramiflorus</i>, Forst. ... Nat. name, `Mahoe'[e-dieresis], <i>R.Cunn., Lyall</i>. ... A brittle, white-barked shrub or tree. <i>(1853,Flora NZ,I Flowering Plants 18</i>)

>  In the old days fungus was plentiful, thriving on the dead trunks of Whitey Wood (Mahoe) trees. <i>(1988,Tales of Pioneer Women,91</i>) [NOTES: Writing of 1940]

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> Their Maori names were unknown to me or I might have indulged my feelings crying monotonously when I was alone, "Mahoe! Mahoe! Mahoe!" (whiteywood). <i>(1971,Our Open Country,70</i>)

>  [Native name] Mahoe .. [Settlers' name] Mahoe .. [Family] Violaceae <i>(1902,Settler's Handbook of NZ, The,121</i>) [NOTES: 1886 T.Kirk in 1902 Settler's Handbook of NZ 121]

>  <b>FEATON</b> MELICYTUS RAMIFLORUS (<i>Forst</i>.) ... The `Hinahina', or `Mahoe'. - This shrub or small tree is abundant throughout the Islands, in lowland districts. <i>Ibid.</i> 177 Mahoe [Maori name] .. Whitewood [Settlers' name] .. Melicytus ramiflorus .. Small tree <i>(1889,Art Album NZ Flora,28</i>)

>  Mahoe (whitewood) .. Melicytus ramiflorus .. The `hinahina' of South Island; timber white, soft, used for gunpowder charcoal <i>(1890,PWD Catalogue of Timbers,11</i>)

>  <b>MORRIS</b> <i>Mahoe, n.</i> Maori name for the New Zealand Whitewood-tree, <i>Melicytus ramiflorus,</i> Forst., N.O. <i>Violarieae. </i> <i>(1898,Austral English,278</i>)

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> <i>(1855,Te Ika a Maui,447</i>)

>  <b>COWAN</b> Hence the name: <i>Patu</i>, to strike or kill; <i>mahoe</i>, the whitewood tree (<i>Melicytus ramiflorus</i>). <i>(1922,NZ Wars, The,1/306</i>)

>  <i>mahoe ... n</i>. N.Z. whitewood tree [Maori]. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),12</i>)

>  <i>mahoe n</i>. small bushy NZ tree with whitish bark. [M] <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,452</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.]]

>  <b>DAWSON</b> Other species which tend to be more wide ranging are mahoe (<i>Melicytus ramiflorus</i>), pigeonwood (<i>Hedycarya arborea</i>), toro (<i>Myrsine salicina</i>) ... <i>(1988,Forest Vines to Snow Tussocks,102</i>)

>  <b>COWAN</b> Here and there a little watercourse ... arboured over by a matted roofing of <i>kotukutuku,</i> ... and<i> mahoe</i> or whitewood and glossy-green <i>karaka. </i> <i>(1926,Travel in NZ,(Vol II South Island) 32</i>)

>  <b>LAING & BLACKWELL</b> In the South the plant is generally known as the <i>Ina-ina</i>, in the North it is called the <i>Mahoe</i>. On Banks Peninsula it is often called the<i> Cow-leaf</i>, as cows are very found of its foliage. Another common European name is <i>Whitey-wood. </i> <i>(1940,Plants of NZ,280</i>)

>  <b>LEVY</b> ... these gave protection to many secondary growths, including wineberry, lacebark, konini, mahoe, bracken fern, water fern, and later, blackberry. <i>(1951,Grasslands of NZ,13,1970 ed.</i>)

>  <b>HUNT</b> Our chief source of revenue was to gather in the bush fungus, which usually grew upon the dead and partly decayed stems of karaka and mahoe trees, dry it in the open air, and sell it to the Chinese merchants ... <i>Ibid.</i> 136 It has in the Mahoe (<i>Melicytus ramiflorus</i>) a violet which is a tree, ... and a forget-me-not (<i>Myosotidium nobile</i>) with leaves as large as those of a rhubarb. <i>(1951,Confessions,20</i>)

>  <b>McLINTOCK</b> Mahoe, whiteywood .. mahoe .. <i>Melicytus ramiflorus </i> <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,3/705-712</i>)

>  <i>mahoe n</i>. the New Zealand whitewood tree [Maori]. <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Whitcombe's),619</i>)

>  Underbush before breakfast and then felled all day. I found a pigeon on its nest in a mahoi ... <i>(Jan 6, 1854,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,1960,1/141 ed.</i>) [NOTES: (A.S. Atkinson)]

>  <b>MOSER</b> [T]he graceful ko-whai (Edwadsia[sic] Microphylla) droops over the banks of a small creek ..., intermingled with the Koro-miko (Veronica) in full flower, and the fresh leaved Mahoe (Melicytus ramiflorus). <i>(1863,Mahoe Leaves,5</i>)

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Mahoe, <i>Col[enso]. Melicytus ramiflorus. </i> <i>(1867,Handbook NZ Flora (List of Names),766</i>)

>  Hinihini or Mahoe (<i>Melicytus ramiflorus</i>). <i>Ibid.</i> 39 Mahoe Melicytus ramiflorus, Forst. <i>(1869,TrNZI,1/III/37</i>) [NOTES: (Buchanan Botany Otago)]

>  <b>BAINES</b> Mahoe is the best for this purpose [rubbing for fire-making] ... <i>(1874,Edward Crewe,234</i>)

>  <b>HECTOR</b> <i>Melicytus ramiflorus</i>, Forst. Mahoe, Hinanhina.--A small tree, 20-30 feet high; trunk often angular, and 7 feet in girth. <i>(1883,Handbook of New Zealand,130</i>)

>  As a cousin to the commonly seen Melicytus ramiflorus or mahoe, the flowers and fruit are the only clues to that link. While mahoe is a small tree that has large leaves and grows in the bush, Melicytus crassifolius occupies quite a different niche. <i>(Oct 2, 2004,Dominion Post,E13</i>)

### mahue
 
 <i>n.</i> a bony barely edible flatfish, also called witch, megrim .

>  Megrim ...Brill, Mahoa, Witch <i>Caulopsetta scapha </i> <i>(1911,Records of the Canterbury Museum,1/197</i>) [NOTES: Waite]

>  17. <i>Caulopsetta scapha</i> Forster. Megrim or Witch. Maori: Mahoa <i>(1929,TrNZI,61/'142</i>) [NOTES: (Young)]

>  <b>GRAHAM</b> NEW ZEALAND MEGRIM or WITCH (MAHUE)<i> Caulopsetta scaphus</i> (Bloch and Schneider) In Napier it is known as Lantern-fish, but for what reason I am unable to say, unless it is from the peculiar transparency when it/is held up to a strong light. Another name given to this useless fish by local fishermen, in Port Chalmers at least, was that of Cadger's-fish. <i>(1956,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,186</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> MEGRIM ... Sometimes called Lantern-fish (because a lantern could be easily shone through it) and known to the Maoris as `mahue' - the fish that is left behind. Known to Christchurch fishermen in early years as the Bludger's Fish. It was the fish they handed out to well-known bludgers who came down to the docks to cadge fish. <i>(1981,Fisherman's Bible,74</i>)

>  Ironically, the most abundant of all New Zealand flounders, the witch or megrim, is useless for eating because of its multitude of long thin hair-like bones and thin watery flesh. The Maori name for this fish - mahue - says it all. It translates as `to be left behind'. <i>(Dec 8, 1982,Evening Post,37</i>)

>  <b>AYLING</b> <i>WITCH </i>(Megrim, mahue) <i>Arnoglossus scapha</i> ... The witch is a thin-bodied left eye floundr with an oval shape ... However, they have no commercial value as they have thin watery flesh and many fine bones. <i>(1982,Collins Guide,307,1984 ed.</i>)

 <i>n.</i>

### mahuta
 
 <i>n.</i> the last strand of a coal-mining pillar

>  <b>HAWES</b> As you worked at the pillar - picking, shotfiring, shovelling an' that - it got thinner an' thinner until in the end it looked like a big black tornado, or a tree holding up the sky. That's why the last strand of the pillar is called a 'mahuta'. Things got very tricky at this stage, because the coal in the mahuta was amazingly easy to win; just one gentle prod with yer pick and the coal just blew out... It was tempting to have one last go at the easy stuff in the mahuta, but get it wrong and you brought the roof down on yer head. <i>(2000,Dream of Nikau Jam, The,85</i>)

 <i>n.</i> <i>Podocarpus spicatus</i>, matai
