### mai-tao
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  ... amongst which [in Queen Charlotte Sound] there are two [trees] that bear a sort of plumb the size of prunes, the one yellow [[Anderson's fn a] These are call'd Karraka by the natives.] and the other black, [[Anderson's fn b] Call'd Mai-tao by the Indians.] [1967 ed. comment sic [Tawa, <i>Beilschmiedia tawa</i>. The `Indian' name that Anderson gives is unknown to the dictionary, and may well be one of the many South Island Maori words that were never collected.]] <i>(Jan 1777,Cook's Journals,3/804</i>) [NOTES: 1777 Feb Anderson Journal in Cook Journals (1967) 3/804]

### maiberry
 
 <i>n.</i> the fruit of the mai (possibly the tree itself)

>  <b>ALLOM</b> ... maiberry or matahi is a small round fruit of the same colour as the tawaberry. <i>(1849,Letter</i>) [NOTES: in Earp. NZ Hbk [p.?]]

>  <b>ALLOM</b> <i>Maiberry, or Mataki</i>, is a small round fruit of the same colour as the <i>tawa </i>berry, ... <i>(1853,Letter,130</i>)

>  ... maiberry or matahi .... <i>(1849,Notes on NZ,2/30</i>) [NOTES: 1849 Notes in NZ (Letters from Settlers) 2/30]

### Maid Marian
 
 <i>n.</i> a native clematis with small, yellowish, fragrant fowers, <i>Clematis foetida</i>

>  <b>MARTIN</b> A common relative [of the bush clematis] <i>(C.foetida) </i>known as MAID MARIAN, contrary to its specific name, is a very fragrant clematis with small yellowish flowers. <i>(1961,Flora NZ,146,1929 ed.</i>)

### maiden
 
 <i>n.</i> short for maiden ewe [applied to ewes the expression appears I think in 1853 Earp NZ (or in his NZ HBK)]

>  238 four-tooth halfbred ewes (maidens) at 13s. ... <i>(Mar 17, 1894,Otago Daily Times</i>) [NOTES: 1894 Mar 17 Otago Daily Times in 1962 Evans Waikaka Saga 113]

>  Maiden merino ewes ought on no account to be put to long-woolled rams of any kind. <i>(1902,Settler's Handbook of NZ, The,94</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> MAIDEN EWES: Normally ewes are first bred from as two tooths. However, in the high country conditions are such that sheep are slower maturing, and they are usually not bred from for another year. These unmated two tooths are referred to as `maiden' ewes. <i>(1949,High Country Days,195</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> <i>Maiden Ewe</i>: Normally ewes are first bred from as two-tooths. However, in the high country, conditions are such that sheep are slower maturing, and they are smetimes not bred from for another year. These unmated two-tooths are referred to as `maiden ewes'. <i>(1966,Boss's Story, The,188</i>)



 
 <i>adj.</i> applied to country; or on West Coast to a beach with gold-bearing BLACK-SAND deposits not yet worked

>  Where what is called the `maiden beach' has all been cut away, it is hopeless to expect a repetition of the `good old times' [of easy gold]. <i>(1906,Mining Handbook,124</i>)

>  <b>PFAFF</b> We went up the creek to prospect ... We pushed on into maiden country, i.e. the Right Hand Branch, and got very good prospects. <i>(1914,Diggers' Story, The,43</i>)

### maiden bush
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>McKAY</b> Since [bushmen] also said `maiden bush', not `virgin bush', that expression was also retained. <i>(1990,James K. Baxter,154</i>)

### maiden calver
 
 <i>n.</i> a cow calving for the first time

>  Each season in early spring a team, of certified inspectors travel New Zealand, inspecting maiden calvers for "traits other than production". <i>(May 31, 2002,Dominion,27</i>)

### maiden ewe
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>CAMPBELL</b> I was off to connections away beyond the Bathurst Plains [Aust], away to the Lachlan River, to initiate myself into the mysteries of `runs', `maiden ewes', `wethers', and `stock' in general, ... <i>(1881,Poenamo,1/2/38</i>)

### maiden heart
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  MAIDEN HEART A term used in Westland to denote the innermost part of the intermediate heart zone in rimu and miro. <i>(1952,NZ Forest Glossary</i>)

### maiden pink
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <i>maiden pink</i> [s] <i>Dianthus deltoides</i> L. <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,46</i>)

### maiden's blush
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>TURNER</b> Other terms of this kind are <i>barmaid's blush</i> or <i>maiden's blush</i> (a drink, either of port and lemonade or rum and raspberry, ... <i>(1966,English Language in Aust. & NZ, The,116</i>)

### maiden's water
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>TURNER</b> Maiden's water (freezingworkers') <i>(1966,English Language in Aust. & NZ, The</i>)

>  <b>COLIN GORDON</b> The <i>gutty</i> pulls down the <i>arse-cut</i> and <i>piss-bag</i>, and may get a spray of <i>maiden's water,</i> which is reputed good for the complexion. <i>(May 17, 1951,Personal Communication</i>)

### maidenhair fern
 
 <i>n.</i> [also as maidenhair]

>  Ferns such as the common maidenhair (<i>Adiantum cunninghamii</i>) and the velvet fern (<i>Lastreopsis velutina</i>) occur only in lowland or coastal forest, whereas <i>Microsorum novae-zelandiae</i> and the mountain tree fern (<i>Cyathea colensoi</i>) are confined to montane forest. <i>(2001,NZ Geographic,No. 49/76</i>)

>  Young foliage on many ferns, such as the rosy maidenhair, <i>Adiantum hispidulum</i>, has a decidedly reddish tinge, and in a few species the tips of unfurling fronds are blue. <i>(Jan 2001,NZ Geographic,No. 49/69</i>)

>  <b>POTTS</b> In days gone by, the maiden-hair stood high in the estimation of the herbalist... <i>(1882,Out in the Open,74</i>)

>  Toward the end of the walk, keep an eye out for the uncommon giant maidenhair fern (Adiantum formosum). <i>(Jan 2, 2003,Dominion Post,C2</i>)

>  An elegant spray, with black hair-like stems and beautiful small, round, quivering leaves.. is New Zealand's true maidenhair fern. <i>(May 11, 1922,Auckland Weekly News,15</i>)

>  Propagation of maidenhair ferns from spores is an easy and reliable method. <i>(Mar 2, 1938,Auckland Weekly News,86</i>)

### maidenhair tree
 
 <i>n.</i> [JD notes ?Ginkgo?? -]

>  <i>(Nov 1957,NZ Timber Journal,59/1</i>)

### maiheru
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>BURTON</b> Many types of fungus were eaten by the Maori. These were known by various names such as hawai, wairuru, tiki-tehetehe, maiheru, tawaka (a species of <i>Agaricus</i>) and hakeka (<i>Himeola</i>). <i>(1982,Two Hundred Years of NZ Food & Cookery,8</i>)

### maihi
 
 <i>n.</i> carved facing-boards on the gable of a Maori building. [Maori maihi[a-macron]]

>  <b>POLACK</b> We passed an extensive grove containing a Wai-tapu [a2-acute]. In this place was deposited the bones of a male and female chief ... The house which enclosed these remains of mortality was built of old canoes, ... [138] It was surmounted with a <i>maihi</i>, or frontispiece, which was decorated with feathers. <i>(1838,New Zealand  (Polack),1/137</i>)

>  <b>POLACK</b> Round the top a head-board (<i>maihi</i>) was affixed, on which a number of obscene figures were carved. <i>Ibid.</i> 1/206 Over the door-ways and window-sills, handsomely carved boards are fixed, painted in flaming red, these are placed horizontally or in a gable form called <i>maihi</i>, surmounted by a wooden image termed the <ik>taupare</i> [a2-acute] <i>warre</i> [e-acute]; some of these figures are exceedingly droll. ... [Caption] The Maihi, a carved board for the front of a house, surmounted by the Toupare [e-acute], or figure-head. <i>(1840,Manners & Customs of the NZers,1/123</i>)

>  The ridge-boards are carved to represent a number of<i> ngarara</i>, or lizards, running along the roof, and the <i>maihi</i>, or gable-boards, have carvings of the mythological animal known as the <i>manaia</i> - probably a kind of taniwha. <i>(1895,TrNZI,27/674</i>)

>  <b>TREGEAR</b> In front of the verandah of a house, short perpendicular slabs (<i>amo</i>), often carved or painted, supported the barge-boards (<i>maihi</i>) of the gable, and formed very striking and highly embellished adornments. <i>(1904,Maori Race, The,273</i>)

>  The carvings (all but the maihi) were finished by the Rotorua school some months ago ... <i>(1933,Letter</i>) [NOTES: Ngata; 1933 June 11 in 1988 Na To Hoa Aroha (Ngata-Buck Correspondence) 3/88]

>  <i>Maihi,</i> facing-boards joining a gable of Maori house, ornamented with incised carving, deeply incised at lower ends, even perforated: the koruru covers the join at gable, a grotesque figure surmounting the koruru: the upright supports at lower ends are amo. <i>(Jun 1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/153</i>) [NOTES: (J.C.Andersen)]

>  <b>BUCK</b> The barge-boards (<i>maihi) were wide planks which were dubbed out with a longitudinal flange (<i>papawai</i>) on the back near the lowere edge. <i>(1949,Coming of the Maori, The,129</i>)

>  <i>(May 1950,NZ Journal of Agriculture,502/2</i>) [NOTES: [OEDS]]

>  <b>MASON</b> Although the house lacks a central gable, there is a smaller one above the window, left. Some attempt has been made to turn this into a maihi - the carved boards of a Maori gable. <i>(1969,Awatea,15,1978 ed.</i>)

>  The ever-reaching strong hand of death is another image commonly spoken about at Maori gatherings today, but it is not clear whether the stylized hand portrayed on meeting house maihi (bargeboards), or on ko (spades), or on some very old examples of taonga have the same korero attached to them. <i>(1984,Te Maori,27</i>)

>  There is St Mary's, one of the most richly decorated Maori churches in New Zealand; the red maihi of a wharenui across the road. <i>(Jul 12, 2003,Listener (New Zealand Listener),61</i>)

>  <b>PHILLIPPS</b> The carved lower portion of the <i>maihi</i>, or bargeboard... is termed <i>raparapa</i>. <i>(1950,Maori Carving,6</i>) [NOTES: 2nd edition]

>  <b>PHILLIPPS</b> From this we may conclude that the maihi were cut and prepared in a timber-factory in Whanganui on order from the builders. <i>Ibid.</i> 135. On the other side we find a human figure near the lower maihi end... Spirals are absent, and borders of whakarare adorn the maihi. <i>(1955,Carved Maori Houses,107</i>)

>  <b>MEAD</b> Besides the very ornate maihi, amo and pae (threshold) it was not unusual for the complete front wall to be a mass of carvings.<i>Ibid.</i> 51 The maihi is plain throughout its length. <i>(1961,Art of Maori Carving, The,12</i>)

>  <b>BARROW</b> [Caption] A vertical panel, one of a pair supporting the left bargeboard (maihi) of the Arawa storehouse named Puwai-o-to-Arawa. <i>(1969,Maori Wood Sculpture,116</i>)

>  In the 1880's the amo and maihi passed into the hands of Archdeacon Henry Williams who in 1913 presented them to the Dominion Museum. <i>(May 2005,Kokiri Paetae,11</i>)

### maihoi
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>POLACK</b> There are many other woods of much service to Europeans, differing in quality, among others: the 30 <i>Warangai</i>.., - 34 <i>Maihoi</i>.. - 44 <i>Akkas</i> of various kinds. 45 <i>Parekireki</i>, &c. Of all these woods there are abundant quantities. <i>(1838,New Zealand  (Polack),2/400</i>)

### maikaika
 
 <i>n.</i>



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Thelymitra <i>pulchella</i>, Hook. fil. ... Nat. name, `Makaika', <i>Lyall. </i> <i>(1853,Flora NZ,I Flowering Plants 244</i>)

>  <b>CHEESEMAN</b> <i>[Thelymitra longifolia, Forst.] Makaika. </i> <i>(1906,Manual NZ Flora,670</i>)

>  <b>RICHARDS</b> <i>THELYMITRA ... longifolia</i> ... A ground orchid having a fleshy leaf ... Common thelymitra, Maori Potato. MAI KUKU (claw). <i>(1952,Chatham Islands,23</i>)

>  <b>MARTIN</b> The flowers of the MAIKUKU (<i>T[helymitra] longifolia</i>) vary much in colour ... . <i>(1961,Flora NZ,311</i>)

>  <b>BURTON</b> Types of rare orchids known as perei (<i>Gastrodia cunninghamii</i> and maikuku (<i>Thelymitra longifolia</i>) were greatly prized for their masses of thick edible tubers. <i>(1982,Two Hundred Years of NZ Food & Cookery,8</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Orthoceras <i>Solandri</i>, Lindl. ... Nat. name, `Makaika', <i>Colenso. </i> <i>(1853,Flora NZ,I Flowering Plants 243</i>)

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Maikaika, <i>Col[enso]. Orthoceras Solandri. </i> <i>(1867,Handbook NZ Flora (List of Names),766</i>)

>  <b>FULLER</b> [?Thelmitra] Perei and maikaika, types of orchis, were sought after. The perei had a mass of tubers which were cooked in the same way as aruhe. <i>(1978,Maori Food and Cookery,7</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> rengarenga

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Arthropodium <i>cirrhatum</i>, Br. ... Nat. name, `Renga Renga', in Northern Districts, and `Maikai Ka', in Southern, <i>Colenso. </i> <i>(1853,Flora NZ,I Flowering Plants 254</i>)

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Maikaika, <i>Col[enso]. Arthropodium cirrhatum. </i> <i>(1867,Handbook NZ Flora (List of Names),766</i>)

>  The blanched bases of the leaves of the harakeke (<i>Phormium</i>), and the roots of the rengrenga or maikaika (<i>Arthropodium cirrhatum</i>), were sometimes roasted and beaten to a pulp and applied warm to ... abcesses. <i>Ibid.,</i> 31 Besides which the following roots and plants were often eaten, viz., the roots (cooked) of the Panahi (<i>Calystegia sepium</i>); of the Maikaika (<i>Arthropodium cirrhatum</i>); ... the roots of the little sugary Ti-koraha (<i>Cordyline stricta</i>), of the large Ti, or `Cabbage Tree' (<i>C. australis</i>), and of the large Fern, Para, (Marattia salicina. <i>(1869,TrNZI,1/15</i>) [NOTES: 1869 Colenso TrNZI 1/1-58 (On the Botany of the North Island) in 1981 Brooker et al. NZ Medicinal Plants 15]

>  <b>BROOKER et al.</b> <i>Arthropodium cirrhatum</i> (Forst.f.) R.Br. Common name: <i>Rock lily</i> Maori names: <i>Rengarenga, maikaika, maika ka </i> <i>(1981,NZ Medicinal Plants,63</i>)

### mail
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  ("CABESE, or Taxispeak if you prefer") <i>mail for you in the front office</i> Usually means you're in trouble with the manager. <i>(Jul 1983,Metro,No. 25 81</i>)

### mail car
 
 <i>n.</i> a vehicle which delivered mail and papers; usually constr. with <i>the</i> = service car (specif. one which carries rural mail for household delivery (or occas. mailbags for delivery to post-ofices) as well as passengers)

>  <b>CRUMP</b> mailcar <i>(1961,Hang on a Minute, Mate,88</i>)

>  Now watch ... the Rosebud mail-car comin' down the zig-zag. <i>(Jul 10, 1919,Quick March,22</i>)

>  Martin heard the mail car go past. <i>(1943,NZ New Writing,1/55</i>) [NOTES: (Thomas `Unto Us")]

>  <b>GASKELL</b> She had returned from town at midday in the mailcar ... . <i>(1947,Big Game,48</i>)

>  <b>MARSH</b> The mail-car goes through at half past five. <i>(1945,Died in the Wool,25</i>)

>  <b>MASON</b> PERA. What time's the mail car? <i>(1969,Awards etc.,24,1978 ed.</i>)

>  <b>NEAVE</b> I was sometimes called upon [c1920s] to drive a mail car to Omarama and Benmore Station on mail days to take surplus passengers and small goods to relieve the bus load. <i>(1980,Land of Munros, The,46</i>)

>  [Caption] Mail cars line the front entrance of the building. <i>(Feb 2001,NZ Memories,44</i>)

### mail day
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a day on which newspapers, mail and provisions have to be collected from some distance, either weekly or monthly.

>  <b>BATES</b> Mail days were extra long. Jack would go off, usually with a pack horse after lunch. <i>(1988,Okoroa,22</i>)

### mail run
 
 <i>n.</i> a mail round in a country area, rural delivery

>  ...James planted many trees on his mail run, some of which survive, including the pines in front of the old Burkes Pass school... <i>(May 1994,NZ Historic Places,47/30</i>)

### mail stop
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) an intersection of rural roads or a station gateway where several residents mail is collected and/or delivered.

>  <b>KERR</b> I'll talk to you tomorrow, face to face, at the mail stop. <i>(2000,High Times in the High Country,49</i>)

### mail train
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>LEITCH</b> The final death knell of the provincial expresses - the old 'mail trains' that ran several times a week over all secondary main lines - was sounded by the arrival in 1954 of the first of Drewry Car Co's 88-seaters, generally called 'The Fiats' because of their twin Fiat 700.040 horizontal six-cylinder diesel motors. <i>(1972,Railways of NZ,195</i>)

### mail-cart
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Reached Young's early from having one man taken 12 miles in the mail-cart. <i>(Mar 6, 1863,Chudleigh Diary,80,1980 ed.</i>)

### mailbox
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  The woman beside the mailbox looked cold in her pale dress as Collins drove towards where she stood. <i>(Aug 7, 1999,Listener (New Zealand Listener),94</i>)

>  They all steam out into the foyer where kids are testing the locks on the tenants' mail-boxes. <i>(Nov 12, 1988,Listener (New Zealand Listener),58</i>)

>  <b>IRELAND</b> the name on the mailbox / fades away <i>(1987,Kevin Ireland : Selected Poems,19</i>)

### mailman
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) operator of a rural delivery service delivering mail, newspapers, bread and often groceries to farms and stations by truck, bus or boat. [May also select and change library books.] (See [rural delivery](../R#rural-delivery))

>  <b>STAPLES</b> The bread, papers and the mail were delivered at the gate by the rural mailman. <i>(1964,Cooks and Shepherds Come Away,12</i>)

>  <b>MCMILLAN</b> The men who drive the stock trucks and the mailman with his bus ... <i>(1981,Tales of the High Country,41</i>)

### maimai
 
 <i>n.</i> a duckshooter's shelter or hiding place usually made of (manuka) brushwood [1845. R. Howitt, `Australia,' p. 103: -->1. (obs) a rough temporary bush shelter quickly put together from branches, etc. (Perhaps originally thought to refer to Maori shelters, thence in early colonial use, thence by trampers and mountaineers); a bush hut]

>  <b>GUNBY</b> Rolls and rolls of crepe paper would be draped to provide a room that looked like a series of "mai-mais" around the periphery of the hall. <i>(1984,Rangiora High School 1884-1984,185</i>)

>  <b>BUTLER</b> The few Maories that inhabit this settlement travel to the west coast by way of this river. They always go on foot, and we saw several traces of their encampments - little <i>mimis,</i> as they are called - a few light sticks thrown together, and covered with grass, affording a sort of half-and-half shelter for a single individual. <i>(1863,First Year in Canterbury Settlement,v/72</i>)

>  <b>WRIGHT</b> They were big rough sheep on the Maimai, fit to make a shearer's heart / Settle down about his boot-tops on the morning of the start. <i>Ibid.</i> 37 But he wouldn't shear at Maimai. <i>(1897,Station Ballads,35</i>)

>  The weather was appalling and every time we lifted our heads above the brush parapet of the maimai we were met with blasting sleet and salt water which stung the eyes and numbed cheeks and any other exposed extremities. <i>(Aug 9, 2003,Dominion Post,F8</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> Perhaps it was this [sc. the Ring of God's blessing] which kept the old maimai church intact for such a long time. <i>(1979,Titi Heritage,98</i>) [NOTES: NB- The term mai-mai is used in Wilson for the makeshift huts on the mutton-bird islands - does it deserve a separate entry from shooting mai-mais in which case I may have to get a further cit.?]

>  Chew into a bit of possum, muttonbird, thar, goat, eel and kangaroo during the Monteith's Wild Food Challenge which started yesterday... It's all in aid of celebrating our pioneer roots, and a lot easier than going bush with a couple of dogs and a knife or sitting in a freezing maimai hoping for unwitting feathered shot-gun fodder. <i>(Jun 1, 2002,Evening Post,2</i>)

>  An instance of the use of such a `mai-mai' [a frame-work lean-to made with saplings; with ferns laced in-and-out for a roof, while the ends are blocked with scrub or fern. `We then stretch a fly over this (or perhaps under) allowing it to overlap some four feet in front, to cover the fire"] was seen at the snout of the Fox Glacier. <i>(May 1895,NZ Alpine Journal,2/7/24</i>) [NOTES: The ref is obscure. This is a guess at its placve of publication but there wouldn't have been an Alp[ine Journal then. Possibly from Harper 1896 Pioneer Work in the Alps of NZ]

>  <b>MOFFAT</b> [I] stripped some bark off a cedar or totara tree [in Nelson], I am not sure which, built a maimai big enough to crawl into, managed to get a fire, caught and roasted a woodhen, boiled some flour in my pannikin, ... [c 1860]. <i>(1896,Adventures by Sea and Land,61,1979 ed.</i>)

>  <b>HARPER</b> ... it is really not necessary to carry canvas [on the West Coast] unless ... one is fastidious as to shelter. It is always possible to build a good `whare'[e-acute] or `mai-mai' with bark stripped from the rata (<i>Metrosideros robusta</i>), totara ..., or cedar (<i>Libocedrus Bidwillii</i>) trees ... <i>(1896,Pioneer Work in the Alps of NZ,35</i>)

>  <b>MORRIS</b> <i>Mia-mia, n</i>. an aboriginal hut. The word is aboriginal, and has been spelt variously. <i>Mia-mia</i> is the most approved spelling, <i>mi-mi</i> the most approved pronunciation. See <i>Humpy.</i> <i>(1898,Austral English,291</i>)

>  <b>VAILE</b> <i>Mai-mai</i> a covert <i>(1939,Pioneering the Pumice,299</i>)

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> Another term brought over from Australia [from the 1860's on] was "maimai' for a rough shelter from the weather. It was said to be a name for a black-fellow's camp, which I have always seen spelt `miamia', and which is called `gunyah' or `wurley', etc. by some tribes. We now apply the name to shooting shelters. <i>(1947,Early Runholding in Otago,10</i>)

>  <b>SLATTER</b> Bert looking out the porthole at the wide Pacific and saying not a duck to be seen, what a helluva mai-mai this is. <i>(1959,Gun in My Hand, A,224</i>)

>  <b>McCALLUM</b> It gets plenty cold in the mai mai early in the morning [duckshooting] <i>Ibid.</i> 98<i>Maimai</i> Duck shooter's place of concealment. <i>(1963,Half-Gallon Jar,39</i>)

>  <b>BAXTER</b> ... More than a shroud/ He needs the maimai's breast of shade,/ ... when loud/ On ragged flights the shotguns played - <i>(1979,Collected Poems,304</i>) [NOTES: 1961-64) 1979 Baxter Collected Poems 304]

>  <b>McLEOD</b> <i>Mimi</i>: Corruption of Maori word for a hide or shelter. [sic] <i>(1972,Mountain World,310</i>)

>  <b>BAXTER</b> (note)<i> maimai</i> word of Maori derivation by which New Zealand duck-shooters refer to the shelter of green branches constructed by the hunters for camouflage. <i>(1979,Collected Poems,632</i>)

>  <b>LELAND</b> ~ There you are, crouching, cold, wet(?), and expectant in your little hut among the reeds next to your favorite lake. Duck season is about to open and you await the sunrise and the game in your DUCK BLIND. <i>(1980,Personal Kiwi-Yankee Dictionary, A,61</i>)

>  <b>BAXTER</b> <i>maimai</i> hide used by duckshooters <i>(1982,Selected Poems,188</i>)

>  <i>mai mai n. N.Z.</i> a duck-shooter's shelter; hide. [< Maori] [sic] <i>(1982,Collins Concise English Dictionary,680</i>) [NOTES: [1986 NZPOD 452; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 296]]

>  <b>SANSOM</b> ... these small fern-tree mai-mais were Frank Woodrow's dog kennels. <i>(1982,In the Grip of an Island,152</i>)

>  They laugh irreverently at the duck-shooting hat that their father offers them - they have no interest whatsoever in the mai mai that was painstakingly cobbled together by their grandfather, and they are far more interested in the opening date of the arts festival than the duck-shooting season. <i>(Feb 26, 2002,Evening Post,25</i>)

>  We encamped for the night in an old maimai. <i>(Apr 22, 1924,Otago Witness,65</i>)

>  With duck-shooting staring the same day, drinkers downing shooters at The Maimai are unfazed by the regular sound of "duck!" followed by a gun blast. <i>(Jun 11, 2002,Dominion,15</i>)

>  A dreary swamp next succeeded, beside which were a few <i>kaiks</i>, the mia-mias of the Maoris, who were here for the eeling season. <i>(1861,NZ Goldfields,23,1976 ed.</i>) [NOTES: 1861 Sep 27 Tuapeka in NZ Goldfields 1861 (1976) 23 [?mistake for whare: ? an Australian reporter]]

>  <b>HEINZ</b> ... he was seen there on 14 May 1866 ... Months later some diggers at Paringa found a mai mai shelter and the remains of two Maori hens (wekas) hanging on a pole. On the ground inside the mai mai was a copy of Wilkie Collins's novel ... <i>(1974,Bright Fine Gold,54</i>) [NOTES: [c1866] Okarito Times in 1974 Heinz Bright Fine Gold 54]

>  <b>FARJEON</b> [In the New Rush] pipes were lighted, and tents and mi-mis hastily thrown up, beneath which the nomades [sic] rested their weary limbs ... <i>(1866,Grif: a Story of Colonial Life,133</i>)

>  <b>WHITWORTH</b> We rigged up a mimi of branches and fern on the flat. <i>(1870,Martin's Bay Settlement,39</i>)

>  <b>MONEY</b> The next day I began building a little `mi-mi,' to serve as a resting place for the night in going back at any time for supplies; ... <i>(1871,Knocking About in NZ,42</i>)

>  <b>ST JOHN</b> In the days of bush fighting it used to be a common occurrence at the end of a day's march, when <i>maemaes</i> had been knocked up by the side of a stream, to see ... men gravely set to work with pannikin ... and `wash' for a prospect. ... [Orig. footnote] A low hut worked up with sticks and interlaced raupo or fern, open in front, with roof reaching to the ground on the windward side. [NB. There were Australian `Volunteers' in the later Maori Wars] <i>(1873,Pakeha Rambles Through Maori Lands</i>) [NOTES: 1873 St. John Pakeha Rambles in 1959 Taylor Early Travs. 555]

>  <b>BODELL</b> We made a Miamia (a small house) composed of sticks tied together and the leaves of the Nikau Tree. <i>(1890,Soldier's View of Empire, A,171,1982 ed.</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>mai mai</i> makeshift shelter of sticks and grass employed by Maori, latterly duckshooters' hide; Macquarie identifies as Aboriginal, yet not used in Australia. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,71</i>)

>  Other evidence that the Maoris camped in this locality was the remains of what I believed were called mai-mais. These were shelters made by plaiting the tips of the leaves of a large flax bush in such a manner as to form an arched canopy. <i>(1914,History of North Otago,43,1978 ed.</i>)

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> In 1851 a number of Australian `squatters' came over and took up runholding in Canterbury, but, generally speaking, their advent was some years later in Otago. ... They brought over a number of Australian aborigine words that were new to this side, and which were used a certain amount, usually in a joking manner as far as I know. I have heard some of them fairly frequently used by the `old hands,' sometimes oracularly and sometimes as a semi-humorous quotation. When I was a boy I heard sheep called `jumbucks,' an Australian aborigine term for them, but with the decease of ex-Australian stockmen the word has gone out of use now. Another term brought over from Australia was `maimai,' for a rough shelter from the weather. It was said to be a name for a blackfellow's camp, which I have always seen spelt `miamia,' and which is called `gunyah' or `wurley,' etc., by some tribes. We now apply the name to shooting shelters. <i>(1947,Early Runholding in Otago,10</i>)

>  <b>HALL-JONES</b> Many a pioneer camped under a tree or a flaxbush while he made a clearing and planted his seeds. The next stage was a maimai, a crude shelter of boughs still used by duckshooters. <i>(1945,Historical Southland,107</i>)

>  <i>mia mia ... n.</i> aboriginal hut, gunyah [Abo]. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),13</i>)

>  <b>McLEOD</b> At the junction of the Wills I built myself a `mimi' and stopped for the night.... I collected a lot of driftwood and made a good fire in front of my mimi. <i>(1972,High Country Anthology,276</i>) [NOTES: 1972 M. Huddlestone `Tale of Murder' in 1972 Mcleod High Country Anthology 276-277]

>  <b>HUNT</b> First of all they just built a maimai : we stopped in it first night we went there. <i>(1981,Speaking a Silence,104</i>)

>  <b>OVERTON</b> ...with the guns all cleaned and made ready the previous night, we set off for the mai-mais... The general practice was for two men to occupy the mai-mais with perhaps one single position some distance off. <i>(1949,Fifty Years Sheep Farming,75</i>)

>  <b>KELLY</b> The <i>maimai</i> was nearly a dugout, a trench about four feet deep by two feet wide and about six feet long. <i>(1968,Gun in the Hills,127</i>)

>  <b>HUNT</b> First they just built a maimai. <i>(1981,Speaking a Silence,104</i>)

>  He has built a maimai, recessed into a hillside and fronted with a scrubby palisade of toetoe and tea tree. <i>(Apr 17, 2004,Dominion Post,E11</i>)

>  <b>SMITH</b> Having selected your pond you proceed to build a Mai-Mai... You creep through the darkness to your Mai-Mai, armed to the teeth with guns, cartridges, decoys, stimulants, refreshments and stimulants <i>(1949,From N to Z,127</i>)

>  Any work on the maimais must be finished before "pegging" day. That is the day when all the other hunters are building their maimais for the season... He personally.. plans the siting and design of the maimais... Horrie and various friends put in a lot of time repairing and maintaining the maimais. <i>(1987,Country Calendar (TVNZ),28</i>)

>  <b>Owen Marshall</b> Ollie was remembering the apiarist's father who'd got shot by a neighbour years before in an argument over maimai possies. <i>(2005,Watch of Gryphons,248</i>)

>  The gallant Aucklander who returns with a large bunch of ducks has paid 30s for his mia mia [sic] and 12s per day for his Maori "guide". <i>(May 17, 1913,NZ Observer,17</i>)

>  We passed a swamp that in the past had been dotted with tidy maimais. <i>(May 29, 2004,Dominion Post,E16</i>)

>  Nearer to our maimai, the whistle of wind through the wings and the chuckling call of mallard heralded their arrival near our decoys. <i>(Jun 7, 2003,Dominion Post,F9</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) [Aus.] 1. a simple shelter, usually made from sticks collected from the local area. [ORIGIN: Aboriginal]

>  <b>HAYES</b> Frank and John McCaw, Alpheus, and John Tripp from Glen Cary Station had a mai-mai on Lake Waitaki where they shot ducks each season. <i>(1978,From the Toss of a Coin,118</i>)

>  <b>MCNAB</b> I tell you Johnston, these big houses are a curse. I wish I were back in a maimai, with a plate of bark and a Dover. <i>(1858,BLANK ENTRY,5</i>) [NOTES: in Wilson & Fraser Book of the Pioneers (1936)]

>  <b>MACGIBBON</b> His abode was a maimai of the simplest kind, being merely two forked sticks, set up seven feet apart, and a ridge pole placed resting on the forks. Over this a blanket was thrown and pegged down to the ground, leaving both ends open. 2. <i>(Jul 17, 1858,Southland Times</i>) [NOTES: in Beattie 1937]

>  .. thousands of hunters throughout New Zealand will sit in cold maimais, watch the sky lighten and wait with excitement as the new gamebird hunting season begins. <i>(Apr 26, 2002,Dominion,28</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> a hut (?esp. thatched, or made of raupo) [more solid or permanent than 1 [also mi mi.]

>  <b>MONEY</b> ... at the junction of the Huranui [Canterbury] with the waters of the lake, we found a diminutive wharry or grass hut, just big enough for four persons to crawl into and pack close; near this was a `mi-mi', or shelter of a different description, to be used as a safe repository for flour and other necessaries, besides paper or any articles that might attract rats. <i>(1871,Knocking About in NZ,28</i>)

>  [?Aust ref.] [Alexander McNab: had Australian experience] This `big house' [built of slabs in 1860] ... was very draughty and McNab at a time when he was suffering from a bronchial ailment, was heard to remark ..., `I tell you what, Johnson, these big houses are a curse. I wish I were back in the maimai with a plate of bark and a run of the dover'. <i>(1984,Knapdale Run, The,18</i>) [NOTES: [a1890 in] 1984 Knapdale Run 18]

>  The ivy on the mimi there is curtained to a screen. <i>(1896,Tom Bracken's Annual,[No. 1 1896; No.2 1897 check]  37</i>)

>  When I'm sitting in my mi-mi / Where the log-fire brightly burns. <i>(1906,Red Funnel,2/12</i>)

>  <b>WRIGHT</b> MAIMAI. - Possibly the name for an old sheep station, although I have not been able to trace one with that name. `Maimai' is, however, the name for an out of way hut; there was one on the Dunstan Range many years ago and another is on Benmore Station. <i>(1945,Station Ballads,63</i>)

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> Their mother used to relate how when the Crawfords came they found only one habitation on the run and it was a decidedly primitive one, being built on the principle of an Australian aboriginal maimai. <i>(1947,Pioneer Recollections,29,1956 ed.</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> The biggest thatched house (Mai Mai) ever built was at Solomon's Point and was still standing in 1934. <i>(1979,Titi Heritage,86</i>)

### main
 
 <i>n.</i> the main course of a meal [earlier this entry was headed 'mains', with the suggestion in a note that this developed as a singular form; but none of the citations below illustrates clearly a singular meaning of 'mains' (TD)]

>  Another main of skin-on crisp-roasted terakihi brought on the pleasantries, the aniseed flavour of the poached fennel perfuming things subtly. <i>(Jul 16, 2005,Dominion Post,E8</i>)

>  Soon we had our mains, and my second entree which I don't believe had been reheated and re-presented. <i>(Sep 2000,North & South,125</i>)

>  The menu.. is pretty basic - three appetisers, three entrees, five mains, four desserts. <i>(Dec 1982,Metro,22</i>)

>  At first sight it all looks cheap, with mains a standard $10, entrees and desserts $4. <i>(Aug 1986,Wellington City Magazine,87</i>)

>  For my own main I splurged out on the Great India Special for One - Murg Massallum. <i>(Sep 1986,Wellington City Magazine,80</i>)

>  For my main I chose the fish. <i>(Mar 1988,Metro,169</i>)

>  The mains came with sauteed spinach and pumpkin, their price (and GST) happily included in the cost of the meal. <i>(Jun 1993,Metro,42</i>)

>  Some Kiwi habits seem hard to shake off, though - mains come with a separate serving of vegetables. <i>(Jul 1995,Cuisine,106</i>)

>  The use of the word <i>mains</i> in the food sense seems to be a peculiarly New Zealand habit. <i>(Nov 16, 1996,Dominion,18</i>)

>  There are also six mains ranging in price from $15.50 .. to $19.50. <i>(Nov 21, 1996,Evening Post,23</i>)

>  <b>GUY</b> Mains such as oven-roasted venison steak with pumpkin and port wine chutney .. make a memorable meal. <i>(1995,Eating Out & Wine Guide,19</i>)

>  Mains included Paella Valenciana... Anyone about to broach a main is advised to avoid a large starter. <i>(Nov 28, 1996,Evening Post</i>)

>  And for mains (the Americans call them entrees), poached Maine lobster with spinach flan, wild mushroom ravioli and champagne sauce and sauteed beef tenderloin with roasted garlic potatoes, grilled Portabello mushroom, black beans and tomato scallion sauce. <i>(May 12, 2001,Dominion,7</i>)

>  In the Nga kai nui department (mains), Red sought the safety of fish (ika); gurnard, in mighty fresh chunks, balances on a mound of mash. <i>(Feb 28, 2004,Dominion Post,E6</i>)

>  The braised lamb shanks "served on garlic and herb mash with light pan jus", like the "creamy mushroom suace" with the Scotch fillets, are almost certainly there to humour that cross-section of our community for whom a restaurant main without as sauce cannot be regarded as "special"... <i>(Mar 7, 2002,Dominion,28</i>)

>  Ferryman Restaurant. All new menu - ... exciting entrees - marvellous mains. We have retainted [sic] some of your favourites ... <i>(Sep 10, 1986,Marlborough Express</i>) [NOTES: 1986 Sep 10 Marlborough Express quoted in 1986 Oct 4 Listener 5]

>  We are close to our mains when the glass is banged down. <i>(Apr 13, 2003,Sunday (Sunday Star Times Magazine),15</i>)

### Main Body
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (historical) WWI NZ soldiers [the `Main' Body left in October, 1914, after a small advance party, and was followed by a series of reinforcements (C.R. Carr)]

>  ... it rather gave me the blues to think of the happy times we had at the same place in the Main Body. <i>(Apr 2, 1916,Letter</i>) [NOTES: 1916 Apr 2 letter in 1965 Malthus ANZAC 158]

>  <b>EVANS</b> The funeral of Private Duncan Blaikie at Waikaka, April 1917, was accorded full military honours ... The late A.N.Z.A.C. was a member of the main body of New Zealand Expeditionary Forces and took part in the terrible action on Gallipoli. <i>(1962,Waikaka Saga,148</i>) [NOTES: c1917 Apr newspaper cutting quoted in 1962 Evans Waikaka Saga 148]

>  <i>Main Body</i>. - Two magic words. If these be whispered into the ear of a sympathetic M.O., they produce excellent results. <i>(1918,New Zealand At the Front,32</i>)

>  <i>Main Body</i>. - Two magic words. If these be whispered into the ear of a sympathetic M.O., they produce excellent results. <i>(1918,New Zealand At the Front,32</i>)

>  Of course, it is well known that many of the Main Body men and certain Reinforcements, ... learned some of the geography of France ... <i>(May 25, 1918,Quick March,16</i>)

>  <b>WESTON</b> In October the main body of the New Zealand Expeditionary Force, with its first reinforcement, also sailed for Egypt. <i>(1918,Three Years with the NZers,7</i>)

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> ... 3,817 horses no less, sailed away with the Main Body (the first mass of soldiers to leave New Zealand in War I for Egypt). <i>(1978,Soldier Country,21</i>)

>  <i>(1982,NZ Almanac,209</i>) [NOTES: [8427 men left October 1914]]

>  I with my mates of the 7th Reinforcements went into camp in the winter of 1915... We are not allowed inclusion in the Main Body, where our mates are. <i>(Nov 17, 1954,NZ Truth,25</i>)

>  The Second reunion of the Main Body men is to be held.. on.. October 16. <i>(Oct 13, 1937,NZ Truth,13</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> occas. used of early immigrants into Canterbury settlement

>  ... `Mainbody' of colonists .... <i>(Apr 5, 1851,Lyttelton Times,6</i>)

### Main Body man
 
 <i>n.</i> (WWI)

>  <b>AITKEN</b> On making a rapid tour I felt that Sergeant Bree (a Main Body man with a very early number, 8/13) on the right had his men too near the trees, ... <i>(1963,Gallipoli to the Somme,99</i>)

>  There are two great primary classes [of diggers] a) Aucklanders b) others. They may be further subdivided into: 1) Nat Goulds 2) Dinks 3) Stokers 4) Main Bodymen. <i>(1918,New Zealand At the Front,32</i>)

### Main Bodyite
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Carried unanimously by all Main Bodyites on active service. <i>(Aug 30, 1916,Chronicles NZEF,I/1 : 1</i>)

>  On the medical staff are quite a number of "Mainbodyites". <i>(Oct 16, 1916,Chronicles NZEF,I/4 : 83</i>)

>  On the medical staff are quite a number of "Mainbodyites". <i>(Oct 16, 1916,Chronicles NZEF,I/4 : 83</i>)

>  Still, they do drop one stripe on landing here, to give the mainbodyites a chance, don't they. <i>(Jan 31, 1917,Chronicles NZEF,i/11 : 257</i>)

### main centre
 
 <i>n.</i> [see also four main centres]

>  <b>MITCHELL</b> Lacking [42] main centre status [the thirteen smaller centres] clamour for the outward and visible signs of city stature - a university, a new airport, a Government Life building, a railway, a piecart. <i>(1972,Half-Gallon Quarter-Acre Pavlova Paradise, The,41</i>)

### Main Divide
 
 <i>n.</i> the Southern Alps

>  <b>CRESSWELL</b> ... and every season now, hundreds of young people visit the snowfields and glaciers of the Main Divide and have the time of their lives. <i>(1951,Canterbury Tales,149</i>)

>  <b>MOORE & IRWIN</b> <i>M. ephedroides</i> is almost leafless with switchy, finely-grooved grey stems, ... being more common in the South Island, east of the Main Divide. <i>(1978,Oxford Book of NZ Plants,50</i>)

>  <b>MARSH</b> ... we began to climb in earnest: up into the outer ramparts of the Main Divide [of Southern Alps] ... <i>(1981,Black Beech & Honeydew,94</i>)

>  <b>BEARDSLEY</b> ... if you looked south you could see the great peaks of the Main Divide thrusting, it seemed, straight out of the sea. <i>(1984,Blackball 08,43</i>)

>  From its catchment on the Main Divide and the Godley Glacier, the braided [Godley] river runs a gauntlet of evocative locations...to discharge into Lake Tekapo. <i>(2008,NZ Geographic,94/62</i>)

### main lot
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) [wool sales] an offering of four or more bales.

>  <b>BISLEY</b> .. prices realised for such [star] lots are frequently below what the same quality would have brought if capable of being offered with the main lots (four bales and over) .. include as much as possible amongst the main lots. <i>(1897,Practical Treatise on Wool and Sheep Breeding,43</i>)

### main range
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  The Malverns are low detached spurs of the main range, appraently what diggers call "made hills," the result of the weathering down of the old peaks, or the terminal moraines of glaciers. <i>(Jan 14, 1875,West Coast Times,2</i>)

>  From the indications and prospecting results, he has no doubt that the auriferous leaders will extend some miles south of the present workings, towards Castle Rock, and in his opinion the asserted prognostications of geologists will be verified by rich discoveries on the eastern side of the main range ... <i>(Sep 27, 1869,Daily Southern Cross,4</i>)

>  The reefs crop out at 1400ft higher or 5000ft above the sea on a saddle between Unknown Creek and Moa Creek, and not in the main range, but in a lateral range flanking the great snowy peaks of the main range. <i>(Apr 3, 1884,Evening Post,2</i>)

>  In view of the increase in the numbers of keas and the information that they were spreading northward along the main range, it was considered imperative that a vigilant watch should be kept on this menace to the sheepowner in the high country. <i>(Mar 31, 1921,Ashburton Guardian,6</i>)

>  THE MANAPOURI-WEST COAST TRACK: THE SHORTEST AND LOWEST PASS OVER THE MAIN RANGE. <i>(Feb 10, 1909,Otago Witness,47</i>)

>  About two miles farther up, at the junction of the two branches of the Maitai, a good open spur, in an otherwise wooded country, offers a fair ascent to the main range. <i>(Jan 25, 1851,Nelson Examiner and New Zealand Chronicle,192</i>)

>  The first of these is on the south end of the main range, two miles away from any old working, and is 15 inches wide, its course being from south-east to north-west, with a dip to the west. <i>(Jun 14, 1897,Evening Post,5</i>)

>  The rock, which was rough granite, was very firm - quite a contrast to the loose rock met with on the main range of the [S]outhern Alps at Mount Cook. <i>(Apr 8, 1911,Grey River Argus,3</i>)

>  <b>PASCOE</b> An unusual exercise following this ten days was to cross over the tops of the main range in the dark. <i>(1952,Land Uplifted High,25</i>)

### main ridge
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>GILKISON</b> The only alternative was an outflanking movement.. and so perhaps be able to make a long high-level march on to one of the main ridges of Aspiring. <i>(1951,Aspiring, New Zealand,20</i>)

### main roll
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  The terms main roll and supplementary roll are not British English; nor naturally are general and Maori rolls and electoral districts. (DNZE. has Maori seats.) <i>(Jan 1999,NZWords,2</i>)

### main shear
 
<b>1</b> <i>v.</i> (Rural) to shear sheep for the first time in a twelve-month period.

>  By November 20 we aim to be down to 30 calves. That's when we're main shearing so we've got to have things pretty well organised. <i>(Nov 9, 2000,Taranaki Daily News,17</i>)

### main shearing
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) the first shearing of a farmer's sheep within a twelve month period.

>  <b>MCLEOD</b> Crutchings, eye clippings, and late-shorn straggler wool make up a number of small lines which are a nuisance during the main shearing. <i>(1954,On the Run,8</i>)

>  <b>ANDERSON</b> After the main shearing and penning up for a board of twenty eight shearers ... <i>(1962,Life's Roundabout, A,24</i>)

>  <b>SALISBURY</b> When the main shearing was in progress, one man was employed as the expert. <i>(1994,I Was So Lucky,17</i>)

### main strength
 
 <i>n.</i> great physical effort

>  <b>ANNABELL</b> They camped [c.1890s].. on the river bank and.. after hauling the canoe up the Marohema rapids by main strength, reached the farm in the afternoon. [1984 fn 'Main strength' was a term used meaning great physical effort.] <i>(1984,200 Years of NZ Food & Cookery,18</i>)

### Main Trunk
 
 <i>n.</i> the main trunk railway line

>  <b>PARK</b> ... all the way along the Main Trunk became a positive, virulent thing. <i>(1951,Witch's Thorn, The,36</i>)

>  <b>TURNER</b> Thus in New Zealand we have the <i>main trunk,</i> the line between Wellington and Auckland ... <i>(1966,Austral English,157</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> There was trouble out of Palmerston North with the electrification of the Main Trunk. <i>(1985,G'day Country,80</i>)

>  The Main Trunk railway runs within a few miles of this proposed National Park. <i>(May 14, 1887,Auckland Weekly News,8</i>)

>  <b>LEITCH</b> The way was now open for the construction of the Main Trunk and for seventy years thereafter no legal liquor was to cross the borders of the King Country. <i>(1972,Railways of NZ,62</i>)

>  The/ Waikato and Ngaruawahia/ the Proposed/ New Capital/ of/ New Zealand/ with/ a Railway from Auckland to the Proposed New Capital, and Thence to Wellington, with Branches to Hawke's Bay and Taranaki/ to Be Called/ the Great Trunk Railway of New Zealand/ by/ A Settler ... 1863 [A.G. Bagnall's caption 1980] `An anonymous Auckland Auckland proposal for the main trunk railway ...' <i>(1980,NZ National Bibliography,Vol.I Part 2 plate facing p. 912</i>)

>  <b>HYDE</b> It has been unofficially recorded that Lord Jellicoe was one of the few to use the communication cord on the Main Trunk, other than in cases of murder or sudden calamity. <i>(1934,Journalese,224</i>)

>  The Auckland Section forms the northern portion of the North Island Main Trunk Railway, terminating eventually at Wellington, on the shores of Cook Strait ... <i>(1892,NZ Official Handbook,225</i>)

>  About 3,000,000 acres are really first-class land, and about 1,000,000 acres fairly good land, all suitable for pastoral purposes, and accessible from the Main Trunk Railway-line and Kawhia Harbour.<i>Ibid.</i> 34 In the North Island there are two separate main systems of railway, destined in a few years to be joined together by the completion of the Northern Main Trunk Railroad. <i>Ibid,.</i> 52 The main trunk railway-line is constructed to Culverden, twelve miles north of the Hurunui, the southern boundary of the district. <i>(1902,Settler's Handbook of NZ, The,6</i>)

>  There was a Tournament held in a Southern town:/ We sent our annual team to as usual to win renown,/ They kissed their mothers .../ And left by the Main Trunk Train. <i>(Jun 6, 1912,Auckland University Carnival Book,46</i>)

>  <b>COWAN</b> ... he was made prisoner ... while surveying [14] the Main Trunk route, and was ... kept chained up ... in a whare at Te Kumi. <i>(1916,Bush Explorers,13</i>)

>  <b>COWAN</b> Fifty-six miles south of Auckland the Main Trunk trains pass the station of Rangiriri. <i>(1922,NZ Wars, The,1/326</i>)

>  The development of this region has followed the completion of the Main Trunk Railway, and is still in progress. <i>(1936,Agricultural Organization in NZ,54</i>)

>  <b>McCORMICK</b> [The] styles [of Taihape public buildings] showed a surprising diversity, ranging from the classical severity of the bank to the pinnacled romanticism of the Anglican church, an exceptionally fine example of Main Trunk Gothic. <i>(1959,Inland Eye,9</i>)

>  <b>SUTHERLAND</b> `Perhaps they can spare them now they've got K's for the Main Trunk hill country,' said John. <i>(1978,Elver, The,52</i>)

>  <b>BINNEY</b> Above the mud of the embankment the Main Trunk train sat, dirty silver, idle. <i>(1985,Long Lives the King,13</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> often = the Limited

>  <b>MACLEOD</b> But best of all was when they caught the Main Trunk ("Vogel's train") to Rangitata. [c.late 1870s]. <i>(1955,Voice on the Wind,22</i>) [NOTES: [c1870s]]

### Main Trunk express
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  The Railway Department certainly made a bad blunder in taking off the second Main Trunk express during the winter months. <i>(Aug 23, 1913,NZ Observer,3</i>)

### main trunk line
 
 <i>n.</i> the railway line between Auckland and Wellington, less frequently between Picton and Dunedin/Invercargill [Geoff Robinson on Morning Report recently (7/6/2001) was heard to talk about Auckland, Wellington & Christchurch being on the Main Trunk Line in reference to air transport]

>  <b>McCORMICK</b> The place is vaguely familiar to most New Zealanders as a station on the main trunk line ... <i>(1959,Inland Eye,7</i>)

>  <b>McCORMICK</b> The place is vaguely familiar to most New Zealanders as a station on the main trunk line ... <i>(1959,Inland Eye,7</i>)

>  <b>SCRYMGEOUR</b> Away towards Hunterville the country showed much bush, with areas partially cleared, and holdings increased towards the eventual artery of transport `the main trunk line'. <i>(1960,Memories of Maoriland,160</i>)

>  A New Zealand Joker's Lament for his Sheila I'm an ordinary joker getting old before my time/ For my heart's in Taumarunui on the Main Trunk Line. <i>(1964,Book of New Zealand, A (J.C. Reid),223</i>) [NOTES: 1964 Peter Cape `Taumarunui' in 1964 Reid Book of NZ 223]

>  <b>MACLEAN</b> The main trunk line from Wellington to Auckland was opened in 1908, .... <i>(1982,Lifetime at School,9</i>)

>  So you pop off to Refreshments for a cupper tea and pie In Taumarunui, Taumarunui, on the main trunk line... The main trunk line was completed in 1908 when the rails were connected at the Manganui-o-te-ao Viaduct, almost exactly halfway between Wellington and Auckland. <i>(Sep 28, 1985,Listener (New Zealand Listener),19</i>)

>  He has a relative who owns 60,000 acres or 70,000 acres of land at Waiouru, on the Main Trunk Line. <i>(1930,NZ Parliamentary Debates,226/721</i>)

>  "Taumaranui on the Main Trunk Line, the last time," he called repeatedly, evoking the name of the old New Zealand folk song as he walked along the platfrom, waving his signal lamp as the Northerner paused for a few minutes on its trip north. <i>(Nov 15, 2004,Dominion Post,A5</i>)

>  Taumarunui, the national icon on the main trunk line, is a sitter. <i>(Nov 28, 1998,Listener (New Zealand Listener),28</i>)

>  European time in Te Kuiti dates from the 1880s, when the Main Trunk Line came through. <i>(Aug 22, 1992,Listener (New Zealand Listener),15</i>)

>  The first train to get '' right through" over the Main Trunk line left the Manawatu railway- station, at Wellington, on Friday night last. <i>(Aug 13, 1908,Auckland Weekly News,40</i>)

>  In the papa land to the west of the Main Trunk line ... very serious damage from landslips is everywhere following the advance of settlement. <i>(1911,TrNZI,43/445</i>)

>  <b>GUTHRIE</b> This township, situated on the Main Trunk line between Palmerston North and Marton, had many years before been dense bush; ... <i>(1930,NZ Memories,290</i>)

>  <b>MYERS</b> They were a mixed lot ... working on the railway construction of the North Island Main-trunk line. <i>(1941,Valiant Love,9</i>)

>  <b>FRAME</b> The Main Trunk Line brings more tourists [to Picton], my aunt said. <i>(1951,Lagoon, The,9</i>)

 <i>v.</i>

### Mainland
 
 <i>n.</i> the South Island (orig. by South Islanders, but ironically, then almost neutrally, by North Islanders: now in general humorous or light-hearted colloquial application) [1952 See large and humorous correspondence in Auckland <i>The Herald </i>or <i>Star</i> in Dec 1952 (in S Kits from PSA)]

>  Shot like a Mainland cheese commercial and paced much the same way too, this is insistently bittersweet stuff that is impossible not to immerse tourself in.[sic] <i>(Mar 12, 2000,Dominion Sunday Times,F4</i>)

>  Even after the Royal Visitors had escaped safely back to the Mainland to a little peace and quiet, there would be no reason to close the island down again. <i>(Jan 21, 1953,NZ Observer,14</i>)

>  Several interesting land birds inhabit the [Snares] island, ... the Auckland Island Snipe <i>Gallinago Aucklandica</i>); the grass bird (<i>Sphoeneacus fulvus</i>), although now rare on the mainland, was frequent on this little island ... <i>(1891,NZ Journal of Science,1/161</i>)

>  I'm sure South Islanders are right in claiming that they live on the `mainland' columns of Auckland Herald) <i>(1949,Journeys,34/56</i>)

>  <b>WINKS</b> One last warning: We Americans promise not to confuse the South Island with The Glorious Mainland ... <i>(1954,These New Zealanders,54</i>)

>  <b>6</b> The `mainland' has struck back. This week-end .... 19 Government M.Ps .... will visit Christchurch. <i>(Nov 11, 1962,Dominion</i>)

>  <b>CAMERON</b> The South Island is sometimes called Te Waka o Maui (the Canoe of Maui) which suits South Islanders' sense of pride in being on the `mainland'. <i>(1965,New Zealand (various authors/titles),33</i>)

>  <b>GRUNDY</b> As freight was much too expensive to return bottles to the mainland the piles grew year by year as blots on the [Chatham Island] landscape. <i>(1968,Who'd Marry a Doctor?,41</i>)

>  <i>Mainland, the n.</i> S. Is. of N.Z. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),12</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> Well, so Otago won - ... A victory for the good old Mainland. <i>(1973,NZ Jack,106</i>)

>  <b>LELAND</b> <i>mainland</i>: This depends where you hail from. If you're from the North Island, that's whjere it is. Likewise with the South. <i>(1980,Personal Kiwi-Yankee Dictionary, A,62</i>)

>  <b>TUWHARE</b> (`Heemi') Glad that others from the/ Mainland were arriving toward the dawn/ like us. <i>(1982,Year of the Dog,11</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> Strange they should be selling rabbits around here while down in the Mainland they are desperate to be rid of them ... <i>(1985,G'day Country,57</i>)

>  <i>Mainland NZ joc</i>. the South Island. <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,452</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 296]]

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>Mainland, The</i> the South Island, self-declared. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,71</i>)

>  [init l.c.] In the deepest recesses of the mainland, they're [sc. gumboots] supposedly called Southland Slippers. <i>(Sep 24, 1988,Dominion,8</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> the New Zealand main islands

>  let / him have that too if that's the way / he wanted it. Yes the mainland / is 800 kms away / & mainlanders from the north / whisper. <i>(1984,Landfall,150/177</i>) [NOTES: (Kidman `Going to the Chathams')]

>  It's strange, coming from the mainland, to hear the term islanders refer not to Pacific Islanders, but to something totally different. There's certainly no thought of racial prejudice on the islands. Half the inhabitants are Maori or Moriori descendants but skin colour is not a factor. <i>(Dec 14, 1987,Dominion</i>)

>  <b>LANGDALE-HUNT</b> ... Hawson, having been paid out, returned to mainland New Zealand where he later married my aunt ... <i>(1985,Last Entail Male,58</i>)



 
<b>3</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <b>HUNT</b> What I missed, from the mainland and that? ... One old man at Ruapuke used to say,`It's looking bad weather over there at New Zealand,' ... We say `mainland', and we do know what's going on over there. We say `over there at New Zealand' as a joke nowadays. <i>(1985,I'm Ninety-five,270</i>) [NOTES: (Wattie, Stewart I.)]

### mainland island
 
 <i>n.</i> (Environment) an area of mainland (as opposed to offshore) vegetation where predatory mammals, such as rats and possums, are controlled or removed to allow native animals and plants to survive; an onshore sanctuary for indigenous plant and animal species

>  Near the top of the spur that leads to the tops, I pass a deer-exclusion fence around a monitoring site, one component of the recovery project, set up using techniques developed on Kapiti Island with a view to developing an 825-hectare, pest-free "mainland island". <i>(Jun 12, 2004,Dominion Post,E16</i>)

>  It is all part of a mission to create a mainland island in the 1250-hectare (3125-acre) East Harbour Regional Park... Thanks to Mr Hunt and 20 other members of his Mainland Island Recovery Operation the dawn chorus is being heard more frequently around Eastbourne. <i>(Apr 22, 2004,Dominion Post,A7</i>)

>  This is a "mainland island", where DOC is trying to put back what's been lost: namely the native birds, reptiles, insects and plants driven to local extinction by introduced pests. <i>(Dec 28, 2005,Dominion Post,C2</i>)

>  The term mainland island derives from the conservation work done on many offshore islands in the 1970s, 80s and 90s. <i>(Jul 2007,NZ Geographic,86/82</i>)

>  The whio result was reported last month at a meeting near Mangaweka of DOC staff working on intensive pest control at sites known as "mainland islands" and volunteers from private mainland island-type projects around New Zealand... Unlike real islands or the fenced-off Karori Wildlife Sanctuary, mainland islands are open to invasion or re-invasion by animal pests which must be kept at low numbers for targeted native species to thrive. <i>(Aug 20, 2003,Dominion Post,B5</i>)

>  He says DOC is doing a good job in its intensively managed islands and so-called "mainland island" reserves (areas of parks and reserves that are rigorously policed for pests and other threats to the native wildlife). <i>(Jul 31, 2004,Dominion Post,E2</i>)

>  MAINLAND ISLANDS - TEN YEARS ON [hdg]Thanks to mainland islands, major improvements have been made in the way conservation work is done. <i>(Jun 2007,Conservation Action,42</i>)

### Mainlander
 
 <i>n.</i> [(cf MAINLAND) ca1951 Used as a nom-de-plume in 2ZB request session.]

>  <b>WEBBER</b> `You <i>are</i> a Mainlander, though,' said the Bloke, `and that makes it just as hard.' <i>(1962,Look No Hands!,93</i>)

>  <i>(Apr 1, 1966,Palmerston North Tribune,8/7</i>) [NOTES: [OEDS]]

>  Real Mainlanders wear their short collars up. <i>(Jun 1999,North & South,121</i>)

>  Whitehouse is not known for his diplomacy, but neither are Mainlanders, who countered with scathing anti-Jonah rhetoric. <i>(Feb 12, 1994,Listener (New Zealand Listener),23</i>)

>  Come on Wellingtonians, we can't let a Mainlander best us. <i>(May 5, 1984,Listener (New Zealand Listener),43</i>)

>  As more Aucklanders abandon their city for a new life in the south, the distinctiveness of Mainlanders and Jafas can only become more tangled - almost as much as Auckland's traffic. <i>(Jun 27, 2004,Sunday Star Times,C4</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> one from mainland New Zealand

### mains
 
 <i>n.</i> cockfights

>  <b>SEDDON</b> Cock fighting was a favoured sport on the goldfields and some very gallant birds were bred for this old English pastime. At Marsden, a secluded mining township in the bush some distance from Kumara, many cock fights were fought and a lot of money changed hands over the fate of these fights called `mains.' One of the cockers gave my father a beautiful white cock, his comb dubbed, trimmed and fighting fit. <i>Ibid.</i> 348 The clamorous challenges of the old English game cocks melted the hearts of some of the committee-men but cock fights were not part of the popularity of the [Greymouth] Poultry Show and mains did not materialise <i>(1968,Seddons, The,16</i>) [NOTES: c1880s in Seddon The Seddons (1968) 16]

### mainstream
 
 <i>adj.</i> -

>  Brash promotes National as a mainstream party in contrast to Labour, which stands for minorities and rampant political correctness. ... At this point, "mainstream" seems to be an almost infinitely flexible political principle, big enough to include respectable prostitutes and wealthy former bankers who know the price of bread (but not, incidentally, of milk). <i>(Jul 10, 2005,Sunday Star Times,C2</i>)

>  Broadly, National's mainstream is that of the "We're all one" New Zealander who, according to Brash, wants to work hard and get ahead." Labour's mainstream, says Cabinet Minister Steve Maharey, comprises those who "want a vibrant, diverse New Zealand culture" and who value "tolerance and inclusion". <i>(Oct 1, 2005,Dominion Post,D8</i>)

### maire
 
 <i>n.</i> any of several native trees, or their durable timber, especially black maire, maire tawake. [ORIGIN: Maori, also in early use spelt maide, mairi, etc.]

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Maire, <i>Col[enso]. Olea</i> sp. (in the south of N. Isl.). (generally made of<i> mairi,</i> a very heavy native timber). <i>(1867,Handbook NZ Flora (List of Names),766</i>)

>  <b>CHEESEMAN</b> <i>O[lea] Cunninghamii, Hook.f. </i>... A lofty ... forest-tree 30-60 or even 70 ft. high ... Marlborough, extremely rare ... <i>Maire; Maire-rau-nui; Black-maire. </i>... Wood dark-brown, often streaked with black; very hard, dense, and heavy; ... <i>(1906,Manual NZ Flora,437</i>)

>  [MARIE ?spg] <i>Olea Cunninghamii</i> maire <i>(1916,NZ Association of Nurserymen, Trade Register</i>)

>  The Ake (<i>Dodonaea viscosa</i>), and the Maire, (<i>Santalum Cunninghamii</i> at the North, and <i>Olea sp</i>. at the South,) supplied hardwood for war implements ... <i>Ibid.</i> 46 The Maire:- two, or more, very distinct genera, containing several trees, (<i>Santalum Cunninghamii</i>, and <i>Olea</i> sp.,) are confounded under this Native name; althogh the natives themselves generally [47] distinguish them pretty clearly, - calling the <i>Olea</i>, Maire-rau-nui. Both were by them called maire, from the fact of both being hard-wooded ... <i>Ibid.</i> 50 (<i>known hard woods</i>.) The Mairetawhake, (<i>Eugenia Maire</i>); the Rohutu (<i>Myrtus pedunculata), ... The Maire (Santalum Cunninghamii) </i>... the Manoao (<i>Dacrydium Colensoi</i>), a small hard-wooded pine ... <i>(1869,TrNZI,1/III/34</i>) [NOTES: (Colenso Botany North Isd)]

>  <b>KIRK</b> MAIRE.- <i>(Olea cunninghamii</i> and <i>O. lanceolata.</i>) ... BLACK MAIRE.- (<i>Olea apetala.</i>) ... MAIRE.- (<i>Santalum cunninghamii</i>.) These afford fine-grained timber of great density ... All are commonly called `Maire' alike by settlers and Natives. Black maire usually attains the largest dimensions, and is sometimes 40 feet high or more. <i>Santalum</i> is the least of the group, but even stems of this ... make durable fencing rails. ... All the kinds are confined to the North Island ... <i>(1875,Durability NZ Timbers,21</i>)

>  <b>KIRK</b> MAIRE.- <i>(Olea cunninghamii</i> and <i>O. lanceolata.</i>) ... BLACK MAIRE.- (<i>Olea apetala.</i>) ... MAIRE.- (<i>Santalum cunninghamii</i>.) These afford fine-grained timber of great density ... All are commonly called `Maire' alike by settlers and Natives. Black maire usually attains the largest dimensions, and is sometimes 40 feet high or more. <i>Santalum</i> is the least of the group, but even stems of this ... make durable fencing rails. ... All the kinds are confined to the North Island ... <i>(1875,Durability NZ Timbers,21</i>)

>  Another source of perplexity has arisen from the application of the native name `maire' to all the species alike, as well as to the sandalwood (<i>Santalum cunninghamii</i>), some forms of which closely resemble <i>Olea lanceolata</i> and <i>O. montana</i> in foliage and general appearance ... <i>(1882,TrNZI,14/375</i>)

>  <i>maire</i>, a tree (Olea cunninghamii). <i>(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/144</i>) [NOTES: Andersen Maori Words in English]

>  <i>Ma&#769;.i-re</i> (ma.&#769;.e.-r&amacr.; <i>colloq.</i> m&imacr.&#769;.r&emacr.),<i> n</i>. [Maori.]<i> (Bot.) Any one of several New Zealand trees : - (a) Olea lanceolata, of the order Jasmineae; -called white maire by Auckland bushmen. (b) Fusanus Cunninghamii,</i> of the order <i>Santalaceae</i>; called also <i>New Zealand sandalwood. (c) Olea Cunninghamii</i>, of the order<i> Jasmineae; - often called</i> black maire <i>by bushmen</i>. (d) Olea <i>apetala</i>, of the order <i>Jasmineae</i>; - called also <i>broad-leaved maire. (e) Olea montana</i>, of the order <i>Jasmineae</i>; - called also <i>narrow-leaved maire. (f) Eugenia maire</i>, of the order <i>LMyrtaceae</i>; - called by the Maoris <i>maire-tawhaka.</i> <i>(1898,Merriam-Webster Third International,2026</i>)

>  <b>MORRIS</b> <i>Maire, n</i>. A Maori name applied to three kinds of trees; viz. (I) <i>Santalum cunninghamii,</i> Hook., a sandal-wood; (2) <i>Olea</i> of various species (formerly <i>Fusanus</i>); (3) <i>Eugenia maire</i>, A. Cunn., Native box-wood, but now usually confined to N.O. <i>Santalaceae. </i> <i>(1898,Austral English,279</i>)

>  <b>LAING & BLACKWELL</b> <i>Olea Cunninghamii (The Black Maire*)[(The Maire*)</i>]. A large tree, with whitish branches. ... The wood is dark brown or black, and is hard, heavy, and durable. ... [fn *] This name is also applied to Mida [salicifolia], ... , and to Eugenia, ... <i>(1907,Plants of NZ,334,1940,p. 355 ed.</i>)

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> <i>Maire</i> (ii) 1. n. <i>Olea cunninghamii</i> and other species of <i>Olea</i> <i>(1957,Dictionary of the Maori Language,168</i>)

>  <i>maire n</i> -s [Maori] : any of several New Zealand trees with dense heavy wood: as a : a New Zealand tree of the genus Olea - usu. used with a descriptive qualifier; compare BLACK MAIRE, WHITE MAIRE<i> b </i>: a small light-barked tree (<i>Eugenia maire</i>) with white flowers followed by red berries <i>c</i> : a small and often shrubby tree (<i>Mida myrtifolia</i>) that is related to the Asiatic sandalwood and has glossy glabrous usu. alternate leaves and small brownish green flowers in axillary clusters followed by small red drupes <i>(1961,Merriam-Webster Third International,1363</i>)

>  <b>YATE</b> Mairi - a tree of the <i>Podocarpus</i> species, growing from forty to sixty feet high ... Its bark ... resembles that of a healthy young oak ... <i>(1835,New Zealand (various authors/titles),41</i>)

>  <b>WAKEFIELD</b> Mairi, (<i>Podocarpus macronata</i>.) This is also one of the largest trees of the forest. <i>(1858,Handbook for New Zealand, The,41</i>)

>  <b>POLACK</b> <i>Mairi or maidi</i>, (cedrus Zelandicae,) is the closest grained and toughest of woods in the country. <i>(1838,New Zealand  (Polack),2/397</i>)

>  <b>YATE</b> Tawa (<i>Laurus Tawa</i>) is a frequenter of damp and deeply-shaded woods, with leaf and branches similar to those of the Mairi-tree; ... <i>(1835,New Zealand (various authors/titles),42</i>)

>  <b>SKEY</b> And even when our way we take,/ Where maire logs perdue,/ <i>(1889,Pirate Chief and the Mummy's Complaint..., The,143</i>)

>  <b>SARGESON</b> ... Miles promised to take me to a part of the bush where the trees were nearly all maire, and there were pigeons feeding in hundreds on the maire berries; ... <i>(1973,Once Is Enough,63</i>)

>  <b>GEORGE</b> He carved more [back bearings] from maire, put them in and the car was on the road again. <i>(1990,Ohakune,205</i>)

>  <b>LEE & KENDALL</b> [?Mida] Maide; Name of a certain tree; <i>(1820,Grammar & Vocabulary of the Language of NZ,173</i>)

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> [?Olea ?Eugenia] [Word list] maidi a tree bearing red berries like the bay wood very hard the natives make their clubs of it. <i>(Aug 1843,Journal,52</i>)

>  <b>SHORTLAND</b> Maire is a species of yew; its wood is one of the hardest in New Zealand. <i>(1856,Traditions & Superstitions,184 fn</i>)

>  <b>BAUGHAN</b> <i>Maire (my-ray)</i>: A native timber. <i>Ibid.</i> 54 <i>Kauri</i> and <i>Totara, Rimu</i>, and <i>Matai</i> and <i>Maire</i>,/ Red-as-blood <i>Rata</i>, and bright-as-blood <i>Pohutukawa,/ Manuka</i> dark-ey'd, Convolvulus star-ey'd - / The glittering of you that morning! [fn 2. attached to the italicised names] <i>Kauri</i>, etc. (<i>Kow-ree,</i> To[acute]-ta-ra, Ree[e acute]-moo, <i>Mah[a acute]-ti,</i> Mi-reh, Rah[a acute]-tah, Po-hoo-too-kah[a acute]-wah, <i>Mah[a acute]-noo-kah)</i>: New Zealand Bush trees. <i>(1908,Shingle-Short,13</i>)

>  <i>maire n</i>. NZ tree with heavy close-grained wood. [M] <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,452</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.]]

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Maire, <i>Col[enso]. Santalum Cunninghamii</i> (in the north of N. Island). <i>(1867,Handbook NZ Flora (List of Names),766</i>)

>  <b>HECTOR</b> Maire - a small tree ten to fifteen feet high, six to eight inches in diameter; wood hard, close-grained, heavy, used by Maoris in the manufacture of war implements. Has been used as a substitute for box by wood-engravers. Black maire, <i>N.O. Jasmineae</i>; also Maire-rau-nui, <i>Olea Cunninghamii.</i> Hook., fil., Black M., forty to fifty feet high, three to four feet in diameter, timber close-grained, heavy, and very durable. <i>(1883,Handbook of New Zealand,132</i>)

>  [Native name] Maire .. [Settlers' name] Sandalwood .. [Family] Jasmineae <i>(1902,Settler's Handbook of NZ, The,122</i>) [NOTES: 1886 T.Kirk in 1902 Settler's Handbook of NZ 122]

>  <b>HAY</b> The Maire (<i>Santalum Cunninghamii</i>) is not a large tree, but the wood is extremely hard, heavy, and finely grained. ... Bushmen call it `Black Maire', to distinguish it from the following:- The Maire-Tawhake (<i>Eugenia Maire</i>), or `White Maire'. The Maire-aunui (<i>Olea Cunninghamii</i>), which ... is a much bigger tree than the maire, but does not yield such valuable timber. <i>(1882,Brighter Britain,2/193</i>)

>  <b>CHEESEMAN</b> [Santalum--> Hook; Mida-->, Raoul.] <i>F[usanus] Cunninghamii, Benth and Hook.f. </i>... A small slender tree 10-25 ft. high; ... <i>Maire; New Zealand Sandalwood</i>. ... The wood is hard and dense ... <i>(1906,Manual NZ Flora,624</i>)

>  <b>MARTIN</b> 5. Maire or sandalwood (<i>Mida myrtifolia</i>) leaves alternate or opposite, 2-3 in., ... <i>(1961,Flora NZ,164,1929 ed.</i>)



 
<b>3</b> <i>n.</i> = maire tawake (Syzygium maire, formerly Eugenia maire)

>  <b>HODGSKIN</b> Maree, a fine large tree, produces a mohogany-coloured wood. Rema, a beautiful, high, and majestic tree, growing upwards of 80 feet in length ... <i>(1841,Narrative of Eight Months Sojourn in NZ, A,27</i>)

>  <b>DIEFFENBACH</b> It is made of the hard wood of the maire (Eugenia maire), ... <i>(1843,Travels in NZ,2/123</i>)



 
<b>4</b> <i>n.</i> <i>Phebalium nudum</i> a slender aromatic shrub, Thames north, usu. MAIREHAU

### maire-raunui

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Maire-raunui, <i>Col[enso]. Olea Cunninghamii. </i> <i>(1867,Handbook NZ Flora (List of Names),766</i>)

Both were by them called maire, from the fact of both being hard-wooded ... <i>(1869,TrNZI,1/III/46-47</i>) [NOTES: (Colenso Botany North Isd)]

>  <b>HAY</b> The Maire (<i>Santalum Cunninghamii</i>) is not a large tree, but the wood is extremely hard, heavy, and finely grained. ... >  <b>CHEESEMAN</b> <i>O[lea] Cunninghamii, Hook.f.</i> ... A lofty ... forest-tree 30-60 or even 70 ft. high ... Marlborough, extremely rare ... <i>Maire; Maire-rau-nui; Black-maire.</i> ... Wood dark-brown, often streaked with black; very hard, dense, and heavy; ... <i>(1906,Manual NZ Flora,437</i>)

>  <b>COCKAYNE</b> ... the maire-rau-nui (<i>Olea Cunninghamii</i>), an extremely strong timber. <i>(1910,NZ Plants,41</i>)

>  Of our shrubs and smaller timber trees, several are of strikingly beautiful growth ...<i> (e.g.)</i> the Houhere, <i>Hoheria populnea </i>..; the Mairehau, <i>Phebalium nudum</i>;..; the Northern Maire, <i>Santalum Cunninghamii;</i>.. and the truly evergreen Ngaio, <i>Myoporum laetum ... </i> <i>(1869,TrNZI,1/III/5</i>) [NOTES: (Colenso Botany North Isd)]

>  <b>MOORE & IRWIN</b> <i>PHEBALIUM NUDUM,</i> mairehau.<i> A small shrubby tree typical of dry ridges in kauri forest and not extending further south. No bush scent is sweeter than that of the dullreddish leaves. </i> <i>(1978,Oxford Book of NZ Plants,90</i>)

>  <b>BROOKER et al.</b> <i>Phebalium nudum</i> Hook. Maori name: <i>Mairehau </i> <i>(1981,NZ Medicinal Plants,88</i>)

### mairi
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [maire](../M#maire))

### Mairoa dopiness
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a sheep sickness arising from a soil deficiency [orig. in the Mairoa area.] (See [bush sickness](../B#bush-sickness))

>  Mairoa 'dopiness' or sheep sickness: ... <i>(Aug 20, 1931,NZ Journal of Agriculture,119</i>)

>  Excellent results have been obtained in the control of 'bush sickness' at Waimiha and 'Mairoa dopiness' at Ngapaenga from the use of cobaltised superphosphate. <i>(Jun 15, 1939,NZ Journal of Agriculture,493</i>)

>  Later, New Zealand investigations showed that similar-appearing diseases in this country (bush sickness, Mairoa dopiness and Morton Mains disease) could also be cured and prevented by cobalt. <i>(Mar 15, 1956,NZ Journal of Agriculture,239</i>)

### mairoero
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [(cf MIROROI)](../(#(cf-miroroi)))

>  <b>MONRO</b> Behind Toutuki, [the prospective Otago settler] may explore the mountain dreaded by the natives, on account of its being the favourite residence of the mairoero. This is a wild man of the woods, strong, cunning, and mischievous, and addicted to running off with young people and damsels. His body is covered with coarse and long hair, which also flows down from the back of his head nearly to his heels. To com-[263]pensate for this excessive quantity behind, his forehead is said to be bald. He was vividly described to us by a Maori who had seen one long ago, when he was a little boy, and was of opinion that `there is not a more fearful wild-fowl that your <i>mairoero</i> living.' <i>(1844,Notes of a Journey,262</i>) [NOTES: 1844 Monro Notes of a Journey (Nelson Examiner July 20 et seq.) in 1898 Hocken Contributions 262-263]

### maither
 
 <i>v.</i> moan, complain [misspelling of Brit. dialect mither - see ODE]

>  <b>DURRELL</b> Oh, she's [the crane] all right,' said Alan, [the light housekeeper] laughing, `she just maithers a bit when she's got a load on, that's all. <i>(1966,Two in the Bush,74</i>)

### maizena cake
 
 <i>n.</i> a cake made with cornflour [is MAIZENA a N.Z. patent cornflour?]

>  Carmichaelia is perhaps the most characteristic of the many remarkable endemic genera ... represented from the North Cape to Foveaux Strait by numerous species ... but in all localities recognised by the settler as native broom and by the Maori as <i>makaka. </i> <i>(1952,TrNZI,29/501</i>)

>  <b>COCKAYNE</b> Confined to the north are - ... the mangaeo (<i>Litsea calicaris</i>), the makamaka (<i>Ackama rosaefolia</i>), the tawhero (<i>Weinmannia sylvicola</i>), the toatoa (<i>Phyllocladus glaucus</i>), and some other trees and shrubs. <i>(1910,NZ Plants,46</i>)

>  <b>LEVY</b> In the far north the kauri and its associates, taraire, mangeao, makamaka, tawhero, toatoa, kawaka, predominate, ... <i>(1950,Grasslands of NZ,87,1970 ed.</i>)

>  <b>MARTIN</b> 2. Makamaka (<i>Ackama rosaefolia</i>) 7-21 serrate leaflets; ... [north N.Island]. <i>(1961,Flora NZ,152,1929 ed.</i>)

>  <b>MOORE & IRWIN</b> <i>ACKAMA ROSAEFOLIA.</i> Makamaka is a small tree with light green foliage set off by brownish stems and twigs. <i>(1978,Oxford Book of NZ Plants,76</i>)

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Ackama <i>rosaefolia</i>, A.Cunn. ... Nat. name, `Maha Maka', <i>A.Cunn. </i> <i>(1853,Flora NZ,I Flowering Plants 79</i>)

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Maka-maka, <i>A.Cunn. Ackama rosaefolia.</i> ... Makamaka, <i>Col[enso]. Ackama rosaefolia.</i> <i>(1867,Handbook NZ Flora (List of Names),766</i>)

>  They are by any standard exceptional trees, but the hero of our story, who goes by the name of makamaka, is also exceptional. ... Makamaka, with the botanic name of <i>Ackama rosifolia</i>, has leaves similar to those of a rose bush, having paired leaflets along the central midrib with a single leaflet at the leaf tip. <i>(Oct 1, 2005,Dominion Post,E13</i>)
