### makawhiti
 
 <i>n.</i> aua, sea mullet

>  <b>HECTOR</b> 58. SEA MULLET. ... The Makawhiti or Aua of the Maoris (<i>Agonostoma Forsteri</i>) ... is a common fish ... It is commonly called Herring, from its general resemblance in size and form to that fish, but is easily recognisable from the true Herring by having two fins on the back, ... The Picton Herring, a dried fish commonly known throughout the colony, is the Aua preserved by smoking. <i>(1872,Edible Fishes,114</i>)

>  [Maori or Settlers' Name] Herrings, Makawhiti .. [Scientific Name] <i>Agonostoma forsteri </i> <i>(1878,TrNZI,10/326</i>) [NOTES: (P. Thomson, Otago)]

>  <b>SHERRIN</b> MAKAWHITI (<i>Agonostoma Forsteri</i>). ... Called the sea-mullet in other places, but the makawhiti or aua by the Maoris. It is a common fish, Dr. Hector says, obtained at all seasons of the year by fishing from the wharves in the harbours. In Dunedin it is known as the mullet. ... The Picton herring, a dried fish, commonly known throughout the colony, is the aua, preserved by smoking. <i>(1886,Handbook Fishes of NZ,65</i>)

### make
 
 <i>v.</i> of auriferous blacksand, to gather, be exposed, or wash up on the beach; of auriferous material, to build up

>  <b>H/3/53</b> The quartz ... changes in character from crystalline and brown ferruginous in the higher to dense and bluish-grey in the lower parts of the workings - in fact the latter quality represents a so-called `new' or `second make'. <i>(1875,AJHR</i>) [NOTES: (Report on Goldfields)]

>  Though a rich patch of black-sand often makes in a single tide it is evident that the first working of the beaches makes a noticeable inroad on the store of gold available. <i>(1908,NZ Geol. Survey Bulletin,No 6 (N.S.): 153</i>)

>  On the sea-beaches the `black-sanders' often have to wait many weeks or months before a payable patch of black-sand makes. <i>(1908,NZ Geol. Survey Bulletin,No.6 (N.S.) 25</i>) [NOTES: (Morgan, Mikonui)]



 
 <i>v.</i> (Rural) [sheep] to move of its own accord

>  <b>LOUGHNAN</b> make: when a sheep travels of its own accord it makes up the spur or face. Or it makes back to its favourite basin. <i>(1981,Loughnan Glossary,51</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> of sheep

>  <b>NEWTON</b> ... but it was this dog's `kindness' that so impressed me. The sheep were big, strong, touchy Merino wethers, easily upset, and when Boy finally walked them into the ring they were like three pets. ... When those sheep entered the ring they were really `made', and Bill could have done anything with them. <i>(1971,Ten Thousand Dogs,80</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> of a sheep dog

>  Castle Hill was the best country I've been on to `make' a dog and it was while there that I at last met with some success on the trial ground. I'd picked up a young heading dog ... which ... was the greatest dog I've ever seen at getting on good terms with - or `making' - sheep. Sheep just never seemed to give any trouble with him. <i>(1971,Ten Thousand Dogs,66</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i>



 
 <i>n.</i> a (sexual) conquest; spec. a woman of easy virtue ? phrase: easy make = easy lay

>  `A widow's an easy make,' He [sic OEDS] said, `You pedal and let her steer.' <i>(1951,Landfall,5/98</i>) [NOTES: [OEDS]]

>  <b>BAXTER</b> NORAH ... You thought I'd be an easy make. A Maori bag. Hot Stuff. <i>(1982,Collected Plays,46</i>) [NOTES: (1968) 1982 Baxter Collected Plays (Wide Open Cage) 46]



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a stage in the maturing of cheese.

>  <b>TINSLEY</b> The 'make' that had matured for thirteen days was taken off the shelves and loaded on to a trolley. <i>(1985,Cheese Punchers' Ball, The,71</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>v.</i> (Rural) [ sheep] to move willingly when being worked by a dog.

>  There were, of course, some high class runs, but the sheep were difficult to "make." <i>(Jul 22, 1971,NZ Farmer,63</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> I'd picked up a young heading dog called Smoke .. was the greatest dog I've seen at getting on good terms with - or 'making' sheep. <i>(1971,Ten Thousand Dogs,66</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>v.</i> an absol or intrans. use of make `reach':

>  <i>Make</i>.-(1) (Of sheep) to travel; making up, making back, etc.; (2) (of men) arrive at, get to; e.g., <i>m</i>. the station. <i>(Jan 20, 1934,Press, The,15</i>) [NOTES: Acland Gloss]

>  <b>THOMSON</b> We had only found and skinned twelve before we reckoned it was time to make homewards if we wanted to reach camp before dark. <i>(1952,Deer Hunter: The Experiences of a NZ Stalker,184</i>)



 
<b>3</b> <i>v.</i> sc. money

>  I have always made on every horse I have sold in N.Z. yet. <i>(Oct 18, 1864,Chudleigh Diary,149,1950 ed.</i>)

### make a better door than a window
 
 <i>phr.</i> in expressions said ironically to a person blocking one's view: [JM UK also]]

>  <b>BAKER</b> ... <i>to make a better door than a window</i> (used of a person <i>(1941,NZ Slang,53</i>)

>  <b>PARTRIDGE</b> <i>You make a better door than a window.</i> A C. 20 New Zealand c.p., addressed to a person getting in the light. B., 1941. Cf. glazier? ... in the Dict. [P8: 1365] <i>(1949,Dictionary of Slang Addenda,1229</i>)

### make a box of
 
 <i>phr.</i> to make a mess of

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>make a box of</i> make a mess of; eg `He was going well in his first woodchop, until he got distracted by sweat in one eye, missed his groove and made a box of it.' NZA. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,71</i>)

### make a pig's breakfast of
 
 <i>phr.</i> (See [cf. rough as a pig's b](../R#rough-as-a-pig's-b))

>  The only thing I learned was how to knock the palings off - from the back, with venomous gusto and a hammer - if you make a pig's breakfast of it. <i>(Nov 19, 2004,Dominion Post,B15</i>)

### make a quid
 
 <i>phr.</i> to make money

>  Let's take a walk down to the shed / And list to what they say; / There's So and So, he's on again, / He'll make a quid to-day. <i>(Feb 23, 1911,Grey River Argus,2</i>)

>  `Get this,' he insists. `To make a quid I've got to swim my cattle out ....' <i>(Nov 27, 1977,Sunday Times (NZ),17</i>)

### make a rise
 
 <i>phr.</i> make a sum, make a profit

>  If he made "a rise" by the sale of gold before the days of Gabriel Reed he must either have had a very confidential buyer, or have been a better "sheperd" of his claim than most men would have been when the temptation was to tell the story of his discovery. <i>(Apr 24, 1877,West Coast Times,2</i>)

>  It was Halliday who first conceived the robbery as a "rise" for Christmas. <i>(Jan 1, 1887,Auckland Weekly News,20</i>)

>  <b>HARPER</b> True, there are still many working the alluvial ground [on the West Coast], but those making more than `tucker' are few, and it is many years since `a rise' of importance has been made by anyone. <i>(1896,Pioneer Work in the Alps of NZ,30</i>)

>  <b>BELL</b> I have been knocking round the Colonies since then, pretty hard up generally - but not always. I made a rise in Queensland once, at the Towers, but it all went soon; and now I have given New Zealand a turn ...; and have had the good fortune to drop on you. <i>(1899,In the Shadow of the Bush,30</i>)

>  <b>PFAFF</b> I bought and sold flour ..., got a 3-1/2 oz. nugget from the bottom of a hole that had been left, made a rise of [pounds]300 out of a pocket of gold like horse beans. <i>(1914,Diggers' Story, The,115</i>)

>  <b>VEITCH</b> By mid-June [1864] disappointed miners were returning from Picton to try once again to `make a rise' from the banks of the Clutha where it was possible to realise at least a couple of pennyweights a day using only the humble cradle. <i>(1976,Clyde on the Dunstan,30</i>)

### make a sale
 
 <i>phr.</i> to vomit (See [sale](../S#sale))

>  <b>PARTRIDGE</b> <i>Make a sale</i> . To vomit: New Zealand low: C. 20. B., 1941. <i>(1949,Dictionary of Slang Addenda,1105</i>)

>  <b>BAKER</b> Underworld slang ... t <i>o make a sale</i> , to vomit <i>(1941,NZ Slang,53</i>)

### make a smoke
 
 <i>phr.</i> to make a smoke signal [ca1952 Dec Hollyford Valley, Davie Gunn (advising tyro trampers) HWO `Make a smoke for the steamer at Greenstone [on Lake Wakatipu].'] (See [also put up (a) smoke](../A#also-put-up-(a)-smoke))

>  Arriving at last at the lake, we made a smoke as a signal for a canoe, and shortly afterwards had the satisfaction of seeing one approaching from Hiruharama; the Poihipi's pa, situated on the opposite side of the lake, which is here about five miles broad. <i>(Dec 2, 1857,Hawke's Bay Herald,2</i>)

>  But the placing of the straw trusses to make a smoke so that the pilot could see how the wind blew had to be very hasty, because the pilot was a little out in his estimate of time. <i>(Oct 26, 1921,Evening Post,7</i>)

>  Even the Australian aborigines can "make a smoke" eloquent. <i>(May 23, 1882,Nelson Evening Mail,2</i>)

>  Each scientist, he advises, should take with him some matches in a well corked glass bottle, as if he strayed from his party and lost his way he could make a smoke by setting fire to the tussock grass. <i>(Oct 18, 1907,Star, The,4</i>)

>  <b>KENNAWAY</b> Before starting we arranged a signal, often used up country, agreeing that if either party found the sheep, they should <i>make a smoke </i> to telegraph the fact to the other; . <i>(1874,Crusts: a settler's fare due south,60</i>)

### make a welter of
 
 <i>phr.</i>

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>welter</i>, phr. <i>make a welter of it</i> to overindulge or go to extremes; early C20. NZA <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,121</i>)

### make one's marble good
 
 <i>phr.</i> ingratiate oneself with (? from drawing marbles in Art Unions or lotteries)

>  <b>ANDERSON</b> I always made my marbles good with Cookie [the cook at the bush camp] by cutting up firewood ... <i>(1898,Bushfalling Reminiscences,1947 ed.</i>) [NOTES: [1898] A.Anderson's Bushfalling Reminiscences 1898 (1947) in 1977 Mulinder A Pioneer Family 41]

>  He `made his marble good', he alleged, by paying up a score he owed, having for that purpose chewed the ear of an acquaintance he had providentially met in the street. <i>(May 15, 1909,NZ Truth,204/7/2</i>)

>  ... you knew [in WW1] that [Death] would miss you if your marble was good. <i>(Oct 1, 1918,Quick March,13</i>)

>  Some tap the boss before they join, / And their record clearly shows / By this they make their marble good. <i>(1926,Transport Workers' Song Book,31</i>)

>  <b>FULLARTON</b> I was making my marble good [sc. with a woman] <i>(1944,Troop Target,26</i>)

>  <b>SLATTER</b> Making his marble good with the missus - Definitely on a promise - <i>(1968,Pagan Game, The,163</i>)

### make salt to one's kail
 
 <i>phr.</i> cf. kale n.

>  If the miners got the land free they would not be able to make salt to their kail on it. <i>(1864,Saturday Review,IV/13</i>)

### make tucker
 
 <i>phr.</i> make (just) enough to live on [Goldfields (see DNZE at tucker n.2)]

>  Those doing well do not wish it to be made known, and those making wages say that they are "making tucker." <i>(Jan 25, 1862,Otago Witness,5</i>)

>  The miners' gettings were were expressed in such terms as "making tucker," "eeking out an existence," or by some other equally expressive phrase. <i>(Dec 19, 1900,Otago Witness,19</i>)

>  He was living in poor circumstances, and started work, he says, in the old diggings in the hope of making tucker. <i>(Mar 26, 1910,Evening Post,14</i>)

### make up to
 
 <i>v.</i> to court

>  <b>LUSH</b> John Lynch is decidedly `making up' to Betsy. <i>(Nov 5, 1855,Auckland Journals,166,1971 ed.</i>)

### maker's edge
 
 <i>n.</i> the thin grey cutting edge left on the shears by the manufacturer.

>  <b>OGONOWSKA-COATES</b> <i>Maker's edge</i>. The thin grey cutting edge left on the shears by the manufacturer. <i>(1987,Boards, Blades & Barebellies,97</i>)

### making room
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>BARRY</b> The making room was 26 ft. by 24 ft. and contained two 800 gallon vats, two presses and an Anderson Mill. <i>(1966,In the Grip of an Island,271</i>)

### makings
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>PARTRIDGE</b> When I done all my sugar and never even had the makings, he went very hostile because I never told him I was swept. <i>(1935,Slang Today,287</i>)

>  <b>DEVANNY</b> He grinned and took out the `makings'. <i>(1930,Bushman Burke,26</i>)

>  <b>MUNDY</b> [... make a cigarette.] <i>(1948,There's Gold in Them Hills,14</i>)

>  <b>LLEWELLYN</b> The normal Kiwi ... his clothes in a,heap beside him, the `makings' h <i>(1949,Troopships,7</i>) [NOTES: [OEDS]]

>  <b>PEARSON</b> [check] Rogers offered him a cigarette but McKenzie shook his head and pulled out the makings in a way that seemed to suggest that tailor-mades were a stigma of middle-class ideology. <i>(1963,Coal Flat,70</i>)

>  <i>makings ... 2</i>. Also called <i>rollings. U.S., Austral., & N.Z. sl.</i> the tobacco and cigarette paper used for rolling a cigarette. <i>(1982,Collins Concise English Dictionary,682</i>) [NOTES: [1986 NZPOD 453; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 296]]

>  To display a native custom, I drag out a yellow packet of makings, 'Pocket Edition', and rather ostentatiously roll one. <i>(1962,One Foot at the Pole,26</i>)

>  I hand 'em on the makings, gentlemen, I hand 'em on the makings. <i>(Sep 15, 1939,Dominion,13</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> `the backbone', the essential part.

>  <b>WRIGHT</b> They're the makings of the diggings - blokes like him that try and try. <i>(1897,Station Ballads,19</i>)

### Makino cake
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <i>MAKINO CAKE</i> butter ... sugar ... flour ... mixed peel ... almonds ... eggs ... baking powder ... raisins ... grated rind of one lemon, dried cherries ... <i>(1915,Our Boys Cookery Book,108</i>)

### mako shark
 
 <i>n.</i> [aka mako]

>  The fish was the second record mako to be snared by Hutt fishermen in recent weeks. <i>(Apr 3, 2000,Evening Post,15</i>)

>  <b>PAUL et al.</b> [index to common names 283-293] shark, mako 145-61 <i>(1987,Fishes: A Bibliography</i>)

>  <b>AYLING</b> <i>MAKO SHARK Isurus oxyrinchus</i> (Blue pointer, mackerel shark, bonito shark) <i>(1982,Collins Guide,51,1984 ed.</i>)

>  Collins's <i>Guide to the Sea Fishes of NZ</i> says the mako shark, the classic fighting fish, is found mainly in the temperate waters of northern New Zealand. <i>(Mar 25, 1991,Listener (New Zealand Listener),32</i>)

### makomako
 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Anthornis melanura</i>, bellbird, korimako, mocker [cons var here] (See [see bellbird. b) see wineberry](../S#see-bellbird.-b)-see-wineberry))

>  The mako interrupts the silence with his melodious notes. <i>(1841,NZ Journal,2/29/51</i>) [NOTES: (Dieffenbach.)]

>  ANTHORNIS MELANURA. (BELL-BIRD.) ... <i>Native names</i>. Mako, Makomako, Komako, Kokomako, Korimako, Kohimako, Kokorimako, Titimako, and Kopara. Of the above names, Korimako is most generally used by northern and Makomako by southern tribes. The Ngatiawas call this bird Rearea; and the natives of the Bay of Plenty distinguish the male and female as Kokorohimako and Titapu. <i>(1873,Birds of New Zealand,91,{1888 2nd edn 1/85} ed.</i>)

>  I have got a great many birds since you have been away, which I shot with your gun or a catapult (excuse spelling), and which I stuff and hang up by the beak. ... One of them is a New Zealand bittern ... I have also the mako-mako, silver-eye, riro-riro, miro-miro, fanny etc. ... <i>(Aug 16, 1875,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,2/399,1960 ed.</i>) [NOTES: (?Bill Atkinson, Nelson, 15 years)]

>  Rail and pukeka were caught by snares set in their sphagnum moss runs, ... Tui and mako (bell birds) were killed by the birder hutching beneath a cone of fern fronds ... <i>(1928,BP Bishop Memoirs,Vol. 9(5): 362</i>) [NOTES: 1928 Baucke Manuscript in Skinner and Baucke The Morioris (Bishop Museum Memoirs Vol. IX No. 5 pp. 343-382) 362]

>  <b>Nalder</b> The Mako-Mako lifts his throat / And pours his liquid joy. <i>(1902,Battle-smoke Ballads,24</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Aristotelia racemosa</i> (also<i> A. serrata</i>), a small tree, wineberry

>  Mako-mako (<i>Aristotelia racemosa</i> ). <i>Ibid.</i> 40 Makomako. Aristotelia racemosa, Hook. fil. <i>(1869,TrNZI,1/III/34</i>) [NOTES: (Buchanan Botany Otago)]

>  ... the small berries of the Makomako (<i>Aristotelia racemosa</i> ) , [and] of the heath-like Totara (<i>Leucopogon Fraseri</i>) ... were eagerly sought after ... by children ... <i>(1869,TrNZI,1/III/32</i>) [NOTES: (Colenso Botany North Isd)]

>  <b>18/79</b> ... the patch of scrub ... contains a few mapau (<i>Pittosporum tenuifolium</i>) and 'lawyer' (<i>Rubus australis</i>) bushes; some miko-miko (<i>Aristotelia racemosa</i>) and manuka ... <i>(1886,TrNZI</i>)

>  In November, kakas, tuis, and mokos were here in great numbers feeding on the honey of the rata-blossoms ... <i>(1895,TrNZI,28/51</i>) [NOTES: (Henry, Dusky Sound)]

>  <i>makomako</i>, a tree (Aristotelia racemosa), wine-berry. <i>(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/153</i>) [NOTES: Andersen Maori Words in English]



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <b>BRUNNER</b> Tried a new species of fruit, the berry of the moko, and found it very palatable [seed has a nauseous, bitter taste]. <i>(1848,Great Journey,88</i>)

>  <b>BARRINGTON</b> Simonin gathered a handful of tutu berries, ... and got a pint of wine ... The native currant also makes first-rate wine, and grows very profusely. [ed fn] <i>Aristotelia racemosa </i>or <i>A. serrata</i>. In Otago it is called New Zealand currant or `Moko-mok'; and is otherwise known as Mako-mako or wineberry. <i>(Mar 16, 1864,Diary</i>) [NOTES: 1864 Mar 16 Barrington Diary in 1959 Taylor Early Travellers 402]

>  <b>MORRIS</b> <i>Mako,</i> originally makomako, ... often but incorrectly called mokomoko <i>(1896,Austral English</i>)

>  <b>ADAMS</b> <i>Aristotelia racemosa</i> moko moko <i>(1897,Counterfeit Seal</i>) [NOTES: (And)]

>  <b>LAING & BLACKWELL</b> <i>Aristotelia serrata (The Wineberry)</i> ... In bush clearings, it is one of the first plants to come up, and would, on this account, be termed by the Americans a `fireweed.' ... It goes by different names in different districts. In Otago, it is the New Zealand Currant, or Moko-mok'. In Canterbury, it is called the Wine-berry. In the North Island, it is the Mako-mako. <i>A. ruliceps.</i> PELZ. <i>(1907,Plants of NZ,246,1940 p. 258 ed.</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  The forest is enlivened by numerous tuis or mocking-birds; a little green parroquet flocks in hundreds to the potato-fields ... The mako-mako, the finest songster in New Zealand, is also found here, being another species of the honey-eater. The large New Zealand pigeon, called `kukupa', finds plentiful food here ... A new kind of rail was formerly very common; ... <i>(1840,Journal of Royal Geog. Society,11/195-215</i>) [NOTES: 1840 Dieffenbach An Account of the Chatham Islands Journal of the Royal Geographical Society 11 pp195-215 in 1984 Holmes Chatham Islands 95]

>  <b>DIEFFENBACH</b> 8. <i>Anthornis melanura, G.R. Gray,</i> ... [188] He-ghobarra [o-grave, a1-macron] of the natives of Queen Charlotte's Sound. <i>Forst</i>. Koho-i-mako of the natives. <i>Yate</i>. Korimaku of the natives of the Norther Island, and Mako mako of the natives of the Southern Islands. <i>Dieffenbach.</i> <i>(1843,Travels in NZ,2/187</i>) [NOTES: 1842 Gray's Fauna in 1843 Dieffenbach Travels in NZ 2/187-188]

>  <b>DIEFFENBACH</b> Sometimes, indeed, the mako-mako [fn Philedon Dumerilii] pours forth ... its melodious notes; or perhaps a hawk [fn Falco brunnea] is seen watching the movements of a smaller bird; or the fan-tail flycatcher busy in the lower branches. <i>Ibid.</i> 2/371 Mako mako - name of a bird <i>(1843,Travels in NZ,1/77</i>)

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> [check] Makomako, a bird. Syn. with korimako. <i>(1848,Leaf from Natural History,8/2</i>)

>  <i>makomako n. NZ </i> bellbird. [M] <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,454</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.]]

>  <b>BULLER</b> [check] <i>Anthornis Melanura</i> (Bell-Bird) ... Native names. Mako, Makomako, ... Korimako, ... Kopara. Of the above names, Korimako is most generally used by the northern and Makomako by the southern tribes. <i>(1873,Birds of New Zealand,91</i>)

>  <i>Anthornis melanura</i>, Sparrm. Bell bird, Korimako, Makomako, which last name settlers often render into mockymock. <i>(Nov 1884,NZ Journal of Science,12</i>) [NOTES: (T.H. Potts]

>  <b>WILSON</b> What musician has evoked a strain that echoes the ... Maka-maka trills from his exultant little throat? <i>(1894,In the Land of the Tui,161</i>) [NOTES: 1891 in 1894 Wilson Land of the Tui 161]

>  <b>ALEXANDER & CURRIE</b> Bell-bird (or Makomako): ... <i>(1906,Treasury of NZ Verse,289</i>)

>  <b>ALEXANDER & CURRIE</b> Bell-bird (or Makomako): ... <i>(1906,Treasury of NZ Verse,289</i>)

>  ... in the cool green depths the mokomoko and the tui make liquid music all day long. <i>(May 10, 1912,Triad, The,13</i>)

>  <b>BAUGHAN</b> The <i>Mako-mak'</i> pull'd and gave to them/ Green feathers from her wing. ... <i>(1908,Shingle-Short,126</i>)

>  <b>PFAFF</b> The singing of the Makos (bell birds) and Tuis in the morning was exquisitely beautiful. [fn] <i>Mako-mako</i>: The bell-bird. <i>(1914,Digger Junior, The,11</i>)

>  [Yellow-breasted tits] are now only rare visitants in the winter, when the bell-bird (<i>Anthornis melanura</i>), or mokomoko, as it was called by the Otago Maoris (not makomako, as in the North), also visits us. <i>(1922,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,4/277</i>) [NOTES: (Bathgate, Otago)]

>  makomako The Maori name of the bellbird. <i>(1958,Whitcombe's Modern Junior Dictionary (8th edn),249</i>)

>  <b>FALLA et al.</b> BELLBIRD <i>Anthornis melanura Other names</i>: Korimako, Makomako, Mockie. <i>(1966,Birds of New Zealand,218</i>)

>  <i> 265.b. Anthornis melanura melanura</i> (Sparrman, 1786) <i>Bellbird (Korimako, Makomako) </i> <i>(1970,Annotated Checklist of Birds of NZ,69,1980 ed.</i>)

>  <b>BRUCE</b> ... but for the Mocki Mock's liquid music ... I was alone with the Creator's handiwork. <i>(1978,Life in the Hinterland of the South Island,73</i>)

>  <i>Bellbird Anthornis melanura</i> (SPARRMAN, 1786) ... <i>Three Kings Bellbird A.m. obscura</i> FALLA, 1948. ...<i> Chatham Island Bellbird A.m. melanocephala </i>GRAY, 1843. ... OTHER NAMES: <i>Korimako, makomako, mockie. </i> <i>(1985,Reader's Digest Book of NZ Birds,290</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Aristotelia <i>racemosa</i> (Friesia, <i>A.Cunn.</i>) ... Nat. name, `Mako-Mako', Cunn. <i>(1853,Flora NZ,I Flowering Plants 33-34</i>)

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Makomako, <i>Col[enso]. Aristotelia racemosa. </i> <i>(1867,Handbook NZ Flora (List of Names),766</i>)

>  ARISTOTELIA RACEMOSA (<i>Hook.,F.</i>) ... The `MAKO-MAKO'.- ... In some districts the tree is known as the `Wine-berry', and wine has been prepared from its abundant and juicy fruit. <i>(1889,Art Album NZ Flora,62</i>)

>  <b>FEATON</b> Makomako [Maori name] .. Wine-berry [Settlers' name] .. Aristotelia racemosa .. Small tree <i>(1889,Art Album NZ Flora,177</i>)

>  Makomako (currant-tree) .. Aristotelia racemosa .. Timber light and straight grained; ... <i>(1890,PWD Catalogue of Timbers,11</i>)

>  <b>CHEESEMAN</b> <i>A[ristotelia] racemosa, Hook.f.</i> ... Makomako, wineberry. <i>(1906,Manual NZ Flora,83</i>)

>  <b>LAING & BLACKWELL</b> In bush clearings, [<i>Aristotelia serrata (The Wineberry</i>)] is one of the first plants to come up, and would, on this account, be termed by the Americans a `fireweed.' ...It goes by different names in different districts. In Otago, it is the New Zealand Currant, or Moko-mok'. In Canterbury, it is called the Wine-berry. In the North Island, it is the Mako-mako. <i>(1907,Plants of NZ,246] p. 258</i>)

>  <i>mako-mako</i>: tree, wineberry. <i>(1947,Reeds' School Dictionary: NZ Suppl.,174</i>)

>  <i>makomako</i> or <i>mako n</i> -s [Maori] 1 : a New Zealand tree (<i>Aristotelia racemosa</i>) of the family Elaeocarpaceae having a small red berry that turns black at maturity and is used for making wine - called also <i>wineberry 2</i> : the New Zealand bellbird <i>(1961,Merriam-Webster Third International,1365</i>)

>  <b>PEARSON</b> mockamock: The wineberry, a small fast-growing tree often the first to establish itself after bush has been felled or cleared. (From Maori <i>makomako</i>) <i>(1963,Coal Flat,421</i>)

>  <i>mako, makomako ... n</i>. small N.Z. tree; N.Z. tiger shark [Maori]. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),12</i>)

>  <b>BURTON</b> Other berries popular as delicacies were the fleshy, white, pineapple-shaped bracts and berries of the kiekie (<i>Freycinetia banksii</i>), the ripe, black berries of the makomako or wineberry (<i>Aristotelia serrata</i>) and the small, sweet berries (known as<i> konini</i>) of the kotukutuku or tree fuchsia (<i>Fuchsia excorticata</i>). <i>(1982,Two Hundred Years of NZ Food & Cookery,7</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <b>McKILLOP</b> The tanekaha tree yields a black or brown dye, and the tuhuhi a blue-black dye from the wood and bark, as does the mako; the whakou also yields a handsome blue. <i>(1849,Reminiscences,162</i>)

>  Just before the fireplace were a few makos which they had been sleeping on <i>(Mar 24, 1861,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,1/696,1960 ed.</i>) [NOTES: A.S.Atkinson]

>  <b>HECTOR</b> [check] <i>Aristotelia racemosa</i>, Hook. Mako.--A small handsome tree, 6-20 feet high, quick-growing, with large racemes of reddish nodding flowers. <i>(1883,Handbook of New Zealand,130</i>)

>  Amongst smaller trees and shrubs the most prominent are the horoeka (<i>Panax crassifolia</i>), and its congeners.. the mako (<i>Aristotelia racemosa</i>), the mapau (<i>Myrsine australis</i>), the pokako (<i>Eloeocarpus hookerianus</i>), also <i>Carpodetus serratus, Myrsine divaricata</i>, both of which are abundant. <i>(1884,TrNZI,17/216</i>) [NOTES: (Kirk)]

>  <b>MORRIS</b> <i>Mako, n</i>. originally <i>Makomako</i>. Maori name for a New Zealand tree, <i>Aristotelia racemosa</i>, Hook., N.O. <i>Tiliaceae</i>, often but incorrectly called Mokomoko. <i>(1898,Austral English,280</i>)

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> The mako or native willow has a little blue- black berry full of seeds which you spit out when eating. <i>(1939,First White Boy Born in Otago,13</i>)

>  <i>mako 2 n</i>. pl. -<i>kos</i>. a small evergreen New Zealand tree. [< Maori] <i>(1982,Collins Concise English Dictionary,682</i>) [NOTES: [1986 NZPOD 454]]

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> <i>Mako</i>, a tree; the bark used as a black dye. <i>Makomako,</i> a tree (<i>Friesia racemosa</i>). <i>(1848,Leaf from Natural History,20/2</i>)

>  <b>BRUNNER</b> Tried a new species of fruit, the berry of the <i>moko</i>, and found it very palatable. <i>(1848,Journal of an Expedition</i>) [NOTES: 1848 Brunner Exped. Middle Isd in 1959 Taylor Early Travs 310]



 
<b>3</b> <i>n.</i> a reshaping of the heard Maori form, often under the false impression that the makomako is a mocking-bird (as is the tui) though some, e.g. Bathgate, attribute spg to variant Otago Maori dialectal form <i>mokomoko.</i> [contrast and compare mocker @n1, mockie; and Marsh cit. spg <i>mok-e-mok</i> prob. referring to tui (see at mocker, mockie)]

>  <b>HUTTON</b> 13. ANTHORNIS MELANURA. Sparm. Bell-bird. Mocker. Korimoko. Moko-moko. <i>(1871,Birds of New Zealand,6</i>) [NOTES: [? Hutton's mis-spelling: cf korimoko]]

>  <b>BATHGATE</b> [Title]: `To the Moko-moko, or Bell-bird' [FN]: `Now rapidly dying out of our land'. <i>(1888,Sladen's Austr. Ballads,22</i>)

>  <b>MORRIS</b> <i>Moko-moko, n.</i> (1) Maori name for the Bell-bird (q.v.). <i>Anthornis melanura</i>, Sparrm. <i>(1898,Austral English,299</i>)

>  <b>LANCASTER</b> The mock-a-mocks. woke there to answer most sweetly. <i>(1905,Sons o' Men,136</i>)

>  <b>STUDHOLME</b> Then the Moki's sweet song peals out like a bell ... <i>(1940,Te Waimate,307</i>) [NOTES: a1917 Effie Studholme in 1940 Studholme Te Waimate 307]

>  [Yellow-breasted tits] are now only rare visitants in the winter, when the bell-bird (<i>Anthornis melanura</i>), or mokomoko, as it was called by the Otago Maoris (not makomako, as in the North), also visits us. <i>(1922,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,4/277</i>) [NOTES: (Bathgate, Otago)]

>  <i>moko-moko n.</i> the bell-bird. [Maori] <i>(1929,Whitcombe's Etym. Dict. Aust-NZ Suppl.,8</i>)

>  <b>GUTHRIE</b> The liquid notes of tuis and moko-mokos rang out in unison, and kaw-kaws and [90] parakeets made splashes of colour as the sun caught their iridescent wings. <i>(1930,NZ Memories,89</i>)

>  <i>Mockie.</i>- Mako-mako, or Moki-mok. (Anthornis melanura). The name is still sometimes used; but the bird is now generally called a `Mokky'. [1951 Glossary merely has after `melanura': The bird is sometimes still called a `mockie'.] ([1874 Lady Barker Station Life],p.93 <i>(Sep 9, 1933,Press, The,15</i>) [NOTES: 1933 Acland Glossary Press 9 Sept 15]

>  <i>moko-moko n.</i> in New Zealand the bell-bird; a small lizard [Maori]. <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Whitcombe's),620</i>)

>  <b>MARSH</b> ... she found herself describing the New Zealand Deepacres, ... how English trees grew into the fringes of the native bush, and how English birdsong, there, was pierced by the ... notes of bell-birds and mok-e-moks.] <i>(1941,Surfeit of Lampreys,14/218,1951 ed.</i>)

>  <i>moko-moko ... n</i>. in N.Z. the bell-bird ... <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),13</i>)



 
<b>4</b> <i>n.</i>

>  188 Makomako (wine berry or currant tree) slab. <i>(1890,PWD Catalogue of Timbers,19</i>)

>  <b>KNIGHT</b> [referemce Rangitikei 1850s] Haura was able to fill his sugar bag with berries from the maku trees that grew plentifully and so beautifully by the edge of the bush on the river, ... . <i>(1975,Poyntzfield,58</i>) [NOTES: c1920s Eliza McKenzie in <i>Outlook</i> in 1975 Knight Poyntzfield 58]

>  <b>KEENE</b> Taking the axe [c1900] ... I searched the bush for a moki <i>(1967,Tang of The Bush,59</i>)

### makora
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [tarapunga](../T#tarapunga))

>  LARUS SCOPULINUS. (MACKEREL- \{RED-BILLED\} GULL.) ... <i>Native names</i>. Tarapunga, Makora, and Akiaki. <i>(1888,Birds of New Zealand,2/55</i>)

### maku
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) a New Zealand-bred cultivar of Lotus clover (Lotus uliginosus syn. L. pedunculatus). [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  More persistent than Maku under intensive or continuous grazing management, More prostrate growth habit than Maku. <i>(1995,Grasslands Range of Forage and Conservation Plants,7</i>)

>  .. the development of maku lotus for the lower fertility country in New Zealand has provided farming with a pioneer legume more efficient in utilisation of phosphate than say, white clover. <i>(Sep 1985,NZ Dairy Exporter,66</i>)

>  When grown on acid and infertile soils, 'Maku' contains sufficiently high levels of condensed tannins .. <i>(Mar 30, 1986,Proceedings 1985 Hill & High Country Seminar</i>) [NOTES: Procedings 1985 Hill & High Country Seminar]

>  Maku lotus is outstanding on very low pH soils. <i>(1986,Proceedings 1985 Hill & High Country Seminar,69</i>) [NOTES: Procedings 1985 Hill & High Country Seminar]

>  .. maku lotus is increasingly being used in drier areas. <i>(Jul 23, 2001,Lifestyle Farmer</i>)

### makuru
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) [Ma.] a New Zealand-bred cultivar of oats.

>  Makuru oat - a greenfeed oat, suitable for New Zealand conditions. <i>(1995,Grasslands Range of Forage and Conservation Plants,72</i>)

### makutou
 
 <i>v.</i>

>  ".. and woe betide the man who took on another job and was working when the ship arrived on the Monday or Tuesday. He would be makutou'd not only in the one firm, but a 'freeze' was probable all round, for one employer passed the word to the others." <i>(1993,Wharfie,p23</i>)

### makutu
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>LEE & KENDALL</b> M|akutu, ... s. Witchcraft. <i>(1820,Grammar & Vocabulary of the Language of NZ,174</i>)

>  <b>MARSDEN</b> The New Zealanders have a strong belief in witchcraft, which they call <i>makutu</i> , and think that those persons who have the power to <i>makutu</i> can kill any person they choose by this art. <i>(1830,Letters & Journals,478,1932 ed.</i>)

>  He had been arguing that Rata probably died because of sorcery (makutu). <i>(1945,Journal of the Polynesian Society,54/110</i>) [NOTES: (Beaglehole)]

>  <i>ma[a]kutu,</i> bewitch; spell, incantation, for purpose of bewitching: commonly regarded as magic, witchcraft, of which there were two varieties; black (evil) and white (good). <i>(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/153</i>) [NOTES: Andersen Maori Words in English]

>  <b>BEAGLEHOLE</b> <i>makutu</i> sorcery, black magic <i>Ibid.</i> 236 He [the tohunga] is therefore likely to hint darkly of sorcery, makutu, as frustrating their efforts when these efforts are not improving ... his patient. <i>(1946,Some Modern Maoris,xxii</i>)

>  <i>makutu</i>: magic. <i>(1947,Reeds' School Dictionary: NZ Suppl.,174</i>)

>  Hinewaka, he speaks, died from <i>makutu</i> , the death-magic! <i>(Aug 1953,Here & Now,21</i>)

>  <b>KEENE</b> spell, curse. <i>(1963,O Te Raki,92</i>)

>  <b>BRATHWAITE</b> MAKUTU Second sight. <i>(1967,Evil Day,380</i>)

>  <b>BAXTER</b> And like a wave of black water/ The makutu hits me. No terror like this,/ <i>(1979,Collected Poems,590,1972 ed.</i>)

>  <b>MASON</b> TINA. You go and chase yourself. Don't you come any of your old makutu over me, Irapeta. <i>(1969,Awatea,46,1978 ed.</i>)

>  <b>FINLAYSON</b> makutu: occult power to do secret hurt to enemies, `black magic'. <i>(1973,Brown Man's Burden,141</i>)

>  <b>BAXTER</b> makutu <i>spell; curse </i> <i>(1982,Selected Poems,188</i>)

>  <i>makutu n. NZ</i> sorcery, witchcraft; a magic spell. [M] <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,454</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.]]

>  Their deaths led some Ngatiporou, even Ngata himself at times of despair, to conclude that Waikato had put a makutu on them. <i>(1986,Na To Hoa Aroha,1/32</i>)

>  My brothers' slang and `funny' sayings delighted me. ... I remember [126] such words as `batty', `hoot' and `spondulicks' and many that ... were corruptions of Maori words. I spell them here as they sounded to me then: <i>ehoa, harka, kupai, kure, munga munga </i>typo, markuta, pooku, tangy <i>(1988,Through the Looking Glass,125</i>) [NOTES: (Meikle)]

>  <b>STEAD</b> 'What's a makutu?' 'It's a curse.' 'Why, what's he done?' <i>(1999,Talking about O'Dwyer,47</i>)

>  When the executed soldier's family heard about the circumstances of his death, I was told they laid a makutu or curse on Major Dyer and ill fortune had pursued him and his family from that time. <i>(Dec 1999,North & South,128</i>)

>  <b>POLACK</b> ... that they bewitched (<i>makutu</i> [a-acute]) his [75] enemies and strengthened the hearts of his friends, ... <i>Ibid.</i> 1/254-255 Among the superstitious feelings of the natives, implicit belief is reposed in dreams, visions, second [255] sight, the efficacy of charms, and divination, the power of the <i>Makutu</i> or curse of an enemy, ... <i>(1840,Manners & Customs of the NZers,1/74</i>)

>  <b>DIEFFENBACH</b> ... the belief in witchcraft (makuta) [sic], to which many [natives] have fallen victims, ... <i>(1843,Travels in NZ,2/16</i>)

>  <b>ANGAS</b> ... his death will certainly be ascribed to the baneful sight of the lizard-gods, and I shall be accused of <i>makutu</i> , or witchcraft. <i>(1847,Savage Life,2/68</i>)

>  <b>MOSER</b> There were old men - firm believers in Makutu (witchcraft) <i>(1863,Mahoe Leaves,65</i>)

>  <b>TINNE</b> The father took down his tomahawk, and creeping up cautiously, lest he himself should be makutu-ed by a look, brained his parent ... <i>(1873,Wonderland of the Antipodes,34</i>)

>  ;By means of makutu a person could be made to offend against some law of tapu without his being aware of it, and in such case illness or death was sent by <i>atua</i> who had been insulted. ... This <i>makutu</i> business was the dangerous part of a <i>tohunga's</i> profession ... <i>(1905,TrNZI,37/35</i>)

>  <b>BAUCKE</b> Yet another decimation tool in the hands of spite and revenge was makutu (witchcraft), faith in which rested on no more solid basis than fear - ... <i>(1905,Where the White Man Treads,41</i>)

>  <b>LAMBERT</b> Tohungaism, and the practice of <i>makutu</i> , ... are all practically dead ... <i>(1936,Pioneering Reminiscences of Old Wairoa,77</i>)

>  <b>COWAN</b> (No doubt the Maoris believed that the food had been placed under a spell of <i>makutu</i> by their enemies in order to bewitch them.) <i>(1940,Sir Donald Maclean,14</i>)

>  <b>FINLAYSON</b> ... the school-kids were boasting about old Tupara putting a makutu curse on Monday. <i>(1944,Brown Man's Burden,16,1973 ed.</i>) [NOTES: also 1944 Finlayson in 1977 NZ Listener Short Stories 17]

>  Of course he knew the makutu was only old women's tales. <i>(1938,Brown Man's Burden,78</i>)

>  <b>Briar Grace-Smith</b> "There's something up with your wife, bro. I tell you, there's a makutu on that girl." <i>(2006,Six Pack, The,23</i>)

>  My dad had thought it might have been a makutu, a curse. <i>(Apr 2, 2005,Listener (New Zealand Listener),26</i>)

>  Instead, his hands spoke for him, describing a makutu (curse) as "silent bullets" with an eloquent gesture, tracing machine guns firing from his eyes. <i>(Nov 12, 1990,Listener (New Zealand Listener),104</i>)

>  I was intrigued by a recent TV news report that a makutu or Maori curse had been placed on three Far North DOC workers. <i>(Oct 31, 1998,Listener (New Zealand Listener),8</i>)

>  Sometimes he claimed to be acting under the supernatural influence of a makutu (spell). <i>(Nov 26, 1988,Listener (New Zealand Listener),16</i>)

>  Another form of mate Maori was makutu (sorcery). Usually a makutu was performed by an opponent out for utu (revenge) for a real or imagined offence. The target of makutu was the mauri (life force). <i>(Dec 6, 1986,Listener (New Zealand Listener),86</i>)

>  Named for the shark (pronounced Mung-or) she starts out as a greedy bully, then becomes demonic when a makutu is put on her. <i>(Oct 13, 2000,Evening Post,5</i>)

>  <b>EDWARDS</b> Our mother had a makutu placed on her when she married our father. <i>(1992,Mihipeka,55</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> to bewitch; to cast a spell (on). [ORIGIN: Maori, Maori, <i> maakutu</i> to bewitch; a spell, incantation: form (esp. the verb) occas. anglicised: found usually in Maori contexts ca1937 R. Mason Makutu'd used by a pakeha busdriver in the King Country.]

>  <b>ELDER</b> ... we were told by the natives there that the dead bodies were three slaves that were killed for makootooing (makutuing) a chief, that is bewitching or praying evil prayers against him that caused his death. <i>(Jan 10, 1934,Marsden's Lieutenants,235</i>) [NOTES: 1825 Jan 10 William Hall in Elder Marsden's Lieutenants (1934) 235]

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> It is generally current that Pango ... had <i>makutu'd </i>(bewitched) 'Hongi and caused his death ... <i>(Mar 25, 1828,Early Journals of Henry Williams, The,117,1961 ed.</i>) [NOTES: 1828 Mar 25 H. Williams Early Journals (1961) 117]

>  <b>CRAIK</b> ... three slaves ... had been killed that morning for <i>makootoing</i> [241] a chief, i.e. bewitching or praying evil prayers against him, which had caused his death. <i>(1830,New Zealanders, The,240</i>)

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> The natives spoke of their having been <i>makutu</i>d, bewitched by us and consequently they were all dying. <i>(Jun 25, 1832,Early Journals of Henry Williams, The,248,1961 ed.</i>)

>  <b>POLACK</b> ... countenance and told me that the young man Waitara [a2,a3-acute], was bewitched or <i>makutued </i>[a-acute]. I enquired how that event came about; he said, that an enemy of an opposing tribe had smoked out of the sick man's pipe, and he promised to show me what he could do in destroying the effects of the makutu [a-acute]; he accordingly approached Waitara, and began a shibboleth to the atua that no person present understood; ... <i>(1838,New Zealand  (Polack),2/242</i>)

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> ... Xt was Lord of us all as now their Atuas who were only devils had no power to makutu and deceive us as before. <i>(Nov 13, 1845,Journal,3/169</i>)

>  <b>WHITE</b> If anyone .... offended us ... we should try to kill them by makutuing them to see if makutu was true. <i>Ibid.</i> 182 They two had makutued all the chiefs who had died there. <i>(1846,Journal,1</i>)

>  <b>GRACE</b> No doubt, had any of our people been taken ill and died, the belief would have been that they were `makutu' (bewitched), that is, that the god of their great Chief had entered the sick person and devoured him for violating his `tapu'. <i>(1928,Pioneer Missionary Among the Maoris, A,67</i>) [NOTES: 1856 Dec 31 T.S.Grace Report of Taupo District in Grace Pioneer Missionary (1928) 67]

>  Heaven itself would be <i>makutued</i> by<i> some</i> fellows that I know. <i>(Jun 15, 1892,Auckland  Star</i>) [NOTES: 1892 June 15 Auckland Star in 1967 Bailey and Roth Shanties 83]

>  <b>BAUCKE</b> <i>(1905,Where the White Man Treads,263</i>)

>  <b>FINLAYSON</b> The doctor would have done no good because Huia was makutu'd. <i>(1938,Brown Man's Burden,72</i>)

>  The opposing hapu.. had made sure of victory by carefully makutu-ing the playing-ground beforehand. <i>(Jul 26, 1913,NZ Observer,10</i>)

>  <b>Bernard Gadd</b> 'That old kui' musta been collecting up bits of my hairs and finger nails and all so she could makutu me and then I have to go with - you know - that nephew of hers.' <i>(1981,Where to Go?,15</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a ceremony of exorcism [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  How terrible it is that the death by drowning of Janet Moses, mother of two children, while allegedly under a makutu ceremony in Wainuiomata, should be related to the Wairarapa. What has not been made clear to me is why Janet Moses was the recipient of the makutu. <i>(Nov 27, 2007,Wairarapa Midweek,18</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <i>Ma[macron]kutu</i>, bewitch; spell, incantation, for purpose of bewitching: commonly regarded as magic, witchcraft, of which there were two varieties; black (evil) and white (good). Makutu (without macron), a kind of kuumara. <i>(Jun 1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/153</i>) [NOTES: (J.C.Andersen)]

>  <b>BAUCKE</b> [The chief] first lowered his mat to his hips ... so that no one sitting in his rear might be able to place a makutu germ in his robe ... <i>Ibid.</i> 263 This woman [a tohungaess] went on a visit to Taranaki, and while there tried to induce Paki to cease his makutu practices, ... But he declined to abate one life! `Good,' she replied, `then it is you and I; I will makutu you and kill you!' <i>(1905,Where the White Man Treads,181</i>)

>  <b>FINLAYSON</b> She was sure someone had put a makutu spell on her. <i>(1938,Brown Man's Burden,70</i>)

>  The natives here indulged in a little makutu humbug last week. <i>(Dec 7, 1872,Auckland Weekly News,5</i>)

>  She was a makutu old lady. <i>(1987,NZ Journal of History,21(1) : 19</i>)

>  This woman.. tried to induce Paki to cease his makutu practices. <i>(Feb 11, 1905,NZ Herald,Suppl. 1</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> ? mate Maori, Maori sickness

>  A bus driver was struck on the head ... by a man who claimed he suffered from Makutu [sic], a Maori sickness, the High Court at Auckland was told yesterday. ... He was not insane, ... yet he was mentally unstable ... He had suffered from makutu, a form of mental illness peculiar to Maoris, and its associated personality disorder. <i>(Mar 30, 1988,Dominion,12</i>)



 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  <b>MARSDEN</b> The alleged cause was that a near relation of Koro Koro's had been poisoned (bewitched - <i>makutu</i>) when on a visit to the Thames. <i>(Jun 13, 1820,Letters & Journals,253,1932 ed.</i>)

>  <b>JOHNSON</b> Others again more credulous impute it, to the malevolent influence of an <i>Atua</i> or evil spirit, or to the individual being<i> makutu</i> or struck by an <i>evil eye</i>. <i>(1847,Notes of a Journey</i>) [NOTES: 1847 Johnson Notes from a Journal in 1959 Taylor Early Travellers 169]

 <i>n.</i> =leaping fish

>  [Native or Settlers' Name] Makuwhiti, Mullet, or Herring .. [Scientific Name] <i>Agonostoma forsteri </i> <i>(1879,TrNZI,11/382</i>) [NOTES: (P. Thomson, Otago)]

### maleesh
 
 <i>exclam.</i> indicating indifference `never mind'; `she's right

>  `Maleesh the wolves,' I said. `Think of the Japs. If they don't get a few inackes??? up smartly, the Russkis'll probably screw some of their necks for practice.' <i>(1945,Sixes & Sevens,7</i>)

>  Not so much of the <i>malesh! </i> <i>(1947,Landfall,1/162</i>)

>  <b>SLATTER</b> Maleesh the condys - I'm a monte for the big double Saturday. I got me contacts - <i>(1968,Pagan Game, The,163</i>)

>  <b>SLATTER</b> `You know, Spandau, this Welsh team isn't at full strength,' I tell him. `Lili Maleesh,' says Spandau, when he's downed another swig. ... `Where were they when I liberated that plonk factory back of Cesena?' <i>(1971,On the Ball,137</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> There was a local horse running that day and when the race came up it was <i>`maleesh' </i>the dog trials. <i>(1971,Ten Thousand Dogs,142</i>)

>  What, fine me fourteen days feloose? Maleesh! <i>(May 10, 1943,NZEF Times,II/98 : 5</i>)

>  Maleesh the lingo. <i>(Sep 6, 1943,NZEF Times,III/115 : 5</i>)

>  I have been attended.. by "maleesh" and mediocre dentists, and by downright butchers. <i>(Jul 2, 1947,65 Years of Guides,16</i>)

>  <b>FARRELL</b> "Maleesh," said Martin. "Forget it. I'm on civvy street now. I'll go out and buy a new one." <i>(1992,Skinny Louie Book,64</i>)

>  <b>COLE</b> "How about keeping on schedule, George!" "Not so much of the malesh!" <i>(1949,It Was So Late,24</i>)

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> Maalesh the condies? <i>(1983,Down from Marble Mountain,255</i>)

>  Maleesh all the impersonal pronouns, said the bloke. <i>(Apr 6, 1942,NZEF Times,I/41 : 6</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  After a while he adopted the "maleesh" attitude. <i>(Dec 21, 1942,NZEF Times,II/78 : 20</i>)

### mallow
 
 <i>n.</i> introduced <i>Malva</i> and <i>Lavatera</i> spp., often distinguished by descriptive epithets <i>Californian, Creeping (Modiola caroliniana), Cretan, Dwarf, French, Ground, Large/ Small-flowered, Prostrate, Red, Tree</i> [see 1969 Standard Common Names for Weeds]

>  <b>HILGENDORF</b> MALLOW (<i>Malva rotundifolia [neglecta], M. parviflora</i>, and <i>M. sylvestris</i>). One or other of these species is to be found as a roadside weed all over the country. <i>(1926,Weeds of New Zealand,120,1967 ed.</i>)

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> Several species of mallow, all native to Europe and Asia, are weeds of roadsides ... Only large-flowered mallow [<i>Malva sylvestris</i>] and tree mallow [<i>Lavatera arborea</i>] are easy to identify. <i>(1981,Weeds of Roadsides,89</i>)

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> MALLOWS (<i>Malva</i> spp.) ... Introduced from Eurasia, these weeds are widespread in New Zealand, common in yards and gateways, and near trees. <i>(1981,Weeds of Lawns,68</i>)

### Mallowpuff
 
 <i>n.</i> a marshmallow-topped biscuit, coated with chocolate

>  .. but as I can't be depended on to tell the diference between a marshmallow and a Mallowpuff, my advice has gone unheeded. <i>(Aug 17, 2005,NZ Herald,A15</i>)

>  Curiously enough, the famous puff sleeves big enough to hold a leg of lamb and a couple of packets of Mallowpuffs were copied around the world. <i>(Jul 18, 1992,Listener (New Zealand Listener),15</i>)

>  Mine's black with two sugars. You'll see some Mallowpuffs. <i>(Jun 30, 2001,Listener (New Zealand Listener),46</i>)

 <i>n.</i>

### malt
 
 <i>n.</i> beer

>  <b>HUTCHINGS</b> In his dirty denim jeans, with his slickered, swept back hair, with his battered ... motor bike and jar of malt, Schmidt was the closest I'd been to a bodgie - or the external image of what I perceived a bodgie to be. <i>(1987,Tall & Short Stories,81</i>)

### malt sandwich
 
 <i>n.</i> a beer

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>malt sandwich </i>a beer; used Mark Leishman, <i>Today Tonight</i>, TVNZ, 2 September 1987. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,72</i>)

### malthoid
 
 <i>n.</i> a roofing tar-paper material

>  [Advt] "MALTHOID" - As a floorcloth is cheaper and will outlast the most expensive linoleum. <i>(Dec 31, 1909,Otago Daily Times,10</i>)

>  Two clear memories ..., of Jack Gibson and I taking turns with a full roll of malthoid ... <i>(1971,VUWTC'71,40</i>) [NOTES: c1949 in 1971 VUWTC'71 40]

>  THE BIG SWAG [heading] ... the party ... unloaded a discouraging mass of timber, iron, wire-netting, malthoid, nails and sundry hardware ... <i>(1954,Tararua Tramper,4</i>)

>  <i>(1959,Numbers,9/9</i>)

>  <b>CRUMP</b> When all the boards were on [the hut] we covered the whole structure with malthoid and tacked it into place. <i>(1960,Good Keen Man, A,76</i>)

>  <b>MASTERS</b> Among materials and gear carried in along the rugged scrub lined track, were rolls of malthoid and wirenetting, cement and iron, dressed timber for cupboards, door and table, axes, a crosscut saw, a big cast-iron camp-oven, window and various other things. <i>(1960,Back-Country Tales,29</i>)

>  <b>HARVEY</b> Dad had built rather a neat fowl-house out of car-cases and malthoid away up on the hill, after chipping out a large flat piece from the clay. <i>(1964,Any Old Dollars Mister?,2</i>)

>  <b>FERNANDEZ</b> As David lifted the latch to enter the malthoid hut a putrid stench sent him gasping back. <i>(1973,Tussock Fever,39</i>)

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> Cracks came and went, with vagrant drips of water, in the black storm-worn malthoid roof. <i>(1981,Exiles of Asbestos Cottage, The,19</i>)

>  <b>RICHARDS</b> The roof was closely timbered, then covered by strips of Malthoid paper. <i>(1986,Off the Sheep's Back,35</i>)

>  <b>MASTERS</b> The hut is picturesquely situated near the edge of the bush, ... It is a malthoid construction. <i>Ibid.</i> 29 The hut is a malthoid construction, with beech pole framework, ... <i>(1960,Back-Country Tales,21</i>)

>  It was an oblong bach, with creosoted wooden sides (later painted), a malthoid roof, and no running water or electricity. <i>(2008,NZ Memories,74/48</i>)

>  <b>CONEY</b> The English malthoid... almost trips me, dragging at my sprigs... <i>Ibid</i>. 124 Like flies around an Australian, the side clusters to borrow sprigs for a wide range of boots, and sprig tighteners for metric and AF dimensions. <i>(1986,Playing Mantis,112</i>)

>  <b>CONEY</b> Over the grass, up the large concrete step, and on to English malthoid that lines the wooden steps leading up to our dressing room. <i>(1986,Playing Mantis,112</i>)

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> It's an old malthoid-roofed bush house. <i>(1965,Open Country,167</i>)

### malu
 
 <i>n.</i> a thigh tattoo worn by female Samoans [ORIGIN: Samoan] (See [cf. pe'a](../P#pe'a))

>  Film-maker Lisa Raouma presents <i>Measina: Stories of the Malu</i>, a short film detailing the mythological origins of the traditional Samoan tattoo for women... The film also includes conversations with contemporary Samoan women and their experiences of the malu in their lives. <i>(Aug 26, 2003,Independent Herald (Wellington),11</i>)

>  Meanwhile, Lisa Taouma's video installation at the Adam, <i>Measima Samoa</i>, provides a rare revelation of the function and beauty of the Samoan female tattoo on the upper legs, the malu. <i>(Sep 10, 2003,Dominion Post,A12</i>)

>  "We are all aware as Samoans that if a malu or tatau is tattooed without the agreement of the family its results are fatal," Stanley told the <i> Samoa Observer </i> newspaper. Editor of the paper, Mataafa Keni Lesa, said according to superstition if a male is tattooed with a malu it can cause both the wearer and the tattooist to die. <i>(Dec 6, 2006,NZ Herald,A7</i>)

### mamaku
 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Cyathea medullaris</i>, the black tree fern; occas. its prepared edible pith Compare silver fern. [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  <b>CROWE</b> The Rev. Taylor.. describes a few occasions when he ate mamaku pith boiled in tutu juice - a dish he describes as 'quite acceptable'. On his West Coast expedition in 1846, Heaphy resorted to mamaku as a food many times, and although he generally found it disapopointing, he did comment that 'when mixed with wine, sugar and spice in a tart, it might be mistaken for baked apple.' <i>(1997,Field Guide to Native Edible Plants of NZ,117</i>)

>  <b>FORSTER</b> ... I got a Man who went & fetched me a Fern-tree with root and all, he called it <i>Ma[macron]a[macron,grave]gu</i>, & as I pointed to a large ferntree near me, with leaves whose underside is white, he shewed me the difference & called the latter <i>Po[grave]nga.</i> This Tree is full of tender pulp, ... and I believe this Fern is nearly related to the <i>Sago</i>-tree, which is likewise a Fern. <i>(Nov 1773,Resolution Journal of J.R. Forster, The,3/426,1982 ed.</i>)

>  cassada - karto'to and mam|mok [katote <i>and</i> mamaku] <i>(1826,Boultbee Journal,110,1986 ed.</i>)

>  <b>WAKEFIELD & WARD</b> ...mumoki, called by the Europeans cassada. <i>(1837,British Colonization of NZ,324</i>)

>  <b>BIDWILL</b> I also saw here for the first time the gigantic tree fern (Mummuke), the young fronds of which are eaten by the natives, as well as the soft part of the head of the trunk, corresponding to the cabbage of the palm. <i>(1841,Rambles in NZ,100,1952 ed.</i>)

>  <b>DIEFFENBACH</b> ... in former times the food of the natives consisted of sweet potatoes, taro (Caladium esculentum), fern-root (Pteris esculenta), ... the pulp of a fern-tree (Cyathea medullaris) called korau or mamako, ... <i>(1843,Travels in NZ,2/17</i>)

>  <b>HEAPHY</b> They [the Maoris] gave us a pot of potatoes and some <i>mamaku</i>, the baked stem of the fern tree. 1846 <i> Ibid.</i> 232 <i>Mamaku</i>, when mixed with wine, sugar, and spice in a tart, might be mistaken for baked apple. <i>(1846,Notes of an Expedition to Kawatiri and Araura</i>) [NOTES: 1846 Heaphy Exped. to Kawatiri in 1959 Taylor Early Travs. 229]

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> [We] received several presents of Pitau the inside of the stem of the mamaku or korau the Cyathea medullaris, which forms a wholesome and palatable food ... <i>(Dec 10, 1846,Journal,4/149</i>)

>  <b>JOHNSON</b> They were living on fern root and <i>mamako</i>, the soft pith of the tree-fern (<i>Cyathea medullaris</i>) <i>(1847,Notes of a Journey</i>) [NOTES: 1847 Johnson Notes From Jrnl in 1959 Taylor Early Travs 147]

>  <b>BRUNNER</b> Found a mamakou, which we cut down. <i>(1847,Great Journey,44</i>)

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Cyathea <i>medullaris</i>, Sw. ... Nat. name, `Korau' of the northern tribes, `Mamaku' of the southern, <i>Col. </i> <i>(1855,Flora NZ,II Flowerless Plants 7</i>)

>  <b>HAAST</b> ... but I also ... saw the Cyathea medullaris (the mamaku of the natives), ... <i>(1861,Report on Topographical Exploration of Nelson,148</i>)

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Mamaku, <i>Col[enso]. Cyathea medullaris</i>. Mamuk, <i>Hector. Cyathea medullaris</i>. [?Otago pron.] <i>(1867,Handbook NZ Flora (List of Names),766</i>)

>  Mamaku. Cyathea medullaris, Swartz <i>(1869,TrNZI,1/III/51</i>) [NOTES: (Buchanan Botany Otago)]

>  Also, the cooked leaves and herbaceous tops of [32] the Toi (<i>Barbarea Australis</i>), and of the Poroporo, or Raupeti (<i>Solanum nigrum</i>); and the baked inner stems and sago-like pith of the large black fern tree, Korau, or Mamaku (<i>Cyathea medullaris</i>). <i>(1869,TrNZI,1/III/31-32</i>) [NOTES: (Colenso Botany North Isd)]

>  Mamakus are also easily distinguished. Apart from the dense black hair on the fronds, the trunks bear hexagonal scars where old fronds have fallen away. <i>(Nov 30, 2002,Dominion Post,F15</i>)

>  A remarkable feature ... is the social character of the arborescent ferns, more especially the Mamaku (<i>Cyathea medullaris</i>, Swartz.), and the Weki (<i>Dicksonia squarrosa</i>, Swartz.) ... <i>(1870,TrNZI,2/90</i>) [NOTES: (Kirk)]

>  <b>BAINES</b> Then there were the magnificent tree-ferns, one kind growing some forty feet high, called punga by the natives; another sort ... they call mamaku; and yet another giant fern, the pura ... [with] a bulbous-looking root, like Brobdingnagian dahlias. <i>(1874,Edward Crewe,198</i>)

>  The most common species are the poka (<i>Dicksonia squarrosa</i>) and the katote (<i>Hemitelia smithii</i>); the stem and fronds of the latter are sometimes used as food for cows. The mamaku (<i>Cyathea medullaris</i>) is local, and has not been observed south of Halfmoon Bay. <i>(1884,TrNZI,17/217</i>) [NOTES: (Kirk)]

>  <b>PAULIN</b> Pieces of mummock were brought away for cooking purposes, but though the Maories are said to prize it highly I cannot say that our party appreciated it much. <i>(1889,Wild West Coast,107</i>)

>  <b>BAUCKE</b> I have forgotten `mamaku' (the tree fern) - fanning itself in the mid- heat of summer. <i>Ibid.</i> 281 Hence, when ... a large tangi or other party reduced his pataka or rua kumera contents, he ... made shift with fern-root, berries, or mamaku (tree fern) until the next year's crop came in. <i>(1905,Where the White Man Treads,16</i>) [NOTES: [OED]]

>  <b>CHEESEMAN</b> <i>C[yathea] medullaris, Swartz</i> ... Trunk 20-50 ft. high ... <i>Korau; Mamaku; Black Tree-fern. </i> <i>(1906,Manual NZ Flora,948</i>)

>  They could [22] have satisfied most of the `kinaki' [ed fn: Relish eaten with other food] wants from the coast, but preferred forest products. You found the incoming Ngati-Porou along the coast following the fishing grounds and the warm areas for kumara and taro cultivation, while the `tangata whenua' stuck to the hinter-land and forests, faring on `mamaku' [ed fn: Edible tree fern (<i>Cyathea medullaris</i>)], `aruhe', eels and huahua. <i>(May 22, 1930,Na To Hoa Aroha,2/21,1987 ed.</i>) [NOTES: (Ngata)]

>  <b>HYDE</b> When they have passed the mamuk' [[end note] tree-fern which grows right in the middle of the path], they know the Day's Bay fetish has been properly observed. [note 234] Presumably a contraction of mamaku (cyathea medullaris). The term seems peculiar to Robin Hyde - it was never in general use. <i>(1938,Godwits Fly, The,116,1970 ed.</i>)

>  <i>mamaku</i>: large tree-fern. <i>(1947,Reeds' School Dictionary: NZ Suppl.,174</i>)

>  <b>BUCK</b> <i>Tree fern pith</i> (mamaku), <i>and fern root </i>(aruhe) <i>were placed before them</i>. <i>(1949,Coming of the Maori, The,1/34</i>)

>  <b>LEVY</b> ... such species as lancewood, karamu, toro, ramarama, koromiko, kanono, kohuhu, kawakawa, hangehange, rangiora, five finger, patete, toru, tree daisies, broadleaf, kamahi, mahoe, tarata, karo, lacebark, wineberry, fuchsia, kowhai, with tree ferns, weki, and mamuku. Toetoe, phormium, cabbage tree, with swamp coprosma and hupiro may figure as a developmental phase on wet soils. <i>(1951,Grasslands of NZ,87,1970 ed.</i>)

>  The kaponga or brown tree fern is not nearly so handsome as the mamaku, or black tree fern, the latter often being a chief's prerogative for the white heart in the middle of the young leaves, which was baked and eaten. <i>(Aug 12, 1957,Dominion</i>) [NOTES: correspondence column]

>  <i>mamaku</i> [s] <i>Cyathea medullaris</i> (Forst.f.) Swartz <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,46</i>)

>  <b>BURTON</b> [Title] Mamaku (tree fern) In springtime, the rough outer bark of selected tree ferns (Cyathea medullaris) was chipped with a sharp instrument to allow the sometimes bitter juices to trickle out. <i>(1982,Two Hundred Years of NZ Food & Cookery,7</i>)

>  <i>mamaku n.</i> black tree fern <i>(1985,Collins Compact Dictionary,297</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.; nf. 1986 NZPOD]]

>  With a tall woody trunk up to 20 m in height and graceful arching fronds forming an umbrella-like canopy, the mamaku (<i>Cyathea medullaris</i>) is a truly spectacular plant, and a stand on a hillside is one of the more evocative sights in the New Zealand landscape. <i>(2001,NZ Geographic,No. 49/72</i>)

>  Mamaku is the tallest and darkest, its black trunk pockmarked with the oval scars of old leaves. <i>(Jan 3, 2004,Dominion Post,C2</i>)

>  The pith of the trunk and leaf bases of mamaku (<i>Cyathea medullaris</i>) were frequently eaten by Maori, especially when travelling, and the king fern or para (<i>Marattia salicina</i>) was cultivated for its tuberous root, which was condisered a great delicacy when roasted. <i>(2001,NZ Geographic,No. 49/77</i>)

>  Jim didn't scorn the products of the bush, ferns whose roots made succulent eating, the shoots of Pikopiko and Kouka, the pith of the mamaku fern and of the nikau, as well as the many berries whose merits he knew as well as any Maori. <i>(Sep 1962,Te Ao Hou,40: 18</i>)

>  ..and on days when he found nothing, leftover dried slices of mamaku pith and cooked tawa kernels... <i>(Apr 30, 2006,Dominion Post,E12</i>)

>  The tallest and fastest growing native fern is Cyathea medullaris, the Mamaku. <i>(Sep 15, 2001,Dominion Weekend Magazine,10</i>)

### man alone
 
 <i>n.</i> a type (or stereotype) of the independent, outdoors New Zealander. [also attrib.]

>  We have, if you like, extended the myth of what the New Zealand character is. The man alone battling against nature, facing banks, and the expertise with a piece of No 8 wire. <i>(Apr 4, 1987,Listener (New Zealand Listener),21</i>)

>  The narrative voice in Stephen Sinclair's first novel is a familiar one, one that has appeared in "man alone" novels from John Mulgan's landmark book that gave the genre its name in 1939 to Owen Marshall's <i>Harlequin Rex</i> last year. <i>(Sep 2, 2000,Listener (New Zealand Listener),46</i>)

>  <b>FRANCIS</b> <i>Man Alone</i> [chapter heading] <i>(1983,Wildlife Ranger,72</i>)

>  "Mulgan's only novel was a remarkably mature evocation of God's own country going to the dogs in the post-World War I decades. Its iconic reputation as a book that celebrates rugged individualism and self-sufficiency is in potent conflict with the socialistic implications of its title that derives from Ernest Hemingway's statement in <i>To Have and Have Not</i> that 'a man alone ain't got no bloody f---ing chance'." - Peter Simpson <i>(Mar 20, 2004,Listener (New Zealand Listener),61</i>)

>  [Colenso] seems to have been the original Man Alone. <i>(Jul 27, 2004,TV1</i>) [NOTES: Peter Ellis in documentary on Colenso.]

>  Long-time feminist Sandra Coney believss Brash is appealing to the "man alone" part of the Kiwi psyche. <i>(Apr 11, 2004,Sunday Star Times,C5</i>)

>  For Mackenzie, this isolation may have been from necessity, but it was his ability to deal with this aloneness that enabled him to access this landscape like no other settler of his time. In fact, Mackenzie's story may have been the foundation of the "man alone" genre that still permeates New Zealand film and literature to this day. <i>(Mar 13, 2004,Listener (New Zealand Listener),46</i>)

>  It would probably take a scholarly thesis, however, to examine the way that Crump first popularised, then personified and finally parodied the Man Alone myth that has run though this country's literature like a strand of No. 8 wire... "The notion of the Man Alone figure goes way back to the 19th century and to a certain extent it occurs in Australian writing as well; so he comes out of a long tradition, really... The coup de grace was the Toyota ad campaign of the 80s and 90s in which Man Alone tackled the wilderness in a Japanese car. <i>(Feb 20, 1999,Listener (New Zealand Listener),33</i>)

>  Kiwis make myths of `man alone' type efforts against the juggernaut of state authority, but cringe weekly when it comes to doing something for themselves. <i>(Dec 27, 1983,Dominion Sunday Times,6</i>)

>  But fortunes change, Man Alone cliches and thriller expectations are subverted and gender roles are picked up and dropped again with the ease of a game of charades... <i>(Jan 31, 2004,Listener (New Zealand Listener),53</i>)

>  Against the triumphalism of the New Zealand narrative as the "Man Alone" story, a Puritan hangover, Robin Hyde is emerging as the ghost-writer in the sky, retrospectively altering New Zealand literary history, not so much as "Woman Alone", although she was that, but as a communal writer; one writing to include the dispossessed, the outcast, one writing against the dominant paradigm. <i>(Jul 31, 1999,Listener (New Zealand Listener),44</i>)

>  Mitchell linked the typical architect's house of the 1940s to the "man alone" theme of New Zealand literature. <i>(May 12, 1984,Listener (New Zealand Listener),101</i>)

>  He is legend - even among Southern Men, especially after he survives a "man alone" experience in the bush. <i>(Nov 28, 1998,Dominion,22</i>)

>  Could he do something about the title, as <i>Talking of War</i>, just a the moment, seemed that bit too ambiguous. "In consultation with my chief reader," Kerby wrote, "and bearing in mind the fact that Hitler is master of Europe, [192] what did he think of either 'A man Alone', or 'Escape from Death'? The editor felt the former was the one to go for. ... The title Muligan settled on for the novel has at times been remarkably misread. In his own country, the phrase 'man alone' became a glib response to what is supposed to define an aspect of national maleness, what Orsman's <i>Dictionary of New Zealand English,/i> designates 'a type (or stereotype) of the independent outdoors New Zealander.' It is as though the novel is assumed to celebrate Johnson's raw struggle with land and society, and that Muligan proposed that experience as asdmirably defining. <i>(2003,Long Journey to the Border,191</i>)

>  Kiwis were once magnificent at doing the man-alone number, rumbling around a sparsely populated country like the proverbial lonely old man and managing to deliver the man-of-few-words speech when encountered, but our personality has undergone a profound change. <i>(Aug 20, 2005,Listener (New Zealand Listener),60</i>)

>  <i>The Man Alone - And Two Other Guys</i> plays on the image of the classic Kiwi bloke of John Mulgan's novel <i>Man Alone</i> - the book that was said to define our national identity. <i>(Jan 25, 2004,Sunday Star Times,A8</i>)

>  Brad was talking about the man alone thing, too, which I didn't know ... The New Zealand idea of the stoic man. <i>(Apr 3, 2004,Listener (New Zealand Listener),11</i>)

### man drought
 
 <i>n.</i> a shortage of eligible single men [also Australian]

>  [heading] She's a hard road to find any man Downunder New Zealand and Australia in the throes of 'man drought' <i>(Jul 27, 2005,NZ Herald,A3</i>)

>  You could hardly have missed the widespread media coverage of the "man drought" facing New Zealand (the mind boggles at possible hose bans). <i>(Sep 11, 2005,NZ Herald,Herald on Sunday 6</i>)

>  Salt attributes our "man drought" to the fact that young single males are more likely to stay away permanently or come back with an overseas born partner. .. Richard Bedford says the man drought is real but not nearly as bad as Salt says - and OE isn't causing it. .. Man Drought is due, suggests Bedford, to the compounding and cumulative effects of four factors: more young men than women dying in accidents and from suicide; higher net migration loss among men; more women than men coming to New Zealand from some countries and a tendency for males to be under-counted at censuses. <i>(Nov 2005,North & South,39-40</i>)

>  FORGET the brain drain, this is the age of the man drought. <i>(Aug 19, 2005,Whakatane Beacon,4</i>)

>  .. so are unattached <i>Beacon</i> girls Diane, Fiona, Debbie and Melanie victims of a man drought that is gripping New Zealand? <i>(Aug 19, 2005,Whakatane Beacon,1</i>)

 <i>phr.</i> (Rural) a farmer.

### man space
 
 <i>n.</i> [see bloke's shed]

>  A neat & tidy 3 bedroom home with loads of man-space. <i>(May 12, 2010,Horowhenua Chronicle</i>)

>  Our contact j-m has a fully restored e-type in his car fleet and the best personal wine cellar I've ever seen. He'd designed his new house around his undergorund cellar. Man space. <i>(Sep 18, 2004,Internet - see Notes</i>) [NOTES: http://www.drury.net.nz/2004/09/]

"Having one's own space is a way to define and establish an identity," says Sam Martin, author of Manspace: A Primal Guide to Marking Your Territory. There's even a social-historical reason for men to imbue the motorised man-space with male potency. <i>(2010,Internet - see Notes</i>) [NOTES: http://nz.lifestyle.yahoo.com/womens-health/sex-relationships/article/-/5938058/boys-and-their-hoods/]
