### mana
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <i>mana n</i> -s [of Melanesian & Polynesian origin; akin to Hawaiian & Maori <i>mana</i> impersonal supernatural force or power that may be concentrated in objects or persons and that may be inherited, acquired, or conferred <i>(1961,Merriam-Webster Third International,1372</i>)

>  <i>mana n. Anthropol. 1.</i> (in Polynesia, etc.) a concept of a life force associated with high social status and ritual power. 2. any power achieved by ritual means. [< Polynesian] <i>(1982,Collins Concise English Dictionary,684</i>) [NOTES: [1986 NZPOD 455; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 298]]



 
 <i>n.</i> authority, prestige, or influence. [Maori] [[Ma. <b>mana</b> integrity, charisma, prestigue, formal adj.] cf. P.M. Ryan Dict. Modern Maori (1995) 126.]

>  <i>~ n.</i> power, influence, authority, prestige [Maori] <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Whitcombe's),619</i>)

>  <b>BRATHWAITE</b> MANA Personal prestige. MANA TAPU Personal sancity. <i>(1967,Evil Day,380</i>)

>  <i>mana ... n.</i> power, influence, authority, prestige [Maori]. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),12</i>)

>  <b>FINLAYSON</b> mana: prestige and authority. <i>(1973,Brown Man's Burden,141</i>)

>  Understanding what is required of them, the artists strive to build into their artistic creations the mana (power) that will move people. <i>(1984,Te Maori,24</i>)

>  <b>HOWE</b> Mana - `prestige' - but actually much more than this. It includes charisma, power, control and influence. People can accrue mana by means of birth, leadership qualities increasingly shown, knowledge, and the ability to increase the prestige of all things Maori. Increasing age may bring mana. It is earned, though sometimes given by a group to someone or to a tribe beacause of actions deemed to be worthy of them. Loss of mana is a loss of one's dignity and sense of worth. Total loss of mana could mean loss of life's meaning. <i>(1985,Towards Taha Maori,20</i>)

>  Speaking steadily, calmly, remorselessly, making in his throat from time to time that small cough which is part of kawa, he [a Maori acitivist] appeals to their sense of pride, their mana. <i>(Feb 28, 1987,Listener (New Zealand Listener),11</i>)

>  It has been well-known that the Waikatos did not allow that they had lost "mana" over that country. <i>(Feb 20, 1871,Evening Post,2</i>)

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> He replied it was true he should not be able to understand what was said but still the `mana' or efficacy of the prayers there offered up would be of as much avail to him as to the rest of the congregation. <i>(Jun 4, 1848,Journal,5/219</i>)

>  The greatest reception the Governor has had from natives, the most loyal reverence for the Queen's name and `mana', the most intelligent acquaintance with the English tikanga [fn. Policy.], ... are to be found in the Ngapuhi ... <i>(Mar 21, 1858,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,1/367,1960 ed.</i>) [NOTES: (F.D. Bell)]

>  At Whanganui they object to the form of deed conveying the `mana' or power over the land to the Queen. <i>(May 25, 1859,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,1/460,1960 ed.</i>) [NOTES: (D. McLean)]

>  <b>BUDDLE</b> `When he returned these tribes were asked ... `Will you have this man for a King?' The reply was `Ae'. He asked again `Will you give all the power (mana) and all the land to the King?' They replied `Ae', again. <i>Ibid.</i> 17 Another object aimed at is the preservation of what they call the `mana' of the Chiefs. This word means authority, power, influence. <i>Ibid.</i> 18 [inherent in land] `Kia mau te mana o te whenua' is another expression now in frequent use, i.e., `hold fast the mana of the land'. ... This is altogether a new application of the term; perhaps it has ben adopted in consequence of the Queen's Sovereignty over the Island having been translated as the Queen's mana. But it certainly did not originally mean that which is now claimed for it, viz., a Chief's `manorial right'. This use of the word was not heard until this Maori King movement originated it. (See Paul's speech, p. 51.) <i>(1860,Maori King Movement,13</i>)

>  <b>BUDDLE</b> `When he returned these tribes were asked ... `Will you have this man for a King?' The reply was `Ae'. He asked again `Will you give all the power (mana) and all the land to the King?' They replied `Ae', again. <i>Ibid.</i> 17 Another object aimed at is the preservation of what they call the `mana' of the Chiefs. This word means authority, power, influence. <i>Ibid.</i> 18 [inherent in land] `Kia mau te mana o te whenua' is another expression now in frequent use, i.e., `hold fast the mana of the land'. ... This is altogether a new application of the term; perhaps it has ben adopted in consequence of the Queen's Sovereignty over the Island having been translated as the Queen's mana. But it certainly did not originally mean that which is now claimed for it, viz., a Chief's `manorial right'. This use of the word was not heard until this Maori King movement originated it. (See Paul's speech, p. 51.) <i>(1860,Maori King Movement,13</i>)

>  <b>MANING</b> ... it was ... from the deck of a small trading schooner ... that I first cast eyes on Maori land. It <i>was</i> Maori land then; but, alas! what is it now? Success to you, O King of Waikato. May your <i>mana</i> never be less! <.I>Ibid.</i> (vi) 90 ... all these qualifications combined made him unquestionably `a great <i>rangatira'</i>, and, as he had never been defeated, his mana was in full force. <.I>Ibid.</i> (xv) 224 Now then for <i>mana. Virtus, prestige,</i> authority, good fortune, influence, sanctity, luck, are all words which, under certain conditions, give something near the meaning of <i>mana</i>, though not one of them gives it exactly; but before I am done, the reader shall have a reasonable notion (for a pakeha) of what it is. <i>(1863,Old New Zealand Houses,(i) 3</i>)

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> The chiefs seeing that their position was lost, and that in proportion to the alienation of the land their <i>mana</i> - power, went with it, ... began to bestir themselves. <i>(1868,Past and Present of NZ,120</i>)

>  <b>CAMPBELL</b> ... should the future, unhappily, foment squabbles between the two races, the time might come when we should be glad to shelter ourselves under the `mana' (the protection) of good old Kanini. <i>(1881,Poenamo,3/4/166</i>)

>  ...but as soon as that deadly invention, gunpowder, came in use both knight & chief rapidly lost their mana or reputation for doughty deeds. ... To the breaking-down of the position and the mana of the chiefs. <i>(1893,TrNZI,25/485</i>) [NOTES: (Hamilton)]

>  <b>MORRIS</b> <i>Mana, n.</i> a Maori word for power, influence, right, authority, <i>prestige</i>. See chapter on <i>Mana</i>, in `Old New Zealand' (1863), by Judge Maning. <i>(1898,Austral English,281</i>)

>  <b>DIEFFENBACH</b> <i>' Mana</i>- command, authority, power.' <i>(1843,Travels in NZ,1/371</i>)

>  <b>TREGEAR</b> The word <i>mana</i> itself has no English equivalent, but it may be best rendered in this connection as `prestige', that is, `influence derived from former achievements and from a confident expectation of future success'. <i>(1904,Maori Race, The,317</i>)

>  <i>Ma&#769;.na </i>(ma.&#769;.n&adot.), <i>n</i>. [Maori.] The authority, influence, or prestige enjoyed by a person on account of his rank, character, or success in life, or for any other reason. [<i>N. Z. </i>and <i>N. S. W</i>.] In its Maori use, <i>mana</i> has a wide signification and is applied to both persons and things. In English, it is technically used of the authority or prestige of a Maori chief, but is colloquially applied to other persons, esp. public officers and politicians. <i>(1898,Merriam-Webster Third International,2026</i>)

>  MANA. - A Maori word for prestige. <i>(1917,New Zealand At the Front,xiv</i>)

>  <b>BUCK</b> Some tribes openly stated that <i>mana</i> and prestige came from the canoes, but right to land occupation came from these prior [sc. prior to the main `fleet'] inhabitants who were termed <i>tangata-whenua</i>, the people of the soil or aborigines. <i>(1929,Coming of the Maori, The,17</i>)

>  <i>~ n</i>. authority, right. [Maori] <i>(1929,Whitcombe's Etym. Dict. Aust-NZ Suppl.,7</i>)

>  <b>LAMBERT</b> A new site is now being sought ... but the difficulty is to find a spot where the <i>mana</i> of Kopu was paramount. <i>(1936,Pioneering Reminiscences of Old Wairoa,176</i>)

>  <b>HYDE</b> When I come back [to the pa] they treat me as if I'm not there, and my husband, he's the big fellow - got the big <i>mana</i>, eh? <i>(1938,Nor the Years Condemn,169</i>)

>  <i>mana</i>, authority, control, influence, power (physical or psychic), an attribute of objects as well as of man: the meaning is like that of `chair,' everybody knows it but few can define it. <i>(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/149</i>) [NOTES: Andersen Maori Words in English]

>  <b>BEAGLEHOLE</b> <i>mana</i> power, authority, prestige, psychic force <i>(1946,Some Modern Maoris,xxii</i>)

>  <i>mana</i>: prestige, power, influence, authority. <i>(1947,Reeds' School Dictionary: NZ Suppl.,174</i>)

>  <b>SINCLAIR</b> Nevertheless they possessed considerable power by the virtue of mana (a word meaning something more than `prestige') which resulted from exalted descent and past achievements. <i>Ibid.</i> 302 [Maori glossary] <i>mana</i> Prestige; influence;authority <i>(1959,History of New Zealand,21</i>)

>  ~ A Maori word meaning `power, influence, prestige'. It has been found useful and adopted in English. When a man is disgraced his `mana is lessened. <i>(1958,Whitcombe's Modern Junior Dictionary (8th edn),249</i>)

>  <b>KEENE</b> <i>mana</i>: prestige, influence, control. <i>(1963,O Te Raki,192</i>)

>  His <i>mana</i> was from heaven, and not like the <i>mana</i> of a piece of paper. <i>(Nov 13, 1890,Otago Witness,24</i>)

>  The mana of Tana [Umaga] would travel to South Africa and Australia. <i>(Jul 11, 2005,NZ Herald,1</i>)

>  European law and mana are again to be lowered in the eyes of the Maoris. <i>(Sep 6, 1879,Auckland Weekly News,7</i>)

>  <b>DIEFFENBACH</b> Mana - command, authority, power <i>(1843,Travels in NZ,2/371</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> prestige; authority (moving into general English use, often as a specialist or technical term: see OEDS) spg also with quotation mark or italics

>  The old man is anxious to have the `mana' of the undertaking and so far I am quite willing to humour him. <i>(1856,McLean Papers,3/53</i>)

>  <b>REEVES</b> Very few words have been adopted from the vigorous and expressive Maori. The convenient `mana', which covers prestige, authority, and personal magnetism; .... <i>(1898,Long White Cloud, The,403</i>) [NOTES: [on NZ speech]]

>  Spain's diminishing mana.... <i>(Jan 10, 1899,Evening Post,6</i>)

>  <b>IRVINE & ALPERS</b> A few expressive terms have been adopted into daily speech from Maori -<i> whare</i> (a hut), <i>pa</i> (a village) and <i>mana</i> (prestige) are examples; <i>(1902,Progress of NZ in the 20th Century,249</i>)

>  <i>Ma&#769;.na </i>(ma.&#769;.n&adot.), <i>n</i>. [Maori.] The authority, influence, or prestige enjoyed by a person on account of his rank, character, or success in life, or for any other reason. [<i>N. Z.</i> and <i>N. S. W</i>.] In its Maori use, <i>mana</i> has a wide signification and is applied to both persons and things. In English, it is technically used of the authority or prestige of a Maori chief, but is colloquially applied to other persons, esp. public officers and politicians. <i>(1898,Merriam-Webster Third International,2026</i>)

>  <b>DRUMMOND</b> The liberal leader's `mana' was gone ... <i>(1907,Life & Work of Seddon,54</i>)

>  <b>MULGAN</b> Never yet had he been beaten by a crowd, and if he [351] yielded now his mana would be irretrievably damaged and the whole cause would suffer. <i>(1934,Spur of Morning,350</i>)

>  <b>HYDE</b> The mana of the Bulletin was great, and had its awkward moments for its many imitators over here in New Zealand. <i>Ibid.</i> 115 His rejection at an earlier contest certainly did have that startling emotional effect upon him, but both his mana and his political backing have been rejuvenated, ... <i>(1934,Journalese,21`</i>)

>  <b>ALLAN</b> Sufficient mana adhered to him to ensure him a job. <i>(1937,Hedge-Sparrow, The,255</i>)

>  <b>LAWLOR</b> Terry's <i>mana</i> was great indeed [with the other children]. <i>(1938,House of Templemore, The,151</i>)

>  <b>SOLJAK</b> New Zealand colloquialisms which are of Maori origin include: ... <i>mana</i>: power, prestige <i>(1946,New Zealand (various authors/titles),116</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> Archdeacon Harper, our vicar, was a great personality and had a <i>mana</i> all of his own. <i>(1950,My First 80 Years,44</i>)

>  ... Men lose a lot of mana [? `authority'] in the home. <i>(Oct 31, 1951,Radio NZ / National Radio</i>) [NOTES: 2YA Women's Panel.]

>  <b>McGILL</b> Perhaps the most useful and expressive of our loans from the Maori is <i>mana</i>, reputation, honour, etc. Another is <i>haka</i>, now becoming well known wherever our football teams go. <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,2/679</i>) [NOTES: Wall NZ Speech]

>  <b>LEE</b> Clever organiser Bert Davy knew that Ward was a shadow of the man who had done great things in Seddon's day, but he knew that the Ward mana persisted, ... <i>(1965,Rhetoric at the Red Dawn,117</i>)

>  <b>HOLCROFT</b> My mana rose locally, and the serial brought almost as much satisfaction to the Dales ... as it did to me. <i>(1984,Way of a Writer, The,171</i>)

>  <b>HOLCROFT</b> My mana rose locally, and the serial brought almost as much satisfaction to the Dales ... as it did to me. <i>(1984,Way of a Writer, The,171</i>)

>  She said the difference in mana from the traditional whanau to the Mongrel Mob was that, with the mob, mana was not based on family links and members were accountable only to themselves. <i>(Sep 9, 1987,Dominion,8</i>)

>  That's why Peters was there in the Anthill lobby at 4.30 one morning, clutching for some odd reason, a plastic Unicef bag in his hand, but otherwise immaculate in double-breated suit, as if to the mana born. <i>(Apr 22, 1991,Listener (New Zealand Listener),35</i>)

>  Mr Hill's mana ought to be worth something now. <i>(Aug 13, 1904,NZ Observer,17</i>)

 <i>phr.</i> [ORIGIN: Maori]

### Mana Motuhake
 
 <i>n.</i> a Maori political party, founded by former Labour MP Matiu Rata

>  The Alliance represents a new departure in New Zealand political organisation, It is perhaps the first "confederal" party in New Zealand political history... The constitutional structure of the Alliance itself is, to say the least, rudimentary., In broad brush terms, the 5 party coalition (NewLabour, Mana Motuhake, the Democrats, the Liberals and the Greens) is organized as follows... <i>(Nov 1992,NZ Political Review,1(8) : 21</i>)

>  Perhaps the Mana Motuhake Party has not yet earned an entry and the Hon. Matiu Rata is not an important enough contemporary politician to justify his inclusion. <i>(Mar 24, 1984,Listener (New Zealand Listener),93</i>)

>  Mana Motuhake has contested all four Maori seats in every general election since 1981 and is, in effect, a <i>national</i> political party. <i>(Feb 3, 1992,Listener (New Zealand Listener),29</i>)

>  Not Kopu. She got involved in a political party, Mana Motuhake. <i>(Jun 12, 1999,Listener (New Zealand Listener),14</i>)

>  Mana Motuhake was never intended to be subservient to anybody ... To be under a Pakeha structure? Absolutely no. <i>(Jun 6, 1999,Listener (New Zealand Listener),35</i>)

### mana whenua/manawhenua
 
 <i>n.</i> tribal authority; iwi holding that authority [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  In 1991, by an Act of Parliament, Ngati Whatua o Orakei, the hapu of Auckland's Ngati Whatua iwi was recognised as holding manawhenua - tribal authority - in the Auckland isthmus. <i>(Apr 2004,Wel-com (Wellington),5</i>)

>  The only title Maori will get is a vague customary rights "mana whenua" title, acknowledging their ancestral connection to the beach they may have fished off for hundreds of years. <i>(Dec 14, 2003,Sunday Star Times,C3</i>)

>  Ngati Awa and Tuwharewtoa Bay of Plenty also claim mana whenua status for the land (the right to speak for the land) ... <i>(Dec 28, 2005,Whakatane Beacon,1</i>)

>  It is my duty as mana whenua to feed those who hunger for kaimoana. <i>(Oct 4, 2003,Listener (New Zealand Listener),34</i>)

>  Neither Parliament nor Pakeha law could touch the rights and responsibilities her tribe held as manawhenua, she said. Manawhenua status stemmed from the gods and encompassed the concepts of ownership, regulation, management, control and a spiritual dimension best but inadequately described in English as "dominion". <i>(Aug 26, 2004,Dominion Post,A2</i>)

>  Nga Rauru, with manawhenua from the northern side of the lower river, have never been back on the hill. <i>(Dec 14, 1996,Listener (New Zealand Listener),41</i>)

>  Nobody holds that right, to sell mana whenua. <i>(Apr 22, 1995,Listener (New Zealand Listener),24</i>)

>  He said the Bill's direction that new District Health Boards must form partnerships with manawhenua [those who hold customary authority over an area] "will excite factionalism and fighting." <i>(Aug 10, 2000,Dominion</i>)

>  Mrs Turia.. told Parliament yesterday that mana whenua (tribal groups) had different rights from others "in certain situations". Mr Samuels said mana whenua was a historical concept arising from Maori tribal wars. It acknowledged traditional boundaries and customs and still had a place in relations between certain hapu (subtribes) and iwi (tribes). <i>(Nov 16, 2000,Dominion,3</i>)

>  Asked why his iwi objected to Waitaha's inclusion, he said, "It's about mana whenua. It's only those three iwi .. that had mana whenua in Tauranga Moana." <i>(May 30, 2006,NZ Herald,A6</i>)

 <i>v.</i>

 <i>n.</i>

### mana-munching
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Many kaumatua disapprove of the trend towards just anybody wearing moko (described by some as "mana-munching"). <i>(May 17, 1997,Listener (New Zealand Listener),36</i>)

>  Mr Delamere said the trouble with the iwi organisations was the amount of "mana-munching" by individuals within them... "Everyone wants to be a chief. Those who miss out get a lawyer and start fighting," he said. <i>(Apr 5, 1997,Dominion,2</i>)

>  Maori MPs should stop "mana munching" and concentrate on real problems, Labour Maori Affairs spokesman Dover Samuels said yesterday. <i>(Jul 20, 1998,Dominion,2</i>)

>  "Mana-munching" is the new lingo for those who fragment into ever smaller groups in search of status and funding for their grievances. <i>(Dec 2, 2001,Sunday Star Times,C1</i>)

>  While we assist individual claimants, we actively discourage the "mana-munching", competition and fragmentation of claims that you criticise. <i>(Dec 9, 2001,Sunday Star Times,A8</i>)

### manaaki/manaakitanga
 
 <i>n.</i> hospitality, goodwill, caring [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  We've seen over the past 25 years that the manaaki, the graciousness of other Maori MPs have been taken for granted. <i>(1998,Sunday Star Times,C2</i>) [NOTES: Probably issue in June 1998]

>  <b>HOWE</b> Manaaki - this term is of special goodwill. It refers to acts of kindness, hospitality and goodwill. It is related to the term `mana', and refers to a person's finer qualities rather than what a person has in terms of material goods. If a Maori shows warmth, respect and kindness to people, they are showing manaakitanga. The most humble homes can show this quality as much as wealthy homes - it is giving from the heart. <i>(1985,Towards Taha Maori,20</i>)

>  ...Maori concepts of manaakitanga (literally, caretaker-ship) will allow all to enjoy our natural resources, within the bounds of environmental sustainability. <i>(2004,Salient,9/23</i>)

>  Trainee Kaitohutohu Himiona Herbert approaches his career work with a strong Maori perspective. He says his dealings with Maori clients are based on the concept of manaakitanga - welcoming people in and making them feel at home with the same sense of warmth and connection that they would encounter on a marae. <i>(2003,Internet - see Notes</i>) [NOTES: from careers.govt.nz (Career Services publications Issue 10, July 2003)]

 <i>n.</i>

### manaia
 
 <i>n.</i> the grotesque beaked figure often seen in Maori wood carving. [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  <b>IHIMAERA & LONG</b> the manaia / bows his head in shame, / and the shopkeeper keeps on smiling. <i>(1982,Into the World of Light,197</i>)

>  <b>BAKER</b> The manaia nestled beneath his swanny, the safety it promised felt like a threat, and the dangers of his journey now real and focused. <i>(2000,Kokopu Dreams,67</i>)

>  The ridge-boards are carved to represent a number of <i>ngarara,</i> or lizards, running along the roof, and the <i>maihi, or gable-boards, have carvings of the mythological animal known as the <i>manaia</i> - probably a kind of taniwha. <i>(1895,TrNZI,27/764</i>)

>  <b>McLINTOCK</b> [check this] Apart from the naturalistic figure, every type of full-faced figure has a <i>manaia</i> to match. ... The head of the <i>manaia</i> can, in each case, be recognised a half of the head of the appropriate matching figure divided down the middle of the face. <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,2/410</i>)

>  <i>manaia n.</i> motif in Maori carving with bird-like head and human body. [M] <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,455</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.]]

>  We sell a lot more traditional-styled garments like the manaia with the head of a bird, the body of a man, and the tale [sic] of a fish. <i>(Feb 5, 2003,Capital Times,6</i>)

>  As for the manaia, we need only look at a number of Maori carvings such as lintels and canoe-carvings to discover how many varieties of curious figures the Maori carver made of it. <i>(Apr 1956,Te Ao Hou,14: 32</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Hippocampus abdominalis</i>, sea-horse Maori

### manaoa
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) [Ma.] silver pine or <i>Dacrydium kirkii</i> of the family Podocarpaceae. [cf manoao, (<i>Halocarpus kirkii</i>, and <i>Manoao colensoi</i>, silver pine]

>  <b>KIRK</b> The wood of the manaoa is of a light-brown colour. <i>(1889,Forest Flora,192</i>)

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Manaoa, Dacrydium Colensoi. <i>(1867,Handbook NZ Flora,II 766</i>)

>  The open pumice land is covered with a tangled mass of manuka and manaoa, with tussock on the colder flats. In developing pumice country a great saving can be effected in clearing costs if the manuka and manaoa scrub are burnt standing two or three years ahead of cultivation. Dense manaoa scrub is difficult and costly to clear in its green state, but it carries a fire well and its burnt stubs soon rot. <i>(Jul 16, 1945,NZ Journal of Agriculture,17</i>)

### manatu
 
 <i>n.</i> the small deciduous ribbonwood

>  <b>CHEESEMAN</b> <i>[Plagianthus betulinus, A.Cunn.] Ribbon-wood </i>of Europeans;<i> manatu</i> of the Maoris. <i>(1906,Manual NZ Flora,78</i>)

>  <b>RICHARDS</b> <i>P[lagianthus] chathamicus</i> ... MANATU. <i>(1952,Chatham Islands,64</i>)

>  <b>MOORE & IRWIN</b> <i>PLAGIANTHUS BETULINUS, manatu.</i> The riverbank ribbonwood is considered by some botanists to be more correctly known as <i>Plagianthus regius</i> <i>(1978,Oxford Book of NZ Plants,72</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> <i>Plagianthus regius</i> AND RIBBONWOOD ... Maanatu ... Small deciduous tree. <i>(1982,Stewart Island Plants,92</i>)

### manatungo
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>YATE</b> `Manatungos,' or remembrancers, ... are always either suspended round the neck or to the ear; they are mostly made of the ponamu, the green stone found only in the Southern Island. <i>(1835,New Zealand (various authors/titles),151</i>)

### manawa
 
 <i>n.</i> a small native mangrove tree, Avicennia marina, of tidal mudflats of the northern North Island. [ORIGIN: Maori] (See [mangrove](../M#mangrove))

>  <b>LEE & KENDALL</b> Man|awa, <i>s</i>. ... Also name of a certain tree. <i>(1820,Grammar & Vocabulary of the Language of NZ,174</i>)

>  <b>POLACK</b> <i>Manawa and Tuputupu,</i> two varieties of the well-known mangrove, cover the mud banks of the rivers and creeks of the country in which they flourish. <i>(1838,New Zealand  (Polack),2/398</i>)

>  <b>DIEFFENBACH</b> The arms of the Parenga-renga inlet ... generally terminate in swamps, grown over with what is called the New Zealand mangrove (Avicennia tomentosa), named manawa by the natives. <i>(1843,Travels in NZ,1/204</i>)

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Avicennia <i>tomentosa</i>, L. ... Nat. name, `Manawa', <i>Cunn. </i> <i>(1853,Flora NZ,I Flowering Plants 204</i>)

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Maanawa, <i>Col[enso]. Avicennia tomentosa. </i> <i>(1867,Handbook NZ Flora (List of Names),766</i>)

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> The Manawa (<i>Mangrove</i>) is very abundant, and prized for the soap manufactory, from the great quantity of potash it contains. <i>(1868,Past and Present of NZ,205</i>)

>  b. <i>for tanning</i>; the Toatoa, or Tanekaha ..., the Makamaka (<i>Ackama rosaefolia</i>), so closely allied to the Towai; the Manaawa, or White Mangrove (<i>Avicennia tomentosa</i>,) ... <i>(1869,TrNZI,1/III/50</i>) [NOTES: (Colenso Botany North Isd)]

>  <b>HAY</b> The Manawa, or `mangrove' (<i>Avicennia officinalis</i>), is very plentiful in the north, ... <i>(1882,Brighter Britain,2/197</i>)

>  [Native name] Manawa .. [Settlers' name] Mangrove .. [Family] Verbenaceae <i>(1902,Settler's Handbook of NZ, The,122</i>) [NOTES: 1886 T.Kirk in 1902 Settler's Handbook of NZ 122]

>  <b>COLENSO</b> We also noticed the absence of ... the shore-loving <i>Maanawa</i> (=Mangrove) ... <i>(1888,Fifty Years In Maoriland,43</i>)

>  <b>CHEESEMAN</b> <i>[Avicennia officinalis, Linn.]</i> NORTH ISLAND: Muddy creeks and estuaries from North Cape to Opotiki and Kawhia on the west. <i>Manawa; Mangrove. </i> <i>(1906,Manual NZ Flora,567</i>)

>  <b>LAING & BLACKWELL</b> <i>Avicennia officinalis (The Mangrove)</i> ... Maori name, <i>Manawa. </i> <i>(1907,Plants of NZ,352,1940 p. 373 ed.</i>)

>  <b>55/153</b> <i>ma[a]nawa</i>, an amphibolic tree (Avicennia officinalis), mangrove. Without the macron, manawa, the meaning is bowels, heart, breath, patience, mind, spirit: in the place-name Manawatu, heart startled and standing still for a momentl or, heart at rest and peace. <i>(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society</i>) [NOTES: Andersen Maori Words in English]

>  <b>59/1</b> <i>(Nov 1957,NZ Timber Journal</i>)

>  <b>NORRIS</b> When the peat had been burnt [at Rukuhia Swamp, Hamilton] there would be a network of manawa branches, and a subsequent fire would destroy these. <i>(1964,Settlers in Depression,201</i>)

>  <i>mangrove noun </i>any of a group of low trees with exposed roots, usually found in tropical tidal swamps; <i>manawa. </i> <i>(1979,Heinemann NZ Dictionary,658</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) a New Zealand-bred cultivar of ryegrass, Lolium multiflorum. [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  <b>LEVY</b> Dominance of any one species, excepting perhaps the very palatable Paroa Italian ryegrass and Manawa ryegrass (short rotation) influences palatability. <i>(1970,Grasslands Range of Forage and Conservation Plants,135</i>)

>  <b>LEVY</b> The strength of Manawa is its high winter and early spring growth, its palatability to stock and its good nutritive quality. <i>(1970,Grasslands Range of Forage and Conservation Plants,175</i>)

>  Ryegrass cultivars differ markedly in susceptibility. Ruanui and Ariki are often more severely infected [with blind seed disease] than Manawa and Paroa, probably because the latter two cultivars flower later. <i>(Oct 1974,NZ Journal of Agriculture,19</i>)

>  Nitrate levels are likely to be higher with vigorous-growing grasses such as tama, moata and manawa. <i>(Oct 1986,NZ Dairy Exporter,6</i>)

>  Similar characteristics to Manawa hybrid ryegrass. <i>(1995,Grasslands Range of Forage and Conservation Plants,19</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> the heart [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  Witi is a young man, a city man, right in the midst of this great transplant of Maori manawa - heart - from the open hills to the concrete jungle. <i>(Nov 1973,Te Ao Hou,74: 61</i>)

### manawhenua manamoana
 
 <i>n.</i> (Marine) customary authority over land and sea [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  Ngati Hinewaka, me ona Karangaranga, being the tangata whenua who hold manawhenua manamoana over the proposed mataitai reserve areas, have applied to the Minister of Fisheries for the reserves. <i>(Jul 2, 2008,Wairarapa News,30</i>)

### manawhenua moana
 
 <i>n.</i> (Marine) customary authority (over a sea area) [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  .. the Kairakau Lands Trust, being tangata whenua who hold manawhenua moana over the area, applied to the Minister of Fisheries for mataitai reserve ... <i>(Nov 4, 2006,Dominion Post,C15</i>)

>  PUBLIC MEETING .. being representatives of the tangata whenua who hold manawhenua moana over the proposed area at Raukokore, East coast. <i>(Oct 25, 2006,Whakatane Beacon,14</i>)

### manchester
 
 <i>n.</i> goods, such as sheets and pillowcases, which are, or were originally, made of cotton.

>  BLACK'S FOR BARGAINS/ WE'RE GIVING UP/ MANCHESTER AND COTTON/ and SILK DRESS GOODS/ ... THE BALANCE OF OUR/ MANCHESTER AND NAPERY/ Clearing at Half price <i>(Nov 16, 1933,Evening Post,21</i>)

>  [Advt for Cole's Cash Drapery Company] UNEQUALLED BARGAINS IN STERLING MANCHESTER LINES SPECIAL NEW YEAR SHEETING BARGAIN. ... Irish Linens. <i>(Jan 3, 1911,Evening Post,11</i>)

>  <b>FOWLER</b> In fact at Maitland, a country town, nearly one hundred miles from Sydney, I went over a drapery and general store larger and more lavishly appointed, incredible as the assertion may appear, than the great Manchester warehouses of St. Paul's Churchyard. <i>(1859,Southern Lights and Shadows,13,1975 ed.</i>)

>  Manchester News of Momentous Importance Special Sale of White Calico <i>(May 10, 1921,Quick March,12</i>)

>  <b>MITCHELL</b> <i>Manchester</i>: Japanese cotton goods. One proud Lancashire lass who told a Dunedin shop assistant, `I come from Manchester', was answered, `Yes, there are a lot of people up from the country today.' <i>(1972,Half-Gallon Quarter-Acre Pavlova Paradise, The,183</i>)

>  <b>LELAND</b> <i>manchester</i>: In the industrial revolution the city of Manchester specialized in the mass production of things made of cloth. As a result the manchester department of your local New Zealand department store sells the sheets, towels, etc. The DRY GOODS counter. <i>(1980,Personal Kiwi-Yankee Dictionary, A,62</i>)

>  <i>manchester n. Austral. & N.Z. 1.</i> goods, such as sheets and pillowcases, which are, or were originally, made of cotton. <i>(1982,Collins Concise English Dictionary,684</i>) [NOTES: [1986 NZPOD 455; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 298]]

>  <b>McGill</b> Halfway up were those intricate little drawers for cottonreels and balls of wool... Above them manchester, wide thin drawers for sheets. <i>(1995,Gold in the Creek,21</i>)

### manchester department
 
 <i>n.</i> a section of a store which sells manchester goods.

>  [James Smith's advt] MANCHESTER DEPARTMENT Just arrived, new shipment of WHITE COUNTERPANES, COLOURED QUILTS, AND PRINTED BEDSPREADS. <i>(Oct 2, 1922,Evening Post,11</i>)

### manchester goods
 
 <i>n.</i> [In 1907 October issues of the Evening Post, DIC, Kirkaldie and other advertisers do not use manchester (goods, etc), but Linens/ Drapery/ Towels/ Sheetings/ etc.]

>  <b>MILLER</b> Whereas in former times, flax, <i>kumeras</i>, fern roots, snared birds, pigs and shellfish, etc., sufficed for their simple needs, they were now irrecoverably enslaved by wants, tastes and ambitions unfelt by their ancestors, and must have 'tobacco and gunpowder, clothing and blankets, iron pots, agricultural implements, knives and muskets, and Manchester goods.' <i>(1958,Early Victorian NZ,83</i>) [NOTES: Quote from R G Jameson, 1841, <i>New Zealand, South Australia, and New South Wales</i>, p. 235.]

>  SPECIAL PROCESS/ ... All-wool Colonial Blankets./ ... SPECIAL PRICES in MANCHESTER GOODS. <i>(Jul 3, 1922,Evening Post,6</i>)

>  [Advt] Drapery, Manchester Goods, Men's and Boys' Clothing. <i>(Apr 26, 1879,Auckland Weekly News,14</i>)

>  [Advt.] Superb values in Manchester Goods. <i>(Nov 23, 1922,Auckland Weekly News,9</i>)

### mandate hui/mandating hui
 
 <i>n.</i> a meeting organised to ratify policy or procedure [ORIGIN: Maori, also mandating hui-a-iwi]

>  A series of Information Hui will be held throughout New Zealand as a precursor to Mandating Hui for entering into settlement negotiations with the Crown. These Mandating hui will be held later in 2005 <i>(Oct 1, 2005,Dominion Post,C13</i>)

>  Following is the text of the presentation given at the Mandate Hui held 16-31 August 2003. <i>(2003,Internet - see Notes</i>) [NOTES: pnbc.co.nz/Hui%20presentationFrame1Source1]

>  Mrs Parker also noted that a Pakakohi tribal mandate hui of November 1 1997 had resolved to object to the Ngati Ruanui claim to Pakakohi lands ... <i>(2000,Internet - see Notes</i>) [NOTES: www.waitangitribunal.govt.nz/.../viewchapter.asp?reportID=eb26d788-e7c4-4200-ad15-9bc69481745f&chapter=3]

>  At a Waitaha hui-a-iwi held at Hei Marae, Te Puke, in July 2006, it was resolved that a charitable trust be established as the body to negotiate a settlement of Waitaha Historical Treaty of Waitangi claims with the Crown. MANDATE HUI ARE BEING HELD For descendants of the tupuna ... <i>(Feb 27, 2008,Dominion Post,D6</i>)

>  HE PANUI TAPUIKA MANDATING HUI [heading] 2nd NOTICE .. The following mandate Hui [sic] is to be held .. Mandating Hui will be held: Sunday 17 August ... <i>(Aug 2, 2008,Dominion Post,C14</i>)

### mandating hui-a-iwi
 
 <i>n.</i> a meeting to ratify policy or procedures, especially to do with Maori land rights. [ORIGIN: blend] (See [mandate hui](../M#mandate-hui))

>  NGATI TAMA PUBLIC NOTICE OF MANDATING HUI-A-IWI. Notice is hereby given of a Mandating Hui-a-Iwi for Treaty of Waitangi negotiations to be held ... <i>(Jul 2, 2009,Wellingtonian,24</i>)

### Manfed
 
 <i>n.</i> Manufacturers' Federation [ORIGIN: abbreviation]

>  Yet the recent attack by the Manufacturers Federation.. on the spending plans of Treasurer Winston Peters had serious casting problems. Here was Manfed in a stunning new role... <i>(Apr 12, 1997,Listener (New Zealand Listener),7</i>)

### manga
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [munga (food)](../M#munga-(food)))



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>WAKEFIELD</b> [also 93] ...manga or baracoota. <i>(1845,Adventure in NZ,1/182</i>)

>  <b>HECTOR</b> A very common fish ... is well known throughout the colonies as the Barracoota (<i>Thyrsites atun</i>), which name is no doubt borrowed from the Barracouda Pike of the tropical parts of the Atlantic. This fish is a favourite with the Maoris, who call it the Manga or Maka. <i>(1872,Edible Fishes,109</i>)

>  [Maori or Settlers' Name] Manga, Barracouta .. [Scientific Name] <i>Thyrsites atun</i> <i>(1878,TrNZI,10/326</i>) [NOTES: (Thomson, Otago)]

>  Maka, Makaa, or Manga - the barracouta. <i>(1945,Maori Place-Names of Canterbury,61</i>) [NOTES: c1880 South Island Maori Lists `Wahi Mahika Kai' in 1945 Beattie Maori Place-names of Canterbury 61]

>  <b>SHERRIN</b> BARRACOUDA (<i>Thyrsites atun</i>). ... Dr. Hector describes barracouda as a very common fish, well known throughout the colonies as the barracouda, which name is no doubt borrowed from a fish of a similar shape, the barracouda pike of the tropical parts of the Atlantic. This fish is a favourite with the Maoris, who call it the manga, or maka. <i>(1886,Handbook Fishes of NZ,11</i>)

>  THYRSITES ATUN (Euphrasen). <i>Barracouta; Manga</i> [a2-macron]. ... Distribution: New Zealand coasts (rarer in the north). <i>(Jun 1921,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,4/118</i>) [NOTES: Phillipps Notes on the Edible Fishes]

>  <b>PHILLIPPS</b> <i>Thyrsites atun</i>(Euphrasen). BARRACOUTA; MANGA. <i>(1927,Bibliography of NZ Fishes,47,1971 ed.</i>)

>  The Moriori used wooden fishhooks but only for kahawai and manga (`barracouter' <i>Thyrsites atun</i>). The `barracouter' hook is a canoe-shaped bit of wood inlaid on the one side with a strip of bright shell of <i>Haliotis. </i> <i>(1928,BP Bishop Memoirs,9(5): 360</i>) [NOTES: 1928 Baucke Manuscript in Skinner and Baucke The Morioris (Bishop Museum Memoirs Vol. IX No. 5 pp. 343-382) 360]

>  <b>BEST</b> ...manga. This is a long fish without scales,... <i>Ibid.</i> 49 In mango [o-macron] the generic term for sharks, the final vowel is long, as is also the case with the name of the barracouta, manga [a-macron]. <i>(1929,Fishing Methods,44</i>)

>  38. <i>Thyrsites atun</i> Euphrasen. Barracouta. Maori: Manga. <i>(1929,TrNZI,61/147</i>) [NOTES: (Young)]

>  <b>POWELL</b> BARRACOUTA (<i>Thyrsites atun</i>), Manga of the Maoris, is a long narrow ]fish attaining a lenght of almost 4 feet and a weight of 8 lbs. It is found throughout New Zealand and Australia, and usually occurs in surface shoals. <i>(1947,Native Animals of NZ,71</i>)

>  <b>BUCK</b> A specialized but rather crude hook ... was used for catching barracouda (<i>Manga[a]</i>). <i>(1949,Coming of the Maori, The,220</i>)

>  <b>PARROTT</b> THE BARRACOUTA <i>Thyrsites atun (Euphrasen)</i> ... Maori name: MANGA. <i>(1957,Sea Anglers' Fishes of NZ,151</i>)

>  <b>GRAHAM</b> SMOOTHHOUND DOGFISH (MANGA) <i>Emissola antarctica</i> (Gunther) The name Smoothhound is given to one of the two New Zealand species of Dogfish the other being the Spined Dogfish. In Australia where it is common it is known as Gummy from its blunt pavement-like teeth. <i>Ibid.</i> 310 BARRACOUTA (MANGA) <i>Leionura atun dentatus</i> (Bloch and Schneider) <i>(1956,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,71</i>)

>  <b>DOOGUE & MORELAND</b> BARACOUTA ... <i>Other names: Thyrsites atum</i>; manga (Maori). Only the common name barracouta is used in New Zealand. The South Africa name snoek would be much more appropriate for this species. <i>(1966,Sea Anglers' Guide,279</i>)

>  <b>LEE & KENDALL</b> M|anga; ... a certain fish; <i>(1820,Grammar & Vocabulary of the Language of NZ,175</i>)

>  boracoota [sic] - mo'kka [manga] <i>(1826,Boultbee Journal,113,1986 ed.</i>) [NOTES: also in 1826-28 Boultbee Vocabulary in 1979 Begg 286 (|= macron precedes) baracoota, mo|kka [manga]]

>  <b>DIEFFENBACH</b> 27. <i>Thyrsites atun</i>, var. <i>altivelis</i>. ... This fish is named "Maga' by the natives of Queen Charlotte's Sound, where it was seen by the Forsters. Solander saw it first in Murderer's Bay. <i>(1843,Travels in NZ,2/209</i>) [NOTES: 1842 Gray's Fauna (Richardson's Fish) in 1843 Dieffenbach Travels in NZ 2/209-210]

>  <i>Thyrsites atun</i>(Euphrasen). Barracouta. Manga. <i>(1938,TrRSNZ,68/415</i>) [NOTES: Graham Fishes of Otago Harbour]

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> [also OED] <i>Mangiao</i>, a tree; the ash of this country. <i>(1848,Leaf from Natural History,20/2</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> a dogfish or gummy shark, a small shark.

>  <b>FORSTER</b> They caught a<i> smooth Hound (Squalus Mustelus)</i> about 5 feet long ... [ed. fn] Probably the manga (<i>Emissola antarctica</i>). <i>(May 2, 1773,Resolution Journal of J.R. Forster, The,2/269</i>)

>  <i>Mustelus antarcticus</i> Guenther. GUMMY; MANGA. <i>(1927,Bibliography of NZ Fishes,7,1971 ed.</i>)



 
<b>3</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a New Zealand-bred cultivar of turnip, a brassica forage crop. [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  Manga turnip - suits a wide range of soil types. Clubroot-resistant. <i>(1995,Grasslands Range of Forage and Conservation Plants,72</i>)

 <i>n.</i>

 <i>n.</i> (See [mangemange](../M#mangemange))

### mangai
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Ratana was a prophet who, in November 1918, claimed to have witnessed the presence of God in Aotearoa/New Zealand. According to Ratana, who later became known as the Mangai, or "God's mouthpiece". Maori people stood high in the Lord's favour, and,like the ancient Israelites, had been singled out as the instrument of His will. <i>(Feb 27, 2004,Dominion Post,B4</i>)

>  Ratana had been chosen as mangai - mouthpiece - between God and man. <i>(Jul 31, 1999,Listener (New Zealand Listener),34</i>)

### mangaio
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Mr. Tremain.. showed us some screwdrivers which he had fitted with handles of mangaio, a native wood, which, from its toughness and lightness, is admirably adapted for such a purpose. <i>(Aug 27, 1887,Auckland Weekly News,18</i>)

### mangal
 
 <i>n.</i> a plant community associated with mangroves

>  Having immigrated to New Zealand 18 years ago from Florida, we are familiar with tropical "mangals" as the plant communities associated with mangrove swamps are usually termed. However, tropical mangrove communities differ somewhat from our local version.... In contrast, the New Zealand mangrove forest is a monoculture... <i>(Jan 2004,NZ Geographic,No. 67/6</i>)

### Mangapanian
 
 <i>adj.</i> Geol. classification

>  In most areas sheets separated by thick mudstone and sandstone units occur in Opoitian, Waipipian and Mangapanian rocks. <i>(Nov 1978,Geological Society of NZ Newsletter,46/3</i>)

### Mangaroa Ash
 
 <i>n.</i> Geol. defn

>  Another widespread silicic airall tephra reported from southern North Island is the Rangitawa Pumice and its possible correlatives, the Omahina Tephra (Pillans 1979), the Lower Finnie Road Ash (Seward 1974 ; Pillans & Kohn 1981), and the Mangaroa Ash (Neeser et al. 1980), all with fission-track dates in the two-standard deviation age range of 370 +- 70 ky BP (Pillans & Kohn 1981; Kyle & Seward 1984; Kohl 1986, oral presentation). <i>(Nov 1988,Geological Society of NZ Newsletter,82/36</i>)

### mangeao
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  47 Mangeao slab. <i>(1890,PWD Catalogue of Timbers,16</i>)

>  <b>BAINES</b> I was fortunate in getting some mangio-boards to build her with, from my friends out the sawmill, and ... a good supply of rata vines for timbers, and Pohutukawa for knees; ... <i>(1874,Edward Crewe,127</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a handsome tree of the northern North Island, <i>Litsea calicaris</i>

>  Since the 1970s, keen Forest and Bird members and others with an interest in forest health have observed, with increasing alarm, the mysterious demise of the native mangeao the tree <i>Litsea calicaris</i>. The mangeao is a well-branched tree growing to about 12 metres, occurring as a component of the rainforest from North Cape to Mokau in Taranaki and the East Cape. <i>(Nov 2002,Forest & Bird,306/7,7 ed.</i>)

>  Mangeao .. Litsea calicaris .. Timber white, strong, and of great elasticity; ... <i>(1890,PWD Catalogue of Timbers,11</i>)

>  Mangi, or mangeao (<i>Tetranthera calicaris</i>) <i>(1892,NZ Official Handbook,150</i>)

>  <b>MORRIS</b> <i>Mangi</i>, or<i> Mangeao, n</i>. Maori name for a New Zealand tree, <i>Litsea calicaris,</i> Benth. and Hook. f. <i>(1898,Austral English,282</i>)

>  `Mangi - remarkably tough and compact, used for ship-blocks and similar purposes.' <i>(1873,Catalogue of Vienna Exhibition</i>)

>  <b>KIRK</b> MANGEAO.- (<i>Tetranthera calicaris</i>.) A small tree, most plentiful north of the Auckland Isthmus; ... <i>(1875,Durability NZ Timbers,21</i>)

>  <b>TREGEAR</b> The priest set a twig of <i>mangeao</i> (Tetranthera calicaris) into the bank; ... <i>(1904,Maori Race, The,207</i>)

>  <b>CHEESEMAN</b> <i>L[itsaea] calicaris, Benth and Hook.f</i>. ... A perfectly glabrous closely branched leafy tree 30-40 ft. high ... NORTH ISLAND: ... to Rotorua and East Cape. ...<i> Mangeao; Tangeao</i>. ... wood strong, tough, and elastic ... <i>(1906,Manual NZ Flora,603</i>)

>  <b>LAING & BLACKWELL</b> <i>Litsea calicaris [(The Mangeao).</i>] A leafy, shining tree, 30 ft.- 40 ft. in height. ... Maori name <i>Mangeao. </i> <i>(1907,Plants of NZ,175,1940 p. 183 ed.</i>)

>  <b>COCKAYNE</b> Confined to the north are - ... the mangeao (<i>Litsea calicaris</i>), the makamaka (<i>Ackama rosaefolia</i>), the tawhero (<i>Weinmannia sylvicola</i>), the toatoa (<i>Phyllocladus glaucus</i>), and some other trees and shrubs. <i>(1910,NZ Plants,46</i>)

>  mangeao [mangi ? f. Morris in 1911 edn] <i>(1934,Webster's New International Dictionary,2nd ed.</i>)

>  <b>WODZICKI</b> [Turbott found in 1948 on Great Island, Tree Kings] no seedlings of any other trees ...wharangi (<i>Melicope terrata</i>), ... mangeao (<i>Litsaea calicaris</i>) Three Kings' milk-tree (<i>Paratrophis smithii</i>) ... tawapou (<i>Sideroxyloncostatum</i>) <i>(1950,Introduced Mammals of NZ,162</i>)

>  <i>mangi, mangeao n.</i> New Zealand tree with hard wood [Maori]. <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Whitcombe's),619</i>)

>  <i>mangeao n.</i> N.Z. tree with hard wood [Maori]. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),12</i>)

>  <i>mangeao n</i>. NZ tree with tough light brown wood. [M] <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.]]

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Mangeao, <i>Col[enso]. Tetranthera calicaris. </i> <i>(1867,Handbook NZ Flora (List of Names),2/766</i>)

>  The Tangeao or Mangeao, (<i>Tetranthera calicaris</i>,) is a small tree, also confined to the Northernmost parts of the Island ... <i>(1869,TrNZI,1/III/46</i>) [NOTES: (Colenso Botany North Isd)]

>  Under the Forests Act ... Government can ... conserve the valuable trees ... such as the rimu ...mangiao (<i>Tetranthera calicaris</i>) ... <i>(1875,TrNZI,7/187</i>) [NOTES: (Firth)]

>  <b>HAY</b> There is the cabbage-tree palm, with bare shank and top-knot; the nikau palm, with weird and wondrous frondage; the lancewood, ...; the fern-tree, .... There is the twisting, squirming rata; the gaunt ... kahikatea; the golden kowhai; the dark velvet-covered rimu; the feathery red tawai; the perfumy mangiao; ...<i>Ibid.</i> 2/194 The Mangiao (<i>Tetranthera calicaris</i>) is a smaller tree, but one that yields a timber ... useful to carpenters and joiners. <i>(1882,Brighter Britain,2/187</i>)

>  <b>121</b> [Native name] Mangeao .. [Settlers' name] Mangeao .. [Family] Laurineae <i>(1902,Settler's Handbook of NZ, The</i>) [NOTES: 1886 T.Kirk in 1902 Settler's Handbook of NZ 121]

>  <b>BODELL</b> ... that fine Timber Mangeao got scarce, I closed the Mill. <i>(1890,Soldier's View of Empire, A,214,1982 ed.</i>)

### mangemange
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) a climbing fern <i>Lygodium schizaeaceae</i> [used by rural-dwellers and farm workers for mattress-filling]. [ORIGIN: Maori, also manga manga below]

>  <b>RICHARDS</b> The bedding and mange-mange mattress would be neatly rolled and stacked along one end. <i>(1986,Off the Sheep's Back,44</i>)

>  <b>EASY</b> For a mattress manga manga was used. This is a small lowgrowing shrub with twisted wiry black stems and practically no foliage and very springy, a natural innisprung [sic] mattress. [manga manga; mange-mange] <i>(1991,Looking Backwards,38</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Lygodium articulatum,</i>a climbing fern. [ORIGIN: Maori, various spellings]

>  They now made a basket like a [check] Eel Pot which they wove of Mongu-mongu. <i>(Apr 20, 1842,Journal of Ensign Best,354,1966 ed.</i>)

>  <b>HAYNES</b> ...climbing fern, here [? Auckland] called the monga monga <i>(1865,Ramble in the NZ Bush, A,21</i>)

>  ... among the Northern tribes, the creeping fern Mangemange, (<i>Lygodium articulatum,) was generally used to bind the outward thatch ... </i> <i>(1869,TrNZI,1/III/34</i>) [NOTES: (Colenso Botany North Isd)]

>  The slopes of the hills are usually covered with a dense forest ... the forms being ... Toro (<i>Persoonia Toro</i>, A Cunn.) ... and immense tussocks of an undescribed Astelia, and cutting grasses (<i>Gahnia lacera</i>, Stend., <i>G. arenaria</i>, Hook. f.) ... and Mange-mange (<i>Lygodium articulatum</i>, Swartz.), ... render all progress tedious ... <i>(1870,TrNZI,2/90</i>) [NOTES: (Kirk)]

>  <b>BAINES</b> [He] passed the night on the table in the `living-room' [of the bushman's hut], a couch he [42] mightily improved by an extemporary mattrass [sic] of Mongo Mongo vines, ... somewhat resembling the horsehair Gulliver might have seen at Brobdignag [sic]. <i>(1874,Edward Crewe,41</i>)

>  <b>HAY</b> The Mounga-mounga (<i>Lygodium articulatum</i>) is the delight of persons camping out. It has a stem [202] like small twine ... Bunches of it make capital bedding, ... spg <i>mongi- </i> <i>(1882,Brighter Britain,2/201</i>)

>  <b>BARLOW</b> A couple of sacks [on stakes] ... [form] the [camp] bed, on which is laid either dried ferns or Mongi-mongi as a mattress. <i>(1888,Kaipara and Waitemata Echo,139</i>)

>  <b>TRACEY</b> ... with a mungi-mungi vine for our line ... . <i>(1925,Piriki's Princess,88</i>)

>  <b>NICHOLAS</b> These [fish-catching baskets] were made of the bark of a tree called <i>manghee manghee</i>, and were ingeniously contrived; ... <i>(1817,New Zealand (various authors/titles),1/343</i>)

>  <b>NICHOLAS</b> Fish baskets made of the bark of an tree manghee manghee. <i>(1817,Voyage to Australia & NZ,1/343</i>)

>  <b>DIEFFENBACH</b> Under the name of repo, or raupo, it is a most useful building material to the natives ... [bundles of which] they tie together with the mangi-mangi, a climbing fern, the Lygodium articulatum of Achille Richard. <i>(1843,New Zealand (various authors/titles),1/426</i>)

>  <b>JOHNSON</b> There is scarcely a bare stem to be seen, even the tallest are embraced by a variety of twining plants, parasites and creepers, amongst which the <i>kia-kia</i>, decked at every joint with bunches of iris-shaped leaves, and the <i>mangamanga,</i> a creeping fern, are the most conspicuous; the <i>kareo</i>, also, forming a network of cane-like stems, hangs its bunches of glossy leaves pendant from every bough, and many others of equal beauty join to form a verdant arch, midway between the spreading branches of the noble trees to which they cling. <i>(1847,Notes of a Journey</i>) [NOTES: 1847 Johnson Notes from a Journal in 1959 Taylor Early Travellers 121]

>  <b>HOOKER</b> A remarkable genus of climbing Ferns, with slender wiry stems, found in all tropical countries. ... Lygodium <i>articulatum</i>, A Rich. ... Nat. name, `Mange-mange', <i>Col. </i> <i>(1855,Flora NZ,II Flowerless Plants 47</i>)

>  <b>ANGAS</b> We passed several primitive landmarks ... one being composed of three upright posts, with balls of mange mange - a dried creeper - fastened on top, and festoons of the [31] same material connecting the posts. <i>(1850,Savage Life,2/30</i>)

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Mange-mange, <i>Col[enso]. Lygodium articulatum. </i> <i>(1867,Handbook NZ Flora (List of Names),766</i>)

>  The roof was ... covered with raupo - sometimes with an inner sheeting of totara-bark - while an upper layer of toetoe-grass, secured by ropes of mangemange ..., preserved the raupo from wet. <i>(1903,TrNZI,35/22</i>)

>  <b>TREGEAR</b> The roof was well fastened down with ropes of the climbing fern (<i>mangemange</i>: Lygodium volubile) and the carved work of the gables was inlaid with haliotis (<i>paua</i>) shell. <i>(1904,Maori Race, The,92</i>)

>  <b>CHEESEMAN</b> <i>L[ygodium] articulatum, A.Rich. ... Mange-mange.</i> ... The tough and wiry stems were formerly twisted into ropes by the Maoris ... <i>(1906,Manual NZ Flora,1023</i>)

>  <b>COCKAYNE</b> The mange-mange (<i>Lygodium articulatum</i>) is a beautiful climbing-fern, whose masses of tough slender stems wound round one another make a substitute for a wire-wove mattress .... <i>(1910,NZ Plants,33</i>)

>  Mattresses in the bunks made from dried mangi-mangi ... [pron. mungi- mungi] creeper found in the bush in Northland. <i>(1951,Radio NZ / National Radio</i>) [NOTES: [c1914] 1951 H.S. McCarroll The Days of the Kauri Bushmen [Radio Talk 1: TS]]

>  <b>REISCHEK</b> Early that morning we travelled north-west to the top of a high, narrow range of precipices, overgrown with short, thick scrub and manga-manga, which made it so dense that I had to cut the way with my hunting-knife. <i>(1930,Yesterdays in Maoriland,88,1933 ed.</i>)

>  <b>COWAN</b> So ... the scouts left their secret bed among the ferns and the <i>mangemange</i>[es-acute] and crept cautiously out to the Waimarino track. <i>(1935,Hero Stories of NZ,185</i>)

>  <b>BOSWELL</b> Our mattresses were of mange-mange, a bush creeper of dense tangled growth, sufficiently springy if not very soft when green, .... <i>(1956,Dim Horizons,39,1955 ed.</i>)

>  <b>MARTIN</b> A climbing fern often forming an almost impenetrable tangle in the forest of the far north is that known as [117] the MANGE-MANGE (<i>Lygodium</i>) ... <i>(1929,Flora NZ,116,1961 ed.</i>)

>  <b>KEENE</b> <i>mangemange</i>: a climbing fern. <i>(1963,O Te Raki,192</i>)

>  <b>MOORE & IRWIN</b> <i>LYGODIUM ARTICULATUM</i> (Schizaeceae). The strangest thins about mangemange is that the true stem is very short but the leaf goes on growing at its tip and may be many metres long. In kauri forest these tough wiry leaves wind round trees and shrubs in an impenetrable network. Such a tangle makes thin strong vines suitable for securing thatch on a whare, as wire might be used today. <i>(1978,Oxford Book of NZ Plants,216</i>)

>  <b>RICHARDS</b> The bedding and mange-mange mattress would be neatly rolled and stacked along one end. <i>(1986,Off the Sheep's Back,44</i>)

>  <b>Gibson</b> ...maunga-maunga creeper [Auckland] <i>(1924,That Gibbie Galoot,16</i>)

>  ...the springy stems and leaves of both waewaekoukou (<i>Lycopodium volubile</i>) and mangemange (<i>Lygodium articulatum</i>) form a good bush mattress. <i>(2001,NZ Geographic,No.49/77</i>)

>  One of the greatest ornaments there was the mangemange, or Lygodium articulatum, displaying as it twined around the branches its varied form of foliage. <i>(Dec 17, 1887,Auckland Weekly News,8</i>)

>  Stanton Del land lay on his mungamunga bed in his whare by the Auckland Harbour. <i>(1900,Auckland Weekly News,27</i>)

>  New Zealand's extraordinary climbing fern, the Maori's mange-mange.. belongs to a family which has changed very slightly, if at all, since Triassic days. <i>(Dec 28, 1922,Auckland Weekly News,15</i>)

>  I climbed to old slip faces where the bush had gone, replaced by grassy colonising plants - the densely-laced cushions, the myriad small, round leaves of pohuehue, the toetoe, the mangemange, the sharp grasses. <i>(Nov 17, 2002,Sunday Star Times,C9</i>)

### mangeo
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [mangeao](../M#mangeao))

>  <i>Tetranthera calicaris Mangeo, Mangeao</i>.) Used in the vapour bath by lying-in women to promote the lochial discharge. <i>(1905,TrNZI,37/118</i>)

### mangere
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) [Ma.] a New Zealand-bred cultivar of ryegrass.

>  Dairy Exporter April 13 He used Mangere ryegrass before switching to Nui. <i>(Apr 1980,NZ Dairy Exporter,13</i>)

### Mangere clover
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) subterranean clover.

>  Some years after it [subterranean clover] was first recorded it had become fairly common and was known in the Auckland district as 'Mangere clover.' Subterranean clover was first recorded in New Zealand as a rare plant in this district and was known for many years as Mangere clover. <i>(Jun 15, 1956,NZ Journal of Agriculture,518</i>)

### Mangere Pole
 
 <i>n.</i> a variety of climbing bean

>  <b>MATTHEWS</b> Among climbing French beans the stringless varieties 'Shiny Fardenlosa' and 'Mangere Pole' are popular. <i>(1986,NZ Garden Book,199</i>)

>  Varieties of Climbing beans are:.. Mangere Pole: an excellent late-season cultivar... Pods are long, shiny dark green, stringless, flat and very attractive. <i>(1987,Yates' Garden Guide,63</i>)

>  Grown in the same way as Mangere pole, but in the warmer areas here, they [sc. yard long beans] can be very vigorous and highly productive annual climbers. <i>(Apr 1992,NZ Gardener,25</i>)

### Mangere rail
 
 <i>n.</i> [also Mangare rail]

>  A notice in the Gazette of 1st September is to the effect that the taking or killing of the Mangare rail, a bird indigenous to the colony of N.Z., is absolutely prohibited from the date of the publication of the notice. <i>(Sep 15, 1904,Southland Times,2</i>)

>  <i>Cabalus modestus Hutton. Little Chatham Islands Rail; Mangare rail. Buller (1905) considered this almost certainly extinct. </i> <i>(1923,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,6/76</i>) [NOTES: Myers Endemic Birds]

>  Saw a bird I never saw before. Nearly black ... It looked like a platypus flying with penguins flippers only short and round, not long like penguins. ... [[ed note] Mangere (Chatham I.) rail.] <i>(May 27, 1905,Chudleigh Diary,428,1950 ed.</i>)

>  <i>Cabalus modestus </i>Hutton. Little Chatham Islands Rail; Mangare [sic: chk] rail. Buller (1905) considered this almost certainly extinct. <i>(1923,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,6/76</i>) [NOTES: Myers Endemic Birds]

>  <i>Cabalus ... modestus.</i> ... It was found only on Mangere Island, and ... it is now extinct ... <i>(1952,Chatham Islands,78</i>)

### mangi
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [mangeao](../M#mangeao))

### mangi mangi taipo
 
 <i>phr.</i> as an insult to Maoris [c1880] [various spellings]

>  <b>MEIKLE</b> My brothers' slang and `funny' sayings delighted me. ... I remember [126] such words as `batty', `hoot' and `spondulicks' and many that ... were corruptions of Maori words. I spell them here as they sounded to me then: <i>ehoa, harka, kupai, kure, munga munga typo, markuta, pooku, tangy. </i> <i>(1988,Through the Looking Glass,125</i>)

>  The young New Zealanders ... were greatly amused at the dark colour of [the aboriginal's] skin, and laughed at him for being so ugly; calling him `<i>Mango, Mango</i>' or `black fellow'. <i>(1847,Savage Life,1/281</i>)

>  <b>HOWE</b> Wockie knew the Maori for good-day and what we pakehas thought was Maori for big black devil. They were tena koe and mangi mangi taipo, but when he went to call out in a friendly way to these [Maori] strangers he forgot which meant which. <i>(1964,Stamper Battery,85</i>)

### mangi-mangi
 
 <i>n.</i> variant of mangemange

>  <b>MALCOLM</b> Their mattresses were filled with mangi mangi. <i>(1982,Where it all Began,29</i>)

>  <b>BARLOW</b> I would get clear ... to become entangled ... in a bunch of Mangi-mangi, a fine wiry-stemmed creeper, which hangs in clusters from the trees. <i>(1888,Kaipara and Waitemata Echo,85</i>)

### mangiao
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [mangeao](../M#mangeao))

### mangio
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [mangeao](../M#mangeao))

### mangle
 
 <i>n.</i> a bicycle; a grid

>  <b>MORRIESON</b> `Just leave your mangle where it is,' said Mervyn Toebeck, nodding at the bicycle. <i>(1974,Predicament,32,1984 ed.</i>)

### mango
 
 <i>n.</i> a dogfish or small shark, often specifically gummy shark, <i>Mustelus antarcticus</i> [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  <b>NICHOLAS</b> A shark Ma[acute]gho <i>(1817,New Zealand (various authors/titles),2/336</i>)

>  <b>LEE & KENDALL</b> M|ango,<i> s</i>. A shark. <i>(1820,Grammar & Vocabulary of the Language of NZ,175</i>)

>  <b>POLACK</b> My companion eyed it much, ... and observed, that the mango, or shark, was a rich treat to the New Zealanders. <i>Ibid.</i> 1/323 The mango or<i> shark</i> (Squalus); <i>pilot-fish</i> (Scomber ductor); <i>flying-fish</i> (Exocoetus volitans);<i> hammer-headed shark </i>(Squalus zygoena); frequent the coast, expecially the River Thames, in vast shoals, and are preserved by the natives as winter food. <i>(1838,New Zealand  (Polack),1/101</i>)

>  <b>COLENSO</b> This bay is a favourite resort of several species of Squalus in the summer season; at which time the natives ... take them in great numbers. They call them Mango. [ed. fn.] Dogfishes, i.e. small sharks. <i>Mustelus</i> and <i>Squalus</i> sp. <i>(1844,Excursion in the Northern Island of NZ</i>) [NOTES: See also 1959 Taylor Early Travs. 50]

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> The whole of the long range of the New Zealand Coast is abundantly supplied with beautiful fish. Of this variety of 78 species I will name a part, viz. Mango [Mako] shark of many kinds ... [<i>Glossary</i>, 119 mango (mako?) shark; manga dogfish.] <i>(1843,NZ Journal,40</i>)

>  <b>WAKEFIELD</b> mango [=dogfish] <i>(1845,Adventure in NZ,1/93</i>)

>  <b>SHORTLAND</b> There was `mango' or shark, `koura' or cray fish, `huahua' or potted birds, besides preserved eels, and a variety of other dainties. <i>(1851,Southern Districts of NZ,70</i>)

>  Otene & his people came down to see us, bringing large stores of tupakihi which I quaffed with pleasure*, also mango ... Rogan fastidious about smell sent them to a distance to eat it. [fn.] The old women whose peculiar duty it is to squeeze the tupakihi berries never wash their hands by any chance. The only approach to it is in squeezing the tupakihi juice ... <i>(Dec 30, 1860,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,1/673</i>) [NOTES: (A.S. Atkinson)]

>  [Native or Common Name] Mangu, Barracoota .. [Scientific Name] <i>Thyrsites atun </i> <i>(1877,TrNZI,9/485</i>) [NOTES: (P. Thomson, Otago)]

>  [Native or Settlers' Name] Mangu, Barracoota .. [Scientific Name] <i>Thyrsites atun</i> <i>(1879,TrNZI,11/382</i>) [NOTES: (P. Thomson, Otago)]

>  Mako or Mango - the tiger-shark. [??=MAKO] <i>(1880,South Island Maori Lists</i>) [NOTES: c1880 South Island Maori Lists `Wahi Mahika Kai' in 1945 Beattie Maori Place-names of Canterbury 61]

>  <b>HAY</b> In the bays and tidal rivers are the mango or sharks - the most highly-prized food-fish - the tamure or schnapper, the whapuka or rock-cod, the kahawai or mackerel, the porahi or herring, the kanae or mullet, the patiki or sole, and many others. On the shores are oysters, mussels, cockles, mutton-fish, crabs, and other shell-fish in profuse abundance. In the fresh-water creeks are eels (tuna), lampreys (pipiharau), and whitebait (inanga). <i>(1882,Brighter Britain,2/151</i>)

>  <b>SHERRIN</b> TIGER SHARK (<i>Lamna glauca</i>). ... In New South Wales it is called the blue pointer, and, as Mr. Hill tells us, is `not only a very powerful, but perhaps the most bold of any of the large sharks visiting our shores. ... [118] Although there is some doubt on the subject, <i>Lamna glauca</i> is considered to be the mako of the Maoris, which is mainly found in the neighbourhood of Mayor Island. ... Mango, it should be remembered, seems [119] almost a generic name for shark in the North, as there is the mango-taniwha, a large variety; the mango-pare, the hammerheaded shark; the mango-reremai, the common dog-fish; the mango-pekepeke, and the mango-aruroa. <i>(1886,Handbook Fishes of NZ,117</i>)

>  <b>MORRIS</b> <i>Mango, n.</i> Maori name for the <i>Dog-fish</i> (q.v.), a species of shark. <i>(1898,Austral English,282</i>)

>  <i>Ma&#769;.ngo</i> (ma.&#769;.ngo.; <i>colloq</i>. m&adot.ng&#769;.o.), <i>n</i>. [Maori.] (<i>Zoo.l.</i>) A kind of shark. See MAKO [Local, N. Z.] <i>(1898,Merriam-Webster Third International,2026</i>)

>  <b>BEST</b> The following fish were caught with hook and line: araara, maomao, moki, mango, ngoiro, pakirikiri, kahawai, tamure, tarakihi, and warehou. The kanae, kehe, kopipiro, moho, nanu and other species were taken by net. <i>Ibid.</i> 49 In mango [o-macron] the generic term for sharks, the final vowel is long, as is also the case with the name of the barracouta, manga [a-macron]. <i>(1929,Fishing Methods,43</i>)

>  <i>mango[o],</i> a sea-fish, shark, or dog-fish; a general name, adopted by Pakeha for shark, with erratic pronunciation, including manggo. <i>(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/153</i>) [NOTES: Andersen Maori Words in English]

>  <b>MORELAND</b> SPOTTED GUMMY SHARK [<i>Mustelus antarcticus</i>] ... Other names are spotted dogfish, smooth hound, gummy and mango (Maori). <i>(1967,Marine Fishes,10</i>)

>  <b>DOOGUE & MORELAND</b> SPOTTED GUMMY SHARK ... <i>Other names: Mustelus antarcticus</i>; smooth hound, spotted dogfish, gummy; mango (Maori). <i>(1966,Sea Anglers' Guide,181</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> GUMMY SHARK <i>Mustelus antarcticus</i>: These harmless members of the shark family <i>Triakidae</i> live on smooth, sea bottoms ... They are slenderly built and have a fairly smooth skin, and are good eating. Also known as Sweet William Shark, Spotted dogfish, gummy, and by the Maori name `mango'. <i>(1981,Fisherman's Bible,224</i>)

>  <b>McNAUGHTON</b> My father caught ten sharks this way, grabbing their tails and sending them arcing out over our heads to the rocks behind, with us all watching and shouting out 'Mango, Mango' yelling and jumping about on the rocks. <i>(1986,Countless Signs,43</i>) [NOTES: From Patricia Grace (1975) : Waiariki]



 
 <i>n.</i> a $50 note

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>mango </i>Kiwi $50 note; because note is orange, like the fruit; often used in card games like poker for high stakes. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,72</i>)

### mango pare
 
 <i>n.</i> hammerhead shark (Sphyrna zygaena) [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  Synonyms: <i>Squalus zygaena; Zygaena malleus; Cestracion ztgaena</i>. The hammer-headed shark, or mango pare (<i>Sphyrna zygaena</i>), appears for the most part to live in the open sea ... <i>(1924,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,6/262</i>) [NOTES: (Phillipps)]

>  <b>PHILLIPPS</b> <i>Sphyrna zygaena</i> (Linnaeus). HAMMER-HEADED SHARK; MANGOPARE. <i>(1927,Bibliography of NZ Fishes,7,1971 ed.</i>)

>  <b>MORELAND</b> HAMMERHEAD SHARK [<i>Sphryna lewini</i>] ... The Maori name mango-pare is still in use but is met with only rarely. <i>(1967,Marine Fishes,12</i>)

>  <b>DOOGUE & MORELAND</b> HAMMERHEAD SHARK ...<i> Other names: Sphyrna lewini</i>; mango-pare (Maori). <i>(1966,Sea Anglers' Guide,180</i>)

>  The kowhaiwhai is called mangopare (hammerhead shark) - a fish considered to be guardian by Ngati Toa. <i>(Mar 21, 2006,Kapi-Mana news,41</i>)

### mango pounamu
 
 <i>n.</i> great blue shark

>  <b>DOOGUE & MORELAND</b> GREAT BLUE SHARK ... <i>Other names: Prionace glauca</i>; blue shark, blue whaler shark, blue pointer; mango-pounamu (Maori). <i>(1966,Sea Anglers' Guide,178</i>)

>  <b>MORELAND</b> GREAT BLUE SHARK [<i>Prionace glauca</i>] ... Other names include blue whaler shark, blue-pointer, and mango-pounamu (Maori) <i>(1967,Marine Fishes,10</i>)

### mango reremai
 
 <i>n.</i> = tope [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  <i>Galeorhinus australis</i> Mackeay. Tope. Maori: Mangoreremai <i>(1929,TrNZI,61/394</i>)

### mango ripi
 
 <i>n.</i> thresher shark

>  <b>GRAHAM</b> THRESHER SHARK (MANGO-RIPI) <i>Alopias caudatus</i> Phillipps <i>(1956,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,75</i>)

>  <b>DOOGUE & MORELAND</b> THRESHER SHARK ... <i>Other names: Alopias vulpinus</i>; thrasher shark; mango-ripi (Maori). <i>(1966,Sea Anglers' Guide,182</i>)

### mango tuatini
 
 <i>n.</i> white shark [ORIGIN: Maori] (See [also tuatini](../A#also-tuatini))

>  <b>DOOGUE & MORELAND</b> WHITE SHARK ... <i>Other names: Carcharodon carcharias</i>; great white shark, white pointer, man-eater; mango-tuatini (Maori). <i>(1966,Sea Anglers' Guide,185</i>)

>  <b>MORELAND</b> WHITE SHARK [<i>Carcharodon carcharias</i>] ... White pointer, great white shark, and maneater, are widely used alternative names, Mango-tuatini is Maori and not in regular use. <i>(1967,Marine Fishes,12</i>)

### mangrove
 
 <i>n.</i> any of a group of low trees with exposed roots, usually found in tropical tidal swamps. <i>Avicennia officinalis</i>,manawa (See [also manawa](../A#also-manawa))

>  <i>mangrove</i> n. a tree that grows on the muddy banks of estuaries. [Port. mangue, and E. grove] <i>(1929,Whitcombe's Etym. Dict. Aust-NZ Suppl.,7</i>)

>  <i>ma[a]nawa</i>, an amphibolic tree (Avicennia officinalis), mangrove. <i>(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/153</i>) [NOTES: Andersen Maori Words in English]

>  mangrove A tropical tree that grows in water. Common on Australian and northern New Zealand coasts. <i>(1958,Whitcombe's Modern Junior Dictionary (8th edn),250</i>)

>  <b>LAING & BLACKWELL</b> ... This is the mangrove of the Auckland coast. It is to be found on all tidal flats north of Kawhia on the West, and of Opotiki on the East Coast. <i>(1940,Plants of NZ,373</i>)

>  <b>McLINTOCK</b> <i>mangrove</i>, manawa <i>Avicennia resinifera </i> <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,3/705-712</i>)

>  <i>mangrove</i> [s] <i>Avicennia resinifera </i>Forst.f. <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,46</i>)

>  ...very low flat Islands all cover'd with a sort of Mangrove trees and several places of the Shores of both sides of the [197] River were cover'd with the same sort of wood. [f@n1] The N.Z. mangrove is <i>Avicennia officinalis</i>. <i>(Nov 11, 1769,Cook's Journals,1/196,1955 ed.</i>)

>  <b>NICHOLAS</b> The <i>mangrove </i>is found in the low and marshy shores in great abundance, and is likewise considerably larger than in New South Wales. <i>(1817,New Zealand (various authors/titles),1/231</i>)

>  <b>MARKHAM</b> The Mangrove grows in the Swamps and there is good Duck shooting among them. <i>(1834,New Zealand (various authors/titles),34,1963 ed.</i>)

>  <b>YATE</b> The cocoa-nut tree, which abounds in the Friendly Islands, appears, in the distance, much like the mangroves, and the kahikatea, or white fir, of New Zealand; ... <i>(1835,New Zealand (various authors/titles),9</i>)

>  <b>HOOKER</b> The traveller ... on arriving in New Zealand, finds himself surrounded by a vegetation that is almost wholly new to him; with little that is at first sight striking except the Tree-fern and <i>Cordyline</i> of the northern parts, and nothing familiar, except possibly the Mangrove; ... <i>(1853,Flora NZ,I Flowering Plants xxvii</i>)

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> <i>(1855,Te Ika a Maui</i>)

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Mangrove. <i>Avicennia officinalis. </i> <i>(1867,Handbook NZ Flora (List of Names),766</i>)

>  <b>TINNE</b> [We] crossed the little forest of mangroves which divide the Tauranga and Kati-kati tides ... <i>(1873,Wonderland of the Antipodes,42</i>)

>  <b>BAINES</b> It was here ... over by those mangroves, that I once was aground, if you could call such soft mud ground; ... <i>(1874,Edward Crewe,120</i>)

>  [Native name] Manawa .. [Settlers' name] Mangrove .. [Family] Verbenaceae <i>(1902,Settler's Handbook of NZ, The,122</i>) [NOTES: 1886 T.Kirk in 1902 Settler's Handbook of NZ 122]

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> <i>Manawa, ... Avicennia tomentosa mangrove. </i> <i>(1892,Dictionary of the NZ Language,86</i>)

>  <b>MORRIS</b> <i>Mangrove, n</i>. The name is applied to trees belonging to different natural orders, chiefly littoral, Species of these, <i>Rhizophorea mucronata</i>, Lamb, and <i>Avicennia officinals,</i> Linn., are common in Australia; the latter is also found in New Zealand. <i>(1898,Austral English,282</i>)

>  <i>Man&#769;.grove</i> (m&abreve.n&#769;.gr&omacr.v; 277), <i>n. [Add.] (b) </i>The sterculiaceous tree <i>Heritiera littoralis. [Local, N. Z.] </i>It is called<i> sundri</i> in India and <i>looking-glass tree </i>by English gardeners. - <i>River mangrove. (Bot.)</i>, the tree <i>Aegiceras majus. - White mangrove. (Bot.),</i> a verbenaceous tree of New Zealand Avicennia officinalis). [Local, N <i>(1898,Merriam-Webster Third International,2026</i>)

>  <b>CHEESEMAN</b> [<i>Avicennia officinalis, Linn</i>.] NORTH ISLAND: Muddy creeks and estuaries from North Cape to Opotiki and Kawhia on the west. <i>Manawa; Mangrove. </i> <i>(1906,Manual NZ Flora,567</i>)

>  <b>LAING & BLACKWELL</b> <i>Avicennia officinalis (The Mangrove)</i> ... Maori name,<i> Manawa. </i> <i>(1907,Plants of NZ,352,1940 p. 373 ed.</i>)

>  <b>COCKAYNE</b> <i>(1910,NZ Plants & Their Story</i>) [NOTES: (And)]



 
 <i>n.</i> esp. mangrove flat(s), mangrove swamp

### manoao
 
 <i>n.</i> a north Auckland forest tree related to the rimu. [Maori] Common Error: do not confuse with monoao, a scrub plant. [<i>Halocarpus kirkii</i>, aka Kirk's pine [There is confusion in the OEDS article with MONOAO <i>Dracophyllum subulatum</i>, possibly springing partly from the mistaken attribution in 1963 Poole and Adams 26 (repeated in the index p. 247), itself possibly taken over from 1961 (1982) Allan Flora of NZ 1/109, itself possibly taken over from Cheeseman.]

>  [MANOUA] Manoua (<i>Dacrydium Colensoi</i>). A small tree, found at an altitude of 1000 to 2000 feet, at Dunedin, and at the sea level on the west coast. Leaves of two kinds, spreading and imbricate.... Manoua (<i>Dacrydium laxifolium</i>), is a very doubtful species, being difficult to distinguish from the last <i>[D. Colensoi]</i>. <i>Ibid.</i> 49 Manoue. Dacrydium laxifolium <i>(1869,TrNZI,1/III/32</i>) [NOTES: (Buchanan Botany Otago)]

>  <i>(known hard woods.) </i>The Mairetawhake, (<i>Eugenia Maire</i>); the Rohutu (<i>Myrtus pedunculata), ... The Maire (Santalum Cunninghamii</i>) ... the Manoao (<i>Dacrydium Colensoi</i>), a small hard-wooded pine ... <i>(1869,TrNZI,1/III/50</i>) [NOTES: (Colenso Botany North Isd)]

>  <b>KIRK</b> MANOAO.- (<i>Dacrydium colensoi</i>.) <i>(1875,Durability NZ Timbers,22</i>)

>  <i>Dacrydium Colensoi</i> Hook. .. Manoao, silver-pine. <i>(1918,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,1/143</i>)

>  <b>POOLE & ADAMS</b> <i>D[acrydium] kirkii</i> F. Muell. ex Parl. Monoao [sic]. Tree reaching 25 m. Bark light brown. ... L[owland], M[ontane]. Hokianga to Auckland, local. Colensoi</i>); but correct in appendix p1100 Manoao (Colenso). <i>Dacrydium Colensoi</i>(p1101 has Monoao <i>Dracophyllum subulatum</i>: so Williams.) <i>(1963,Trees and Shrubs,26</i>)

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Manawau, <i>Geolog. Surv. Dacrydium Colensoi</i>.... 766 Manoao, <i>Col[enso]. Dacrydium Colensoi. </i> <i>(1867,Handbook NZ Flora (List of Names),766</i>)

>  <b>SATCHELL</b> ... the family having been established in the days when the white man came alone into the native settlement and picked his wife from the bright-eyed kotiros of the hapu. [fn. ### Manpower
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>HILLIARD</b> Anyway, I wrote to the Manpower (they had the Manpower then, this was during the war) ... I wrote and asked them if they could let me have a man to give a hand on the place. <i>(1963,Piece of Land, A,81</i>)

### Manpower Act
 
 <i>n.</i> [There seems to be no Manpower Act or Manpower Regulations as such. What we had instead were various amendments to the National Service Emergency Regulations promulgated first in 1940, when they referred mainly to the Services and spoke of Placement Officers and which I suspect were the "Manpower Regulations" of the time. The first relevant regulation seems to be 1942 Statutory Regulations 5/19 The National Service Emergency Regulations 1940, Amendment No. 8: There shall from time to time be appointed.. such number of District Man-power Officers as may be required. Ibid. 21. In any essential undertaking.. (a) Except with the permission in writing of the District Man-power Officer, the employer shall not terminate.. the employment of any person for the time being employed therein.. except in case of emergency... (b) Except with the permission in writing of the District Man-power Officer, no person for the time being employed in the undertaking shall leave his employment... Ibid. 24. The Minister may.. direct all persons of any specified class or of specified classes.. to register for employment with the District Man-power Office.]

### manrope lantern
 
 <i>n.</i> a bottle lantern (See [(cf. bush/West Coast lantern)](../(#(-bush/west-coast-lantern)))

>  <b>WASHBOURNE</b> The `manrope' party were so called because they had to descend to their claim by means of man ropes in the Gorge of Slate River. ... Lanterns were an unknown luxury on the diggings and one of the party invented by breaking the bottom out of an `Old Tom' bottle, and this was carried upside down with a candle dropped into the neck of it. This has always since been known as a `manrope lantern' [on the Nelson goldfields, c 1857-59] <i>(1933,Reminiscences of Early Days,5</i>)

### mantelette
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Col. Gold was more lucky than some people suspected in getting such a lumbering train home safely. He now says it was only a reconnoitering party and nothing more, but as he had previously been down with a light and suitable escort to look at the pas and then ordered a bullet proof machine to be made (mantelette I believe it is called, how spelt I know) people don't believe this statement; ... <i>(May 6, 1860,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,1/576,1960 ed.</i>) [NOTES: (James M. Atkinson)]

### manu
 
 <i>n.</i> the proprietary path or area on a muttonbird island over which a proprietor has the right to birds

>  <b>WILSON</b> Ever since the beginning, to `take' meant journeying to the islands and living there for the season and using hands or any device to catch the birds. ... to `take' also includes an `attempt to take'. During the torching season the birds run down the paths to the sea at night. Paths are used as boundaries by the [56] supervisors and in some cases the dividing line is the middle of the path. If one pursues a bird and grabs it over the other edge of the path one can be prosecuted, even although the bird came from the pursuer's manu. <i>(1979,Titi Heritage,55</i>)

### Manu Korero
 
 <i>n.</i> a Maori speaking competition [ORIGIN: Maori, (from the net) The Manu Korero competition returns to the Far North next year. Panguru Area School Principal Mina Pomare requested to host the event at a July meeting of the PPTA. A strong contingent of Tai Tokerau principals led by Pa Tony Brown won the tono from 2 other regions to host the 2005 national Manu Korero . The prestigious competition expects to attract 2000 secondary students, television and radio broadcast. 2005 will be the 40th anniversary of Manu Korero, the first competition was won by Donna Awatere.]

>  "I reckon [te reo] is growing, all the things they do today, like kapa haka and manu korero, that's like getting all the young ones into it." <i>(Dec 2005,Tearaway,48</i>)

### manu tukutuku
 
 <i>n.</i> a Maori kite [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  Manu tukutuku or kites used to be a common part of traditional Maori life. <i>(May 14, 1990,Listener (New Zealand Listener),110</i>)

 <i>n.</i> (See [manuka](../M#manuka))

 <i>n.</i>

A scarcely less common plant than [<i>L. scoparium</i>], attaining a greater size (20 feet) ...] <i>(1853,Flora NZ,I Flowering Plants 70</i>)
