### Manufacturers' Federation
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  The Old Guard, at the other end of the spectrum, are the Manufacturers' Federation and the Federation of Labour... [26] The Manufacturers' Federation... It dates back to 1897. The federation liaises with the government, but also provides some information services to members along with access to concessional business, medical and vehicle insurance schemes. <i>(Dec 6, 1986,Listener (New Zealand Listener),24</i>)

### manuhiri
 
 <i>n.</i> a visitor to a marae; a guest or person from outside [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  manuhiri, manuwhiri (n): guest, visitor, as in Pakeha in Aotearoa <i>(Oct 21, 1989,Listener (New Zealand Listener),77</i>)

>  The women of the manuhiri (visitors) respond with their call. <i>(Dec 19, 1999,NZ Catholic,7</i>)

>  It's still raining in the morning and the manuhiri avoid the puddles at the powhiri. <i>(Feb 5, 2006,Sunday Star Times,C3</i>)

>  <b>MARTIN</b> As soon as the weather cleared after my arrival, all the Maoris who were working on the land came up with their wives and children to inspect the Ma-nu-hi-ri [a-grave], i.e., <i>the stranger. </i> <i>(1884,Our Maoris,4</i>)

>  <b>MOSER</b> Harry and I, as manawhiri's, [35] (strangers) were not forgotten ... <i>(1863,Mahoe Leaves,34</i>)

>  <b>FINLAYSON</b> manuhiri: see whare. <i>(1973,Brown Man's Burden</i>)

>  <i>Sponsoring the manuhiri</i> [heading] Have you ever thought about what is involved in sponsoring a refugee? ... The theme for this year's refugee Sunday is `many manuhiri'. The manuhiri are the `people coming in', and New Zealand has a tradition of welcoming strangers that goes back to the very first people who settled here. More recently, New Zealanders have continued that tradition and established an international reputation for their refugee resettlement programme. <i>(Jul 19, 1986,PSA Journal,12</i>)

>  Paul Temm feels essentially unchanged by his experience on the tribunal, sitting at marae around the country, listening to the elders vent their frustrations in Maori, hongi-ing with both tangata whenua and manuhiri (visitors), chatting over tea and kai. <i>(Jun 21, 1986,Evening Post</i>)

>  <b>HOWE</b> Manuhiri - visitors. The manuhiri are those visitors to a marae who come from outside it. Though manuhiri may be related to the hosts, they are usually not close family. <i>(1985,Towards Taha Maori,20</i>)

>  The manuhiri are gathered by the gate. Unbelievably, they are gathered before the home people. <i>(2001,Huia 4: Contemp. Maori Fiction,171</i>)

>  People who are not white are manuhiri, visitors, as indeed we all are in the eyes of Maori. <i>(Jan 18, 2003,Listener (New Zealand Listener),6</i>)

>  The giving of gifts from the manuhiri is to show gratitude and to help the tangata whenua look after the marae. <i>(Feb 26, 2002,Evening Post,15</i>)

>  He werry kind to t'e Manuhiri, t'e visitor. <i>(1917,New Zealand At the Front,162</i>)

>  He has the same dream when he sees the cooks trying to feed two bus-loads of manuhiri (visitors) using just two gas cookers. <i>(Sep 28, 2002,Dominion Post,A16</i>)

>  Manuhiri must know when they come to Rotorua that they are going to be safe. <i>(Jan 17, 2006,NZ Herald,1</i>)

### manuka
 
 <i>n.</i> a common New Zealand scrub bush or tree which makes excellent firewood, a species of Leptospermum; tea-tree. [Maori manuka[a1-macron]]; bush manuka Leptospermum ericoides, tree manuka, white manuka, kanuka bush `as a ush-tree', i.e., `large', or less probably `growing in the bush' cp. Scrub manuka Leptospermum scoparium, a shrub or small tree; kahikatoa, leptosperm, tea-broom, tea-tree (titri); also occas for L. ericoides, kanuka, tree manuka: (See [also kanuka](../A#also-kanuka),[manuea](../M#manuea))

>  tea tree bush - man|nook [manuka] <i>(1826,Boultbee Journal,110</i>) [NOTES: also in 1826-28 Boultbee Vocabulary in 1979 Begg 283-]

>  This tree..is probably a species of Leptospermum. It is found abundantly at New Zealand,..and is named Kaetatowa, or Manuka, by the natives. <i>(Feb 18, 1832,London Medical Gazette,750/1</i>) [NOTES: [OEDS]]

>  Manuka .. Leptospermum scoparium .. Wood of a reddish colour, hard, and elastic; ... <i>(1890,PWD Catalogue of Timbers,11</i>)

>  <b>MORRIS</b> <i>Manuka n.</i> the Maori name for <i>Tea-tree</i> (q.v.). Properly, the accent is on the first syllable with broad <i>a</i>Vulgarly, the accent is placed on the second syllable. There are two species in New Zealand <i>white</i> and <i>red</i>; the first, a low bush called Scrub-Manuka,. <i>L. scoparium,</i> R. and G. Forst., the <i>Tea-tree</i> used by Captain Cook's sailors; the second, a tree <i>Leptospermum ericoides,</i> A. Richard. <i>(1898,Austral English,283</i>)

>  <b>LEVY</b> From the above low-forest association, or often directly from the manuka-kanuka associations, ... arise the larger trees of the forest proper, kamahi, rewarewa, hinau, totara, matai, southern beech, kaikomako, wharangi, rimu ... <i>(1951,Grasslands of NZ,87,1970 ed.</i>)

>  <b>BARKER</b> ... whilst [the boat] was steadied by ... holding on to branches of manuka and other scrub, I scrambled out ... <i>(1867,Station Life in NZ,120,1870 ed.</i>)

>  Manuka (<i>Leptospermum ericoides</i>). A large tree, attaining a diameter of 3 feet. <i>(1869,TrNZI,1/III/32</i>) [NOTES: (Buchanan Botany Otago)]

>  Manuka. Leptospermum scoparium, Forst. <i>(1869,TrNZI,1/III/41</i>) [NOTES: (Buchanan Botany Otago)]

>  <b>TINNE</b> My horse ... pitched heavily ... sending me [5] head first among the manuka or ti-tree scrub ... I had to stop at an `accommodation house' (or way-side inn) to sponge him down. <i>(1873,Wonderland of the Antipodes,4</i>)

>  <b>KIRK</b> MANUKA - RAWIRI - TEATREE.- (<i>Leptospermum ericoides</i>.) <i>(1875,Durability NZ Timbers,14</i>)

>  English Name Native name Botanical Name Manuka Manuka .. <i>(1889,Catalogue, Canterbury Court Exhibition,35</i>)

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> When in a hurry, or feeling orthographically tired, he [the southerner] calls the big manuka `kaikatoa.' A southern Maori once said to me:- `We did not pronounce the word manuka like some northerners who throw the accent forward and say `man-u-ka,' nor like the bushmen, who say `m'nooka,' but about halfway between these two pronouncing each part clearly as mah-nook-a.' Unless my hearing deceives me this throws the emphasis on the central syllable while leaving the first syllable clearly pronounced. It is a remarkable fact that while the manuka is so common its name is so seldom used in the composition of place-names. <i>(1944,Maori Place-Names of Otago,39</i>)

>  <i>ma[a]nuka</i>, a shrub (Leptospermum scoparium), commonly called tea-tree, because Cook's people used the fresh young leaves infused for tea; but very often mis-spelt ti-tree - ti being something totally different, the cabbage tree (Cordyline). The tree-manuka is kanuka, with smaller leaves and smaller flowers than the scrub-manuka, and it is the kanuka that is usually sold for firewood. I spoke to a dealer about it, and he said they dodged the confusion by selling it as T.T. There is a crimson-flowering variety of manuka known in England as a rarity, but by means of slips (not seed) it has become not uncommon. The starry-flowered manuka would have been adopted as the national flower but for the fact that it is too prolific, and apt to become a nuisance. It has a second name, kahika[a]toa but that is discouraged on account of its spelling being so nearly the same as kahikatea, the white pine, though in sound they are quite different, the macron in hahika[a]toa causing an apparent accent which, when observed, clearly differentiates the two words: it is not an accent, however, but a lengthening of the vowel. <i>(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/144</i>) [NOTES: Andersen Maori Words in English]

>  <b>KEENE</b> <i>manuka</i>: tea-tree. <i>(1963,O Te Raki,39</i>)

>  <b>BAUCKE</b> <i>(1905,Where the White Man Treads,212</i>) [NOTES: [also 1928]]

>  <b>LAING & BLACKWELL</b> <i>Leptospermum scoparium (The Manuka)</i>... Maori names <i>Manuka, Kahikatoa</i>. Colonists' [English] name, Tea-Tree. <i>Ibid.</i> 272, 274 [286] To the Maoris the tree was known as the manuka. By the bushmen it is generally called tea-tree. It has acquired this name because early voyagers and colonists sometimes used its [284] pungent leaves in place of tea. <i>(1907,Plants of NZ,272,1940 p. 286 ed.</i>)

>  <b>BAUGHAN</b> <i>Kauri</i> and <i>Totara, Rimu</i>, and <i>Matai </i>and <i>Maire,/ </i>Red-as-blood <i>Rata,</i> and bright-as-blood <i>Pohutukawa,/ Manuka </i>dark-ey'd, Convolvulus star-ey'd - / The glittering of you that morning! [f@n2 attached to the italicised names] 2. <i>Kauri, ... Mah</i>[a acute]-<i>noo-kah</i>): New Zealand Bush trees. </i>Ibid.</i> 75 [fn] (mah-noo-kah): Here pronounced, as often by the settlers, with the stress on the second syllable [previously stressed on first syllable]. A shrub with small aromatic tea-like leaves. <i>(1908,Shingle-Short,54</i>)

>  <b>BAUCKE</b> To the right ... lie wave upon wave of manuka, gorse, and fern-clad hills, ... <i>(1905,Where the White Man Treads,212,[also 1928] ed.</i>)

>  <b>BOOTH</b> We made rough cots with wickers [sic, = rickers] and slabs, ... and covered them with a thick layer of minuka [sic] branches... <i>(1912,Five Years in NZ,51</i>)

>  <b>HILGENDORF</b> MANUKA (<i>Leptospermum scoparium</i>), also called tea tree (of which titri is an inverted corruption), or scrub, <i>tout court</i>, is a very persistent weed on much land that is surface sown, especially in the North Island. <i>(1926,Weeds of New Zealand,126,1967 ed.</i>)

>  <b>HECTOR</b> <i>Leptospermum ericoides</i>, A. Rich. Manuka.--A slender tree 10-80 feet high highly ornamental, more especially when young. <i>(1883,Handbook of New Zealand,126</i>)

>  <b>DOMETT</b> Ma'nuka ....<i> O</i>. Myrtaceae; <i>G</i>. Leptospermum; <i>S</i>. L. scoparium, or L. ericoides. "A large shrub or small tree; leaves used as tea in Tasmania and Australia, where the plant is equally abundant." In the poem it is called indiscriminately manuka, broom, broom-like myrtle, or leptosperm. The settlers often call it `tea-broom.' <i>(1883,Ranolf and Amohia,1/303</i>)

>  <b>TOWNSEND</b> The principal forest trees of any value are red and white pine - Mano-ka - very hard wood - Bukkateer, very good floor boards - honeysuckle and Eno - from the bark of the last I have obtained a fine dye of deep scarlet colour; the wood is as hard as iron. <i>(Nov 16, 2004,Letter,8</i>) [NOTES: In <i>Absolutely Positively Wellington</i> 0/9/2004, p. 9]

>  Frequently, manuka is confused with its close relative, Kanuka (Kunzea ericoides). <i>(Aug 1991,NZ Gardener,9</i>)

>  Most New Zealanders are familiar with manuka (Leptospermum scoparium), one of our most attractive and widespread native shrubs. <i>(Aug 1991,NZ Gardener,9</i>)

>  The stout kahikatea and the white-starred manuka.. have been replaced by white dusty roads, fencing wire and rails.. flocks and herds. <i>(1948,Our Own Country,25</i>)

>  (A Settler) A great deal of the [wood is] a kind of scrub (very much like your shrubbery wood) or minuka. <i>(1849,Otago Journal,6/88</i>)

>  ... although there is such an extent of wood at this place it is not all useful, a great deal of it being merely a kind of scrub very much like your shrubbery wood or minuka, a sort of little use but for firewood, for which it is excellent. <i>(Sep 13, 1849,Letters from Otago,83,1978 ed.</i>)

>  The evening was spent ... by ... a blazing fire, fed with small sticks of Manuka, ... <i>(1851,New Zealand (various authors/titles),Part V (Church in the Colonies XX) 79</i>) [NOTES: 1848 Selwyn in 1851 NZ Part V (Church in the Colonies XX) 79]

>  <b>SHORTLAND</b> The leave[s] of the `manuka' (<i>leptospermum scoparium</i>) furnished Captain Cook with a substitute for tea ...; and ... he used the small branches of this shrub and of the tree `rimu', <i>(dacrydium cupressinum,</i>) in equal proportions, to make a decoction from which he brewed spruce beer, frequently referred to by him as a valuable anti-scorbatic. <i>(1851,Southern Districts of NZ,128</i>)

>  <b>McLINTOCK</b> Manuka, tea tree .. kahikatoa, kanuka (white), manuka (red) .. <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,3/705-712</i>)

>  <b>12</b> <i>manuka ... n</i>. N.Z. tea-tree ... [Maori]. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins)</i>)

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> Manuka is native to New Zealand and the southern half of Australia. <i>(1982,Weeds of Roadsides,91</i>)

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> The little blue ornament remained very dear to her, very close and comforting, among the scrub, manuka, and the ancient remembering cries from the stalking brown woodhens. <i>(1981,Exiles of Asbestos Cottage, The,18</i>)

>  <b>BELICH</b> <i>manuka </i>common bushy shrub <i>(1986,NZ Wars, The,337</i>)

>  <i>manuka n.</i> a New Zealand tree with strong elastic wood and aromatic leaves. Also called: <i>ti-tree.</i> [< Maori] <i>(1982,Collins Concise English Dictionary,688</i>) [NOTES: [1986 NZPOD 458; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 299]]

>  In consequence I travelled untill after dark and then lay down under a few Manouka bushes. <i>(May 21, 1841,Journal of Ensign Best,315,1966 ed.</i>)

>  <b>FITTON</b> [Grass can be grown] upon grounds partly cleared of the tea-tree - <i>manuka </i>- and the fern. <i>(1856,New Zealand (various authors/titles),74</i>)

>  <b>W H S ROBERTS</b> Writing of the Waimea Plain in 1856: - `The ground was well grassed, but the speargrass was too plentiful. The pasturage was very good. There was a good deal of scrub in the bends of the Mataura known as `wire plant' (Olearia virgata). There were also manuka and koromiko. Maori-heads, a kind of grass tree about three feet high, abounded in the swamps, and in some we saw toitoi grass and raupo. We did not pass any cabbage trees to-day ... we proceeded slowly through tomatakuru scrub, speargrass, Spaniard, snowgrass, and other native grasses ... we camped in a bend with good feed at the Elbow and a swamp on one side. Maori cabbage abounded ...' <i>(1856,Diary</i>) [NOTES: 1856 W.H.S. Roberts Diary in 1947 Beattie Early Runholding 18]

>  <b>HURSTHOUSE</b> ... the perfumed manouka, the scarlet myrtle and giant fuchsia, the red and yellow parrot's bill, the splendid clematis or virgin's bower; ... <i>(1857,New Zealand (various authors/titles),2/365</i>)

>  <b>THOMSON</b> <i>Valuable ... furniture planks are sawn from the Rimu, Kakikatae, or white pine (Dacrydium excelsum)</i>, Matai, Mairi, and Manuka (<i>Leptospermum scoparium</i>). <i>(1859,Story of New Zealand, The,1/20,1974 ed.</i>)

>  Here [at Taeri] was the tall and graceful black pine; the manukau, a shrub like the [Victorian] tea-tree scrub, only more greenly leaved; a few broad leaved grass-trees; the mappo, the kolu, the red pine, and others too numerous for me to remember; but their leaves were ... delightful to one accustomed to the dingy foliage of Victorian forests. <i>(Sep 27, 1861,NZ Goldfields,23,1976 ed.</i>) [NOTES: 1861 Sep 27 Tuapeka in NZ Goldfields 1861 (1976) 23]

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Manuka, <i>Col[enso]. Leptospermum scoparium. </i> <i>(1867,Handbook NZ Flora (List of Names),766</i>)

>  <i>~ n.</i> a shrub, the tea tree, of which the twigs are used to make brooms, and the leaves by the bushmen as tea. [Maori] <i>(1929,Whitcombe's Etym. Dict. Aust-NZ Suppl.,7</i>)

>  <i>Manuka</i> - Usually pronounced ma-<i>nu</i>-ka or ma-<i>noo</i>-ka in Canterbury and Otago and the West Coast, not <i>ma</i>-nuka as in the North Island. See tea tree. (C.) <i>(Nov 4, 1933,Press, The,15</i>) [NOTES: Acland Glossary]

>  <i>manuka</i>: flowering shrub or tree with hard wood, tea tree. <i>(1947,Reeds' School Dictionary: NZ Suppl.,174</i>)

>  <b>MACFARLANE</b> ...great hillsides of flowering manuka - North Islanders call this plant `marnaka', South Islanders `manooka', while the New Zealand farmers refer to it as a `bloody nuisance' <i>(1948,This New Zealand,39</i>)

>  <b>SARGESON</b> (gloss.) manuka: the commonest shrub or tree found in New Zealand, highly aromatic and sometimes called tea-tree, because the leaves were used by early bushmen to make a beverage. <i>(1974,I Saw in My Dream,273</i>) [NOTES: 1949 Sargeson I Saw in My Dream (1974 ed. Rhodes) 273]

>  The Maori name of a shrub or a small tree, `tea-tree', which makes excellent fireword; its leaves were used as a substitute for tea by early settlers. <i>(1958,Whitcombe's Modern Junior Dictionary (8th edn),251</i>)

>  <i>manuka n </i>-s [Maori] : either of two New Zealand woody plants that often tend to overgrow grazing land and form dense scrub: a : NEW ZEALAND TEA TREE b : KANUKA <i>(1961,Merriam-Webster Third International,1378</i>)

>  <b>FRAME</b> [...] and sometimes they escaped from the pasture and were lost in the manuka forest. Forest it was, for here the manuka was not scrub, but trees six to ten feet tall. <i>(1961,Ice Cold River,99</i>)

>  <b>SARGESON</b> manuka: a shrub or small tree; collectively, a thick scrub of these trees. <i>(1964,Collected Stories,301</i>)

>  <b>PAYTON</b> I consoled myself with looking forward to endless baths there in the summer, and retired into the <i>manuka</i> to dress. <i>(1888,Round about NZ,128</i>)

>  Coastal shrub species such as flax, toetoe, hebe and manuka were planted. <i>(Nov 2006,Footnotes,26 / 7</i>)

>  <b>HILL</b> One enterprising group offered a chainsaw as First Prize, a load of manuka as Second Prize, and a box of matches as Third Prize. <i>(1985,Moaville Magic,13</i>)

>  If the leaves run softly through your fingers, you're stroking a kanuka; if they rasp, it's a manuka... <i>(Dec 26, 2005,Dominion Post,B2</i>)

>  <b>HYDE</b> A small boy scooted up with a bunch of scarlet manuka clenched in his grubby hand. <i>(1937,Wednesday's Children,242</i>) [NOTES: 1989 edition]

>  <b>POLACK</b> This wood [Kaikatoa], called to the southward, Manuka [a1-acute], is remarkably hard and durable, ... <i>(1838,New Zealand  (Polack),2/395</i>)

>  <b>POLACK</b> This wood, called by the southern tribes Manuka [a1-acute], is remarkably hard and durable. <i>(1840,Manners & Customs of the NZers,2/258</i>)

>  <b>WADE</b> The Manuka, or, as it is called in the Northern part of the island, Kahikatoa, ... is a mysterious plant, known in Van Diemen's Land as the tea tree. <i>(1841,Journey in NZ,75</i>)

>  <b>SAM. STEPHENS</b> The ground is at present covered with high fern, Manooka - brush wood, flax and a grass called Toi-Toi. <i>(1841,Letters,41</i>)

>  <b>TUCKETT</b> The prevailing species of tree are remo, totara, and tall manuka, all indicative of an inferior soil. <i>(May 24, 1844,Diary</i>) [NOTES: 1844 May 24 Tuckett Diary in 1898 Hocken Contributions 223]

>  <b>HEAPHY</b> The dwarf <i>Manuka</i>, or `tea-tree', concludes the list of flowering plants. ... The infusion of the leaf of this plant forms an excellent substitute for tea: the taste is agreeable, and the effect slightly narcotic. It is much used as a beverage at the whaling stations. <i>(1842,New Zealand (Heaphy),46</i>)

>  <b>DIEFFENBACH</b> At the summit of the hills we find but little wood; and the manuka [fn Leptospermum scoparium.] and kahikatoa [fn Leptospermum ericoides.], the esculent fern and euphorbium [fn Euphorbium glaucum.], and the epacris parviflora, form the chief part of the vegetation. <i>Ibid.</i> 28 The manuka supplies the place of the tea-shrub. <i>Ibid.</i> 2/371 Manuka - a shrub (<i>Leptospermum scoparium</i>)] <i>(1843,Travels in NZ,1/27</i>)

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> The Kahikatoa, of this there are several kinds, the dwarf which never reaches the size of a tree, the Manuka or fine leaf, ... <i>(1844,NZ Journal,106,1956 ed.</i>)

>  `About one-half of the district ... is covered with fern and various shrubs, chiefly the Tupaki, ... `One-fourth of the district presents a more level surface, being covered with dwarf Manuka, fern, and ... tufts of grass <i>(1843,Report of Auckland Agricultural Society,20</i>) [NOTES: 1843 First Report of the Committee of the Auckland Agricultural Society in 1853 Swainson Auckland 20]

>  <i>(1844,Report of Select Committee on NZ</i>) [NOTES: [Dieffenbach: manuko: Andersen]]

>  <b>WAKEFIELD</b> 13. Kahikatoa, or Manuka, (<i>Leptospermum Scoparium</i>.) A tree of stunted growth, flourishing in clayey barren soils, and producing a hard red wood. <i>Ibid.</i> 240 ... very broken ground, covered with fern, toitoi, grass, and tall manuka. <i>Ibid.</i> 461-462 An unmixed growth [462] of stunted <i>manuka</i> is a bad sign, a quantity of ti-shrubs, commonly called the cabbage-tree, a very good one. <i>(1848,Handbook for New Zealand, The,143</i>)

>  <b>EARP</b> Of the [Wairarapa] plain, about 100,000 acres is covered with fine timber, chiefly totara, matai, hiora, kahikatea, manuka &c., all highly valuable for ... cabinet work and carpentry. <i>(1853,New Zealand (various authors/titles),113</i>)

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Leptospermum<i> scoparium</i>, Forst. ... Nat. name, `Kahi katoa' and `Manuka'. <i>(1853,Flora NZ,I Flowering Plants 69</i>)

>  <b>ADAMS</b> ... and though we must have been at least eighty miles distant from the land, the scent of manuca, a beautiful heath which grows to a considerable size, was like the perfume of a hayfield. <i>(1853,Spring in the Canterbury Settlement, A,14</i>) [NOTES: (Griffiths Collection)]

>  <b>GRACE</b> The digger.. stood his long <i>manuka</i> stick against the wall. <i>(1914,Tale of a Timber Town,31</i>)

>  The Bush Manuka, as it is called, attains a considerable size. <i>(1871,TrNZI,3/187</i>) [NOTES: (Potts)]

>  <b>DOMETT</b> <i>Manuka</i>: A large or small tree; leaves used as tea in Tasmania and Australia where the plant is equally abundant' (Hooker). In the poem it is called indiscriminately manuka, broom, broom-like myrtle, or leptosperm. The settlers often call it `tea-broom'. <i>(1872,Ranolf and Amohia,146fn</i>)

>  <b>BARKER</b> ... dry Menuka bushes, which make the best touchwood for lighting fires. <i>(1873,Station Amusements in NZ,257</i>)

>  <b>KENNAWAY</b> Three days later we began to boil the twigs of a small shrub, - <i>`manuka' </i>by name, - drinking the rather nauseous concoction as a substitute for tea. <i>(1874,Crusts: a settler's fare due south,70</i>)

>  <b>BAINES</b> ... on either side [of the harbour] much of the land was covered with a growth of manuka (tea-tree), rewa-rewa, towai, karaka, and other smaller kinds of timber ... <i>(1874,Edward Crewe,144</i>)

>  <b>HAY</b> The Manuka or Manukau (<i>Leptospernum scoparium et ericoides</i>) is the `ti-tree' of the settlers. In one condition it is low shrubbery, not unlike heather, called then Rawiri by the Maori. ... There are red and white varieties. The Maori particularize it as Kahikatoa, when in tree condition. <i>(1882,Brighter Britain,2/195</i>)

>  <b>D'AVIGDOR</b> ... from the branches hang bunches of that wonderful lichen, `old man's beard', and slender lance-wood emerges ... from the convolvulus-leaved but thorny `lawyers' and the low manuca scrub. <i>(1888,Antipodean Notes,152</i>)

>  [Native name] Manuka (kahikatoa) .. [Settlers' name] Manuka .. [Family] Myrtaceae <i>(1902,Settler's Handbook of NZ, The,122</i>) [NOTES: 1886 T.Kirk in 1902 Settler's Handbook of NZ 122]

>  <b>FEATON</b> LEPTOSPERMUM SCOPARIUM. (<i>Forst</i>.) ... The `MANUKA' or `TEA-TREE'. <i>Ibid.</i> 178 Manuka [Maori name] .. Tea-tree [Settlers' name] .. Leptospermum scoparium .. Shrub Manuka [Maori name] .. Tea-tree [Settlers' name] .. Leptospermum ericoides .. Shrub <i>(1889,Art Album NZ Flora,156</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> as an attrib merging into loose combinations: esp. manuka bush

>  The rationale for using both manuka and American oak chips is that the latter mellows the excessive tannins of the former. <i>(Jan 2001,Cuisine,134</i>)

>  46 Manuka slab. <i>(1890,PWD Catalogue of Timbers,16</i>)

>  <b>MANSFIELD</b> The manuka and sheep country - very steep and bare - yet relieved here and there by the rivers and willows - and little bush ravines - <i>(1907,Urewera Notebook,39,1978 Gordon ed.</i>)

>  <b>BAUGHAN</b> An' wasn't she a picture ! pouring good an' hot, / Tea (not <i>manuka</i>-brew, but Tea !) from the pot. <i>(1908,Shingle-Short,75</i>)

>  <b>MANSFIELD</b> There was nothing to be seen but wave after wave of tussock grass - patched with purple orchids and manuka bushes covered with thick spider webs. <i>Ibid.</i> 452 In a steamer chair, under a manuka tree that grew in the middle of the front grass patch, Linda Burnell dreamed the morning away. <i>(1912,Stories,109,1984 Alpers ed.</i>)

>  Manuka cultivars are available from most garden centres. <i>(Aug 1991,NZ Gardener,11</i>)

>  In populous centres, in untenanted swamp and manuka waste are plutonic vapours. <i>(Dec 6, 1913,NZ Observer,17</i>)

>  In consequence I travelled untill after dark and then lay down under a few Manouka bushes. <i>(May 21, 1841,Journal of Ensign Best,315,1966 ed.</i>)

>  <b>43</b> Soil nearly all excellent - some manuka bush - mostly ... flax land. <i>(Dec 21, 1848,Torlesse Papers,1958</i>)

>  Camped in manuka scrub near No.18. <i>(Jan 15, 1851,Torlesse Papers,191,1958 ed.</i>)

>  <b>MACKIE</b> We were glad now and then to throw ourselves under a Manuka bush and partake of some bread and cheese provided us by our friends. I saw a small bittern and gathered an excellent Lycopodium half trailing half climbing plant. <i>(Mar 31, 1853,Traveller under Concern,95,1973 ed.</i>)

>  ON SALE Stout Manuka posts and rails, any quantity. <i>(Jul 1, 1857,Lyttelton Times,1</i>)

>  <b>BAINES</b> We then cut ten `manuka' pins, two feet long, sharpening one end ... into a thin wedge. <i>(1874,Edward Crewe,156</i>)

>  <b>HAAST</b> [We] camped ... under the shelter of a manuka grove, ... <i>Ibid.</i> 429 Thus we obtained several portions ... of totara piles belonging to a Wata (provision store), and of spears; ... and a fork made of manuka wood. <i>(1879,Geology of the Provinces of Canterbury & Westland,100</i>)

### manuka belt
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a long band of land carrying native manuka Leptospermum scoparium.

>  <b>NEWTON</b> The block is situated up the Kaiwhata River from Homewood , and adjoins Te Apiti station, running back to an area of Maori country in the wild manuka belt. <i>(1969,Big Country of the North Island,46</i>)

### manuka blight
 
 <i>n.</i> a black blight which kills manuka scrub

>  <i>(Apr 1950,NZ Journal of Agriculture,337/1</i>)

>  MANUKA BLIGHT [heading] Since first noted in the Canterbury district in 1937, the scale insect <i>Eriococcus scoparium</i>, has ... become widely established. <i>(Oct 1959,Tararua Tramper,7</i>)

>  The manuka blight was first introduced into the [Hawkes Bay] region in 1945 ... [fn] The blight is caused by the action of a small sucking insect which feeds on the sap of the manuka and which secretes large quantities of a clear sticky substance known as `honey-dew'. A black sooty mould forms on this secretion. A combination of sap loss and mould slowly kills the plant. <i>(Oct 1954,NZ Geographer,110</i>)

>  <b>TURNER</b> In New Zealand a disease <i>manuka blight</i> is used to control the scrubby manuka or tea-tree. <i>(1966,English Language in Aust. & NZ, The,142</i>)

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> Manuka blight is caused by a scale insect <i>Eriococcus orariensis,</i> which is native to Australia where manuka has long been adapted to it. <i>(1982,Weeds of Roadsides,91</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> Manuka on Stewart Island is sometimes blackened but seldom killed by manuka blight. <i>(1982,Stewart Island Plants,52 ed.</i>)

>  The deliberate spread of manuka blight, growing in popularity in some areas, is not favoured by departmental officers. <i>(Apr 4, 1950,Southern Cross (Wgton),10</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a sooty mould formed on honey-dew exuded by a scale insect infesting red manuka and kanuka.

>  The infestation of manuka species that has become known as manuka blight was found about 2 years ago on three varieties of ornamental manuka in the Hastings district. <i>(Feb 1956,NZ Journal of Agriculture,15</i>)

>  <b>TAIT</b> .. with the advent of manuka blight, real progress was made. <i>(1958,Farms & Stations of NZ Vol II,321</i>)

>  Has 'Manuka blight' been established in the North Island and what is the cost of getting it started? Manuka blight is an insect which sucks sap from the plant and secretes 'honey dew' on which sooty mould fungi form. Manuka blight is very likely to be already established on your land. <i>(Dec 1964,NZ Journal of Agriculture,533</i>)

>  <b>FEARON</b> Reg had bought the block as a run-off for cattle, in the hopes that the recently introduced manuka blight would destroy all the young manuka on the block. <i>(1980,Te Wharau,51</i>)

### manuka broom
 
 <i>n.</i> a broom made of manuka twigs. Leptospermum scoparium manuka (cf tea-broom)

>  <b>YATE</b> ...from the berries [manuka] has been designated Tea tree] <i>(1835,New Zealand (various authors/titles),48</i>)

>  <b>DOMETT</b> [see MANUKA] <i>(1872,Ranolf and Amohia,149</i>)

>  <b>LAING & BLACKWELL</b> [Index entry] Manuka, ... 274 [286]/ ` [sic] broom, 274 [289]/ ' [sic] rauriki, 274 [289] [[p. 274] The Maoris made use of [<i>Leptospermum scoparium</i>, manuka] for their paddles and spears, and a bunch of twigs makes an excellent broom.] <i>(1907,Plants of NZ,449</i>)

>  <b>LAING & BLACKWELL</b> It is the colonial counterpart of the English broom and gorse, and is as beautiful as either of these.] <i>(1940,Plants of NZ,286</i>)

>  Mother would draw out the embers with a long scraper, and brush out the [153] oven with a big manuka broom dipped in cold water. ... After the bread came out, trays of apples ... would go in to bake, ... or a special `dog's cake' made of pollard, bran, suet, and butter-milk. <i>(1940,Tales of Pioneer Women,152,1988 ed.</i>)

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> It was forenoon, and Kara had ... made the beds, and swept the mud floors of the two houses with a home-made <i>manuka</i> broom when two visitors came to see her. <i>(1948,Taina,29</i>)

### manuka bush
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) an individual tree or shrub of the native manuka Leptospermum scoparium.

>  <b>KENNEDY</b> In front, the manuka bushes, like grey wraiths lit up by the strong lamps of the coach, glided past into the gloom. <i>(1876,Colonial Travel,246</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> .. he was having his morning smoko under a big manuka bush on the side of the road as we pulled up for a yarn. <i>(1969,Big Country of the North Island,227</i>)

>  <b>FERNANDEZ</b> They might get the load hung up in the manuka bushes. <i>(1973,Tussock Fever,38</i>)

### manuka country
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) land that carries little besides manuka scrub.

>  Is brown-top grass suitable for poor manuka country, height about 2,000 ft above the sea level? Brown-top (Agrostis canina) should do well on poor manuka country in the Tolaga Bay district. <i>(Sep 15, 1913,NZ Journal of Agriculture,349</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> .. it winds its way through wild, broken manuka country. <i>(1969,Big Country of the North Island,45</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> There is a block of heavy manuka country on Te Awaiti ... <i>(1969,Big Country of the North Island,25</i>)

>  It was tough pumice and manuka country, she recalled. <i>(Jul 7, 2000,Otago Daily Times,12</i>)

### manuka drain
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a ditch lined with manuka branches as obstructions to stock or to prevent slipping.

>  It was found that properly constructed manuka drains were the answer to the problem of keeping stock out. The manuka is stacked in the drains at an angle, thick butts down, and covered with rushes and then earth. <i>(May 15, 1945,NZ Journal of Agriculture,499</i>)

>  The first stage in gumland development is filling in holes and draining wet patches with manuka fascine drains. <i>(Jul 16, 1945,NZ Journal of Agriculture,19</i>)

### manuka gully
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a steep sided valley shrouded in manuka growth.

>  <b>BEACH</b> We still had some time left so we spent a couple of hours out in open country checking out some manuka gullies ... <i>(1993,Over 30 Years Pig Hunting,106</i>)

### manuka honey
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a honey with antibiotic properties [ processed from hives in manuka country. ]

>  But how many Kiwis' relationship with the land of their birth is defined by the contents of the overseas gift boxes - Vegemite and Buzz Bars, L&P and manuka honey, and rugby and the haka? <i>(Feb 5, 2002,Evening Post,15</i>)

>  For over 10 years I have scientifically investigated what many New Zealanders have accepted as common wisdom: our local manuka honey is superior treatment for wounds and infections. Manuka honey is gathered exclusively in New Zealand from the manuka bush, Leptospermum scopariu[m], which grows uncultivated throughout our country. <i>(Sep 2003,NZ Nursing Review,4(5)/10</i>)

>  Otago Manuka Honey.. was judged the best honey. All the judges commented on its full flavour, smooth consistency, perfect sweetness, luscious mouth-feel and long finish. <i>(Oct 19, 2003,Sunday (Sunday Star Times Magazine),13</i>)

>  Dashes to the toilet and manuka honey are part of a Kerikeri man's strategy for breaking the world marathon speech record. <i>(Apr 8, 1999,Dominion,17</i>)

>  Manuka honey prices have already increased ... <i>(Mar 24, 2002,Sunday Star Times,A7</i>)

>  .. the value of manuka honey in relation to colony welfare and development is considered ... <i>(Sep 1949,NZ Journal of Agriculture,225</i>)

>  Five days later., relieved to see Kevin sitting up in bed feeding himself, the rents in his swanny sewn up, his saddlebags packed with manuka honey, roasted barley and dried meat, Sean.. rode off down the drive. <i>(2000,Kokopu Dreams,204</i>)

>  Considerably more [money] is being paid for manuka honey with a high level of antibacterial activity that is increasingly used for medicinal purposes, such as wound dressings and cough syrups. <i>(Sep 5, 2003,Dominion Post,C5</i>)

>  If manuka honey is the aim, care should be taken to see that the site chosen is suitable.. Bush areas in north Taranaki cannot as a rule be relied on to produce a major crop of manuka honey and the kamahi flow is usually depended on to provide any surplus feed stores for the following spring. <i>(May 15, 1956,NZ Journal of Agriculture,427</i>)

>  Once a month they [sheep] are drenched with a mix of garlic, molasses and manuka honey as a worm preventative. <i>(2000,Lifestyle Farmer,Autumn 14</i>)

>  <b>38</b> New Zealand manuka honey and its related Australian cousin, jellybush honey, are attracting worldwide interest for their antibacterial and antiseptic properties...Dr Molan has established a UMF (unique manuka factor) for rating manuka honey according to antibacterial activity, with "active" manuka honey, scoring 10 or more, being recognised as having superior antibiotic strength. <i>(Nov 2000,Pacific Wave</i>)

>  ...she has developed a unique range of seven products using New Zealand ingredients including avacado oil, Pacific Blue lavender, kawakawa, flax and manuka honey, that guarantee "no stink or sting". <i>(Aug 26, 2004,Dominion Post,D3</i>)

>  <b>WALKER</b> ... `manuka' and eucalyptus honey. .... <i>(1902,Zealandia's Guerdon,57</i>)

>  <b>HYDE</b> Manuka honey ... dark, sweet, gathered by the droning wild bees whose nests are high up in rimu or manuka ... is the best of all. <i>(1934,Journalese,110</i>)

>  The big hope for Convita, which has been around and profitable for more than two decades, is its investment in Manuka honey technology... Bill Bracks, chairman of Comvita, told the gathered media: "New Zealand should be planting Manuka now, not pine forests. When this technology becomes mainstream in the next 18 months, Manuka honey has the potential to reach upwards of $40 a kilo." <i>(Nov 16, 2003,Sunday Star Times,D10</i>)

>  NZ Trade and Enterprise food and beverage manager Leone Evens said "indigenous foods" such as green lipped mussels, manuka honey and avocado oil had created a unique Kiwi cuisine. <i>(May 21, 2005,Dominion Post,A16</i>)

>  [Hdg] Manuka honey is nature's antibiotic. A New Zealand scientist leading world research into the healing powers of manuka honey is setting up a clinical trial to compare the effectiveness of biologically "active" manuka honey against other honeys. <i>(Oct 10, 2000,Dominion,9</i>)

>  German scientists have identified what causes the antibacterial ingredient in manuka honey, which has long been touted as having medicinal properties .. the ingredient is a natural compound formed in manuka plants known as methylglyoxal. <i>(Jan 28, 2008,Dominion Post,C10</i>)

### manuka manna
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> ... thence we reached a manuka grove which abounded in manna I collected a large quantity I am convinced we might have soon obtained several pounds ... the taste is sweet like honey and flour it is occasioned by a small grub which makes a perforation <i>(Nov 19, 1845,Journal,3/176</i>)

>  <b>LAING & BLACKWELL</b> As a result of injuries caused by sap-sucking insects the leaves are sometimes covered with a white crystalline substance, known as manuka manna. The substance is probably a complex sugar, but so far has not been thoroughly investigated. <i>(1940,Plants of NZ,289</i>)

>  <b>BROOKER et al.</b> Pia (or manuka manna) is composed of mannitol (see p.69 [ngaione]). [reference Cambie and Seelye 1959 Note on the identification of manuka manna NZJSc 2: 498] <i>(1981,NZ Medicinal Plants,72</i>)

### manuka oil
 
 <i>n.</i> oil from the manuka plant

### manuka scrub
 
 <i>n.</i> A stunted variety of manuka, distinct from bush-manuka; tree manuka (cf. scrub flax) [[1919 July 29] Ver.(WB). = Vernacular names used by settlers supplied by W.Best, Otaki (And)]

>  <b>SHORTLAND</b> ... we were unable to find any fire-wood, but some low blackened `manuka' scrub which had been burnt the year before, ... <i>(1851,Southern Districts of NZ,167</i>)

>  Manuka scrub, of which there is a patch of from 20,000 to 30,000 acres on the north bank of the Courtenay, can be turned to good account by the residents in its neighbourhood for fencing purposes and fuel. Its leaves are a tolerable substitute for tea, and it possesses considerable astringent properties. <i>(Jun 28, 1851,Lyttelton Times,6</i>)

>  <b>ADAMS</b> Immediately on the other side of the river id a considerable tract of land, known as the Manuca Scrub. <i>(1853,Spring in the Canterbury Settlement, A,59</i>)

>  <b>SMITH</b> At about three miles from Waitahanui the path leaves the lake, and goes some distance inland, through manuka scrub ... <i>(Jan 22, 1858,Notes of a Journey</i>) [NOTES: 1858 Jan 22 Smith Notes of a Journey in 1959 Taylor Early Travellers 371]

>  <b>GRACE</b> They were all cold, wet and hungry, and, for the last two hours, had been huddled together in the manuka scrub. <i>(Oct 19, 1863,Journal</i>) [NOTES: 1863 Oct 19 T.S.Grace Journal in Grace Pioneer Missionary (1928) 119]

>  MYRTLE FAMILY. Cook's Tea tree, Scrub Manuka, or Kahikatoa (<i>Leptospermum scoparium</i>). <i>(1869,TrNZI,1/III/32</i>) [NOTES: (Buchanan Botany Otago)]

>  <b>PYKE</b> Already the ... flood was sweeping him away, when he clutched a low-bending bough of Manuka scrub, and by its aid drew himself ashore on the island. <i>(1873,Wild Will Enderby,(1889,1974) 3/iii/83</i>)

>  It is, further, covered with manuka scrub fern and tutu. <i>(Jun 21, 1876,Chudleigh Diary,250,1950 ed.</i>)

>  <b>BATHGATE</b> ... the other [gully] was in some parts overgrown [275] with manuka scrub, with here and there a few dells ... <i>Leptospermum ericoides </i> <i>(1881,Waitahuna Memories,274</i>)

>  <b>HOWARD</b> From about 900 feet upward the forest merges into a manuka scrub ... and mountain pine (<i>Dacridium Bidwillii</i>) up to ... about 1,500 feet. <i>(1940,Rakiura,xviii</i>)

>  <i>ma[a]nuka,</i> a shrub (Leptospermum scoparium), commonly called tea-tree, because Cook's people used the fresh young leaves infused for tea; but very often mis-spelt ti-tree - ti being something totally different, the cabbage tree (Cordyline). The tree-manuka is kanuka, with smaller leaves and smaller flowers than the scrub-manuka, and it is the kanuka that is usually sold for firewood. <i>(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/144</i>) [NOTES: Andersen Maori Words in English]

>  <b>MARSH</b> `Here you are, old fellow,' said my father coming through the stunted manuka scrub with a billy-ful of spring water. <i>(1981,Black Beech & Honeydew,86</i>)

>  <b>MARSH</b> The air up here [in the Southern Alps] was wonderfully fresh and smelt [33] aromatically of manuka scrub patching warm tussocky earth. <i>(1980,Photo-Finish,32</i>)

>  <b>MORELAND</b> We entered on a dreary extent of moraine - a chaos of ridges and stones which filled the valley. In part it was overgrown by thickets of manuka scrub and tangles of hooked 'lawyer'. <i>(1911,Through South Westland,51</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) wild shrubby growth of manuka Leptospermum scoparium that naturalises hill country.

>  <b>SEWELL</b> ..we pulled up, under a manuka scrub, all of us wet to the skin ... <i>(Jan 1856,Journal</i>) [NOTES: in 1980 McIntyre Sewell Journal 1853-1856 215]

>  <b>DEANS</b> The dense manuka scrub which covered the hills in those days [1870s] made conditions for mustering very difficult ... <i>(1964,Pioneers on Port Cooper Plains,104</i>)

>  <b>MANTELL</b> Emerged from the wood which finished gloriously with manuka scrub, swamp, fallen trees and mud holes. <i>(Jan 14, 1852,Diary,122</i>) [NOTES: in Beattie (1947) Pioneers Explore Otago]

>  <b>KENNEDY</b> Through the tall herbage, manuka scrub and spiked toi-toi grass, there trotted, with wagging ears, stray wild pigs ... <i>(1876,Colonial Travel,294</i>)

>  The natural covering of much of this class of land is now manuka scrub, blackberry or gorse. <i>(Apr 15, 1944,NZ Journal of Agriculture,251</i>)

>  The land is mainly easy rolling contour with some steep sidlings, covered generally by red tussock, fern and manuka scrub. <i>(May 1968,NZ Journal of Agriculture,55</i>)

>  <b>ALLEN</b> This warmth and the heavy rainfall cause a phenomenal growth of manuka scrub which covers the farm land so quickly. <i>(1980,Farm on the Wanganui River, A,20</i>)

>  <b>GORDON</b> There was little manuka scrub and only isolated patches of bush. <i>(1996,Trouble with Stock Agents, The,75</i>)

### manuka stick
 
 <i>n.</i> (esp. used for garden stakes etc.)

>  <b>FRAME</b> I hoisted the line further in the air with the old manuka-stick prop ... <i>(1985,Envoy from Mirror City,170</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a long stick fashioned from a manuka branch

>  .. and only need a few aimed raps or prods with a manuka stick to keep them stringing through the race. <i>(Mar 23, 1967,NZ Farmer,33</i>)

### manuka tea
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) an effusion of manuka leaves [commonly drunk in pioneer times]. [also manuka brew]

>  <b>HAMMOND</b> .. if the Estate put a manager on it they'll have to pay him something - unless he's willing to live on fern-root and skim-milk and drink manuka tea. <i>(1966,This Man's Father had my Father's Farm,146</i>)

### manyies, ?manyes
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>SUTTON-SMITH</b> Action terms related to the movements of the marbles are, Throughs and No Throughs, Tracks and Clears, Stops and Rolls, Lay-Up, Manyies, Kiss, Ons, Gees, Haystacks, Stays, Spiders Can't Cross a River etc. Action Terms related to taking an opponent's marbles are Fires, Double-Hits and Forces. <i>(1953,Unorganized Games,2/769</i>)

>  <b>SUTTON-SMITH</b> Some of the typical terms used in marbles referred to the act of shooting and the movement of the marbles. Those related to ways of shooting marbles were cribbing, firsts, fudging, funks, funnicks, funnigans, flunks, forces, knee-high, knuckle-down-stiff, knuckles, and lasts. Terms connected with the movement of the marbles were: cannons, clears, dead-stick, double-hits, fires, funs, gees, haystacks, in smug, keeps, kills, kisses, lay-ups, manyes, ones, pink, ringer, rolls, scat, screws, skinned, skun, spiders, sported-off, stays, stops, tracks, throughs. <i>(1972,Folkgames of Children,175</i>)

### maomao
 
 <i>n.</i> an edible New Zealand fish (varieties termed `blue' or `pink') commonly found in rocky northern waters. <i>Scorpis aequipinnis</i> and <i>S. violaceus</i>, also blue maomao, =hui [NB Natusch separates 2 species of Scorpis the maomao and the hui (see hui); Williams hui (iv) S aequipinnis the SWEEP, a fish] [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  Swarms of juvenile blue maomao school close to the surface, snapping at the wake of swirling bubbles. <i>(Jan 2002,NZ Geographic,No. 55/20</i>)

>  <b>IRELAND</b> Around the point was a secret place we called the maomao rock where we caught feasts of blue maomao, surely the most delicate-tasting fish in the sea. <i>(1998,Under the Bridge & Over the Moon,114</i>)

>  Rurima is famous for its fish; hapuka (<i>Oligorus gigas</i>), ... moki (<i>Latris ciliaris</i>), king-fish (Seriola lalandii),<i> wharehou (Neptomenus brama)</i>, barracoota (<i>Thyrsites atun</i>), mackerel (<i>Scomber australasicus</i>), and the delicious little maomao ... <i>(1873,TrNZI,5/153</i>)

>  The fishing off the island is very good, there being abundance of hapuku, kohikohi, maumau, schnapper, kahawai, tarakihi, in addition to plenty of shell-fish such as koura or cray-fish, crabs, paua,etc. <i>(1885,TrNZI,17/41,9 ed.</i>) [NOTES: (Goldsmith)]

Maomao - (<i>Ditrema violacea</i>). NUMBER OF FISH RECORDED - 85. LOCALITIES - Mokohinou and Portland Island. <i>(1892,TrNZI,24/203</i>) [NOTES: (Thomson)]

>  <b>MORRIS</b> <i>Maomao, n.</i> Maori name for a New Zealand sea-fish, <i>Ditrema violacea. </i> <i>(1898,Austral English,242</i>)

Mr. Griffin, of the Auckland Museum, informs us that both this species and the sweep (<i>Scorpis aequipinnis</i>) are sold as `maomao' in Auckland. <i>(May 1922,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,5/93</i>) [NOTES: Phillipps and Hodgkinson Further Notes on Edible Fishes]

### Maori
 
 <i>n.</i> (attrib. use) Maori(dom) as a field of study

>  <b>BALLIS</b> According to the honoured Maori scholar, Sir James Cowan, [Pomare] should ever be remembered as ... a `medical apostle' who carried `the gospel of health' to the Maori. <i>(1985,In and Out of the World,82</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> [There is a discussion on the word in the `Journal of Polynesian Society,' vol. i. no. 3, vol. ii. no. I, and vol. iii. no. I. Bishop Williams (4th ed.) says that the word means, `of the normal or usual kind.' The Pakehas were not men to whom the natives were accustomed. So Maori was used as opposed to the Europeans, the white-skins. <i>Kuri Maori</i> was a name used for a dog after the arrival of other quadrupeds called also <i>kuri. Wai maori</i> was fresh-water, oridinary as opposed to sea-water. Another explanation is that the word meant `indigenous,' and that there are kindred words with that meaning in other Polynesian languages. First, `indigenous,' or "of the native race,' and then with a secondary meaning, `ours.' (See `Tregear's Maori Comparative Dictionary,' s.v.) The form of the plural varies, The form <i>Maoris</i> is considered the more correct, but the form <i>Maories</i> is frequently used by good writers.]

>  <b>TREGEAR</b> Many of the canoes (<i>waka</i>) of the Maoris are still in evidence to bear witness to the industry, patience, and skill, of the men who designed them. <i>(1904,Maori Race, The,114</i>)

>  <b>GORST</b> ....carried on by the Maories themselves; the Waikato district was very especially under the jurisdiction of the Maori King and his Runanga. <i>(1908,New Zealand Revisited,1</i>)

>  <b>McNAB</b> To all whom it may concern be it known that I Toawick [Tuhawaiki] are now become Rangatera or Chief of these Southern Territories ... Witnesses ... George Moss, Mowry, x his mark. Tomarama, Mowry x his mark. [?1840s] <i>(1913,Old Whaling Days,96</i>)

>  <b>McCRAE</b> Tooi (Tuai) and Teeterree ..., two young Maori who had visited London. <i>(1820,Journal,29</i>) [NOTES: 1928 J.C. Andersen in 1820 McCrae Journal 29]

>  <i> ~ n</i>. an aborigine of New Zealand; the language of the aborigines of New Zealand;- a. pert. to the aborigines of New Zealand. -Maori cabb'age n. see NATIVE CABBAGE.-Maorilander n. sl. a white person born in New Zealand. <i>(1929,Whitcombe's Etym. Dict. Aust-NZ Suppl.,7</i>)

>  <b>STACK</b> Most of the buildings were put up by the Maoris, and were just like the huts they built for themselves. <i>(1935,More Maoriland Adventures</i>) [NOTES: 1935 J.W.Stack More Maoriland Adventures in Wily and Maunsell Robert Maunsell (1938) 134]

>  <i>Maori</i>: native of New Zealand; lanuage of Maori people; anything native. <i>(1947,Reeds' School Dictionary: NZ Suppl.,174</i>)

>  <b>HOWARD</b> [pl] It was at last purchased from the Maori in 1864 by an unwilling Government; ... <i>(1940,Rakiura,xi</i>)

>  <b>ZIMMERMAN</b> I looked at them, and decided that they presented a fairly good miniature of New Zealand, for half of them were Maori and half were Pakeha, and they were having a noisy good time together. <i>(1946,Where the People Sing,101</i>)

>  <b>SOLJAK</b> New Zealand colloquialisms which are of Maori origin include: ... <i>Maori</i>: New Zealand native; this word was originally an adjective meaning `local' and is so used by various Polynesian peoples <i>(1946,New Zealand (various authors/titles),116</i>)

>  <b>SARGESON</b> [l.c. initial] No, I don't know, but there's a yarn his father was a whaler that stayed out here and got in tow with some maori tart. He's part maori for sure though, because once he got me to witness a paper and it was about maori land. <i>(1949,I Saw in My Dream,117,1974 ed. Rhodes ed.</i>)

>  <b>BUCK</b> The smallest sociological unit is the biological family, which the Maoris termed<i> whanau</i>, derived from <i>whanau</i>, to give birth . ... The term <i>hapu</i> (pregnancy) was used to denote this expanded family group for it expressed the idea of birth from common ancestors ... The term<i> iwi</i> (bone) was brought into current use to include all the<i> hapu</i> descended from common ancestors and thus related to each other by a blood tie. To denote the groupings in English, the <i>iwi</i> has been termed tribe and the <i>hapu</i> a sub-tribe. <i>(1949,Coming of the Maori, The,333</i>)

>  This essay is a report on the ... distribution of the Maori [fn 1] in northern New Zealand - ... [fn 1] A distinction may be made between `Maori' and Maoris' as plural forms. The former refers to the Maori people as a whole and as an ethnic and cultural entity; the latter designates individuals of this people (cf <i>The Coming of the Maori</i> and <i>Some Modern Maoris</i>. <i>(Oct 1952,NZ Geographer,104</i>) [NOTES: Metge]

>  <b>SINCLAIR</b> Before the arrival of strangers from overseas among the warring tribes, the inhabitants of New Zealand were Atiawa or Ngapuhi or Waikato. So far as is known, they had no name for their race: the word <i>maori </i>meant normal'. They applied the term to themselves only when, for the first time in their recollection, they encountered another race. <i>(1959,History of New Zealand,114</i>)

>  <b>SHADBOLT</b> He was ... a rolling stone who moved about the country ... beachcombing with Maoris in Northland ... <i>(1959,New Zealanders, The,110,1986 ed.</i>) [NOTES: (Paua Gatherers)]

>  <i>maori n 1</i> pl <i>maori</i> or <i>maoris usu cap a</i> (1) : a Polynesian people native to New Zealand (2) : a member of such people <i>b </i>: the Austronesian language of the Maori <i>(1961,Merriam-Webster Third International,1379</i>)

>  <i> ~ n. </i>New Zealand natives; their language: <i>~ adj.</i> anything pertaining to the Maoris or their language, a N.S.W. fish. <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Whitcombe's),620</i>)

>  <i>Maori ... n</i>. N.Z. natives; their language: <i>adj.</i> anything pertaining to the Maoris or their language; a N.S.W. fish. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Whitcombe's),13</i>)

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> <i>I[i]nanga, inanga</i>, n. <i>1. Whitebait</i>, the adult of the minnow, <i>Galaxias attenuatus</i> (also the fry), and the fry of the smelt, <i>Retropinna retropinna.</i> ... Maoris distinguish between <i>inanga papa</i>, a variety of smelt, <i>Retropinna</i>, and <i>inanga tu[u]tuna,</i> a spotted variety of minnow, <i>Galaxias. </i> <i>Ibid.</i> 364[plurals] [At <i>Taipo</i?> Williams has:] This word is used by Maoris believing it English, and by Europeans believing it maori, it being apparently neither. Colenso suggests <i>tae-po</i>, but this is not used by the Maori. 1979 Webster Rua 212 <i>et pass</i> [pl Maori] Maori who remembered Rua at the time, but were not followers, had very differing opinions about the prophet. <i>(1971,Dictionary of the Maori Language,77</i>)

>  <b>DUFF</b> This raises the question of what we mean by the term Maori. Why should Kupe be termed a Maori, if (according to Maori tradition) he was a Society Islands voyager of some 1,000 years ago who touched at New Zealand in the course of exploring voyages and returned to his homeland? By what greater right can we apply the term to Toi who arrived from the same part of the Pacific 200 years later to settle in New Zealand, or to the numerous voyagers subsequent to Toi and culminating in the Hawaiki Fleet of 1350? Every immigrant from tropical Polynesia did not become a Maori the moment he stepped ashore in New Zealand. The culture which we have come to know as Maori was undoubtedly the end product of a long period of local development, probably not reaching its final perfection of form until after the visit of Tasman. The use of the term Maori (lit. normal, ordinary or proper) to distinguish the Polynesian inhabitants of New Zealand from the intrusive European is even more clearly a late development and certainly post-European.* [fn] Williams (1921) notes no literary use before 1850 of the Maori in its present sense. Scholefield (1945) puts its use back to 1840, in the translation of the Treaty of Waitangi. McNab, quoted by Shrimpton and Mulgan, without the reference being cited, states that its earliest use was in 1818. <i>(1977,Moa-Hunters of NZ,12</i>)

>  <b>SUTTON-SMITH</b> [elderly woman b. Birmingham 1864, emigrated Tuakau 1865, reflecting a contemporary pron ?and spg] In 1868 the birth of another brother made my mother seriously ill. I remember them shifting her away from the district because they feared the attacks of the Maoris. [Pronounced `Moorees' by this elderly woman.] [sic] <i>(1981,History of Children's Play,14</i>)

>  <i>Maori n. 1.</i> (pl. -<i>ris</i> or -<i>ri</i>) a member of the people of Polynesian origin living in New Zealand since before the arrival of European settlers. 2. the language of this people, belonging to the Malayo-Polynesian family.<i> ~adj. 3. </i>of or relating to this people or their language. -<i> Maoriland n. </i> <i>(1982,Collins Concise English Dictionary,688</i>) [NOTES: [1986 NZPOD 458; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 299]]

>  [pl Maori] Wellington Maori, backed by Maori throughout the country, say that the Treaty of Waitangi requires the State to uphold the greatest taonga (treasure) of all, the Maori language, which, they say, has been prejudiced by several Acts of Parliament. <i>(Jun 21, 1986,Evening Post,27</i>)

>  (Profesor Joan Metge) Recent letters opposing the use of the word `Pakeha' claim that it means `foreigner' or `stranger'. ... Whatever the word meant when it was first bestowed, it has taken on a new and special meaning. Maoris today use it to identify non-Maori New Zealanders of European and especially of British stock, people whom they recognise as having a history of residence and commitment to this country. Maoris use it not only to distinguish Pakehas from themselves but to couple them with themselves. The word has meaning only within the New Zealand context, in relation to and partnership with `Maori', the shared reference point being the land of Aotearoa/New Zealand. <i>(Apr 30, 1988,Evening Post,8</i>)

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> [plurals] <i>Taipo</i>, n. <I</i>>Goblin. NOTE. - This word is used by Maoris believing it English, and by Europeans believing it Maori, it being apparently neither. Colenso suggests <i>tae-po,</i> but this is not used by the Maori. <i>(1917,Dictionary of the Maori Language,425</i>)

>  <b>WESTON</b> The Medical Officer ... injected the serum in what the Maories call my <i>puku ... </i> <i>(1918,Three Years with the NZers,70</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> an aboriginal or native New Zealander; pertaining to the Maori. Spg <i>Mouri, Mauri, Mawnee</i>, [? typographical or transcription error], in the earliest texts; <i>mouray Mao[diereis]ri Maori</i> from at least 1842 (with occasional lower case m.) Plural. Mauries, Mauris, Mouries, Maories; Mauri, Mouri, Maori. The plural <i>Maories</i> can be found in written texts to the 1870's or 1880's; and is not uncommon in informal written English today, though usually regarded as a spelling error. The plural <i>Maori</i> has been usual among those with knowledge of Maori; and among those who are sensitive to Maori usage or feeling. <i>Synonyms.</i> Leaving aside the very early (Cook, Banks) <i>Indian </i>q.v., early writers used <i>native</i> (as n and aj.) or <i>New Zealander </i>q.v. as the preferred terms, with<i> aborigine</i> q.v. being common in early writing asociated with `imperial' agencies as the Colonial Office and the Church Missionary Society, and was reinforced by the early appointment of a <i>Protector of Aborigines</i>. Early uses of <i>Maori</i> were usually tagged with an explanatory `native"; and indeed <i>Maori </i>did not replace <i>Native</i> in official use until 1947 when the name of the Department of Native Affairs was replaced by `Maori Affairs'.

>  <b>MARKHAM</b> The old people believe that the (Atua) God of the Parkiars Strangers is killing or eating the Mouries or Natives and they see their strength decrease daily; .... [fn] The Old Men have an Idea that the Atua of the Parkeiahs is kiki-ing or eating up their people or Nation .... <i>Ibid.</i> 117 117 Europeans are not considered so good eating as the Mouries as they are too salt. <i>(1834,New Zealand (various authors/titles),66,1963 ed.</i>)

>  <b>CAPT JOY</b> Latter part fine weather 5 boats out by means of 3 Mowrees <i>(May 22, 1836,Log of Mary Mitchell</i>) [NOTES: 1836 May 22 Capt. Joy <i>Mary Mitchell</i> log in 1913 McNab Old Whaling Days 443]

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> This Maori's house and property were left untouched, he is now like the maniac ... [first use] [Taylor uses <i>Native - boy</i>, occas. New Zealander; but rarely, <i>Maori</i> n for a person in his 139-1849 TS Journal.] <i>(Mar 29, 1839,Journal,2/78</i>)

>  <b>33</b> [first use in log; native not used] Spare hands and Mouries building a house. <i>(Apr 20, 1836,Piraki Log,ed. Anson 1911 ed.</i>)

>  <b>WAKEFIELD</b> [The Missionaries] tried all they could to dissuade the people from selling their land, instancing the way in which the * <i>maories </i>[fn. `natives'] had been driven off the land. [Maoris italicised and asterisked with a footnote `native' shows the word to have been by no means common usage. But Wakefield was writing for English readers. CCRansom note au contraire: W. seems to be instructing English readers on usage and thus this can be used as evidence that the word was to some extent established though not in England. <i>(1840,NZ Journal,1/16/202</i>)

>  We formed in three divisions but the Mauries (Natives) were not to be seen. <i>(Apr 1840,Journal of Ensign Best,217,1866 ed.</i>)

>  <b>SARAH MATHEW</b> I believe we have not seen a single Maori (or native) since leaving Wangari, nor have we fallen in with the least trace of inhabitants. <i>(Apr 25, 1840,Journal</i>) [NOTES: 1840 Apr 25 Sarah Mathew Journal in 1940 Founding of NZ 128]

>  I sent a maury (native), as they call themselves. <i>(1840,NZ Journal,1/17/22</i>) [NOTES: (Geo. Duppa)]

>  The Mauries (natives) [four times] ... the Mauri [pl]. <i>(1840,NZ Journal,1/18/232</i>) [NOTES: (W.D.B. Mantell)]

>  <b>CAMPBELL</b> The Mouries of the tribe [He had previously used<i>natives</i>] <i>(1840,Present State of NZ,9</i>)

>  ... flat-bellied Mauries (natives) <i>(1840,NZ Journal,1/23/292</i>) [NOTES: (T.M. Partridge)]

>  ... we learn that a disposition exists to arrange all points of difference amicably and advantageously for the Europeans as well as the Maories. <i>(Nov 23, 1842,Letter</i>) [NOTES: (1842 Nov 23) 1843 (1968) Letters from New Plymouth 70]

>  Hear, all of you, Pakehas and Mouris. [side-note] Pakeha, White Man. Mouri, Native. <i>(Apr 1840,GBPP,(No.311) 58</i>) [NOTES: 1840 Apr Notes of Nopera's (of Kaitaia) speech by Rev.R.Taylor and Dr.Johnston in GBPP House of Commons 1841]

>  <b>WEEKES</b> [New Zealander, once; native previously] A `Mourai' [Maori] or native thatched the house with a long grass called <i>toi-toi</i> for an old pair of trousers and a shirt. <i>(Apr 13, 1841,Journal</i>) [NOTES: 1841 Apr 13 Weekes Journal in 1940 Establishment of New Plymouth 47]

>  <b>POWER</b> Four days have been spent in ineffectual efforts to induce the Maories to move, ... <i>Ibid.</i> 143 The chiefs generally belong to the finer-looking race, although they have no distinction of blood among themselves, all alike coming under the denomination of `Maori'. <i>(1849,Sketches in NZ,30</i>)

>  <b>POWER</b> Four days have been spent in ineffectual efforts to induce the Maories to move, ... <i>Ibid.</i> 143 The chiefs generally belong to the finer-looking race, although they have no distinction of blood among themselves, all alike coming under the denomination of `Maori'. <i>(1849,Sketches in NZ,30</i>)

>  <b>ROCHFORT</b> [spg maorie] On going down to the beach, some Maories* came ashore in a canoe. They were about building a market-place to trade with the white man. [fn] Maorie, native name of the aborigines. <i>(1853,Adventures of a Surveyor,14</i>)

>  <b>SEWELL</b> [In 1853 Sewell uses Maori, Maoris, Maories and Native(s) eg 292] <i>(1853,Journal,I</i>)

>  <b>MACKIE</b> A custom house boat came off to us manned by N. Zealanders or as they are called here Maoris (pronounced Mouries). <i>Ibid.</i> 77 The settlers are now busily engaged in the harvest, a few employ Maoris - these earn 3/- per day, the whites 6/-. The whites through the island bear but a small proportion to the aborigines, ... <i>(Jan 30, 1853,Traveller under Concern,72,1973 ed.</i>)

>  <b>FITTON</b> The number of the natives, or as they call themselves `Maori', ... has been variously estimated; ... <i>(1856,New Zealand (various authors/titles),28</i>)

>  <b>SMITH</b> There were several Maories in the house ... <i>(Jan 7, 1858,Notes of a Journey</i>) [NOTES: 1858 Jan 7 Smith Notes of a Journey in 1959 Taylor Early Travellers 355]

>  <b>THOMSON</b> No light is thrown on the origin of the New Zealanders from the name Maori which they call themselves. This word rendered, by linguists `native,' is used in contra-distinction to pakeha, or stranger. <i>(1859,Story of New Zealand, The,1/59</i>)

>  <b>ASHWELL</b> I believe there is no hope for the Maoris for years to come. <i>(1860,Letters & Journals,3/351</i>) [NOTES: (F.W. Fenton)]

>  <b>BUTLER</b> In the North I am told that the race is much finer and that these are a miserable remnant of tribes expelled thence. These Maoris are certainly outcasts from the North island according to the universal tradition of the place - but whether or no the other is better I cannot say. <i>(Jun 1860,Forest Creek MS,43,1960 ed.</i>)

>  <b>BUTLER</b> [spg Maori [a-dieresis] They showed me some moa bones which they had ploughed up (the moa, as you doubtless know, was an enormous bird, which must have stood some fifteen feet high), also some stone Maori [a-dieresis] battle-axes. <i>(Jan 1860,First Year in Canterbury Settlement,iii/35</i>)

>  <b>VON TEMPSKY</b> Such were our preparations, thanks to the tardiness of the Maori and the rapidity of action of General Cameron, ... <i>(1864,Memoranda,3</i>)

>  <b>ASHWELL</b> (Letter from a Maori priest) You missionaries should come amongst the Maories. <i>(1867,Letters & Journals,4/525</i>)

>  <b>MONEY</b> A few Maories were in the habit of going over every year ... to see their friends at a pah or village on the Grey river, ... <i>(1871,Knocking About in NZ,30</i>)

>  <b>DOMETT</b> Having a little discussion about the New Zealand war, and to whose fault, that of the colonists or to the Maories, it was attributable; ... <i>(Aug 28, 1873,Diary,102,1953 ed.</i>)

>  <b>BULLER</b> By the English plural of this word, <i>Maories,</i> we understand the aborigines, or natives.<i> Maori</i> in singular, but always <i>Maories</i>, in plural] <i>(1878,Forty Years in NZ,163</i>)

>  <b>BARKER</b> ... few groups of Maories. <i>(1874,Station Life in NZ,24</i>)

>  <b>HAY</b> [pl Maori] The root of the raupo ..., the swamp-grass of which the Maori construct their whare[e-grave], is edible ... Punga-punga, the pollen of the raupo, used to be made into bread. <i>(1882,Brighter Britain,2/210</i>)

>  <b>MORRIS</b> <i>Maori, n.</i> (pronounced so as to rhyme with <i>Dowry</i>). (I) The name used to designate themselves by the Polynesian race occupying New Zealand when it was discovered by the white man, and which still survives. They are not aboriginal as is commonly supposed, but migrated into New Zealand about 500 years ago from Hawaii, the tradition still surviving of the two great canoes <i>Arawa</i> and <i>Tainui</i> in which the pioneers arrived. They are commonly spoken of as the <i>Natives</i> of New Zealand. -->(2) The language of the Maori race. -->(3) Applied to anything pertaining to the Maoris or their language. See Pakeha. -->There is a discussion on the word in the `Journal of Polynesian Society,' vol. i. no. 3, vol. ii. no. I, and vol. iii. no. I. Bishop Williams (4th ed.) says that the word means, `of the normal or usual kind.' The Pakehas were not men to whom the natives were accustomed. So Maori was used as opposed to the Europeans, the white-skins. <i>Kuri Maori</i> was a name used for a dog after the arrival of other quadrupeds called also <i>kuri. Wai maori</i>was fresh-water, ordinary as opposed to sea-water. Another explanation is that the word meant `indigenous,' and that there are kindred words with that meaning in other Polynesian languages. First, `indigenous,' or "of the native race,' and then with a secondary meaning, `ours.' (See `Tregear's Maori Comparative Dictionary,' s.v.) The form of the plural varies, The form <i>Maoris</i> is considered the more correct, but the form <i>Maories</i> is frequently used by good writers. <i>(1898,Austral English,284</i>)

>  (Settler's letter) The mauris (dark men) .... parkias (white men) ... <i>(1841,Settlement of New Plymouth,16</i>)

>  <b>BUTLER</b> [pl] The few Maories that inhabit this settlement travel to the west coast by way of this river. They always go on foot, and we saw several traces of their encampments - little <i>mimis</i>, as they are called - a few light sticks thrown together, and covered with grass, affording a sort of half-and-half shelter for a single individual. <i>(1860,First Year in Canterbury Settlement,v/72</i>)

>  <b>MAJORIBANKS</b> They [natives] call themselves <i>Maori</i>, which means <i>indigenous</i> or <i>aboriginal</i>, in oposition to <i>Pakea</i>, which means stranger. <i>(1844,Travels in NZ,71</i>)

Bishop Williams (4th ed.) says that the word means, `of the normal or usual kind.' The Pakehas were not men to whom the natives were accustomed. So Maori was used as opposed to the Europeans, the white-skins. <i>Kuri Maori</i> was a name used for a dog after the arrival of other quadrupeds called also <i>kuri. Wai maori</i> was fresh-water, oridinary as opposed to sea-water. Another explanation is that the word meant `indigenous,' and that there are kindred words with that meaning in other Polynesian languages. First, `indigenous,' or "of the native race,' and then with a secondary meaning, `ours.' (See `Tregear's Maori Comparative Dictionary,' s.v.) The form of the plural varies, The form <i>Maoris</i> is considered the more correct, but the form <i>Maories</i> is frequently used by good writers. <i>(1845,Adventure in NZ,i/194</i>)

 <i>adv.</i> [ca1935 Havelock <i>to go Maori</i>, of a European, to live in an inferior fashion. <i>to act the Maori</i>, to show uncivilised or aggressive behaviour.]

 <i>n.</i> plural is Maoris or Maori 1. a) a member of the tangata whenua, the Polynesian race that first peopled New Zealand. b) a Polynesian of the Cook Islands. Compare PAKEHA. 2. their languages. Maori adjective (usually capital, can be offensive in some non-Maori use) 1. a) of or pertaining to Maori peoples or culture (often replacing earlier native): `Maori Battalion'; `Maori Affairs Department'; `Maori oven'. b) use can be derogatory or stereotypical: `Maori cannon'. 2. a) indigenous, native to New Zealand: `Maori bug'; `Maori hen'. b) undomesticated (synonym is wild): `Maori cabbage'; `Maori celery'; `Maori dog'. [Maori Maori originally an adjective, normal, usual, ordinary] [For fuller discussion of theories of origin, and applications, of MAORI (and PAKEHA) see Journal Polynesian Soc. 54/4/223-234; [etc.] Maori, <i>maaori </i> see also taha maori, tangata maori, copper maori , pro-Maori include in article Maori as a subject of study: e.g. Maori was admitted at last as a university subject: a Chair of Maori ??? -->pl forms Maori the native race, or several Maoris, is becoming the preferred pl among maoriphiles: Maories quite common until the 1870s;]

>  In 1974 the third Labour government changed the definition of a "Maori" from one based on ethnicity to one based on cultural identity. <i>(Feb 3, 1992,Listener (New Zealand Listener),28</i>)

>  <b>LEE & KENDALL</b> M|aodi, <i>a</i>. Indigenous, native; as, `E t|angata ma|odi; A native man:' `Wai m|aodi; Native water:' `Kai m|aodi; Native victuals.' Also a proper name. <i>(1820,Grammar & Vocabulary of the Language of NZ,176</i>)

>  <b>McCRAE</b> [KORARI MAORI: also note that McC. uses NATIVE, but uses PAKEHA p.23] The hill was covered completely with the flax plant which is called Corade[acute] Mauvre, and which grew in great luxuriance. [Andersen fn.: .<i>korari</i> is usually the tall flower-stalk of <i>harakeke</i> ..., but in the North sometimes used to designate the plant itself. Here McCrae uses the word <i>maori</i> in its original sense signifying `normal, usual, ordinary'. <i>(1820,Journal,22,1928 ed.</i>)

>  <b>BOYD</b> Maori began to realise that they had been pushed into selling by government agents who had become increasingly careless about obtaining the consent of all the owners, ... <i>(1984,City of the Plains,5</i>)

>  <i>maori n 1</i> pl <i>maori or maoris usu cap a </i>(1) : a Polynesian people native to New Zealand (2) : a member of such people b : the Austronesian language of the Maori <i>(1961,Merriam-Webster Third International,1379</i>)

It assumes that Anglo are fair but Maori are not and that hurt me a great deal.' <i>(Mar 14, 1989,Dominion,2</i>)

>  Maori Affairs Act, 1953. "Maori" means a person belonging to the aboriginal race of New Zealand; and includes a half-caste and a person intermediate in blood between half-castes and persons of pure descent from that race. <i>(1953,NZ Statutes,(II) 94/1078</i>) [NOTES: NOTE that this regresses from the 1953 definition]

>  Maori Purposes Act, 1969. Housing assistance for Polynesians... "(2A). For the purposes of this Act, the term "Maori" shall be deemed to include any Polynesian who is a native of any island of the South Pacific Ocean and any person who is a descendant of such a Polynesian if, in either case, - "(a) He is a New Zealand citizen; or "(b) He has lived in New Zealand for five years and is permanently resident in New Zealand." <i>(1969,NZ Sceptic,(II) 127/834</i>)

>  Maori Affairs Act 1953, "Maori" means a person of the Maori race of New Zealand; and includes any descendant of such a person. <i>(1981,NZ Statutes,8 :25(13)</i>) [NOTES: Reprinted Statutes. [Double pagination, volume and act]]

>  Maori Affairs Restricting Act 1989. "Maori" means a person of the Maori race of New Zealand; and includes a descendant of any such person. <i>(1989,NZ Statutes,68 : 1461</i>) [NOTES: See also : 1991 NZ Statutes Ministry of Maori Development Act. 145/1910; 1993 NZ Statutes Te Ture Whenua Maori / Maori Land Act. 4/10.]

>  Having suitably characterised the white strangers as pakeha, the tribes adopted the word maori as a distinguishing descriptive term for themselves. Maori means normal, ordinary or natural, in contrast to the extraordinary or supranormal quality of fair skin. Thus did the two terms Maori and Pakeha mutually define each other. <i>(May 14, 1990,Listener (New Zealand Listener),100</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a children's game

 <i>adj.</i> native, a new formation from MAORI n. use B used attributively, thence adjectivally rather than a borrowing or continuation of use A. Used increasingly for the earlier NATIVE (or New Zealand or aboriginal), and as native came to connote and denote a more general `New Zealand' (from say the 1890's) it was replaced by Maori aj in most uses.
