>  <b>127</b> [l.c. initial] I shot 2 maori dogs at night. <i>(Jan 24, 1850,Torlesse Papers</i>)

>  <b>139</b> `Aware of the advantages ... which the district possesses in an abundance of native labour, and deeming that the Society might do something to advance the improvement of Maori labour by taking notice of the deserving, and encouraging the most meritorious ... the Committee added to the list of prizes a premium of two sovereigns to the best and most trustworthy native servant; ... <i>(Apr 13, 1850,Report of Auckland Agricultural Society</i>)

>  <b>GRACE</b> This Church will be, if finished according to Maori architecture, the greatest monument of national art that New Zealand contains, and which the present race is likely ever to execute. <i>(Dec 31, 1851,Report</i>) [NOTES: 1851 Dec 31 T.S.Grace Report of Turanga District in Grace Pioneer Missionary (1928) 16]

>  <b>MACKIE</b> His Maori labourers soon discovered that I had never handled a sickle before. <i>(Feb 3, 1853,Traveller under Concern,75,1973 ed.</i>)

>  <b>KING</b> `Maori' explanations were invariably invoked to explain catastrophes. When boats were lost on the Hokianga bar, for example, it was because something had been done to offend the taniwha ... <i>(1983,Whina,40</i>)

>  <b>SHORTLAND</b> In the `Maori' version of the Testament, thus [306] saith the Lord, has been rendered `e ai ta te Atua.' As the words in `Maori,' however, do not convey the sense ... of a command, ... we should prefer as a translation ... <i>(1851,Southern Districts of NZ,305</i>)

>  <b>SCOTT</b> The red, white and blue `maori' trees (maori, as already intimated, is the name of the aborigines of New Zealand; but the word is also conventionally used to describe anything that is good, beautiful, or superior) look handsome. <i>(1860,Rough Notes of Travel,32</i>)

>  We may say first that the tangi indicates clearly the Maori attitude to death and shows us this attitude contrasting strongly with the customary Pakeha attitude. <i>(1945,Journal of the Polynesian Society,54/`115</i>) [NOTES: (Beaglehole)]

>  At 3 P.M. a mowry Boat from the river, but the Captain being displeased made them go. <i>(Jun 5, 1840,Piraki Log,109,ed. Anson 1911 ed.</i>)

>  men employed in the bush felling timber; ... A Mowerie boat left for Wongooloa. <i>(Mar 9, 1841,Piraki Log,119,ed. Anson 1911 ed.</i>)

>  <b>SHORTLAND</b> ... and the term <i>Maori</i>, used by the New Zealanders to distinguish their race from Europeans, or from negroes, is the very term found [40] to be used in Hawaii and the adjacent islands for the same purpose. <i>(1856,Traditions & Superstitions,39</i>)

>  Probable Amount of Maori Contribution to Revenue. [heading] 39. In estimating the amount of native contribution to the Customs revenue, it should be remembered that, not only as consumers, but as producers, they swell the amount of its receipts. <i>(Oct 13, 1858,Memorandum by Native Secretary</i>) [NOTES: 1858 Oct 13 Memorandum by Native Secretary in 1949 Rutherford ed. Select Documents 105.39 X.]

>  <b>THOMSON</b> [Thomson habitually uses `New Zealander"(= Maori); here Maori is used as the aj]. Intercourse with civilised men induced New Zealanders to travel abroad. Between 1810 and 1838 many Maori men visited Sydney, Europe and America; but no New Zealand woman had yet seen the civilised world. <i>(1859,Story of New Zealand, The,1/280</i>)

>  <b>BUDDLE</b> Their watchword is, `No further alienation of Maori territory.' <i>(1860,Maori King Movement,20</i>)

>  <b>MOSER</b> It merely represented what my early impressions were on the moral condition of the Natives, and ... closer intimacy with the Maori race has considerably shaken the opinions I then formed. <i>(1863,Mahoe Leaves,58</i>)

>  <b>MANING</b> Then, as the boat came nearer, another burst of <i>haere mai!</i> But unaccustomed as I was then to the Maori salute, I disliked the sound. <i>(1863,Old New Zealand,(I)16</i>)

>  <b>ASHWELL</b> ... boundary mark between Maori and European land ... Maori newspaper. <i>Ibid.</i> IX/469. Maori prisoners. <i>(1863,Letters & Journals,4/438</i>)

>  <b>PHILIPS</b> ... the Maori bucks <i>(1897,Memories of the Past,8</i>)

>  <b>BUCK</b> In the Waimea Plains, an area of soil, known as Maori gravel, was shown to be very rich in available phosphoric acid and potash. This area is an old Maori pre-European cultivation. Pits had been dug to obtain sand and gravel, which, after having the larger stones removed, were spread over the natural loam top soil to render it more suitable for the cultivation of the kumara ... <i>Ibid.</i> 28 The <i>taniko </i>in the embroidered borders of cloaks, is from the point of technique the latest development in Maori weaving. <i>(1929,Coming of the Maori, The,3</i>) [NOTES: Cawthron Lecture]

>  <b>15</b> <i>Maori</i>.- Used as an adjective synonymous with <i>native</i>, except that it seems slightly contemptious. We admire native bush, pasture, or game; but <i>M</i>. hens (woodhens), <i>M.</i> cabbage [the European cabbage run wild--A.W.], and <i>M</i>. onion (Chrysobactron bookeri) are caricatures of their English namesakes. ([1898 Morris Austral-English]) <i>(Nov 4, 1933,Press, The</i>) [NOTES: Acland Glossary]

>  The Maori men labourers are paid the same rate of wages as that paid to ordinary European labourers. <i>(1936,Agricultural Organization in NZ,584</i>)

>  <b>BUCK</b> ... the numerous smaller birds which the skilled Maori bushman knew so well how to lure and snare. <i>(1937,Moa-Hunters of NZ,177</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> as the Maori (also nga Maori): the Maori people as an ethnic or cultural entity

>  <b>SHORTLAND</b> ... the New Zealanders have given the locality of New Zealand to events recorded in ancient tales; which are probably exaggerated accounts of what may have occurred in the countries from which the Maori came formerly. <i>(1856,Traditions & Superstitions,73</i>)

>  <b>LINDSAY</b> <i>G[eranium] dissectum</i>, L., A. var. <i>Carolinianum</i>, Fl. N.Z. ... The `Matuakumera' of the North Island Maori: a term, however, more generally applied to the tuberous edible root. Colenso also assigns the Maori name `Pinakitere'; but it does not clearly appear whether to the type, or to which of its New Zealand forms. <i>(1868,Contributions to NZ Botany,62</i>)

>  <b>GRACE</b> As soon as the Maori saw our object, they opened fire. <i>(1899,Sketch of the NZ War,44</i>)

>  The <i>Taiaha</i>, as a few New Zealanders may know, is an old-time fighting weapon of the Maori. <i>(1917,New Zealand At the Front,1</i>)

>  The Maori treated unexpanded plants of the basket fungus as a great luxury. <i>(Oct 1959,Tararua Tramper,10</i>)

>  <b>BOYD</b> The Maori ran pigs in the swamp and grazed horses, sheep and cattle on the few rough patches of native grass on the side of the old track from Te Awa O Te Atua to Paki Paki and on the margins of the two creeks. <i>(1984,City of the Plains,4</i>)

>  <b>LUCETT</b> [frequ. use here] Nga Maori are keenly alive to the degradation of exposure; ... <i>(1851,Rovings In The Pacific,1</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> the Maori language, the only use of Maori in the early journals: cf [ed fn] This is one of the rare occasions in which the word `Maori', as meaning a `person of the native race', is used by the missionaries. But its use here as meaning `the language of the native race' would seem to indicate that it was not an unusual name, although the missionaries and others normally used `native' or `New Zealander'. [`the Maori language' used in 1834, 1841, 1842: see C.below)]

>  <b>NICHOLAS</b> It is good to read the language of New Zealand / Kapi ta karakea a koraro no New Zealand <i>(1817,New Zealand (various authors/titles),2/347</i>) [NOTES: NB `Maori'= language not used by Nicholas]

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> The Service very pleasant as all the natives assembled and part of it is held in <i>Maori</i>, which keeps up their attention. <i>(Aug 17, 1828,Early Journals of Henry Williams, The,141,1961 ed.</i>)

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> Read the former part of the morning service in <i>mauri. </i> <i>(Sep 21, 1828,Early Journals of Henry Williams, The,144,1961 ed.</i>)

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> The Service very pleasant and as all the natives assembled part of it is held in <i>Maori</i>. [ed 1961 fn.] This is one of the rare occasions in which the word "Maori', as meaning a `person of the native race', is used by the missionaries. But its use here as meaning `the language of the native race', would seem to indicate that it was not an unusual name, although the missionaries and other normally used `native' or `New Zealander'. <i>(Aug 17, 1828,Journal,141,1961 ed.</i>)

>  <b>MARKHAM</b> One [copy] translated into Mowrie <i>(1834,New Zealanders at Gallipoli,[42]</i>)

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> After the English service one in the native tongue Maori was given by my companion to about 50. <i>(Mar 10, 1839,Journal,2/72</i>)

>  [<i>Maori</i> seems used earlier for language : people called <i>natives</i>] On the day of [Maketu's] trial the court was crowded with natives, and every word that was uttered, whether in Maori or in English, was faithfully interpreted ... I have done all in my power to avert this evil, by publishing monthly in the Maori language, ... a gazette ... <i>(Mar 12, 1842,GBPP,(No.569) 193</i>) [NOTES: (language)1842 Mar 12 Hobson Despatch in GBPP 1842 (No.569) 193]

>  <b>BIDWILL</b> In Mowrie (New Zealand language) .... the Mowrie language .... <i>Ibid.</i> 18 .... speak `Mowrie'. <i>Ibid.</i> 42 Now, in proof of the difficulty which I speak of, I can state that I have met with but one person, and never heard of more than four, in all the country, who could speak `Mowrie' so perfectly as to be able to ask even the simplest question, not connected with their trade, in such a manner as immediately to make the Mowries understand what they said; ... <i>(1841,Rambles in NZ,16</i>)

>  <b>E.Hoskin</b> Dear mother, you would be glad to see them and hear them talk the Mowry (Maori) language. <i>(Feb 19, 1842,Letters from Settlers,8</i>) [NOTES: 1842 Feb 19 E.Hoskin in 1843 Letters from Settlers [Letters from New Plymouth (1968)] 8]

>  (Rev. W.Cotton) ... `Maori' (the native language) <i>(1843,NZ Journal,80/35</i>)

>  <b>WEEKES</b> Some minutes elapsing ... I sent Edwards, who was a Maori linguist, to see what was the matter. Knowing what the natives had done ... the time spent in suspense was anything but agreeable. <i>(1846,My Island</i>) [NOTES: c1846 Weekes My Island in 1940 Establishment of New Plymouth 125]

>  This drive for national standards.. that's a good thing ... as long as they don't just teach them Maori. Because Maori doesn't exist. Here we teach Ngati Porou, but up north it's Nga Puhi. They say they speak the right Maori. The Ngati Poneke from Wellington say they speak Maori correctly. We say we do. <i>(Dec 1996,Mana,No. 11 : 83</i>)

>  <b>WAKEFIELD</b> While the two Chiefs were at Cambridge, Professor Lee, from their pronunciation, reduced the <i>Maori</i>, or aboriginal language, into a written one, and composed a grammar and dictionary of it. <i>(1848,Handbook for New Zealand, The,53</i>) [NOTES: sc. language (?or is this an aj)]

>  <b>GRACE</b> With the blessing of God I hope by the end of the year to be able to write a few short sermons in Maori. <i>(Dec 2, 1850,Letter</i>) [NOTES: 1850 Dec 2 T.S.Grace letter in Grace Pioneer Missionary (1928) 12]

>  <b>166</b> The next morning we left our tent standing ... taking the precaution to leave a written notice in `maori', saying to whom they belonged; ... <i>(1851,Southern Districts of NZ</i>)

>  I was much struck by finding the identity of a root of the pronoun of the first person singular in the <i>Maori</i> of New Zealand with the root of the same pronoun in Malayu, and in the T'hay or Siamese, an allied continental language. <i>(1856,Traditions & Superstitions,43 [fn]</i>)

>  Consulted with Maria whether it would be well to go among the Negroes for six months to learn Maori. <i>(Sep 19, 1858,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,1/429,1960 ed.</i>) [NOTES: (A.S. Atkinson, Jrnl)]

>  <b>THOMSON</b> Neither [the whalers'] education nor their knowledge of Maori qualified them for the office of missionaries .... <i>(1859,Story of New Zealand, The,1/296</i>)

>  <b>ASHWELL</b> [Fenton, (Auckland)] I only understood a word or two of Maori! <i>(Oct 1, 1860,Letters & Journals,3/351</i>)

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> ... nor was his [sc. Rev. Henry Williams's] brother less so [sc. indefatigable], who has the honor [sic] of having been the first to translate the New Testament into Maori, ... <i>(1868,Past and Present of NZ,17</i>)

>  <b>MOSER</b> ... yet, at the same time every incident, every characteristic, every expression of Maori opinion, is closely ... given, either as it actually occurred, or fell from the lips of the Maori. <i>Ibid.</i> 3 I know very little of the interior of this country ..., and the smattering of Maori I have picked up, has been about the towns. <i>(1863,Mahoe Leaves,[preface]</i>)

>  <b>MANING</b> `I tell you what we must do ... we will not carry (<i>pikau</i>) the provisions, we will <i>hiki</i> them' (<i>Hiki</i> is the word in Maori which describes the act of carrying an infant in the arms.) <i>(1863,Old New Zealand,(viii) 129</i>)

>  <b>BAINES</b> He also told us stories half in English and half in Maori ... <i>Ibid.</i> 115 One trouble to the learner [of Maori] is that natives will persist in talking easy `pigeon' Maori to new `pakehas"; perhaps that is one reason why so few Europeans `talk Maori well', though many know enough of the language for ordinary purposes, such as buying or selling. <i>(1874,Edward Crewe,96</i>)

>  <b>GRACE</b> I was instrumental in getting Dr. Maunsell to publish a small pamphlet in Maori ... <i>(Dec 18, 1873,Letter</i>) [NOTES: 1873 Dec 18 T.S.Grace letter in Grace Pioneer Missionary (1928) 253]

>  <b>MARTIN</b> It was well for us that we soon learned to speak Maori. <i>(1884,Our Maoris,10</i>)

>  <b>MEREDITH</b> As I could not speak Maori I carried my bridle as a sort of interpreter as to the position of matters ... <i>(1898,Reminiscences and Experiences,24</i>)

>  <b>BAUCKE</b> I brought no introduction - except a knowledge of Maori be called such. <i>(1905,Where the White Man Treads,177</i>)

>  <b>McCRAE</b> But <i>b</i> finds no place in the vocabulary, and <i>uka buka</i> [u-macrons] was evidently regarded as a Maorization of the word `book' though <i>pukapuka</i> is genuine Maori. <i>(1820,Journal</i>) [NOTES: 1928 J.C. Andersen in 1820 McCrae Journal 29]

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> We did not get very far with our Maori, but I used to go to Maori services with Taipo (Joe Benson) and picked up quite a lot of it. <i>(1939,First White Boy Born in Otago,20</i>)

>  Paul Temm feels essentially unchanged by his experience on the tribunal, sitting at marae around the country, listening to the elders vent their frustrations in Maori, hongi-ing with both tangata whenua and manuhiri (visitors), chatting over tea and kai. <i>(Jun 21, 1986,Evening Post</i>)

>  While waiting on the beach ... I had a long talk with a genuine Moriori (quy. Moo[acute]rio[acute]ri?) or real aboriginal of the Chatham islands & some of the southern parts of New Zealand. I shall try & get some account of their language out of him but it will soon be extinct in Wharekauri (Chatham Isd.) as they all now talk Maori. Maori & Moriori are mutually unintelligible but no doubt they are only two dialects of one tongue ...[sic] <i>(Jan 9, 1865,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,2/145,1960 ed.</i>)

>  <b>BULLER</b> There are three classes of persons who talk Maori ... <i>(1878,Forty Years in NZ,176</i>)

>  <b>CAMPBELL</b> He did not now require a sleeping dictionary to learn Maori from. <i>(1881,Poenamo,3/1/129</i>)

>  <b>NICHOLAS</b> TANGATA MAORI in Maori context [sentences] Behold! Jesus Christ is the great and good Atua, the great and good friend to white men and black men; to all men. Na! Jesu Christ ta Atua Nue, ta Atua Pi, ta wanhoungha Nue, ta wanhounga Pi ke ta no-tungata na, Pakkahah, ke ta tangata Maoude, ka te tungata katoa katoa. <i>(1817,New Zealand (various authors/titles),2/342</i>)

>  <b>POLACK</b> [Note: P. uses <i>Maori </i>as a aj] The flax (<i>korari</i>,) forming the dresses is of two or [176] three kinds; the most coarse, called muka, is employed for working the common garment <i>(kakaou</i>[a1-acute] <i>maori</i>[a-acute].) This flax is also made use of for laying up ropes, lines, &c. The superior dress called Kaitaka[a2-a3-acute], is made from silken flax, (<i>hunga hunga</i>[as-acute].) <i>Ibid.</i> 1/199 [caption] Na[acute] <i>muttou maori</i>[a-acute], <i>New Zealand Fish-hooks.</i> <i>(1840,Manners & Customs of the NZers,1/175</i>)

>  <b>BIDWILL</b> Now, in proof of the difficulty which I speak of, I can state that I have met with but one person, and never heard of more than four, in all the country, who could speak `Mowrie' so perfectly as to be able to ask even the simplest question, not connected with their trade, in such a manner as immediately to make the Mowries understand what they said; ... > <i>Ibid.</i> 85 I said that a Pakiha could do no harm in going up, as no place was taboo to a Pakiha; that the taboo only applied to Mowries; and finally, that if the mountain was an atua, I must be a greater atua, or I could not have got to the top of it, ... <i>(1841,Rambles in NZ,42,1952 ed.</i>)

>  Not so [jovial] Mr Govett, the vicar, who is innocent & naive enough to fear the corruption that these worthies may introduce into his cure. This fear of spoiling their `happy valleys' has been epidemic on the ministry here, and has made the missionaries oppose colonization and led them to vamp up all the nauseous maudlin maori fancying stuff that has caused disaster ... here... <i>(Aug 13, 1851,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,1/103,1960 ed.</i>) [NOTES: (J.C. Richmond)]

>  A knowledge of Maori ought to be essential to a bush appointment [as a Maori assessor]. <i>(Aug 29, 1859,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,1/486,1960 ed.</i>) [NOTES: (J. Morgan)]

>  <b>BARKER</b> From the window of the hotel [at Kaiapoi] I saw a few groups of Maories ... <i>(1865,Station Life in NZ,24,1870 ed.</i>)

>  Several Europeans now speak the New Zealand language ... They have never thought .... in <i>Maori;</i> hence, while many of them are ready to speak of the meagreness of the New Zealand tongue, the leanness is entirely on their own side. <i>(1869,TrNZI,1/III/49</i>) [NOTES: (Colenso Maori races)]

>  This paper has been compiled from ... tales collected ... in the Maori language some twenty-five years ago, when there were still a few Maori alive who were acquainted with their ancient lore. <i>(1875,TrNZI,7/3</i>) [NOTES: (Wohlers)]

>  <b>BULLER</b> The word <i>Maori</i>, means whatever is native or indigenous. By the English plural of the word, <i>Maories</i>, [sic] we understand the aborigines, or natives. <i>(1878,Forty Years in NZ,163</i>)

>  <b>COLENSO</b> ... two afternoon Services there, (one in English and one in Maori,) ... -->Maori plant = native plant -- <i>(1888,Fifty Years In Maoriland,15</i>)

>  On [the nettle leaves] being used up I tried the hungry creatures [larvae] with several other leaves of Maori plants, but none would they touch. <i>(1889,TrNZI,21/198</i>) [NOTES: (Colenso)]

>  But Taiho as the Maori says. <i>(May 28, 1891,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,2/583,1960 ed.</i>) [NOTES: (E. Mitchelson)]

>  In Maori, it means `to count'; in Moriori, `to calculate'; ... <i>(1894,TrNZI,26/537</i>)

>  Our progress resolves into two periods, the transition of Polynesian into Maori and the transition of Maori into New Zealander. <i>(May 4, 1930,Na To Hoa Aroha,2/12,1987 ed.</i>) [NOTES: (Buck)]

>  The frequent use [following the Treaty of Waitangi] of the expression <i>nga Rangatira me nga Tangata maori </i>(with a small m) o Niu Tireni (the chiefs and people of New Zealand) appears to have been too cumbersome for official records and was quickly abbreviated to the one word Maori. In fact we may reasonably date at this period the elevation of Maori from a qualifying adjective to the status of a proper noun. <i>(1945,Journal of the Polynesian Society,54/233</i>) [NOTES: (E.H. Schnackenberg)]

>  <b>McLINTOCK</b> In the country proper the Maori has retained his traditional mode of life which is symbolised in the <i>marae</i>. In a literal sense the term means the open courtyard in front of the communal meeting house; today ... it embraces all aspects of community life - community buildings, tribal gatherings, church activities, and recreation. ... For the purpose of this article a Maori is a person having 50 per cent or more of Maori blood. <i>(1959,Descriptive Atlas,72</i>)

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> <i>Pa[a]nui ... 4</i>. n. <i>Public notice.</i> ... NOTE.- This is said, with some probability, to be a moden word invented by the Maoris in connection with their instruction by the early missionaries.: <i>(1971,Dictionary of the Maori Language,257</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>adj.</i> pertaining to Cook Islands Maori

>  <b>TREGEAR</b> ... the natives of Rarotonga and Tahiti call themselves `Maori', as the New Zealander does. <i>(1904,Maori Race, The,555</i>)

>  Indeed we almost doubt if King Ned knows of the existence of the Cook Islands. Still so long as the Maori inhabitants swear allegiance to him, it's all Sir Garnett. <i>(Oct 10, 1905,NZ Truth,17/5/7</i>)

>  Security of tenure was needed before any scheme to resuscitate the citrus industry could be commenced with successful prospects. Recommendations were therefore made to enable one member of the family to have, by communal aggreement in the Native Land Court, a definite area of the land held within the <i>kopu tangata</i>. Consequent upon such an agreement the occupier was confirmed as the owner under Maori custom and became capable of pledging the freehold to the administration as security for financial and material advances. <i>(Oct 1951,NZ Geographer,127</i>) [NOTES: (Johnston `Citrus Industry of the Cook Islands')]



 
<b>4</b> <i>adj.</i> chiefly pejorative derogatory or offensive, to refer to anything inferior or uncivilised in construction, appearance, or style; or connoting (low) cunning. Chiefly in loose combinations merging with compounds. [cf OEDS sb (a) 1 a. Add: also as a term of contempt - check: THE MAORI = Maori blood 1967 Sargeson Hangover iv 30 see MAORI DOG, PT; in some uses approaches the sense of modern CLAYTON'S e.g. MAORI TACKLE, MAORI P.T. [ca1938 (Havelock) To keep a Maori house ("be a dirty housewife") a real Maori cow, (a lean, ill-thrifty animal)]

>  My objection was this, June and July being mid winter and the road being in its Maori state, we could not depend on getting in to Ahuriri or Auckland on the fixed day, and that a bad commencement would bring up a bad report on the line of road. <i>(Jun 23, 1857,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,1/279,1960 ed.</i>) [NOTES: (J.Morgan, Otawhao)]

>  [a Maori dog; hen (=domestic fowl), Maori manners] <i>(1945,Journal of the Polynesian Society,54/231</i>) [NOTES: (S.J. Baker)]

>  <b>BAKER</b> <i>Maori</i> .... [in] its slightly contemptuous adjectival use, as in <i>a Maori dog, a Maori garden, Maori manners</i>. Here the term is employed to signify something unkempt, rather disordered or wild, something a little `uncivilized'. <i>(1941,NZ Slang,44</i>)

>  <b>BENNETT</b> The word <i>Maori </i>itself is occasionally used as a term of contempt-as in `That's a Maori ...' (of a thing). <i>(1943,English as it is spoken in NZ,93</i>)

>  <b>BEAGLEHOLE</b> Again, the word Maori for these [Otaki] people has taken on a secondary meaning when it is used as an adjective referring to any object that is broken, dilapidated, hard to use, or about which people are so casual that it does not function efficiently. Thus a `Maori' door is one on which the catch is broken or which fits so badly that it blows open in the wind. ... A `Maori' gate is always a weird contraption made of bits of wire, string, parts of an old iron bedstead - something so complicated that it defies the ingenuity of the non-expert either to open or close. The owner of the gate jokes about his `Maori' gate. He is rarely sufficiently concerned ever to make a simple workable gate. <i>(1946,Some Modern Maoris,295</i>)

>  <b>FRANCES</b> Maori dumps,' he said. `You can always tell a Maori dump anywhere!' <i>(1964,Johnny Rapana,18</i>)

>  <b>DUGGAN</b> Maori girls, Maori farms, Maori housing: you'd only to hear my father put tongue to any or all of that to know where he stood, solid for intolerance, mac, but solid. <i>(1981,Collected Stories,200</i>) [NOTES: 1963 Duggan `Along Rideout Road ...' in 1981 Collected Stories 200]

>  <b>DAVIN</b> <i>(1970,Not Here, Not Now,194</i>)

>  It's [a cartion strip] perpetuating the idea people have of something as being a real Maori trick, or a real Maori way of doing things. It's derogatory. <i>(Sep 24, 1972,Sunday Times (NZ),47/2</i>)

>  (b) Used colloquially as the equivalent of makeshift, unkempt, uncultivated, etc., e.g., <i>a Maori dog, a Maori hen,</i> (a fowl, not the <i>weka), a Maori onion, Maori manners,</i> etc. <i>(1945,Journal of the Polynesian Society,54/231</i>) [NOTES: (Baker)]

>  <b>MEREDITH</b> He (Wi) [a blind Maori] told me that ... he rode from Mawhia to Gisborne, a distance of about twenty-five miles, on a mere Maori bridle track, by himself. <i>(1875,Adventuring in Maoriland in the Seventies,103,1935 ed.</i>)

>  <b>HILLIARD</b> `Where you from?' ... `Up country. Taranaki say'. `Do you have to make your own tracks to get there?' `Just about.' `Same up home. I'm East Coast.' [218] They kidded each other in a way that would have been offensive with <i>pakehas</i>. When Shirley put a cigarette-butt down the sink ... Netta would say, `That's a real Maori trick, that one! Real East Coast!' <i>(1960,Maori Girl,73</i>)



 
<b>4</b> <i>adj.</i> distinct from the domesticated species'; or occasionally with mildly derogatory connotations `as used by Maoris; fit only for Maori use'. Maori blowfly, cabbage, celery, onion, parsnip, ?peach



 
<b>4</b> <i>adj.</i> `indigenous' as in Maori aniseed, hen, holly, lawyer, vine

>  [=native aj; or ?collocated Maori Kingfisher] Take, for instance, the common Maori kingfisher <i>(Halcyon vagans</i>=Kotaretare). <i>(1897,TrNZI,29/140</i>) [NOTES: (Colenso, Presidential Address)]

>  Maori used as an adjective synonymous with <i>native </i>except that it seems slightly contemptuous. We admire native bush, pasture or game; but Maori hens (woodhens), Maori cabbage [European cabbage run wild, A Wall] and Maori onion (Chrysobactron hookeri] are caricatures of their English namesales. <i>(1933,Press, The</i>) [NOTES: Acland]

### Maori (race) meeting
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Twenty-six speelers went to a Maori race meeting at Otaki a day or two ago, but the brown brother refused to let them play. <i>(Mar 5, 1887,Auckland Weekly News,17</i>)

>  Some years ago part of the seasonable festivities consisted of a truly Maori meeting at Orakei on Christmas Day, all the officials and most of the owners and jockeys being natives. <i>(Jan 29, 1910,NZ Truth,6</i>)

### Maori All Black
 
 <i>n.</i> a member of the national all-Maori All Black rugby team

>  Surely Mr Hirsh [sic] should have a look at Maori All Blacks, Maori golf tournaments, Maori tennis tournaments etc, which discriminate against white people. <i>(May 23, 1986,Dominion,14</i>)

>  Thus he [Ngata] was an active supporter of Maori rugby, both on a tribal level, through the competition for the Prince of Wales trophy, and on a national level, through the Maori All Blacks. <i>(1986,Na To Hoa Aroha,1/33</i>) [NOTES: Sorrenson]

>  Hewitt rated the Maori All Blacks alongside the All Blacks as the best team he had ever been involved with .. "I felt the victory over the 2005 Lions ensured the Maori All Blacks had a place of prominence in New Zealand again." <i>(Nov 28, 2006,Whakatane Beacon,12</i>)

### Maori and Pakeha
 
 <i>n.</i> a children's game

>  <b>PARKER</b> [Taranaki, c1870s-1880s] Before Buffalo Bill gave the children of the world the game of Cowboys and Indians ..., my brothers and I had found a much more imaginative outlet for our youthful exuberance. We played Maori and Pakeha. <i>(1987,Not to Yield,128</i>)

### Maori anise
 
 <i>n.</i> Angelica montana

>  <b>LAING & BLACKWELL</b> <i>Angelica montana (Maori Anise)</i>. ... It is greedily eaten by sheep, and though originally very abundant, is often almost exterminated by them in accessible places. The plant is known to shepherds and runholders as <i>Aniseed. </i> <i>(1940,Plants of NZ,342</i>)

>  <b>MARTIN</b> A third species of Angelica (<i>A. montana</i>), the MAORI ANISE, was at one time an abundant herb ... It has a very strong scent of aniseed. <i>(1961,Flora NZ,288,1929 ed.</i>)

### Maori apple
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  First "kosher", now "Maori" apples challenge Enza statutory monopoly... Aotearoa Horticulture has applied to the export permits committee for consents to ship Maori apples to a variety of destinations. <i>(Nov 22, 2000,Independent Business Weekly,1</i>)

### Maori assessor
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Mr Turton is also of the opinion that it will be adviseable [sic] to appoint Taati te Waru as a Maori assessor. <i>(Aug 29, 1859,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,1/486,1960 ed.</i>) [NOTES: (J. Morgan)]

### Maori axe
 
 <i>n.</i> a pre-European stone adze

>  <b>AYSON</b> Lying near the ovens were a good many Maori axes and spear heads in a perfect state of preservation. <i>(1937,Thomas,82</i>)

>  <b>OWEN & PERKINS</b> He came home..bringing exotic things back like little gold nuggets in bottles and Maori axes and kea feathers. <i>(1986,Speaking for Ourselves,26</i>)

### Maori basher
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Would it, then, really be possible for non-Maori MPs to speak equally freely without being labelled "politically incorrect Maori bashers"? I think not. <i>(Oct 7, 2000,Listener (New Zealand Listener),7</i>)

### Maori basket
 
 <i>n.</i> a kete or kit, a basket plaited from New Zealand flax

>  <b>SEWELL</b> Our ball attire sent over in Maori Baskets. <i>(1853,Journal,1/366,1980 ed.</i>)

>  <b>HAYTER</b> The common way [of cooking is] to suspend a Maori basket, or `kit', as it is termed, containing the article to be cooked, in a boiling pool. <i>(1874,Notes of a Tour in NZ,12</i>)

>  <b>KNIGHT</b> [Reference Porirua 1850s] Father went over his beloved books, ... putting what he must leave in a Maori basket, sent two of us to carry the basket to Miss Cowper and to say `with our father's compliments'. <i>(1975,Poyntzfield,30</i>) [NOTES: c1920s Eliza McKenzie in <i>Outlook</i> in 1975 Knight Poyntzfield 30]

>  <b>GUTHRIE</b> ... he produced a Maori basket with a day-old <i>kuri</i> (dog) inside! <i>(1930,NZ Memories,162</i>)

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> The last time Mr Kennard saw him he was sitting on the roadside fast asleep, his Maori basket beside him. <i>(1939,First White Boy Born in Otago,33</i>)

### Maori Battalion
 
 <i>n.</i> the Maori unit (Pioneer Battalion) of the New Zealand Expeditionary Force in World War I.

>  <i>The August of 1915. A Story of the Maori Battalion</i> By MAJOR F.WAITE, D.S.O., N.Z.E. <i>(Sep 10, 1921,Quick March,23</i>)

>  <b>SORENSON</b> When the Maori Battalion was sent to the Western Front in 1916 Buck was granted his request to transfer to combat duty; he was promoted to second-in-command of the Battalion with the rank of major. <i>(1986,Na To Hoa Aroha,1/29</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> the 28th (Maori) Battalion, the Maori unit of 2NZEF in WWII

>  <b>Ihimaera</b> I watch Laurence being greeted by his fellow friends from the home - and his genuine surprise that a couple of his Maori Battalion mates are there... <i>(2007,Ask the Posts,125</i>)

>  <b>JACKSON</b> The Maori Battalion staged their never-to-be-forgotten raid on the enemy. <i>(1943,Passage to Tobruk,54</i>)

>  <b>BURTON</b> This difficulty will be greatly accentuated ... as his new found desire for life, symbolised in the Maori Battalion, battles with the old inferiority and frustration <i>(1945,In Prison,123</i>)

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> <i>`Kia Ora,' said the Italian [P.O.W.] guards ... </i>Politeness indeed, ... the Maori Battalion must have passed this way. ... `Kia Ora.' This didn't seem to satisfy the guards, however. ... We soon found out they were saying <i>`Che ora?</i>, (What is the time?) <i>(1945,Gunner Inglorious,52</i>)

>  On a large [52] grassed <i>marae</i> behind the store are two meeting-houses. You may be lucky enough to see a <i>hui</i> in progress. Perhaps a welcome home to one of the many Ruatoki boys who are serving with the Maori Battalion. <i>(1948,Our Own Country,51</i>)

>  <b>BEAGLEHOLE</b> Again the training of Maori recruits for the Maori Battalian was organized round geographical-tribal units, ... <i>(1946,Some Modern Maoris,289</i>)

>  <b>HILLIARD</b> War was declared. Several Matiti men volunteered for service with the Maori Battalion and there was a three-day <i>hui.</i> [fn] <i>Hui</i> - assembly with feasting. <i>(1960,Maori Girl,44</i>)

>  Mr H.W. Herewini, a former officer of the 28 Maori Battalion .... <i>(Jun 20, 1963,Dominion,6</i>)

>  <b>GLOVER</b> He later became a commander of the Maori Battalion. <i>(1981,Hot Water Sailor,35,1962 ed.</i>)

>  <b>FINLAYSON</b> `The big land scheme for the Maori Battalion men'. In the second world war Maoris served willingly, without the distrust that marked their attitude to fighting for the pakeha in the first world war. Maori soldiers served in the 28th Battalion (`the Maori Battalion') in North Africa, Crete, and Italy. <i>(1973,Brown Man's Burden,138</i>) [NOTES: 1973 Pearson (ed.) in Finlayson Brown Man's Burden 138 [note 20 to p 112]]

### Maori bed
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> The rest of the hut was taken up by a platform, known as a "Maori bed", to hold four men. <i>(1969,Open Country Calling,224</i>)

### Maori block
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) an area of land owned by Maori but usually administered by the government.

>  <b>ANDERSON</b> It required great efforts to get confirmation of the Maori blocks ... <i>(1962,Life's Roundabout, A,62</i>)

>  <b>WOODHOUSE</b> In 1923 the Waikopiro Maori block was opened up for returned soldiers and the five sections were balloted for. This land was 'tapu-ed' by the Maoris on account of the massacre that had taken place there so many years before. <i>(1988,Tales of Pioneer Women,73</i>)

>  As each Maori block find [sic] its way into European hands, new districts will arise, which in time will lop off large limbs from the boasted grandeur of Auckland. <i>(Jun 15, 1866,Daily Southern Cross,5</i>)

>  A good deal of the timber up here is on selected land. The Government holds some 6000 acres, and there is a Maori block of 2000 acres. <i>(Jul 27, 1901,Poverty Bay Herald,4</i>)

>  <b>Richards</b> A mob of ordinary run cattle were mustered and driven over onto the Maori block with the hope that they would mix and entice the wild cattle out of the bush. <i>(1989,Pioneer's Life, A,48</i>)

>  .. it was this block that the settlers especially wished to have opened; on the Te Ohu block the owners were doing something; but on the Maori block practically nothing had been started. <i>(Sep 26, 1905,Bush Advocate,4</i>)

>  Reverting to 1862 again, the dwellers on the banks of the Buller camped on the Maori block, near what was afterwards known as Gladstone-street ... <i>(Feb 4, 1875,Nelson Evening Mail,4</i>)

### Maori blowfly
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>HAY</b> There is another of our insect enemies which must have special mention and that is the Maori blow-fly. <i>(1882,Brighter Britain,1/155</i>)

### Maori bluebell
 
 <i>n.</i> - [also native or New Zealand bluebell]

>  Dr Falla uses several times Native -, NZ -, Maori bluebell: found at Lake Rotoiti. <i>(Jan 28, 1953,Radio NZ / National Radio</i>) [NOTES: Nature Session]

>  Found at Lake Rotoiti (South Island), the Maori bluebell. <i>(Jan 28, 1953,Radio NZ / National Radio</i>) [NOTES: ca1953 Jan 28 2YA Wellington Nature Session (Dr Falla, Director Dominion Museum):]

### Maori bottom
 
 <i>n.</i> [miners']

>  <b>McKAY</b> It seems to be confined to a comparatively narrow run, which is at a considerable angle against the " Maori bottom," or "Old man reef." <i>(1894,Report on the Older Auriferous Drifts of C.Otago,48</i>)

>  ...over all the basin the upper members were completely shorn away, excepting a strip of `Maori bottom' along the foot of the Pisa Range ... [near Cromwell, Central Otago] <i>Ibid.</i> 33 On the other hand, the sandstone gravels - the `Maori bottom' of alluvial miners -, ... are absent at Banneckburn. <i>Ibid.</i> 42 <i>Sandstone Gravels </i>- These consist of yellowish known sandstones gravels, well rounded, and fairy uniform in size, the material seldom exceeds 6 in. in diameter, and is generally much decomposed. The gravels form the well-known `Maori bottom' of the Maniototo and Manuherikia basins. <i>(1908,NZ Geol. Survey Bulletin,No 5 (N.S.) 18</i>) [NOTES: (Park)]

>  The brownish quartz-sandstone gravels, locally [at Naseby] known as Maori Bottom, are seldom payably auriferous. <i>(1939,NZ Geol. Survey Bulletin,No 39 (N.S.) 108</i>)

>  There are no less than some five of six of these bottoms.. the intervening layers between each bottom being composed of white quartz and conglomerate boulders, intermixed with "Maoris," black sand, and school its detritus. <i>(Mar 6, 1890,Otago Witness,12</i>)

>  <b>McKAY (1897)</b> If driving or trenching across the up-turned edges of the strata had been done, the beds might thus have been proved from the "maori bottom" to the schist rock at comparatively small cost. <i>(1894,Report on the Older Auriferous Drifts of C.Otago,39</i>)

### Maori boundary
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a boundary formed of natural features such as ridges, creeks etc.

>  <b>FEARON</b> Original boundaries were 'Maori boundaries,' using natural features such as ridges, creeks etc <i>(1980,Te Wharau,176</i>)

### Maori boy
 
 <i>n.</i> (an often belittling term for) a Maori man

>  <b>FINLAYSON</b> `Eh, goody-goody all right for the pakeha,' said Monday. `This Maori boy means to have a good time.' <i>(1944,Brown Man's Burden,14,1973 ed.</i>) [NOTES: `Everlasting Miracle']

>  <b>SLATTER</b> The men stripped for action and the Maori boys singing as they work. <i>Ibid.</i> 99 `I've seen some very good Maori boys on the paddock. Real good boys. Winnin or losin. They got a flair for the game. ...' <i>(1959,Gun in My Hand, A,67</i>)

>  <b>SLATTER</b> In the [hash-house] alcoves the families in town for the day, ... the single solitary man who can always be found in a restaurant ... the Maori boys harmonising, their soft voices rising above the the monotonous juke box. <i>(1968,Pagan Game, The,165</i>)

>  <b>LATHAM</b> ... when the U.S. came into the war, there was a small influx sometime late in 1942. They made a few bloomers at first, but the old hands soon put them on the right [27] track about `their girls' and the same in the way they addressed the Maori boys. <i>(1986,WAAC Story, The,26</i>)

>  `D'ere d'hino,' said the Maori boy at the pool table. ... `D'ere d'hino' means literally `there is the oil'. It was originally used to describe orgasm. Now it just means anything that's good. <i>(Jan 1952,Here & Now,21</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> `Escort this man to the brig.' ... The Maori boy [sc. the prisoner] said, `I do what I like.' [141] Fourteen stone of burly Maori was sitting on a mess table. ... His eyes met mine. ... He was close to hysteria, this Maori boy. <i>(1956,Sweet White Wine,140</i>) [NOTES: [(WW2) a Maori]]

>  <b>SLATTER</b> I've seen some very good Maori boys on the paddock. Real good boys. Winnin or losin. They got a flair for the game. <i>(1959,Gun in My Hand, A,99</i>)

>  She was ... working the pump and filling handles four at a time for a bunch of Maori boys. <i>(1966,NZ Short Stories,86,1976 ed.</i>) [NOTES: ->1966 NZ Short Stories (1976) 86 (Wilson `It was Easter')]

### Maori brave
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>FEATON</b> The Maori braves, when they returned to their camp that night, no doubt round the whare fire, recounted to their admiring brethren the glorious deeds that they had done that day. <i>(1971,Waikato War,35</i>) [NOTES: Quotation from 1879 (Griffiths Collection).]

### Maori bread
 
 <i>n.</i> (Cuisine) a leavened bread also known as rewena, often with potato as a rising agent. [occ. rewana]

>  "A big plate," smiles Nick who cooks the kai at home every Friday while his wife Rangiora bakes the Maori bread they sell on Saturdays at the Porirua market. <i>(Sep 12, 1998,Dominion,15</i>)

>  The men were breaking off chunks of Maori bread, dipping it into a mound of grey-brown goo that I swear had just ceased pulsating, and washing the mouthfuls down with slurps of tea from big enamel mugs. <i>(Oct 20, 2001,Listener (New Zealand Listener),43</i>)

>  A tomahawk was picked up, a cloth cap with a bullet hole through the peak and a kit containing maori bread,[sic] a piece of a letter, two carpenters tools &c. <i>(Apr 14, 1860,Taranaki Herald,2</i>)

>  The Maori bread also, which had been cooked in the umus, and looked, smelled, and tasted very much like suety dumpling, was passed round and pronounced "Kapai!" [sic] <i>(Jul 2, 1902,Otago Witness,63</i>)

>  Here we have a cake of Maori bread, called punga punga. <i>(Jan 9, 1890,Otago Witness,17</i>)

>  <b>HUNT</b> (Marama, Kaikoura) ... a pakeha girl would see my crayfish wrapped in a bit of Maori bread in my kit and she'd yell out: `Look at your Maori dinner.' <i>(1985,I'm Ninety-five,98</i>)

>  <b>CRUMP</b> On a wet Sunday Mori baked a few rounds of Maori bread. They were about the same shape as the wooden cart-wheels in old drawings, and I doubt if they tasted much better. <i>(1960,Good Keen Man, A,46</i>)

>  <b>bax</b> just a knife to cut open/ the loaf of Maori bread <i>(1979,Collected Poems,517,1971 ed.</i>)

>  Before we left we called out to Mum, `Don't forget to make us a Maori bread for when we get back.' <i>(1975,NZ Short Stories III,186,1972 ed.</i>) [NOTES: Grace `A Way of Talking']

>  <b>FULLER</b> MAORI BREAD (REWANA PARAOA) (Paraoa is a Maori transliteration of the English word bread.) [Rewana leaven] <i>(1978,Maori Food and Cookery,25</i>)

>  <b>JACKSON</b> Have some bread. You like Maori bread? <i>(1988,Rainshadow,160</i>)

>  Maori bread (Makes 1 x 13cm loaf tin) <i>(Aug 23, 2003,Dominion Post,F5</i>)

### Maori bridge
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a log or fascine thrown across a river or stream.

>  Several Maori bridges have been thrown over the stream .. carried our saddles and bridles over the Maori bridge and crossed the horses above. <i>(Jan 16, 1858,Hawke's Bay Herald and Aruhihi Advocate,2</i>)

### Maori bug
 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Platyzosteria novae seelandiae</i>, black beetle, a native stink-roach, also called black beetle (See [kekerengu](../K#kekerengu))

>  <b>HYDE</b> The ordinary Maori bug is infamous for his odour, but the Spirits Bay bugs are of a size, a speed, a feminine curiosity, quite unparalleled in my poor experience. <i>(1934,Journalese,173</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> We used squashed Maori bugs as poison tips on arrows. <i>(1989,Kiwi Baby Boomers,56</i>)

>  <b>PARK</b> The white larrikins jammed on their flat hats and scurried out of there like Maori-bugs. <i>Ibid.</i> 177 ... rats or mice, cockroaches or Maori bugs, ... <i>(1951,Witch's Thorn, The,43</i>)

>  ... The term <i>Maori bunk</i> for a shelf that serves as a common bed for several people is obviously a New Zealand expression and in no doubt due to the Maori habit of having a common sleeping floor. How well known the term is outside tramping circles I do not know, but the device was not unknown in the inns of rural France, I understand. <i>(Sep 1958,Tararua,28</i>) [NOTES: Ruth Mason `Track and Trail']

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>Maori bunk</i> common bed for several people, usually trampers; not intended to be insulting. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,72</i>)

>  <b>TINNE</b> Fleas, Maori bugs, sand-flies and mosquitoes were bad enough ... <i>(1873,Wonderland of the Antipodes,52</i>)

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> <i>Kekereru</i>, n. <i>black wood-bug. </i> <i>(1892,Dictionary of the NZ Language,59</i>)

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> <i>Kekerengu, =kekereru</i>, n. <i>Periplaneta fortipes, black wood-bug, Maori bug. </i> <i>(1917,Dictionary of the Maori Language,133</i>)

>  <b>HULL</b> Generally speaking, a township [in North Auckland] consists of a `pub', a store, half-a-dozen scattered homesteads, and about thirty-nine million Maori bugs. The Maori bug is about half and inch by an inch, but his influence extends over about half and acre. <i>(1907,College Songs,46</i>)

>  <b>TREGEAR</b> The centipede (<i>were</i>), the caterpillar (<i>whe</i>), the Maori-bug (<i>kekerengu</i>), the birds <i>torea</i> ... and the ground lark (<i>hioi</i>: Anthus N.Z.), a sacred bird, were all supposed to have been brought in the canoes. <i>(1904,Maori Race, The,181</i>)

>  <b>GASCOYNE</b> The ground on which our camp was situated had been bush, and had plenty of logs and stumps on it, and the camp swarmed with `Maori bugs,' as the men called them. The Maori's name for these horrible insects is <i>kekerengu</i>. They are about one inch and a half long and resemble a black cockroach in appearance. <i>(1916,Soldiering in NZ,115</i>) [NOTES: [reference 1880's Parihaka]]

>  <b>BAUME</b> She would have kissed a spider or a Maori Bug. <i>(1933,Half Caste,209</i>)

>  <b>FAIRBURN</b> I pulled your last ... out of a cigar-box full of papers just now, and a Maori-bug crawled out of it. <i>(Jun 8, 1935,Letters,95,1981 ed.</i>)

>  <b>HYDE</b> ...the unlined walls of his shack ... let out neat dark-brown streams of Maori bugs. He had made the discovery that a Maori bug, like a skunk, knows all about self-preservation. If you tread on it, frighten it, ... [157] it lets out wave after wave of sickening-sweet stink. <i>(1938,Nor the Years Condemn,156</i>)

>  <b>VAILE</b> [Glossary] <i>Maori bug. Platyzosteria novae seelandiae: </i>native <i>kekerengu</i>. When crushed emits a dreadful stench. <i>(1939,Pioneering the Pumice,299</i>)

>  <b>ANDERSEN</b> Maori-bug or black wood-bug. <i>(1942,Maori Place Names,449</i>)

>  <b>POWELL</b> MAORI BUG <i>(Platyzosteria novaezelandiae</i>). The evil-smelling flat-backed black cockroach common throughout New Zealand. <i>(1947,Native Animals of NZ,46</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> Worse than seeing niggers with three eyes, or Maori bugs in gumboots, to watch a man step ... from the gritty floor of the sea. <i>(1957,Strip Jack Naked,7</i>)

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> <i>Kekerengu, ... Platyzosteria novaeseelandiae, black wood-bug</i>, or <i>stink-roach. </i> <i>(1957,Dictionary of the Maori Language,113</i>)

>  <b>GILLESPIE</b> ... in places the so-called Maori bug (<i>Platyzosteria novaeseelandiae</i>), and the tree weta (<i>Hemideina megacephala</i>), as well as species of stick insects. <i>(1958,South Canterbury,10,1971 ed.</i>)

>  There are very few cockroaches, the ones met with in habitations being exotic ones; the best known native species is the unpleasant stink-roach, often incorrectly called the `black beetle', and too frequently referred to as the `Maori bug' - a term that should be avoided because it is a name just as objectionable to the Maori as the insect's odour is to the Europeans who coined the name; however, it has been said that the term is a corruption of the original and inoffensive European name of `kauri bug'. <i>(1960,NZ Junior Encyclopaedia,1/351</i>) [NOTES: (D. Miller)]

>  A child hears phrases like ... `Black as a nigger', or hears ... a certain kind of beetle called a `Maori bug'.... <i>(Jul 15, 1963,Dominion,4</i>)

>  <b>McLINTOCK</b> cockroach, black beetle -->kekerengu <i>Platyzosteria novae-seelandiae </i> <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,3/705-712</i>)

>  BUG, MAORI (<i>Platyzosteria novae-zelandiae</i>). Maori bug is the commonly accepted name for the largest endemic a cockroach of New Zealand. ... All stages of the Maori bug are shining black. <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,1269</i>)

>  Platyzosteria soror looks rather like the native black cockroach, Platyzosteria novaeseelandiae, formerly known as the `Maori bug,' ... <i>(Oct 31, 1972,Evening Post,33/7</i>)

>  [The wasp] searches amongst the wi-wi, and the toe-toe for the black bug or Maori bug, kikereru, twice its size. <i>(Feb 26, 1924,Otago Witness,6</i>)

>  <b>GEE</b> Here goes : a broken bottle in the grass;.. wetas in the dunny; Maori-bugs, their stink;.. used frenchies under the bridge.. <i>(1992,Going West,6</i>)

### Maori bunk
 
 <i>n.</i> a bench-type bunk with divisions between sleepers, as in a tramper's hut. (trampers') -->(probably pejorative, based on the Maori custom of sleeping visitors together) ; ca1945 the upper section of a tramping hut where trampers sleep without division and higgledy-piggledy on bracken in one large bunk.

>  <b>PASCOE</b> They dug out a platform for the tent, lined the floor with rocks as a sleeping-bench or Maori bunk, and got stuck into another kea stew. <i>(1958,Great Days in NZ Mountaineering,156</i>)

>  ... The term <i>Maori bunk</i> for a shelf that serves as a common bed for several people is obviously a New Zealand expression and in no doubt due to the Maori habit of having a common sleeping floor. How well known the term is outside tramping circles I do not know, but the device was not unknown in the inns of rural France, I understand. <i>(Sep 1958,Tararua,28</i>) [NOTES: Ruth Mason `Track and Trail']

>  ...design for a hut. . ., to have a large open fire place and a bench type bunk (known in tramping circles as a Maori bunk), was approved. -->a large mattress or bunk base on which many people could sleep <i>Ibid.</i> 78 Two yards from the door stood the bunk house - two rooms with a Maori bunk, capable of sleeping a dozen, in each. <i>(1978,Manawatu Tramping Club Jubilee,17</i>) [NOTES: [reference 1932]]

### Maori burn
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>FRAME</b> And she twisted my arm once more, and caught at my wrist, giving me a vicious Chinese and Maori burn combined. <i>(1963,Reservoir, The,49</i>) [NOTES: (Stink-pot)]

### Maori cabbage
 
 <i>n.</i> [[get cits. from Maori: eg 1898 Morris].]

>  The children were sent out to gather sow- thistle, and what they called Colonial Cabbage. [Note] "Colonial" or "Maori Cabbage" resembled rape and was plentiful in the early days. <i>(1988,Tales of Pioneer Women</i>) [NOTES: Writing of 1940]

>  Maori cabbage is as nutritious and tastes well.. as does its European cousin. <i>(Aug 12, 1943,NZ Farmers' Weekly,37</i>)

>  Interestingly, in the subantarctic the same plant [sc. Macquarie cabbage, <i>Stilbocarpa polaris</i>] has another name, Maori cabbage, which in New Zealand English means especially the sow thistle (Maori puha), and certainly not the subantarctic plant. 'Maori' here probably had the sense of 'growing wild, non-domesticated.. and was applied to the <i>Stilbocarpa</i> by early Macquarie residents. <i>(Aug 2000,NZWords,7</i>)

>  A woman from the pa.. was sitting on the rock beating Maori cabbage. <i>(Feb 19, 1947,NZ Observer,14</i>)

>  <b>McRAE</b> The meal that night would be a typical colonial one - boiled mutton and potatoes, Maori cabbage and sago pudding, washed down with strong tea and brown sugar. <i>(1963,By the Braes of Balquether,40</i>)

>  Those [kales] of less importancre which have gained no place of prominence are buda kale, rape kale, maori cabbage and hungry gap kale. <i>(1953,Crop Production Bulletin,No. 277 : 113</i>)

>  After some scratching around, Lauper found a few tiny potatoes and some Maori cabbage, but it was scant nourishment for two starving men. <i>(Jul 2000,NZ Geographic,No. 47/89</i>)

>  <i>Cabi.bage </i>(k&abreve.bi.ba.j), <i>n. [Add.] ... - Maori cabbage. (Bot)</i> See under MAORI. - <i>Native cabbage (Bot.)</i>, also, a New Zealand term for the Maori cabbage. <i>(1898,Merriam-Webster Third International,2019</i>)

>  What is known as `Maori cabbage' is here [among the Tuhoe] termed <i>paea. </i> <i>(1903,TrNZI,35/87</i>)

>  <i>cabbage, Maori [=] wild cabbage [s] </i> <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,10</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> sowthistle, =puha, =rauriki [(See the long quot. (1953 Otago Witness) at <i>rauriki</i>.) ca1935 Havelock, Marlborough Sowthistle called Maori Cabbage or <i>Puha (Puwha</i> the Maori word), and occasionally rauriki.]

>  <b>MONEY</b> ... a fine piece of bacon was quietly simmering ... with plenty of sow thistles or Maori cabbage to give it a flavour; <i>(1871,Knocking About in NZ,68</i>)

>  see RAURIKI [<i>Maori Cabbage</i> the name for <i>rauriki</i> among pakehas in the King Country `years ago"] <i>(Jan 7, 1953,Otago Star</i>) [NOTES: (I.W.T.Munro)]

>  <b>McCALLUM</b> [Glossary] <i>Puha</i>: Maori cabbage. <i>(1963,Half-Gallon Jar,98</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> wild cabbage (? orig. and chiefly Canterbury) a European turnip (or cabbage) <i>Brassica</i> run wild

>  <i>Ma&#769;.o-ri</i> (ma.&#769;.o.-re.),<i> n. & a. [Add.] </i> <i>Maori cabbage (Bot.),</i> the wild cabbage of New Zealand, descended from cabbages planted by Captain Cook. <i>(1898,Merriam-Webster Third International,2026</i>)

>  <b>KIRK</b> <i> Brassica oleracea </i> <i>(1899,Students' Flora</i>) [NOTES: (And) [?1889]]

>  <b>TREGEAR</b> The so-called `Maori cabbage' was introduced by Cook, and was not an indigenous plant. <i>(1904,Maori Race, The,102</i>)

>  .. F.W.Stubbs ... recorded sixty-two years later [than 1852] in a <i>Timaru Herald </i>memoir what it used to be like in South Canterbury in those hardy times. ... Wild pork, Maori cabbage, damper and tea were the staple diet. <i>(1914,Timaru Herald</i>) [NOTES: 1914 F.W.Stubbs in Timaru Herald in 1971 Ogilvie Moonshine Country 26 .]

>  <b>PFAFF</b> ... they used up everything, and had to live on shellfish and Maori cabbage, ... <i>(1914,Diggers' Story, The,76</i>)

>  An old settler tells me that the `Maori cabbage' was simply a degenerate swede turnip. The Leaves were turnip Leaves; the body was a thin wiry root and unetable - it was the leaves whish were eaten. <i>(1920,TrNZI,52/73</i>) [NOTES: (Beattie, ? Canterbury)]

>  <b>HILGENDORF</b> WILD CABBAGE (<i>Brassica oleracea</i>), often called Maori cabbage, is a rape-like annual found on sea cliffs in both islands. <i>(1926,Weeds of New Zealand,93,1967 ed.</i>)

>  <i>Maori.</i>- Used as an adjective synonymous with<i> native</i>, except that it seems slightly contemptious. We admire native bush, pasture, or game; but <i>M.</i> hens (woodhens), <i>M.</i> cabbage [the European cabbage run wild--A.W.], and <i>M.</i> onion (Chrysobactron bookeri) are caricatures of their English namesakes. ([1898 Morris Austral-English]) <i>(Nov 4, 1933,Press, The,15</i>)

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> Along some rivers [in Otago in the 1860s] Maori cabbage grew and formed a welcome addition to the meagre vegetable supplies of the earliest incomers. ... Speaking of the so-called Maori cabbage, Mr Kennard remarked: `Up along the Kawa-rua [old pronunciation of Kawarau] between Cromwell and the Gorge there was Maori cabbage on the sandbanks, a sign that the Maoris had been there. It was like rape. When T.B.Nelson had Mount Royal he sowed rape with young grass, and an enterprising man put it in Maori kits and sold it in Palmerston as Maori cabbage. <i>(1939,First White Boy Born in Otago,123</i>)

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> Pora is an introduced vegetable run wild. The word `pora' means a ship. The plant flourished up the rivers, and the first settlers called it `Maori cabbage'. The lists also call it Pora-kawa-kawa, but this is amalgamating two names, for the kawakawa is another vegetable run wild. <i>(1945,Maori Place-Names of Canterbury,65</i>)

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> Near the New River there was Maori cabbage [51] up to the horse's belly. <i>(1947,Pioneer Recollections,50,1956 ed.</i>)

>  <b>MACFARLANE</b> On the better and warmer land [in North Canterbury] there was Maori cabbage, very palatable and completely eaten out by the first cattle. This cabbage was brought over from the islands by the Maoris for their own tables, and then went wild in many places [!!! the figure and reference] <i>(1954,Te Raka,40</i>)

>  <i>maori cabbage n usu cap M </i>: the wild cabbage of New Zealand <i>(1961,Merriam-Webster Third International,1379</i>)

>  <i>Maori-cabbage n</i>. New Zealand wild cabbage (from seed left by Capt Cook). <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Whitcombe's)</i>)

>  <i>Maori cabbage [=] wild cabbage</i> [s] <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,46</i>)

>  <b>FULLER</b> Paea, says Elsdon Best, was the name for a Maori cabbage. Apparently the seed was obtained from some early European explorer. <i>(1978,Maori Food and Cookery,8</i>)

>  By the time I returned to the pah I ... was out of provisions, indeed had already experienced the nutritious qualities of maori cabbage and fernroot. <i>(Aug 16, 1849,Torlesse Papers,94,1958 ed.</i>)

>  <b>33/11</b> . ... A plant called the `Maori Cabbage'. Gathered some of the leaves for cooking. ... I believe this plant is a turnip which has degenerated for want of proper cultivation, so that the roots are too small to be of much use, but the tops are excellent boiled as greens. It is supposed to have been introduced by Captain Cook. .... Its appearance is always a sure sign that a Maori encampment has at some former time occupied the site. <i>(1852,Australian & NZ Gazette,1852 ed.</i>) [NOTES: 1851 Aust and NZ Gazette (1852) 33/11 (Journal Canterbury Settler)]

>  <b>W H S ROBERTS</b> Writing of the Waimea Plain in 1856: - `The ground was well grassed, but the speargrass was too plentiful. The pasturage was very good. ... we camped in a bend with good feed at the Elbow and a swamp on one side. Maori cabbage abounded ...' <i>(1856,Diary</i>) [NOTES: 1856 W.H.S. Roberts Diary in 1947 Beattie Early Runholding 18]

>  <b>HARPER</b> The natives live entirely upon fish, birds, fern-root, and Maori Cabbage. <i>(1857,Journal,3/10</i>) [NOTES: (1857 Journal Leonard Harper (First Crossing of Southern alps)) 1921 NZ Alpine Journal 3/10]

>  <b>PASCOE</b> I then took an old spade ... but found only a handful of very small potatoes, and another handful of Maori cabbage. <i>(1960,Over the Whitcombe Pass,47</i>) [NOTES: 1863 Lauper in 1960 Pascoe (ed) Over the Whitcombe Pass 47]

>  <b>BUTLER</b> The only plant good to eat is Maori cabbage, and that is swede turnip gone wild, from seed left by Captain Cook. Some say it is indigenous, but I do not believe it. <i>(1863,First Year in Canterbury Settlement,ix/31</i>)

>  <b>MUELLER</b> - diggers cannot live on mussels, fern-tree tops and Mauri cabbage, and work hard all day. <i>(Dec 1, 1865,My Dear Bannie,78,1958 ed.</i>)

>  The leaves of several smaller plants were also used as vegetables [by the ancient Maoris]; but the use of these in modern times, or during the last forty or fifty years, was commonly superceded by that of the extremely useful and favourite plant - the Maori cabbage, <i>Brassica oleracea</i>, introduced by Cook (nani of the Maoris at the north, and rearea at the south), of which they carefully sowed the seeds. [<i>Williams: nani Brassica campestris</i>, wild cabbage, wild turnnip (Modern). <i>Rearea:</i> 2. Leaves of <i>korau</i> and other greens used as food] <i>(1880,TrNZI,13/31</i>) [NOTES: (Colenso)]

>  ... we see this [reversion to a wild original] in the so-called Maori cabbage, so common near Native settlements, and which has descended from the plants which sprang from the seed given to the Natives by Captain Cook. <i>(Mar 1885,NZ Journal of Science,365</i>)

>  <b>MORRIS</b> <i>Maori-Cabbage, </i>the wild cabbage of New Zealand, <i>Brassica spp.</i>, N.O. <i>Cruciferae</i>, said to descended from the cabbages planted by Captain Cook. [1855. Rev. R. Taylor, `Te Ika a Maui,' p. 206.] <i>(1898,Austral English,285</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) Brassica oleracea or kale.

>  It was considered by many that the maori cabbage and rape were practically identical ... but rape grows with a tap root, while the Maori cabbage has fibrous roots. <i>(Feb 17, 1906,Farmers' Union Advocate,10</i>)

>  <b>MACFARLANE</b> One the better and warmer land there was a Maori cabbage, very palatable and completely eaten out by the first catle. <i>(1954,Te Raka,40</i>)

>  <b>BUTLER</b> The only plant good to eat is Maori cabbage and that is Swede Turnip gone wild, from seed left by Captain Cook. <i>(1863,First Year in Canterbury Settlement,131</i>)

>  <b>MONEY</b> .. with plenty of sow thistles or Maori cabbage to give it a flavour ... <i>(1871,Knocking About in NZ,68</i>)

>  Those [kales] of less importance which have gained no place of prominence are buda kale, rape kale, Maori cabbage and hungry gap kale. <i>(1953,Department of Agriculture Report,113</i>) [NOTES: Dept of Ag Crop Production 277]

>  <b>MCRAE</b> The meal that night would be a typical colonial one - boiled mutton and potatoes, Maori cabbage and sago pudding, washed down with strong tea and brown sugar. <i>(1963,By the Braes of Balquether,40</i>)

 <i>n.</i>

 <i>n.</i> (offensive) a blunder at billiards - where striker's white hits (say) the red and knocks it against the other white; or ? where, in making a cannon, one opponent's white is sunk. [ca1948 (R. Mason)]

 <i>n.</i> an old or decrepit motor vehicle

 <i>n.</i>

It was purely a reaction to the demands of the Maori caucus. <i>(May 27, 2000,Listener (New Zealand Listener),34</i>)

 <i>n.</i>

 <i>n.</i> (roast) weka

### Maori chief
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Two hours' fishing produced red cod, blue cod, flat-head or Maori chief.. and crayfish. <i>(Mar 9, 1922,Auckland Weekly News,15</i>)

>  <b>AYLING</b> <i>MAORI CHIEF>/i> (Black cod) <i>Notothenia angustata (Notothenia microlepidota) </i>... The maori chief, often named the black cod, is a large bottom-living fish similar in shape to the blue cod ... These fish are common ... around the south of the South Island ... <i>Ibid.</i> 276 <i>BLACK COD</i> (Black notothenid, maori chief) <i>Notothenia magellanica</i> ... The black cod is similar to the maori chief but has a deeper, less rounded body ... and does not reach such a large size. ... Black cod are common in inshore rocky areas south of Cook Strait ... <i>(1982,Collins Guide,275,1984 ed.</i>)

>  species name] maori chief .. [scientific name] -- [other name(s)] May be MARBLEFISH [Aplodactylus arctidens] or in southern waters could be Notothenia sp. <i>(Jul 23, 1986,Codes for Species in NZ Waters</i>)

>  <b>PAUL et al.</b> [index to common names 283-293] Maori chief 1165-6, 1869-79 1. <i>Notothenia maoriensis</i> (or<i> M. corciceps</i>) and <i>N. p lepidotera. </i> <i>(1987,Fishes: A Bibliography</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> MAORI CHIEF Mottled which resemble a tattoo give this unusual fish its name. <i>(1981,Fisherman's Bible,164</i>)

>  Some odd fishes now and then turn up in the market, such as the Maori-chief, cat-fish etc ... <i>(1877,TrNZI,10/330</i>) [NOTES: (Thomson)]

>  That very dark-skinned fish, the Maori-chief, <i>Notothenia maoriensis</i> of Dr. Haast, is not uncommon but is rarely seen more than one at a time <i>(1878,TrNZI,11/381</i>) [NOTES: (P. Thomson, Otago)]

>  <b>SHERRIN</b> <i>Notothenia coriiceps</i> Rich. Flat-head, or Maori Chief <i>(1886,Handbook Fishes of NZ,301</i>)

>  18. <i>Cape Saunders</i>. - Trumpeter, Maori chief <i>(1892,TrNZI,24/204</i>)

>  <b>HYDE</b> Nothing bit, except one of the crested fish called Maori Chiefs... The Maori Chiefs have warlike golden-brown faces and beautiful black eyes. <i>(1937,Wednesday's Children,242</i>) [NOTES: 1989 edition]



 
 <i>n.</i> a variety of potato

>  An excellent return of potatoes has been secured from "Maori Chief" seed. <i>(Jan 23, 1917,Levin Daily Chronicle,2</i>)

>  'Arran Banner' has been grown over the years, but like 'Maori Chief' it grew in the bag too soon. <i>(Aug 1991,NZ Gardener,30</i>)

>  [Advt] Seed Potatoes... Maori Chief (a great blight resister and a heavy cropper). <i>(Sep 6, 1916,Wairarapa Daily,1</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) a blight-resistant strain of potato.

>  Maori Chief is the best blight-resister we have. <i>(Sep 20, 1916,NZ Journal of Agriculture,250</i>)

### Maori chord
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  He plays Maori chords, the ones that Pakeha can never grasp when they try to sit in with the man on the guitar. <i>(Mar 9, 2000,Capital Times,19</i>)

 <i>n.</i> [ca1983 Herb Wilford Hastings second hand car dealers, for spotting chrome work with silver or other paint.]

### Maori church
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  It is a Maori Church of comparatively small pretensions, but is, I believe, the first Native-built Church in this land which has a chancel. <i>(Dec 1851,Report</i>) [NOTES: 1851 Dec 31 T.S.Grace Report of Turanga District in Grace Pioneer Missionary (1928) 16]

>  It is a Maori Church of comparatively small pretensions, but is, I believe, the first Native-built Church in this land which has a chancel. <i>(Dec 31, 1851,Report,16</i>) [NOTES: 1851 Dec 31 T.S.Grace Report of Turanga District in Grace Pioneer Missionary (1928) 16]

### Maori clearing
 
 <i>n.</i> (Environment) an area cleared of bush or forest by Maori, and often used for food cultivation (particularly pre-European).

>  On the road we passed through a very large extent of old Maori clearing and remains. <i>(Apr 1, 1862,Wellington Independent,3</i>)

>  In an old Maori clearing a grove of peach trees thrived and splendid fruit was obtained from these trees. <i>(Jul 12, 1929,Evening Post,9</i>)

>  Mr Thomas McKenzie arrived by the ship Adelaide, on March 7th, 1840, and he says the bush then ran down to the water's edge, at what is now Wellington, though there was a Maori clearing used as a potato garden up on a terrace. <i>(Jan 30, 1890,Colonist, The,4</i>)

>  .. as we entered the forest we found securely sheltered by its multitude of lofty mornarchs [sic], a Maori clearing of some acres extent in a creditable state of cultivation ... <i>(Apr 5, 1854,Taranaki Herald,4</i>)

>  .. on which side of the Waiwakaiho it was difficult to say, but it [the smoke] was thought to be from an old Maori clearing on this side. <i>(Feb 6, 1864,Taranaki Herald,2</i>)

### Maori concert party
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  [caption] Maori child wearing a piupiu, typical costume worn nowadays by Maori concert parties. <i>(1971,NZ Heritage,1/74</i>)

### Maori Congress
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  This Maori Congress carry-on serves the rest of us Maori served up on a silver platter. <i>(Feb 23, 1993,Evening Post,4</i>)

### Maori Contingent
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [Maori Battalion](../M#maori-battalion),[Pioneer Battalion](../P#pioneer-battalion))

>  <b>AITKEN</b> Here [at Moaskar Camp, Egypt 1915-1916] the Maori Contingent gave a lead in resourceful ingenuity, and we were content to copy them. <i>(1963,Gallipoli to the Somme,45</i>)

>  After 1914, Rua's lack of enthusiasm towards Tuhoe recruitment for the Maori Contingent started rumours which believed him to be an ally of the Germans. <i>(1983,Land of the Mist,33</i>)

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> When the [Great] War was over, men of the Maori Contingent were still landless despite their war service. <i>(1963,Ratana,16,1972 ed.</i>)

### Maori copper
 
 <i>n.</i> copper Maori

>  <b>HEBERLEY</b> The Natives took a cask of lime, and thought it was cask of flour, they went to work and made dumplings of it and naked them in what is termed a Maori Copper. <i>(1878,Autobiography,31</i>) [NOTES: [1830s]]

>  We roasted half a sheep in a Maori copper, that is first dig a hole in the ground in proportion to the thing you want to cook. Line the bottom and sides with stones as big as a breakfast cup then light a fire over them till they are quite red hot throwing in more loose stones to put on the top. Then take out all the ashes, wrap your meat in green grass put it in the pit, throw the loose stones on top and cover the whole with sods of grass, clay or anything that will keep the steam in, three or four hours will cook your meat to perfection. <i>(Oct 7, 1864,Chudleigh Diary,147,1950 ed.</i>)

>  <b>POATA</b> They must be cooked off a tapu ground in the Maori copper only, custom forbidding any other method. <i>(1919,Maori as a Fisherman, The,10</i>)

### Maori Council
 
 <i>n.</i> a Maori governing body [name given to various governing bodies from the time of the abolition of provinces]

>  A Maori Council consists of members met at haphazard,[sic] without commission to consult, and without authority to give effect to their decrees. <i>(Jul 26, 1862,Daily Southern Cross,4</i>)

>  Embarrassed by this contradiction of its myth-making, the government consulted its in-house quango, the Maori Council. <i>(Aug 16, 1986,Listener (New Zealand Listener),95</i>)

>  The New Zealand Maori Council was a far more conservative organisation in the 1960s and early 1970s than Harrison portrays it... <i>(Dec 14, 2002,Listener (New Zealand Listener),58</i>)

>  "The only message they are getting from a pitiful bunch of leaders like the Maori Council is you are entitled to the airwaves and the bloody oil and everything else that's going ... you are entitled to it..." <i>(Jul 5, 2000,Evening Post,23</i>)

>  The Maori Council cannot give the same advantages as the Government. <i>(Jul 30, 1904,NZ Observer,3</i>)

>  Mr Takamaoana said Maoris were not sufficiently represented, and if not more than four were to be in the next Parliament, he should advise his people to send none, and leave the Pakeha Council to the Pakeha alone. <i>(Aug 20, 1875,West Coast Times,2</i>)

>  The passing of the Maori Councils Act of 1900 ... The Maori Council is a step forward in the direction of progress ... <i>(1903,TrNZI,35/180</i>)

>  <b>bau</b> Besides, who will take leases for 21 years with a Maori council title? <i>(1905,Where the White Man Treads,273</i>)

>  <b>SORENSON</b> But Ngata replied, as he did to numerous such invitations in later years, by saying that he could not leave New Zealand because of political commitments. He had to prepare legislation for the forthcoming session. `You wouldn't have the heart to entice me away from a prospect so alluring! Compare washing up bills with banana groves, Maori Councils with your lotus-eaters!' <i>(1986,Na To Hoa Aroha,1/27</i>)

>  Taiaroa's resolutions in favour of a Maori Council for the Middle Island stand a better chance, as being the first motion ever made by a Maori member ... <i>(Sep 2, 1871,Otago Witness,5</i>)

>  When the pakeha [sic] Council devised a scheme it should be sent to the Maori Council for their approval or rejection; and the same with the Maori Council - all their plans should be submitted to the parkeha [sic] Council. <i>(Jan 13, 1870,Daily Southern Cross,4</i>)

>  We now wish to direct attention to the striking contrast it affords to the whole proceedings of the late Maori Council, which bears upon its surface the stamp of sincerity and truth. <i>(Jan 17, 1863,Taranaki Herald,4</i>) [NOTES: From <i> The Hawke's Bay Times </i> November 18.]
