### Maori difficulty
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [cf. native difficulty](../N#native-difficulty),[native problem](../N#native-problem))

>  <b>MANING</b> Right down to the present time talking of "Young New Zealand," and within a hair'sbreadth of settling "the Maori difficulty" without having been paid for it, which would have been a great oversight, and contrary to the customs of New Zealand. <i>(1863,Old New Zealand,105</i>) [NOTES: Auckland edition)]

### Maori disease
 
 <i>n.</i> = mate Maori

>  <b>BROOKER et al.</b> In individual ailments, if the patient did not derive immediate relief from the medicine prescribed by the doctor, the patient and the family concluded that the failure to respond was due to the sickness being a Maori disease (mate Maori) and beyond the white doctor's skill. <i>(1981,NZ Medicinal Plants,19</i>) [NOTES: 1949 Buck Coming of Maori 548 in 1981 Brooker et al. NZ Medicinal Plants 19]

### Maori dock
 
 <i>n.</i> Rumex flexuosus

>  <b>HILGENDORF</b> MAORI DOCK (<i>Rumex flexuosus</i>) is called also native dock, New Zealand dock, and rolling dock. It occurs throughout both islands on native and artificial pastures, showing no preference for moist country. <i>(1926,Weeds of New Zealand,65,1967 ed.</i>)

>  <i>Maori dock</i> [s] <i>Rumex flexuosus</i> Sol. <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,46</i>)

>  <b>GIBBS</b> <i>... L. boldenarum</i> eggs and larvae have also been found on the native `Maori dock' (<i>Rumex flexuosus,</i> Polygonaceae) ... <i>(1980,NZ Butterflies,159</i>)

### Maori doctor
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>CRAWFORD</b> By a `Maori doctor' I mean a person who professes, from knowledge of the language and customs of the Maoris, and from personal influence among them, to have exclusive power of managing them [e.g. Grey, Donald McLean] <i>(1880,Recollections of Travel in Aust. & NZ,314</i>)

### Maori dog
 
 <i>n.</i> = kuri [also cunning as a Maori dog see also kuri, peropero] (See [also cunning as a Maori dog](../A#also-cunning-as-a-maori-dog))

>  On a number of visits to Hauturu in the 1880s, the notorious tomb-robber and collector of rare bird skins Andreas Reischek reported Maori dogs and feral pigs wandering free all over the island <i>(Jan 2002,NZ Geographic,No. 55/23</i>)

>  I see no reason why the native Maori dog should not be in existence yet in some of the back mountain ranges. <i>(Nov 20, 1890,Otago Witness,23</i>)

>  The Kuri or Maori dog was very much like our own Pomeranian... It was also known to the Maori as peropero. <i>(Apr 29, 1950,Southern Cross (Wgton),8</i>)

>  Along with the kuri, or Maori dog... the kiore was part of the first wave of introductions to this country. <i>(Mar 28, 1992,Evening Post,25</i>)

>  <b>BIDWILL & WOODHOUSE</b> The sheep.. were held close to the huts at night, because of the danger of being worried by Maori dogs. These dogs were originally brought by the Maoris from Polynesia, and somewhat resembled a fox, uttering the same cry. They did not bark like the European species. <i>(1927,Bidwill of Pihautea,7</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>HAAST</b> The measurements of the skulls ... agree very well with those of the ancient Maori dog given by Dr. Hector in Vol. IX of the `Transactions of the New Zealand Institute', page 248. <i>(1879,Geology of the Provinces of Canterbury & Westland,421</i>)

>  <b>TREGEAR</b> The old Maori dogs, nevermore to be seen by mortal eyes (they have all perished, but their hides are on the war-cloaks), were the true uncrossed descendants of the old cattle-dogs which guarded the herds of Arya on the Asiatic plains. <i>(1885,Aryan Maori,94</i>)

>  The `Maori' dog, now extinct, was only an European mongrel <i>(1889,New Zealand After 50 Years,85</i>)

>  The early voyagers and the first residents of New Zealand all agree in stating that the Maori dog was unknown in the wild state. It was a purely domesticated species ... valued ... for supplying the material for their much prized dogskin mats ... <i>(1894,TrNZI,26/219</i>)

>  <b>TREGEAR</b> The Maori dog (<i>Kuri ruarangi</i>) has now entirely disappeared ... <i>(1904,Maori Race, The,166</i>)

>  <b>REISCHEK</b> On the 25th I took leave of my kind host, who gave me a really valuable present - the skin of the extinct [77] Maori dog (<i>Canus maori</i>). This dog, yellow-red in colour, had pointed ears, bushy tail, and was about the size of a spaniel. <i>(1930,Yesterdays in Maoriland,76,1933 ed.</i>)

>  <b>HYDE</b> He left the door open, the firelight near her, a crouching golden dog; not rough-coated dirty white, like poor old <i> kuri</i>, the Maori dog, who has died out. <i>(1938,Nor the Years Condemn,136</i>)

>  kuri, Maori dog, brought with him from the Pacific; now applied to any mongrel: long extinct, and only surviving in the strips of skin with hair attached used as ornaments on the Maori valued cloaks (kaku-waero). <i>(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/153</i>)

>  <b>McLINTOCK</b> DOG, MAORI [title]. The Maori dog (kuri) was not indigenous to New Zealand ... It became extinct some years after the arrival of the European settlers. <i>Ibid.</i> 3/705-12 Maori Dog - kuri mohorangi, kuri ruarangi <i>Canis domesticus</i> <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,1/491</i>)

>  <b>DONNE</b> Kahu-kuri. - On side covered with long white or yellow hairs of the Maori dog. <i>(1927,Maori Past and Present,27</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  As we approached one of the Mauri dogs jumped over board and swam towards some rocks. <i>(Dec 9, 1840,Journal of Ensign Best,263,1966 ed.</i>)

>  There are no native dogs, which are the principal cause of the expense of shepherding in Australia. (There are, however, I should mention, a few Maori dogs run wild, but these might soon be got rid of.) <i>(Jul 20, 1844,Nelson Examiner</i>) [NOTES: 1844 Monro Notes of a Journey (Nelson Examiner July 20 et seq.) in 1898 Hocken Contributions 260]

>  Maori dogs [=?] <i>(1848,Otago Journal,1/8</i>)

>  After a bad night with maori dogs, fleas, stink, &c., started towards Pureora. <i>Ibid.,</i>114 Jock and Flora [his dogs] killed a first-rate maori dog (wild sort). <i>Ibid.</i> 127 I shot 2 maori dogs at night. <i>(Mar 14, 1849,Torlesse Papers,67,1958 ed.</i>)

>  On arriving at the yards I find all the sheep out ... I counted [the sheep] and found 129 missing. I tailed them all day and yarded them at night. Two of old Jims dogs are slain on suspicion and two other Maori dogs are tied up to die as soon as their owner returns. <i>(Feb 20, 1868,Chudleigh Diary,216,1950 ed.</i>)

>  <b>NESFIELD</b> There is nothing to ... worry the sheep in New Zealand unless it is a Maori dog or two; and a few carefully laid baits in the neighbourhood of a Maori Pah soon make the owners of the dogs more careful ... <i>(1881,Chequered Career,36</i>)

>  The Maori dogs and pigs were so numerous as to be a constant source of trouble, allowed as they were to wander about ... <i>(1900,Canterbury Old & New,29</i>)

>  <b>PFAFF</b> The rabbits, too, were in an uncongenial climate, and the Maori dogs were watchful and swift; they too were a failure. <i>(1914,Diggers' Story, The,96</i>)

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> These wild dogs [of early Otago] were the real Maori dogs, and were sometimes yellow and sometimes black-and-white. <i>(1939,First White Boy Born in Otago,148</i>)

>  <b>STUDHOLME</b> Wild or Maori dogs were sometimes rather troublesome on Benmore, and did much damage to the sheep. <i>(1940,Te Waimate,209,1954 ed.</i>)

>  Maori dogs and <i>kumara pits</i> caused sad havoc, however, with this small flock of well-bred sheep. <i>(1940,Establishment of New Plymouth Settlement,193 fn</i>)

>  <b>RICHARDS</b> There had always been trouble [on the Chathams] about Maori dogs worrying the sheep. <i>(1952,Chatham Islands,126</i>)

>  <b>HUNT</b> Maori dogs were a continual source of trouble to the struggling settlers, and their destruction caused far more trouble with the natives than that of either pigs or horses. <i>(1951,Confessions,22</i>)

>  <i>(1953,Breakfast at Six,30,1960 ed.</i>)

>  <b>WINKS</b> Swift-footed Maori dogs almost made the Kiwi extinct, but a small Kiwi farm and an off-shore island reserve are helping to keep the bird alive. <i>(1954,These New Zealanders,163</i>)

>  <b>WHEELER</b> The early sheepmen did not appreciate the Maori dog. At Ernscliffe we were told that, roaming in packs of forty or fifty, from Rotorua, they were the `worst curse of the times'. <i>(1973,Historic Sheep Stations NI,101</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> [attrib. uses]

>  If the measure [ie. dog-registration] can be made to affect the intolerable Maori-dog nuisance, it will be of great service. <i>(Aug 8, 1857,Lyttelton Times,3</i>)

>  ... mates, from their `clearing' on the other side of the Maori-Dog range; Sam Barriball, the `hatter' whose `claim' was supposed to be somewhere on the Glenroy river; ... <i>(Dec 11, 1911,Triad, The,21</i>) [NOTES: A.A.Grace (The Fate of Morland)]

>  <b>BELL</b> Well he knew it not just Maori, cause they all left Hawaiki,/ And he knew he must warn other tribes and wished he was a bikie./ But of course there wasn't any roads, only little tracks,/ Just wide enough for little sleds and Maori wild dog packs. <i>(1974,Ballads of a Racegoer,9</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a wild dog or kuri [often involved in sheep-worrying in late 1800s and early 1900s.]

>  It was impossible to determine the ownership of Maori dogs. <i>(Aug 1900,NZ Farmer,Supplement A & P Conference iv</i>)

>  In a leading article in your columns lately, attention was called to the Maori dogs. <i>(Aug 18, 1888,Egmont Star,9</i>)

>  <b>HAMILTON</b> According to W H S Roberts these animals were Maori dogs gone wild. <i>(1952,History of Northern Southland,33</i>)

>  <b>MCGREGOR</b> Ormond's sheep, grazing on a few clearings, were so ravaged by Maori dogs and wild pigs that he sold the lease in 1875 to Henry Gaisford. <i>(1975,Pioneer Trails of Hawke's Bay,10</i>)

>  There are, however, I should mention, a few Maori dogs, run wild, but these might soon be got rid of. <i>(Oct 5, 1844,Nelson Examiner,3</i>)

>  I arrived here last Tuesday night, in a high state of preservation, both as respects myself and cattle, having lost only one sheep at Porirua, by a Maori dog. <i>(Mar 15, 1843,NZ Gazette,2</i>)

>  .. the bark of a Maori dog will go much further with our Government than the remonstrance of an English man. <i>(Jan 4, 1845,Nelson Examiner,1</i>)

### Maori ear
 
 <i>n.</i> an ear infection, allegedly prevalent among Maori children; ? glue ear

>  A special report on the subject which the medical statistics branch of the Health Department has issued makes this point [of the prevalence of ear, nose, and throat disease among Maoris]. For more than half a century the `Maori ear' had been regarded in medical practice as a most unrewarding condition to treat in more ways than one and by both parties concerned, the report said. If future generations of Maori children retained their greater susceptibility to ear disease, every year would add to the size of the problem till it might become too big ever to be solved. <i>(Sep 2, 1965,Dominion,2</i>)

### Maori English
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>LAWLOR</b> [Dick Harris in Introduction] The reader should be warned that the apparently phonetic spelling of English as it is commonly supposed to be spoken by Hori is not to be accepted as authentic `Maori English'. <i>(1926,Maori Tales,9</i>)

>  It is sometimes stated that a Maori child's spoken vocabulary often lacks idiom. It is true that he has limited access to the wide range of expressions that enrich the English language, but `Maori English' is perhaps excessively idiomatic in its use of colloquial phrases, slang, and short, pithy expressions that convey more meaning than is in bare words. The range is not wide , however, and the expressions are used repetively, often to reinforce a meaning rather than add to it. <i>(1971,Maori Children & the Teacher,42</i>)

>  `Restricted Codes and Maori English' [title] A recent (1971) N.Z. Department of Education publication, Maori Children and the Teacher (henceforth MCT), discusses at some length the question of `Maori English' and the language of Maori children. [<i>Ibid </i>68] I hope to have shown, in this review, that we should not accept uncritically the assumption that Maori English constitutes a restricted code or that Maori children are cognitively deficient. We know that Maori English is different from Standard English and we are aware of some of the factors that make it so. ... evidence and ... <i>(May 1972,NZ Journal Educ. Studies,59 ed.</i>) [NOTES: P.Hawkins]

### Maori epidemic
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Maybe they will be able to settle once for all.. whether the "Maori epidemic" is really smallpox. <i>(Sep 6, 1913,NZ Observer,16</i>)

### Maori fallow
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Some of the enclosures have been allowed to take a Maori fallow - i.e. have gone back to flax bush and milk weed. <i>(Sep 24, 1887,Auckland Weekly News,29</i>)

### Maori farm
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a property farmed by a Maori farmer.

>  .. many imagine that the standard of cleanliness on the Maori farms than on European-owned properties. On half a dozen Maori farms throughout the Bay of Plenty, I saw sheds and machinery that would put to shame many a New Zealand farmer. <i>(Jan 24, 1929,NZ Dairy Exporter,37</i>)

### Maori fashion
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>GRACE</b> We had not been long here before my boys had dressed the pig in the Maori fashion, and the old man had dug up some potatoes and prepared a hangi - an oven of hot stones. <i>(Mar 1851,Journal,10,1928 ed.</i>)

>  <b>CAMPBELL</b> ... he well knew the martrydom he had to go through whilst others only wept - Maori fashion - and feasted. <i>(1881,Poenamo,3/5/172</i>)

>  <b>MACDONALD</b> [Stockyard was] palisaded with upright saplings, ... and fastened to the rails Maori fashion with Akatea, a very tough species of creeper. <i>(1910,Reminiscences,9</i>)

>  <b>COWAN</b> Led by Julian with his slash-hook ... - bushmanlike figure enough was `Wirihana' for he ... travelled bare-legged in Maori fashion all that week's tramp - <i>(1916,Bush Explorers,9</i>)

>  Led by a chainman ... with his slasher, and Wirihana with his tupara - a bushmanlike figure was the Boss, for he cannily packed his trousers in his swag and travelled bare-legged, Maori fashion ... <i>(Apr 10, 1920,Quick March,13</i>)

>  <b>WILY & MAUNSELL</b> Being the middle of winter, the weather was cold and the hut draughty, so a Maori woman, Tupea, Maori fashion, intending to give needed warmth to the mother and child, kept a small fire going in a hole in the ground. <i>(1938,Robert Maunsell,81</i>)

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> I have also seen a burial carried out in Maori style. .... I have caught wekas in the Maori fashion. <i>(1939,First White Boy Born in Otago,15</i>)

>  Piri was only adopted in the Maori fashion - raised by Rata and never legally `adopted'. <i>(1945,Journal of the Polynesian Society,54/112</i>) [NOTES: (Beaglehole)]

>  These birds were sometimes caught with a noose in the Maori fashion, and, although very plentiful at first, soon became scarce. <i>(1947,Pioneer Recollections,5,1956 ed.</i>)

### Maori fire
 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Pennantia corymbosa</i>; kaikomako [(see 1940 L & B 244 ff for Maui legend about this and its subsequent Maori use as a fire stick - that is a sharp-pointed stick worked along the surface of a flat piece of softer wood (eg mahoe)) -->[a1927] Ver (Stony Bay) = Vernacular names heard by J.C. Andersen at Stony Bay, Banks Peninsula) <i>Pennantia corymbosa </i>[fn] called because used for Maori fire - sticks; the tree also personified as Hine - Kaikomako]

>  <i>kaikomako</i>, a small tree (Pennantia corymbosa), bridal-tree, Maori fire. Sometimes kahikomako. The softer of the two woods used by the Maori for producing fire by friction: he can get the blaze in under a minute and a half. <i>(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/151</i>) [NOTES: Andersen Maori Words in English]

### Maori fire-stick
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Maori fire <i>Pennantia corymbosa Ver.</i> Stony Bay, Banks Pen.) [Note. So-called because used for Maori fire-sticks; the tree is also personified as Hine-kaikomako]. <i>(1925,TrNZI,57/934</i>)

### Maori flag
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Another quote: "By what we describe as a racial issue" and "Maori flag". <i>(Jul 19, 2005,Whakatane Beacon,2</i>)

### Maori flax
 
 <i>n.</i> (Environment)

>  <i>wharariki</i>, a plant (Phormiun Cookianum) an inferior Maori flax. <i>(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/160</i>) [NOTES: Andersen Maori Words in English]

### Maori flounder
 
 <i>n.</i> = black flounder

>  <b>GRAHAM</b> NEW ZEALAND BLACK FLOUNDER or RIVER FLOUNDER (PATIKI-MOHOAO) <i>Rhombosolea (Adamsoma) retiaria adams</i> Whitley and Phillipps/ It is known to fishermen and those in the fishing industry as the River Flounder as it is caught mostly inside or near the mouths of rivers and enters lakes where it lives chiefly among weeds. To some it is known as the Maori Flounder. I have helped to catch these fish in various rivers in New Zealand, Both in the North and South Islands, from the Bay of Islands, Bay of Plenty, Canterbury and Otago. <i>(1956,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,204</i>)

### Maori flute
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>164</b> He [bagpipes] sound like one t'ousand koauau, Maori flute ... <i>(1917,New Zealand At the Front</i>)

### Maori freehold land
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Maori freehold land is essentially land which the Native (or since 1945 Maori) Land Court has converted from customary Maori tenure to English freehold tenure and has not been alienated by its Maori possessors. There are a good many specialised terms in this field such as ahiwhenua trust and putea trust. <i>(Jan 1999,NZWords,2</i>)

### Maori fuchsia
 
 <i>n.</i> kotukutuku

>  The sitter [Wetini] is appropriately wearing a spray of <i>kotukutuku</i> (Maori fuchsia) flowers, usually red in colour, hence in the photo they come out white. <i>(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/14</i>) [NOTES: (Greenwood)]

### Maori gourd
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  [Hdg] Origin of Maori gourd a mystery. Did the Maori bottle gourd originate in South America and how did it get to the Pacific? <i>(Jun 3, 2002,Dominion,8</i>)

### Maori grave
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  We will find a quantity of small mounds with a depression on one side giving a miner the idea that someone has been sinking a prospecting hole in some localities. These are called Maori graves (from their resemblance to a grave not filled in). <i>(Sep 25, 1890,Otago Witness,18</i>)

>  At Garston.. there are thousands of "Maori graves" at the rate of several hundred to the acre. <i>(Oct 30, 1890,Otago Witness,20</i>)

>  Eastward of Te Anau and Manapouri lakes there are large areas of crabholes or "Maori graves" caused by falling trees. <i>(Oct 9, 1890,Otago Witness,20</i>)

### Maori gravel
 
 <i>n.</i> [?also to warm up the soil]

>  <b>BUCK</b> In the Waimea Plains, an area of soil, known as Maori gravel, was shown to be very rich in available phosphoric acid and potash. This area is an old Maori pre-European cultivation. Pits had been dug to obtain sand and gravel, which, after having the larger stones removed, were spread over the natural loam top soil to render it more suitable for the cultivation of the kumara ... <i>(1929,Coming of the Maori, The,3</i>)

>  88. Rigg, T., and Bruce, J.A.: The Maori Gravel Soil of Waimea West, Nelson. <i>New Zealand Cawthron Institute Bulletin</i> (1923). <i>(1936,Agricultural Organization in NZ,810</i>)

### Maori half-crown
 
 <i>n.</i> (offensive) a penny

>  <b>BAKER</b> [Note. A list of slang terms for coins as used in New Zealand is given by a writer from that country in the "Bulletin" of 14 January 1899;.. brownie, copper and Maori half-crown, 1d. <i>(1945,Australian Language, The,110</i>)

>  Following are other local money-names... 1d. - <i>Maori half-crown, brownie or copper... </i> <i>(Jan 14, 1899,Sydney Bulletin,(Red Page)</i>) [NOTES: (Letter from <i>Loafer</i>, Tauranga) [Bill Pearson]]

### Maori hay
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) pasture that has been left to revert to seed and weed. (See [ethnic hay](../E#ethnic-hay))

>  <b>NELSON</b> Maori hay: tag or rank feed. <i>(1999,Eugene Nelson Glossary,5</i>)

>  Lank pasture that has gone to seed: Maori hay. <i>(2001,Questionnaire Respondents (DB)</i>)

### Maori head
 
 <i>n.</i> = mokomokai

>  [Hdg] Homecoming ceremony for 11 Maori heads. <i>(Jul 2, 1998,Dominion,1</i>)

### Maori Hen
 
 <i>n.</i> (a nickname)

>  <b>BARRINGTON</b> About this time there is great talk of a man called M'Girk, <i>alias</i> the `Maori Hen', getting gold somewhere out towards the West Coast, ... He has been shepherded by a dozen men on several occasions, but always managed to give them the slip. <i>(Dec 27, 1863,Diary</i>) [NOTES: 1863 Dec 27 Barrington Diary in 1959 Taylor Early Travellers 394]

### Maori hen
 
 <i>n.</i> an old South Island word for weka <i>Gallirallus</i> spp.; the woodhen

>  The same sense of wildness or lack of domestication accounts for the term Maori hen, used on Macquarie - as in New Zealand - for the bird <i>Gallirallus australis</i>. <i>(Aug 2000,NZWords,No. 4 : 7</i>)

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> Was shepherded all night by parties who believed the "Maori Hen" to be getting gold. Killed four Maori hens, cooked them, and had the only good feed for a fortnight. <i>(1959,Early Travellers,398</i>) [NOTES: Quote from Barrington 1864]

>  <b>PFAFF</b> All night the More-pokes, Wekas (Maori hens), Kiwis, and, in summer, the Cuckoos, kept up their cries ... <i>(1914,Diggers' Story, The,11</i>)

>  The weka, or Maori-hen, as it was usually called (<i>Ocydromus australis</i>), another bird of the open country, which is now only met with occasionally in far-back regions, was plentiful and widely distributed throughout the province, ... <i>(1922,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,4/277</i>) [NOTES: (Bathgate, Otago)]

>  Some years ago Mr. Elder turned out some Maori-hens from New Zealand ... <i>(1895,TrNZI,27/572</i>)

>  <b>HARPER</b> ... the weka (<i>Ocydromus Australis</i>), wood-hen or Maori hen, as he is variously called, as good a camp companion as one could wish for, ... <i>(1896,Pioneer Work in the Alps of NZ,36</i>)

>  <b>WHITWORTH</b> We have heard Maori hens (have got one roasting before our camp fire), kiwi, whale birds and what we take to be roa. <i>(1897,Journal</i>) [NOTES: 1897 Wilmot's Journal in 1950 Reed and Reed Farthest West 143]

>  <b>COWAN</b> The <i>weka</i> is a wingless bird, grey brown in colour, about the size of an ordinary fowl, and is often called the wood-hen, or `Maori-hen'. `As cunning as a Maori hen' is a familiar New Zealand bush simile. <i>(1926,Travels in NZ,(Vol II South Island) 63</i>)

>  <b>REISCHEK</b> My way lay through thick bush in the hollows of which lay little swamps full of reeds, out of which Maori-hens (<i>wekas</i>) were peering. <i>(1930,Yesterdays in Maoriland,23,1933 ed.</i>)

>  <i>Maori</i>.- Used as an adjective synonymous with <i>native</i>, except that it seems slightly contemptious. We admire native bush, pasture, or game; but <i>M</i>. hens (woodhens),<i> M.</i> cabbage [the European cabbage run wild--A.W.], and <i>M</i>. onion (Chrysobactron bookeri) are caricatures of their English namesakes. ([1898 Morris Austral-English]) <i>(Nov 4, 1933,Press, The,15</i>) [NOTES: Acland Glossary]

>  <b>AYSON</b> Woodhens (Maori hens we called them [in Dunedin c1850s]) were also very numerous. <i>(1937,Pioneering in Otago,31</i>)

>  <b>STUDHOLME</b> Wekas, or Maori hens, were most plentiful throughout the Waimate County in the early days. <i>(1940,Te Waimate,231,1954 ed.</i>)

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> Weka - the familiar `woodhen' of Captain Cook, and `Maori hen' of the settlers. <i>(1945,Maori Place-Names of Canterbury,64</i>)

>  <i>weka</i>, a ground-bird (Ocydromus sp.), wood-hen, Maori hen; it has wings, but too small for flight, but it can dodge and run well; and whilst its long toes are unwebbed it swims well, and voluntarily takes to the water to investigate distant isles in the great lakes: a most inquisitive and amusing bush-companion, and a courageous mother. <i>(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/147</i>) [NOTES: Andersen Maori Words in English]

>  <b>BRERETON</b> He told stories of woodhens, wekas or Maori hens, as they are variously called. <i>(1947,No Roll of Drums,135</i>) [NOTES: [NelsOn c1900]]

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> The Maori hens were running about among the tussocks, ... <i>(1947,Pioneer Recollections,5,1956 ed.</i>)

>  <i>maori hen n usu cap M </i>: WEKA <i>(1961,Merriam-Webster Third International,1379</i>)

>  <i>Maori-hen n</i>. the New Zealand woodhen, a flightless bird, the weka. <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Whitcombe's),620</i>)

>  <i>Maori-hen ... n</i>. N.Z. woodhen, a flightless bird, the <i>weka. </i> <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),13</i>)

>  Weka, or Maori-hen, is undoubtedly a splendid enemy, but are in most cases fast disappearing, being a ready prey to dogs, ferrets, &c. <i>(1896,Ann. Rep. Dept of Agriculture,23</i>)

>  <b>BARRINGTON</b> Killed four woodhens. ... <i>December 24th.</i> ... We got four Maori hens, we having a good dog with us ... <i>December 25</i> ... we had plum duff boiling ... cooked four Maori hens and had a jolly afternoon; ... [fn. 1959] Weka or woodhen. Miners and others in the South Island attached the word `Maori' to many native plants and creatures. <i>(Dec 23, 1863,Diary</i>) [NOTES: Dec 23 Barrington Diary in 1959 Taylor Early Travellers 393]

>  ... he was seen there on 14 May 1866 ... Months later some diggers at Paringa found a mai mai shelter and the remains of two Maori hens (wekas) hanging on a pole. On the ground inside the mai mai was a copy of Wilkie Collins's novel ... <i>(1866,Okarito Times</i>) [NOTES: [c1866] Okarito Times in 1974 Heinz Bright Fine Gold 54]

>  <b>WHITWORTH</b> The wood-hen, or as it is usually called [in south Westland], the Maori hen ... <i>(1870,Martin's Bay Settlement,60</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> If I ever came here again ... I shall certainly come and stay here [in Central Otago] again ... as there are lots of hills ... besides there is lots of shooting ..., maori hens, rabbits and ducks, etc. etc. <i>Ibid.</i> 165 These maori hens are called Wekas and Lady Barker makes frequent mention of them in her book ... <i>(Jan 31, 1873,Diary,60</i>) [NOTES: 1873 Jan 31 G.M.Wilson Diary in 1973 Wierzbicka Wilson Family 160]

>  There are persons who earn a livelihood yet by catching `Maori hens', as they call them, ... <i>(1875,TrNZI,7/120</i>) [NOTES: (Hood)]

>  <b>10/388</b> In the river beds swarms of Maori hens. <i>(1878,TrNZI</i>) [NOTES: (Buller.)]

>  ... we find ... along the river-beds [in Westland] swarms of Maori hens (<i>Ocydromus australis</i>), etc. <i>(1879,TrNZI,11/388</i>)

>  <b>PYKE</b> fn Weka is the native name of the Wood-hen, or, as it is sometimes called, the Maori-hen. <i>(1881,White Hood and Blue Cap,51</i>)

>  <i>Ocydromus</i>, `Maori Hen.' <i>(1883,TrNZI,5/401</i>) [NOTES: (Scott Macquarie Island)]

>  <b>WHITWORTH</b> ... Maori hen <i>(1887,Hine-Ra,12</i>)

>  <b>PAYTON</b> ... wekas, or maori hens as they are called [around Queenstown]. <i>(1888,Round about NZ,12</i>)

>  <b>MITCHELL</b> ... I travelled overland to Christchurch on two pounds of bread and two Maori hens. <i>(1889,Rhymes and Rambles,19</i>)

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> ... the oily weka 2 calls [20 fn] 2 called `Maori hens' by the early settlers probably becouse all were of the <i>weka </i>sex. <i>(1889,Colonial Couplets</i>) [NOTES: 1889 Williams Colonial Conplets (`An Idyll of the Canterbury Plains Twenty Years Ago') in 1950 Woodhouse Farm and Station Verse 19, 20]

>  <b>PRINCE</b> My only daily companion was a Maori hen (weka) ... <i>(1889,Diary of a Trip,15</i>)

>  <b>PASCOE</b> [Westland diggers carried a] Maorie Hen or a Billy of Mussells and a pannican of Skilly [sic] 1891 Mar 3. Diary <i>Ibid.</i> 135 ... on the Maorie hen ... <i>(1957,Mr Explorer Douglas,13</i>) [NOTES: [1890s] (1957) Pascoe Mr Explorer Douglas 13 [M.S. S.J.B.]]

>  Some years ago Mr. Elder turned out some Maori-hens from New Zealand ... <i>(1895,TrNZI,27/572</i>)

>  Little more than 35 years ago its site [sc. Dunedin] was a wildernesss covered with impenetrable bush or tangled scrub, and its 1ow-lying marsh land the haunts of the pukeko and the Maori hen. <i>(Mar 6, 1890,Otago Witness,18</i>)

>  And this, too, to colonials who had known most of them what it was to hunger and thirst after "damper" or "johnnycake" warranted to stay by a man for three days at a stretch! - to long vehemently for Maori hen only three removes from a watertight boot soaked in hair oil! - who knew most of them what it had been to partake of '"Sunday duff " boiled in a kerosine tin. <i>(Oct 16, 1890,Otago Witness,30</i>)

>  The Maori hen, or weka, is a most determined enemy of the bunny, and vigorously attacks and destroys both old and young. <i>(Jun 28, 1879,Auckland Weekly News,13</i>)

>  <b>MORELAND</b> Of wekas or Maori hens there were plenty. <i>(1911,Through South Westland,107</i>)

>  The harsh cry of a Maori-hen rose and echoed in the undergrowth, and ebbed into silence again. <i>(1952,Landfall,24/304</i>)

>  <b>MORRIS</b> <i>Maori-hen, n</i>. Same as <i>Weka</i> (q.v.). <i>(1898,Austral English,286</i>)

>  Maoris used the [Oamaru] site as a camp, and ... kept up a big fire for cooking eels and Maori hens. <i>(1914,History of North Otago,88,1978 ed.</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>BARRINGTON</b> Caught a bird of the Maori hen species, with a bill six inches long, black and white feathers; we called them white hens, or long-bills. [ed fn] Not precisely identifiable, but may be the Pied Oystercatcher (<i>Haemotopus finschi</i>). <i>(Mar 29, 1864,Diary</i>) [NOTES: 1864 Mar 29 Barrington Diary in 1959 Taylor Early Travellers 404]



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a weka or native swamp hen.

>  <b>BARRINGTON</b> We caught plenty of Maori hens ... <i>(Jun 11, 1863,Diary,179</i>) [NOTES: in Ell (1995) Great Journeys in Old New Zealand]

>  <b>BATHGATE</b> .. the Maori hens only retired for a few seconds to a small bush ... <i>(1881,Waitaruna,221</i>)

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> The Maori hens were running about among the tussocks ... <i>(1956,Pioneer Recollections,IV 5</i>)

### Maori herring
 
 <i>n.</i> ? Maori H. `aua'; English H. `pilchard' (See [cf. herring](../H#herring),[Picton herring](../P#picton-herring))

>  [There were washed up at Picton] all full sized herrings of two kinds, one known as the Maori and the other as the English. <i>(1865,Evening Post,1/117/3</i>)

### Maori hole
 
 <i>n.</i> kumara pit, rua [ca 1954 (R. Mason) `I always use this [Maori-hole], which I learnt from my mother, ex-Wanganui district']

>  The enormous number of these <i>ruas</i> on the volcanics plains of Taranaki and elsewhere shows the extent of former plantations. They are called `Maori holes' by the settlers; ... <i>(1903,TrNZI,35/21</i>) [NOTES: (Walsh)]



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> a hole where Maori had formerly removed gravel (? for adding to kumara etc. gardens to warm the soil)

>  When the Nelson settlement was founded, whole sections of land in the Waimea were almost entirely worthless owing to the many large irregular-shaped pits, or `Maori Holes' from which the gravel had been taken by some former inhabitants. <i>(1894,Journal of the Polynesian Society,4/221</i>) [NOTES: (Rutland)]

### Maori holiday
 
 <i>n.</i> (offensive)

>  <b>LELAND</b> <i>Maori holiday: </i>Like other socioeconomically disadvantaged groups, the Maori is the butt of a number of jokes. (How many Irish, Polish, or Black jokes do you know?) A Maori holiday is THE DAY AFTER PAYDAY. <i>(1980,Personal Kiwi-Yankee Dictionary, A,62</i>)

>  <i>sickie</i>: a Maori holiday <i>(Feb 1981,Avondale College Slang Words in Use</i>) [NOTES: (Goldie Brown Collection)]

>  <b>GORDON & DEVERSON</b> (Similar expressions include <i>Maori holiday</i> = the day after pay-day,... <i>(1985,NZ English (Gordon & Deverson),42</i>)

### Maori holly
 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Olearia ilicifolia</i>

>  Maori holly - the name given to Akeake by the settlers (Olearia ilicifolia) <i>(Mar 1, 1878,NZ Country Journal,II ii 126</i>)

>  <i>Olearia ilicifolia</i> <i>(1878,NZ Country Journal,Vol I 1877-XXII 1898</i>)

>  <b>LAING & BLACKWELL</b> <i>Olearia ilicifolia (The Holly-leaved Olearia</i>)... Much cultivated. Maori holly. <i>(1940,Plants of NZ,446</i>)

>  Musky scented Maori holly, Olearia lilicifolia, made the air fragrant after rain. <i>(1933,Canterbury Mountaineer, The,II/44</i>)

### Maori horse
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a wild horse.

>  Some of the settlers threaten a war of extermination against the pigs and Maori horses. <i>(Nov 10, 1880,Hawera Star,2</i>)

>  Alas, it had run among a mob of unbroken Maori horses, all of which promptly took to their heels! <i>(Jul 8, 1953,Free Lance,33</i>)

### Maori house
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  As a child I never took playmates home because I lived in a 'Maori house'. <i>(Aug 4, 2005,Dominion Post,B7</i>)

>  At 6 P.M. the moury House, by accident, took fire, and with great difficulty the flames were extinguish'd <i>(Jun 24, 1839,Piraki Log,90,ed. Anson 1911 ed.</i>)

### Maori huntaway
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a boulder or rock sent downhill to scatter sheep during a high country muster. (See [Nelson huntaway; Taranaki huntaway](../N#nelson-huntaway;-taranaki-huntaway))

>  <b>SCURR</b> Rolling stone to shift sheep:- Maori huntaway <i>(2000,Rabbiters' Glossary,1</i>)

>  <b>JARDINE</b> First he would try a couple of 'Maori huntaways' so, selecting a good-sized round rock and going to the edge of the bluff he was on, he rolled it over, on a line that would pass close to the sheep. <i>(1978,Shadows on the Hill,61</i>)

### Maori hut
 
 <i>n.</i> usu. a raupo hut

>  Capt Smith. Mauri hut <i>(1841,NZ Journal,2/38/162</i>)

>  ... sleeping in Mauri or native huts. <i>(1841,NZ Journal,2/31/80</i>) [NOTES: (C.B. Brewer)]

>  <b>WICKSTEAD</b> The Maori or Raupo huts are daily losing their occupants and falling into decay, being replaced by substantial <i>cob</i>, or mud-walled dwellings, but oftenest by neat wooden buildings. <i>(Nov 23, 1842,Letter</i>) [NOTES: (1842 Nov 23) 1843 (1968) Letters from New Plymouth 65]

>  Camped at a new maori hut belonging to Maupo and fed on Paradise ducks and three grey ones which I shot. <i>(Mar 1, 1849,Torlesse Papers,63,1958 ed.</i>)

>  <b>SENIOR</b> The recurrence of Maori huts warned us that we were in Maori Country. <i>(1880,Travel & Trout,214</i>)

>  The Maori hut is gimcrack. <i>(Jul 22, 1924,Otago Witness,30</i>)

### Maori ice plant
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <i>Maori Ice Plant, Disphyma australe</i>. Seaside plant which trails over rocks and cliff faces often only a few fet from the sea. Flowers are either creamy-white or pink ... <i>(1971,NZ Heritage,1/266</i>)

### Maori immersion
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [immersion](../I#immersion))

>  [Hdg] Maori immersion pupils 'keeping up'. <i>(Jan 17, 2001,Dominion,9</i>)

>  We are very sympathetic towards the needs of students in the Maori immersion stream of Newtown School... Because we couldn't offer Maori immersion our principal proposed to Newtown School a ministry-approved method of enrolment that would enable immersion students to continue at Newtown School in years Seven and Eight. <i>(Dec 2, 1999,City Voice,18</i>)

>  Parents of pupils attending a Newtown Maori immersion school say they are disappointed that a decision on whether they will move to a posh seaside suburb has been delayed for political reasons. <i>(Jul 9, 2002,Dominion Post,A7</i>)

>  Taki Rua toured Willy Craig Fransen's Maori-language play <i>Taku Waimarie</i> around kohanga reo and Maori immersion schools in the North Island for six solid months this year. <i>(Aug 29, 1998,Listener (New Zealand Listener),38</i>)

### Maori itch
 
 <i>n.</i> usually aplied to irritating skin eruptions, or the bites of fleas or body lice; hakihaki (ake-ake) [ca 1935 Havelock Don't play too much with Maoris. You'll get Maori itch. It burrows underneath your skin and no amount of scratching will get rid of it.]

>  <b>SHORTLAND</b> Those who feed much on [mutton birds] generally suffer from an eruption about the arms and thighs accompanied by intolerable itching. [check this for head word.]] <i>(1851,Southern Districts of NZ, The,226,1844 ed.</i>) [NOTES: [(1844) 1851 Shortland Southern Dists. 226]

>  <b>LYON</b> The natives use the [Ngawha hot] springs largely for skin ailments, notably the akeake, or Maori itch. <i>(1952,Faring South,171</i>)

>  <b>HYDE</b> He didn't want to sleep in the [Maori] shack. The lastr one he struck had been full of fleas, and these thin lads ... were the ones to have Maori itch. When you struck that you were sorry for yourself. <i>(1938,Nor the Years Condemn,148</i>)

>  Think of the pleasure your farthings can bring to the Waikato War Veterans, many of whom are suffering .... from Athletie's Foot, Maori Itch, Pink Tooth-brush ... <i>(1939,Auckland University Carnival Book,39</i>)

### Maori ivy
 
 <i>n.</i> patete

>  ~, a tree (Schefflera digitata), a five-fingered; [sic] Maori ivy (the fruit only being similar). <i>(Jun 1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/155</i>) [NOTES: (J.C.Andersen]

### Maori jasmine
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>LAING & BLACKWELL</b> <i>Parsonsia heterophylla</i> (The Varied-leaved Parsonsia)... Flowers white, scented. (Maori Jasmine). <i>(1940,Plants of NZ,360</i>)

>  Parsonia heterophylla, <i>Maori jasmine, a shrub which climbs by twining stems. </i> <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,3/478</i>)

>  You might identify the common liane, kaihua, also known as Maori jasmine because of its small, white, scented flowers. <i>(1983,Land of the Mist,65</i>)

### Maori job
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  (Similar expressions include <i>Maori holiday</i> = the day after pay-day,<i> Maori sidestep</i> = a head-on charge in rugby, a<i> Maori job/outfit/etc.</i> = something rough or disorganised - compare the use of <i>bush</i> in similar formations.) <i>(1985,NZ English (Gordon & Deverson),42</i>) [NOTES: Gordon and Deverson]

### Maori jog
 
 <i>n.</i> of horses, a pace between a trot and a walk [1940's Auckland: Jean Watson (21.12.84)]

### Maori justice
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  When I first read about this sadistic person's crimes, I said to family members that if true "Maori justice" still prevailed, he and his associates would have got a fast exit from this world a long time ago. ... Instead of these misfits causing misery to the rest of us conformists, perhaps we should reinstate true "Maori justice". <i>(Nov 16, 2005,Whakatane Beacon,2</i>)

### Maori kilt
 
 <i>n.</i> a Maori mat

>  <b>KENNEDY</b> The native who had not risen to the supposed dignity of a full suit appeared in a Maori kilt - a mat tied round his waist giving free-play to his bare legs and feet. <i>(1877,Colonial Travel,232</i>)

>  <b>LYSNAR</b> They [Maori hunters] are often seen returning from the chase with their fierce-looking dogs, great-limbed men, wearing the Maori kilt or shawl (a piece of coloured blanket or flax mat wrapped round their loins), with wild pigs strapped across their shoulders, ... <i>(1924,New Zealand (various authors/titles),79</i>)

### Maori King
 
 <i>n.</i> a) the leader of an originally 19th century Maori independence (`king') movement. b) the acknowledged leader of a confederation of North Island tribal groups. (See [Maori Queen. Compare King Country](../M#maori-queen.-compare-king-country))

>  There is probably not a single village of any size in which all belong to the king's party and hence I think that the Government ... might override the law-giving party of the maori king. <i>(May 10, 1859,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,1/456,1960 ed.</i>) [NOTES: (J. Morgan)]

>  <b>BUDDLE</b> The disputed land at Waitara is claimed by the Maori King party because the King's mana has reached it ... <i>(1860,Maori King Movement,18</i>)

>  <b>GRACE</b> The moment I heard that it was intended to discuss the propriety of making a Maori King, [76] I refused to be present; ... <i>(Mar 24, 1858,Letter</i>) [NOTES: 1858 Mar 24 T.S.Grace letter in Grace Pioneer Missionary (1928) 75-76]

>  <b>ASHWELL</b> The Maori King ... <i>(1860,Letters & Journals,3/359</i>)

>  <b>CARBERRY</b> I am told that many of the Maoris and the Maori King himself are opposed to the Murdering system adopted by some of the Natives ... <i>(Nov 18, 1863,Journal</i>)

>  <b>BULLER</b> The `Maori King' had now become a fact. <i>(1878,Forty Years in NZ,412</i>)

>  <b>COWAN</b> At this meeting [in 1854], too, the idea of a Maori king for the Maori people was discussed and fervently approved. <i>(1922,NZ Wars, The,1/148</i>)

>  <b>WILY & MAUNSELL</b> Of the two non-signing chiefs referred to in Maunsell's report, one was the great Potatau Te Whero-whero, the first Maori King. <i>(1938,Robert Maunsell,69</i>)

>  <b>BEAGLEHOLE</b> Finally, the Totara [Otaki] people have never been very favourably inclined towards the concept or the fact of the Maori king. It is true that two of the early king-makers were associated with local Maoris so that there are connections between Totara and Waikato genealogies. <i>(1946,Some Modern Maoris,290</i>)

>  <b>SINCLAIR</b> ... it was the first of a series of great inter-tribal meetings which marked the rise of a Maori national movement and led, in 1858, to the election of a Maori king. <i>(1959,History of New Zealand,110</i>)

>  <b>MASTERS</b> A fitting close it seemed to a grand day's sport with grand companions, in an untamed and beautiful corner of the land of the Maori King. <i>(1960,Back-Country Tales,199</i>)

>  The defection of William Thompson, the King-maker, was the most serious blow that the Maori King could have received. <i>(May 24, 1879,Auckland Weekly News,12</i>)

### Maori King movement
 
 <i>n.</i> - [also Maori King party]

>  <b>LAMBERT</b> The idea of the Maori King Movement was mooted. <i>(1983,Illustrated History of Taranaki,34</i>)

>  The [Tainui] iwi gave birth to the Maori King Movement, the latter a reaction to the demands for Maori land by British settlers in the late 1850s. <i>(Nov 1999,North & South,60</i>)

>  ... the native on the line interested in the Maori King movement being generally speaking opposed to the opening of the road until the Maori King has given his consent. <i>(Dec 9, 1857,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,1/328</i>) [NOTES: (J.Morgan)]

>  <b>SMITH</b> I believe that at the meeting called together to erect this, the Maori King movement was first mooted. <i>(Jan 18, 1857,Notes of a Journey</i>) [NOTES: 1858 Jan 18 Smith Notes of a Journey in 1959 Taylor Early Travellers 365]

>  If, however, the maori king party as arule should continue to demand payment from the carrier as a maori it must be taken up. <i>(May 23, 1859,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,1/460</i>) [NOTES: (J. Morgan)]

>  <b>MACDONALD</b> The `Maori Land League' and the `Maori King Movement' were growing into consistency and power. .the Maori kingfisher see <i>(1910,Reminiscences,65</i>)

### Maori kingship
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  It is too early to say what course the Maori `kingship' will now take. <i>(Oct 2, 1933,Na To Hoa Aroha,3/111</i>) [NOTES: (Ngata)]

### Maori kiss
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> a hongi, pressing noses

>  Maori Kiss, see Hongi <i>(1940,Where the White Man Treads</i>) [NOTES: c1940s TS Index to 1928 Baucke Where the White Man Treads Victoria University Library: Fildes collection 1788]

>  <b>ROBAK</b> Maori kiss: a greeting consists of rubbing noses <i>(1944,Dictionary of International Slurs,53</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> (Cuisine) a biscuit

>  <i>(1963,Atlas Cookery Book,58</i>) [NOTES: 10th edition; get quote if necessary]

>  Maori Kisses... When cold stick two together with jam, and put a little icing on the top. <i>(1924,Help the Babies Cookery Book,132</i>)

>  MAORI KISSES (<i>Very Nice</i>)/ Two ... tablespoons of sugar, 4 tablespoons of butter, 1/2 cup shelled walnuts, 1 cup chopped dates, 1 cup of flour, 1 tablespoon cocoa, 1/4 teaspoon vanilla ... 1 teaspoon baking powder... <i>(1955,New Home Cookery,146</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>Maori kisses</i> old-fashioned Kiwi biscuits using cocoa, milk, butter, flour, sugar and baking powder; recipe in David Burton's <i>Two Hundred Years of New Zealand Food and Cookery</i>, 1982. <i>(1989,Dinkum Kiwi Dictionary, The,66</i>)

### Maori kit
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [kete](../K#kete),[kit](../K#kit),[Maori basket](../M#maori-basket))

>  The pockets of the [billiard] table were small Maori kits, fastened on with nails. <i>(Jul 2, 1910,NZ Free Lance,12</i>)

>  <b>FITTON</b> The natives generally bring their produce to market in neatly made baskets, plaited from flax and known by the name of `Maori kits' <i>(1856,New Zealand (various authors/titles),68</i>)

>  <b>SMITH</b> .. our guide ... fell upon a <i>kite</i> of potatoes, in which he made a respectable hole; ... [ed fn] A bag-shaped basket woven of flax, cabbage-tree leaves, &c.; frequently called a `Maori kit'. <i>(Jan 13, 1858,Notes of a Journey</i>) [NOTES: 1858 Jan 13 Smith Notes of a Journey in 1959 Taylor Early Travellers ?p]

>  <b>BARKER</b> I give my few simple orders to the cook, and prepare to pack a "Maori kit', or flat basket made of flax.... with preparations for our luncheon. <i>(1873,Station Amusements in NZ,7</i>)

>  <b>FERGUSON</b> She was evidently obtaining supplies of necessary stores; and the various parcels. though placed in a Maori kit, seemed to weigh considerably. <i>(1893,Bush Life in Australia & NZ,287</i>)

>  <b>MANSFIELD</b> I got a Maori kit - <i>(1907,Urewera Notebook,62,1978 Gordon ed.</i>)

>  <b>BAXTER</b> We can go out with Maori kits to gather/ Watercress,... <i>(1979,Collected Poems,585,1972 ed.</i>)

>  <b>ANDERSON</b> [Reference c1882-1883, Malvern, Canterbury] ... they walked nearly four kilometres [to school] ... their books and lunch packed in a "Maori-kit' (coarse string bag) slung over their backs. <i>(1981,Both Sides of the River,21</i>)

>  To Kiwi readers the transformation is not just dramatic but also hilarious. A Maori kit, or kete, is actually a string shopping bag. <i>(Jan 1989,Pacific Way,10</i>) [NOTES: (Sharp New Zildish)]

>  <i>kete</i>, flat basket made of flax: a basket of like shape but made of prepared flax dyed with cross-pattern in black called Maori-kit: the first very commonly used for potatoes or kumara and shell-fish in earlier days of settlement; the latter commonly used in later days up till now. <i>(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/152</i>) [NOTES: J.C.Andersen]

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> We ... were enlivened by a good hour's <i>tangi</i> between our guide and the woman ... at the end of which our guide ... knocked off, and fell upon a <i>kite</i> of potatoes ... [ed. fn] A bag-shaped basket woven of flax, cabbage-tree leaves, &c.; frequently called a `Maori kit'. <i>(1959,Early Travellers,360</i>)

>  <b>FITTON</b> The natives generally bring their produce to market in neatly made baskets, plaited from flax, and known by the name of `Maori kits'. <i>(1856,New Zealand (various authors/titles),68 [fn]</i>)

>  ... our guide ... fell upon a <i>kite</i> of potatoes, in which he made a respectable hole; ... [ed fn] A bag-shaped basket woven of flax, cabbage-tree leaves, &c.; frequently called a `Maori kit'. <i>(Jan 13, 1858,Notes of a Journey</i>) [NOTES: 1858 Jan 13 Smith Notes of a Journey in 1959 Taylor Early Travellers ?p]

>  <b>BAINES</b> Mine is best, as you will admit when you come to overhaul the Maori kits in which it is packed. <i>(1874,Edward Crewe,213</i>)

>  <b>MANSFIELD</b> -I got a Maori kit - <i>(1907,Urewera Notebook,62,1978 Gordon ed.</i>)

>  [c1890s] Shouting colourful epithets at them, and waving a Maori kit which contained her supper in a `Blue Peter', she [the tipsy woman] chased them down the platform. <i>(1914,History of North Otago,117,1978 ed.</i>)

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> When T.B.Nelson had Mount Royal he sowed rape with young grass, and an enterprising man put it in Maori kits and sold it in Palmerston as Maori cabbage. <i>(1939,First White Boy Born in Otago,123</i>)

>  <b>LEE</b> Late at night mother would come home with a scrag end of meat, a pie-dish half full of a sago pudding, or the remains of a leg of mutton, a cold chop or a sausage or two, a jug of soup, stale scones or cakes, a fragment of this or a portion of that, all wrapped in newspaper and carried in a Maori kit. <i>(1934,Children of the Poor,88,1949 ed.</i>)

### Maori land
 
 <i>n.</i> land held under customary title or as Maori freehold land [Jim Cameron writes: The legal definition of Maori land in DNZE is confusing. <i>Maori customary land</i> is land held in accordance with tikanga Maori. <i>Maori freehold land</i> is essentially (there are minor qualifications) land that has been converted to English freehold tenure and has not been alienated to non-Maori. There are several trusts for various forms of Maori landholding - <i>ahi whenua trusts, kitiakai trusts, putea trusts, whanau trusts</i> and <i>whenua topu trusts</i>. Note also <i> status orders</i>. See Te Ture Whenua Maori Act 1993.]

>  <b>SCOTT</b> I've lost the rent money for the Maori land my father has leased up the river. <i>(1900,Tales of the Colonial Turf,113</i>)

>  Reports from County Councils have shown a marked improvement in rate payments from Maori land. <i>(Aug 26, 1943,NZ Farmers' Weekly,7</i>)

>  All four classes dealt with Maori lands in much the same manner - paying for large areas with goods and articles of small value. <i>(May 21, 1887,Auckland Weekly News,28</i>)

>  <b>NESFIELD</b> Spiritism is a very old institution in Maori-land, and conversations with departed spirits, through the medium of the `tohunga,' were held ... prior to the arrival of the Pakeha. <i>(1881,Chequered Career,45</i>)

>  <b>CAMPBELL</b> There was to be a future for Maoriland under the Pakeha, and we were craving to know when the first page of its history was to be written - ... <i>(1881,Poenamo,Bk4/5/279</i>)

>  <b>BARLOW</b> They are supposed ... to represent the first ... animal life that the English favoured Maoriland with. <i>(1888,Kaipara and Waitemata Echo,15</i>)

>  <b>LAMBERT</b> But while it may not be possible to claim for Tuhoeland a monopoly of fairy-folk, it is true that nowhere in Maoriland are their such varieties of legends ... <i>(1936,Pioneering Reminiscences of Old Wairoa,58</i>)

>  <b>MARSHALL</b> There was bush on the Maori land beyond the estuary, but most of the hills were brown in the summer. <i>(1984,Day Hemingway Died, The,21</i>) [NOTES: (Littlejohn Interview)]

>  <b>KING</b> [caption] The ohu, or working bee, which cleared and sowed the Panguru block. By 1932, groups like this were bringing Maori land into production all over the North Island. <i>(1988,Apirana Ngata</i>)

>  Never once do I remember hearing [in Tauranga in the 1930s] about the confiscation of Maori land although the name `the Quarter Acres' for an area of town sections allotted to Pakeha after the Battles of Gate Pa and Te Ranga (1864) was commonly used. <i>(1988,Through the Looking Glass,130</i>) [NOTES: (Meikle)]

>  Maori land and the terms relating to it are of course unique to New Zealand. DNZE defines it (in part) as land for which Maori customary or legal title is recognised as valid. This has a circular air and the contrast of customary and legal tile is confusing, since customary title is itself a title recognised by the law. At law, Maori land is land owned by Maori under customary or Maori freehold title. <i>(Jan 1999,NZWords,2</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> land belonging to the Maori; (later) land set aside for Maori use; Maori reserve. [(perhaps merely an attrib. use of MAORI adj.) HWO]

>  The Government must also bear in mind that these [public] houses are forced on the Aborigines. A European squats down on Maori land and the Government grant him a license [sic]. The natives object to it and protest against it but in vain. <i>(Jun 17, 1857,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,1/278</i>) [NOTES: (J.Morgan, Otawhao]

>  <b>SCOTT</b> Sent money for the Maori land my father had leased ... <i>(1900,Colonial Turf,113</i>)

>  <b>MACDONALD</b> He [Grey] bought great areas of Maori land for the Colony, and kept the Maoris and Exeter Hall party quiet ... with `Sugar and Blankets' and promises ... <i>(1910,Reminiscences,62</i>)

>  <b>461</b> Tripp held that I could not buy or lease any more Maori land but I could let my stock on it and pay Inia when he asked for it. <i>(Oct 10, 1912,Chudleigh Diary,1950</i>)

>  No, I don't know, but there's a yarn his father was a whaler that stayed out here and got in tow with some maori tart. He's part maori for sure though, because once he got me to witness a paper and it was about maori land. <i>(1949,I Saw in My Dream,117,1974 ed. Rhodes ed.</i>)

>  Maori Land' means Maori land within the meaning of the Maori Land Act, 1931 <i>(1950,NZ Statutes</i>) [NOTES: Noxious Weeds Act]

>  <b>SHADBOLT</b> The Waikai place was a small patch of swampy Maori land just outside Te Ika. A couple of cows grazed, and there was a big garden. <i>(1965,Among the Cinders,22</i>)

>  <b>CHEESEMAN</b> A wealthy man had bought the Maori-land. <i>(1886,Rolling Stone, A,I-7</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) land in Maori ownership that may be leased to others to farm.

>  <b>GORDON</b> After he tamed that he added two smaller blocks, both of them maori land. A few years later another block came up, which I wasn't interested in at the time, because I wasn't enthusiastic about purchasing Maori land. <i>(1987,People, Places & Paddocks,135</i>)

>  His holding is bound on two sides by Maori land. <i>(Jun 3, 1909,Weekly News,17</i>)

>  .. they not only make it difficult for a farmer to acquire Crown lands, but deliberately shut up for occupation the millions of acres of Maori lands ... <i>(Jun 3, 1909,Weekly News,44</i>)

>  It is estimated that there are between 70,000 and 80,000 acres of Maori land in Taranaki not being adequately farmed and capable of carrying a good deal more population. <i>(Nov 5, 1953,NZ Farmer,10</i>)

>  Some of it [reverted land] was Maori land, but despite the reported difficulties of acquiring the freehold of Maori land, Guy Robertson found that by pressing on with determination and patience the legal processes were not all that formidable. <i>(Nov 21, 1963,NZ Farmer,3</i>)

>  Eric Albert couldn't raise a cent as he struggled to farm Maori land ... <i>(Jun 1994,NZ Farmer,8</i>)

>  I'm pleased that the project is on Maori land and that the trustees' work has been recognised. [Maori leasehold] <i>(Jun 19, 2002,Southland Times,3</i>)

### Maori Land Court
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  The Maori Land Court will then be asked to vest the reservation in a body corporate, which will include representation from the tangata whenua (local people), appointed by the court in trust for the community. <i>(Jun 29, 1988,Dominion,2</i>)

>  Maori freehold land is essentially land which the Native (or since 1945 Maori) Land Court has converted from customary Maori tenure to English freehold tenure and has not been alienated by its Maori possessors. <i>(Jan 1999,NZWords,2</i>)

### Maori land question
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>WALLACE</b> The Maori land Question has ... been made a political scapegoat. <i>(1891,Rural Economy of Aust. & NZ,227</i>)

### Maori lawyer
 
 <i>adj.</i> <i>Rubus</i> spp. (bush-) lawyer

>  <b>GOLDIE</b> That plague to man in all New Zealand bush - `Maori Lawyers'. <i>(1862,Journal</i>) [NOTES: 1862 Goldie Second Journal in 1947 Beattie Pioneers Explore Otago 94]

### Maori lease
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) Maori-owned land leased for grazing or farming.

>  <b>ANDERSON</b> I had already approached the Commissioner of Crown Lands in the early stages of my negotiations and his department had replied that they would favourably consider leasing it to me, but not until I had secured confirmation of the Maori leases, because, if I fell down on the Maori leases, I could make demands on the Crown for roading facilities. <i>(1962,Life's Roundabout, A,62</i>)

### Maori lemon
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>RAFTER</b> In winter they had "Maori lemons", a cross between a lemon and an orange; curiously enough, the children ate the skin of the "Maori lemons" and threw the juicy pulp away. <i>(1972,Never Let Go!,57</i>)

### Maori look-out
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>SHADBOLT</b> `Where to?' `Over to the Maori look-out. ...' ... The Maori look-out was a point just beyond the western arm of the bay, a jagged and rock-strewn cliff patchy with native bush. ... In the days when only Maoris lived there, on a hill-top pa behind the beach, they used the point to watch for enemy war-canoes travelling across the harbour. Scouts planted there would give warning of an attack. <i>(1965,Among the Cinders,123</i>)

### Maori Made
 
 <i>n.</i> see toi iho

### Maori Magna Charta
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>BUICK</b> [title of chapter IV] THE MAORI MAGNA CHARTA <i>(1936,Treaty of Waitangi,98ff</i>)

>  <b>BAKER</b> >The Maori Magna Charta</i>, for the treaty of Waitangi. <i>(1941,NZ Slang,59</i>)

>  <b>MATTHEWS</b> T.Lindsay Buick, in his book on the Maori Magna Charta, gives a full and interesting account of the proceedings: ... <i>(1940,Matthews of Kaitaia,128</i>)

### Maori mat
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> in common use for a Maori (flax) cloak; but also for a flax mat (cf. MAT) (See [mat](../M#mat))

>  <b>JOHNSON</b> [This] ... with two good blankets wrapped up in a Maori mat, is the personal baggage <i>(1847,Notes from Journal</i>) [NOTES: 1847 Johnson Notes from Jrnl in 1959 Taylor Early Travs. 119]

>  <b>BAINES</b> But in days gone by, before the pakeha visited the land, the above style of house was almost a necessity, Maori mats being quite an insufficient barrier against the cold during the winter nights, with the wind in the south. <i>(1874,Edward Crewe,231</i>)

>  <b>CAMPBELL</b> ... after rolling yourself in your blanket, horse-rug, or Maori mat, you could sleep most comfortably ... <i>(1881,Poenamo,Bk3/3/155</i>)

>  <b>PASCOE</b> [Letter to Harper, 10 May] He was good, covered with a beautiful Maori mat ... <i>(1895,Letter</i>) [NOTES: 1895 Pascoe Mr Explorer Douglas (1957) 192]

>  ... second-hand Maori mats. <i>(Jan 27, 1906,NZ Truth,32/5/5</i>)

>  <b>HYDE</b> They had carried Massey through packed streets in Wellington, in ... a coffin draped with the Union Jack and a fine Maori mat. <i>(1938,Nor the Years Condemn,116</i>)

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> The Maori mats were made of feetow (whitau or dressed flax). ... They were hung over one shoulder and fastened under the other ... <i>(1939,First White Boy Born in Otago,11</i>)

>  A powhiri, the lament usually reserved for the death of a great rangatira was performed. The coffin was covered with a Maori mat and borne by Cabinet ministers ... <i>(May 1984,Metro,124</i>) [NOTES: (Peter Shaw)]

>  The bride wore a lovely Maori mat, and Tamatoa the Salvation Army uniform. <i>(Jul 17, 1890,Otago Witness,31</i>)

>  If you met a native in his blanket or Maori mat, there was more seen of him than was required. <i>(Oct 1, 1887,Auckland Weekly News,7</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  Stopped at `Tommy's' [a Maori]. Kikied the `tiners' and the fish. Slept on a Maori mat, wrapped in a blanket: 4 in a bed-place - heads and tails. <i>(Aug 15, 1842,Piraki Log,143,ed. Anson ed.</i>)

>  [headline] Preparing pillars and Maori mats The entrance to Government House, Wellington, will soon have a Taranaki flavour - carved pillars and woven mats made in the Te Atiawa (Taranaki) style Petone Technical Institute students carved pou out of a 100-year-old totara pile ... Te Atiawa women are still plaiting the mat to be laid at the main door. <i>(Oct 31, 1986,Evening Post,6</i>)

### Maori melon
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>MILLER</b> By the end of 1844 the garden contained 'cabbages of various kinds.. Maori melons,.. apple and pear trees.' <i>(1958,Early Victorian NZ,145</i>) [NOTES: From JD & Sarah Greenwood, Letters, 1844.]

>  <b>GREENWOOD</b> We have now in the garden [at Motueka] ... vegetable marrows, Maori melons, spinach. <i>(Nov 10, 1844,Letter</i>) [NOTES: 1844 Nov 10 Sarah Greenwood in 1960 Drummond Married and Gone to NZ 78; ALSO 1844 J.D. and Sarah Greenwood The Greenwood Letters 1842-1860 [TS] in 1958 Miller Early Victorian NZ 145]

### Maori mile
 
 <i>n.</i> (jocular) an indeterminate distance, similar to an Irish mile. -->an indeterminate distance; an `Irishman's mile' (cf MAORI TIME) [ca 1953 Upper Hutt.]

### Maori mint
 
 <i>n.</i> a native mint plant, aka hioi

>  <b>LAING & BLACKWELL</b> <i>Mentha Cunninghamii (Cunningham's Mint)</i>. ... A small endemic species. (Maori mint). <i>(1940,Plants of NZ,387</i>)

>  <b>STARK</b> <i>HIOI (Maori Mint) </i>mentha cunninghamii. This slender prostrate herb may be found in both the North and the South Islands. <i>(1979,Maori Herbal Remedies,18</i>)

>  <b>BROOKER et al.</b> <i>Mentha cunninghamii</i> Benth. Common name: <i>Maori mint </i>Maori name: <i>Hioi </i> <i>(1981,NZ Medicinal Plants,60</i>)

### Maori Mission
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  The sermon was of course on Maori Mission work... My attention was attracted by.. one of the old mission crosses of the Catholic Maori Mission. <i>(Oct 8, 1887,Auckland Weekly News,7</i>)

>  <b>SIMMONS</b> The Maori missions in the Auckland province in the period from 1843 to 1850 really fall into four distinct areas. <i>(1984,Pompallier,78</i>)

>  <b>SIMMONS</b> Since 1850 he [sc. Pompallier] had tried to get Italian Franciscans for the Maori Mission. <i>(1984,Pompallier,168</i>)

>  <b>GEORGE</b> Father Maillard.. had served in various missions in New Zealand and was made superior of the whole Marist Maori Mission. <i>(1990,Ohakune,313</i>)

>  It does a fellow good to see a Maori Mission station. <i>(Feb 1936,Marist Messenger,22</i>)

>  She was a missionary of many years standing as a member of the Maori Mission League of St. Teresa. <i>(Jan 1955,Marist Messenger,25</i>)

>  The concept of "AHI KAA" arose from one of the Maori Mission conferences. <i>(1989,Aspects of Apostolates of Society of Mary,99</i>)

>  I conclude by assuring my readers and supporters of the Marist Maori Mission that I make practical my good wishes.. in my prayers. <i>(Jul 1955,Marist Messenger,13</i>)

>  Hoani Papita seemed an appropriate name for this Marist missionary who was seen as the herald of a new more hopeful era for the Catholic Maori mission in Wellington. <i>iBID.</i> ** The newly-ordained Soulas was appointed to the Hawke's Bay Maori mission at Meanee, following his arrival from France in 1879.<i> Ibid.</i> 40 Venning was the first New Zealand Marist to be appointed to the Maori mission. <i> Ibid.</i> 97 Haoto Paora College... was established from the vision and commitment of those in the Maori Mission. <i>Ibid.</i> 99 Some of the Mission Areas changed the name of this Apostolate from 'Maori Mission' to 'Maori Pastoral Area'. <i>(1989,Aspects of Apostolates of Society of Mary,29</i>)

>  The land wars ruined any prospect of a Maori mission. <i>(May 13, 1992,NZ Tablet,14</i>)

>  [Hdg] Auckland's Great Maori Mission. <i>Ibid.</i> 6 Fit instruments have brought the Maori Mission to the heights. <i>(Feb 1937,Marist Messenger,5</i>)

>  Mgr. Luck went to Europe in search of new missionaries to serve the Maori Missions. <i>(Apr 1946,Marist Messenger,12</i>)

>  His [sc. Fr. Rohan's] sympathetic understanding.. and his wonderful spirit of cooperation mean a very great deal to the Maori mission. <i>(Mar 1946,Marist Messenger,21</i>)

>  In 1878 the Bishop of Wellington provided for the restoration of the Maori Mission in his diocese by sending to the natives Father Soulas S.M. <i>(May 1946,Marist Messenger,1</i>)

>  Matters of vital interest to the Marist Maori Missions were discussed. <i>(Jun 1946,Marist Messenger,13</i>)

>  I am fully aware of my gratitude to the Sister, Brothers and Fathers who keep.. The cause of our own Maori Mission in the apostolic enterprise of our friends. <i>(Jul 1955,Marist Messenger,11</i>)

>  One of the interesting features will be the Maori camp at the Show Grounds, where 330 Catholic Maoris chosen from Maori mission stations will assemble. <i>(Feb 16, 1938,Auckland Weekly News,26</i>)

>  The work in the Maori mission field is carried on almost entirely by the young Maori clergymen and lay-readers. <i>(Oct 27, 1911,Maoriland Worker,6</i>)

### Maori Missionary
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Shortly before the sudden illness which took this great Maori Missionary from earth we met a young man from Hamilton. <i>(Feb 1936,Marist Messenger,21</i>)

>  The fine building was the gift of two splendid Catholics, one of whom was baptised by the Maris Maori Missionary who is known lovingly as the Apostle of Hawke's Bay... This trip with a Maori Missioner had been an object lesson. <i>(Jan 1955,Marist Messenger,25</i>)

### Maori Missioner
 
 <i>n.</i> [cf also more modern use by Marists etc.]

 <i>n.</i> a pest of citrus fruit in Queensland (<i>Phyllocoptrula oleivora</i> (Ashm.))

 <i>n.</i> a Ford Mark II Zephyr

### Maori Oats
 
 <i>n.</i>

### Maori onion
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a fleshy Bulbinella spp. growing in grassland areas.

>  .. burning, if frequent, results in an increase of such unpalatable native plants as Maori onion, cotton plant and bidibidi ... <i>(May 3, 1945,NZ Farmer,11</i>)

>  <b>ANDERSON</b> I let my eyes feast on the tall prickly Spaniards and the patches of golden Maori onions, growing along the roadside. <i>(1966,Over the River,113</i>)

>  .. we have a dreary stony flat, covered with rough vegetation and the yellow spike-flower - an orchid, I believe - sometimes called the Maori onion. <i>(Dec 7, 1872,Otago Witness,2</i>)

>  <b>MACFARLANE</b> The Maori onion, a yellow hyacinth, comes up each year over many cooler tussock faces, but its only useful contribution is the amount of humus its dying fronds return to the soil. <i>(1954,Te Raka,40</i>)

>  <b>Relph</b> Maori onion (<i> Bulbinella augustifolia </i>) is a small bulb whose bright flowerheads grow to 30-60 centimetres high in the spring and add to the attractiveness of the tussocklands. <i>(2007,From Tussocks to Tourists,55</i>)

>  <b>MORELAND</b> One sometimes finds an upland covered with the orange gold of the Maori onion - <i>Bulbinella Rossii</i>. <i>(1911,Through South Westland,xii</i>)

>  <b>MOORE & IRWIN</b> <i>BULBINELLA ANGUSTIFOLIA, Maori onion.</i> The eastern foothills of the Canterbury and Otago mountains are the home of this species, but it must once have spread further out on to the plains. ... All parts have a peculiar but not at all onion-like smell. <i>(1978,Oxford Book of NZ Plants,184</i>)

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> Chaffey, despite his extraordinary toughness, is said to have nearly poisoned himself with brews of varying hideosity from trees, leaves, shrubs, modest growths such as Maori onion, ... <i>(1981,Exiles of Asbestos Cottage, The,71</i>)

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> MAORI ONION (<i>Bullbinella</i> spp.) Native growing about 40cm tall from small bulbs. Seldom seen in densely populated areas or highly productive farmland, but abundant in some elevated regions including hill pasture in Otago. ... <i>(1981,Weeds of Roadsides,91</i>)

>  <b>DAWSON</b> <i>Bulbinella hookeri</i> ranges from the North Island mountains to North Canterbury; <i>B. angustifolia</i>, sometimes inappropriately known as `Maori onion', ranges through the drier eastern mountains of the South Island from North Canterbury southwards; ... <i>(1988,Forest Vines to Snow Tussocks,181</i>)

>  The Moa hunters were more favoured ... in this district [Canterbury] with a supply of food, ... there being an abundance of `Maori onion', fern-root, and birds, together with caves to dwell in. <i>(Nov 1884,NZ Journal of Science,295</i>)

>  <b>DON</b> Some slopes are golden with the `Maori Onion', others bright with the `Maori Tobacco'. <i>(1907,Chinese Mission Work,22</i>)

>  <b>MARTIN</b> Where the ground was moist but scarcely swampy the MAORI ONION (<i>Chrysobactron hookeri</i>) with tall spikes of yellow flowers added a touch a colour. <i>(1961,Flora NZ,249,1929 ed.</i>)

>  <b>WRIGHT</b> ..on this self-guided nature walk, which passes through beech forest and clearings brightly coloured with yellow Maori onion (<i>Bulbinella hookeri</i>) in summer. <i>(1990,Tramping in South Island Forest Parks,95</i>)

>  Along the line in various patches we noted numerous Celmesias Antherecum Hookeru [sic] with its golden flowers in tufts on spikes, resembling the Scillas to some extent, and called Maori onion. <i>(Nov 30, 1892,North Otago Times,4</i>)

>  What lady in the Row ever enjoyed such a gallop, where the sense was stimulated with the bracing warrior perfume of manuka, grey in bloom, and the flying track was burnished with the golden wealth of waving Maori onion? <i>(Dec 25, 1901,Otago Witness,5</i>)

### Maori option
 
 <i>n.</i> the right of a person of Maori descent to choose whether to register on the Maori or the general electoral roll. [Electoral Act 1993, s. 76.]

>  As the next election appears on the horizon, a sense of futility is being sown in my psyche. Its source had been traced to the latest bit of political manoeuvring under the convenient title of the Maori Electoral Option... The final burlesque is when one' realises that the Maori option is provided every five years when the electoral cycle is every three years. <i>(May 4, 2001,Evening Post,4</i>)

>  Even the Dictionary of New Zealand English (DNZE) (1997) is patchy in its coverage of legal terms, having for example no entry for disputes tribunal, family protection, incorporated society, Maori option, protection order, resource consent. The most recent edition of Butterworths New Zealand Law Dictionary (1995) is useful but commonly gives specialist definitions taken from the interpretation sections of statutes <i>(Jan 1999,NZWords,1</i>)

### Maori orchard
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  On the fertile river silt he experimented with a few fruit trees - a "Maori orchard" he called it - where friends could enjoy the fruits of his planting. <i>(Feb 21, 1969,Listener (New Zealand Listener),4</i>)

### Maori oven
 
 <i>n.</i> a hangi,umu or copper maori consisting of a hole dug in the ground, lined with hot stones, on which the raw food is placed wrapped in leaves, and the whole covered (See [cf also native oven](../C#cf-also-native-oven),[earth oven](../E#earth-oven),[see Williams)](../S#see-williams)))

>  [photo caption] The potatoes being taken out of the Maori oven. <i>(Jan 14, 1903,Otago Witness,41</i>)

>  [photo caption] Tangi at Ruapuke: opening the "umu" or Maori oven. <i>(Oct 24, 1900,Otago Witness,28</i>)

>  <b>YATE</b> When the stones are heated to redness, they are taken out of the oven, and the place cleared from any remains of burning wood. <i>(1835,Account of NZ, An,108</i>)

>  Then followed dinner, served <i>al fresco</i> in Maori style; fish, potatoes and cabbage, perfectly cooked, all steamed in Maori ovens. <i>(Oct 1, 1863,Letters from Canterbury, NZ,75,1914 ed.</i>)

>  Mason and J.M. opened up Maori Oven in afternoon. <i>(Apr 19, 1866,Puketoi Station Diary</i>)

>  <b>BATHGATE</b> Not long since Ramshorn discovered some Maori ovens with Moa bones in them, and he dug out a great many ... for the Museum. <i>(1881,Waitaruna,144</i>)

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> <i>Koohua</i>, n. 1. <i>maori</i>[sic] <i>oven</i>; 2.<i> boiler. </i> <i>(1871,Dictionary of the NZ Language,56</i>)

>  When the rain ceased we examined a number of old Maori ovens ... <i>(1891,NZ Journal of Science,1/195</i>)

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> [W. also uses native o <i>Kohua</i>, n. <i>1 maori oven. 2 boiler. </i> <i>(1892,Dictionary of the NZ Language,65</i>)

>  A little further on were groups of Maori ovens, and quantities of small fragments of moa-bones. <i>(1895,TrNZI,27/237</i>)

>  The food was cooked in the <i>umu</i>, or Maori oven of which numbers may be seen in any bay on Banks Peninsula. <i>(1900,Canterbury Old & New,153</i>)

>  KO-HIA (<i>passiflora tetranda</i>). - ... The seeds are bruised into pulp, and heated in a Maori oven; ... <i>(1902,Brett's Colonists Guide,743</i>)

>  KO-HIA (<i>passiflora tetranda). - ... The seeds are bruised into pulp, and heated in a Maori oven; ... <i>(1902,Brett's Colonists Guide,743</i>)

>  <b>BAUCKE</b> `Coppa Maori,' `Maori oven,' etc. - these are the slang names given by those who know no better. By the natives it is called an `Umu' and universally a `Haangi'. <i>(1905,Where the White Man Treads,102</i>)

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> <i>Kohua, 1.</i> n. <i>Maori oven</i>. ... 2. A vessel used for boiling food by means of heated stones, <i>boiler</i>. [so 1957/126] <i>(1917,Dictionary of the Maori Language,148</i>)

>  ... Quarterman can make one of his famous Maori ovens for the vegetables - ... <i>(Dec 11, 1911,Triad, The,22</i>) [NOTES: A.A.Grace (The Fate of Morland)]

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> I remember when some heavy flax was cleared at Goodwood several large Maori ovens were uncovered ... <i>(1939,First White Boy Born in Otago,99</i>)

>  <b>MATTHEWS</b> Certain of the assembly immediately set to work heating the Maori ovens in readiness for the coming feast. <i>(1940,Matthews of Kaitaia,20</i>)

>  <b>WHEELER</b> Here also we inspected a pile of blackened stones, the remains of a Maori oven. <i>(1973,Historic Sheep Stations NI,47</i>)

>  <b>BAXTER</b> And by the bay itself-were cliffs with carved names/ And a hut on the shore beside the Maori ovens. <i>(1979,Collected Poems,45,1946 ed.</i>)

>  <b>McLEOD</b> When you come to think of it, this [brick bread oven] is only the European version of the Maori oven. <i>(1970,Glorious Morning,37</i>)

>  Scraps of moa bones are frequently found charred in the Maori ovens. <i>(Oct 9, 1890,Otago Witness,20</i>)

>  <b>YATE</b> When the stones are heated to redness, they are taken out of the oven, and the place cleaned from any remains of burning wood; a part of the hot stones are then placed in the oven again; and a wreath of damped leaves is laid round the outside, to prevent the earth from falling in, or the food from rolling to the side. <i>(1835,New Zealand (Heaphy),108</i>)

>  In a little time hollows among the "wild Irishman" bushes were pointed out to the credulous as Maori ovens. <i>(Dec 7, 1897,Otago Witness,50</i>)

>  (|= macron precedes) oven, o|o|hmoo [umu]] <i>(1826,Boultbee Journal</i>) [NOTES: [1826-28 Boultbee Vocabulary in 1979 Begg 283]]

>  A Mauri oven deserves that a few lines should be devoted to it. <i>(Dec 12, 1840,Journal of Ensign Best,265,1966 ed.</i>)

>  So ... they cut off a large slice of the blubber, which was cooked in a Maori oven, and eaten with great relish. <i>(Jul 20, 1840,Nelson Examiner</i>) [NOTES: 1844 Monro Notes of a Journey (Nelson <i>Examiner</i> July 20 et seq.) in 1898 Hocken Contributions 257]

>  <b>BRUNNER</b> The natives searching for food found a recently-made Maori oven and a <i>wari. </i> <i>(1848,Journal of an Expedition</i>) [NOTES: 1848 Brunner Exped. Middle Isd in 1959 Taylor Early Travs. 278]

>  <b>POWER</b> In the interior of the Pa the Wahines[e-acute], or matrons, are busy weaving flax-mats, cleaning potatoes or fish, or engaged in the superintendence of a Maori oven, or a huge gipsy-looking cauldron, called a `go-ashore', and can only afford to greet the visitor with a whining `tena koe pakeha', sighing as though they were very much to be pitied. <i>(1849,Sketches in NZ,160</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> the remains of an old Maori oven, or that which resembles such remains, found on the Canterbury Plains (See [cf. Maori hole](../M#maori-hole))

>  <b>ELWELL</b> The plain was. ... frequently broken by what are known as `Maori Ovens', that is large holes, almost round, with sloping sides about four feet deep. <i>(1878,Boy Colonists,28</i>)

>  Close by this whare there were three depressions, each six feet across and 2 feet 6 inches deep. These were Maori ovens, ... <i>(1914,History of North Otago,43,1978 ed.</i>)

>  <b>STUDHOLME</b> Keep a look out for Maori ovens and holes. <i>(1940,Te Waimate,150</i>) [NOTES: (Canterbury)]

>  <b>AYSON</b> He was riding Frank full tilt down a ridge when the pony went bang into a Maori oven covered with rubbish. <i>(1937,Thomas,115</i>)

>  <b>BARRY</b> If he came to a maori oven or swampy patch, he would lift the lantern above his head, as a signal to stop. <i>(1966,In the Lee of the Hokonuis,372</i>)

### Maori overdrive
 
 <i>n.</i> driving in neutral [1985 Jan 11 Wellington Teachers' Club used in Wellington]

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>Maori overdrive</i> sliding your car in neutral downhill; offensive. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,72</i>)

### Maori painkiller
 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Drimys axillaris (Wintera axillaris)</i>, horopito, pepper tree, (cf. painkiller)

>  <b>FEATON</b> A decoction of the leaves [sc. of Horopito or Drimys axillaris] is not uncommonly used by bushmen and others to allay pain and is honoured with the name of "Maori Painkiller". <i>(1889,Art Album NZ Flora,13</i>) [NOTES: Cite given in Orsman database as "A decoction of the leaves is known as Maori painkiller, and I have heard the tree itself so called. "]

>  The New Zealand South Island pepper-plant, <i>Wintera colorata</i> (better known under its old name, <i>Drimys axillaris</i>), is one of our few magnolias ... Among bushmen a decoction of the leaves used to be much in vogue as a medicine, under the name of `Maori painkiller'. tree itself so called <i>(1925,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,8/107</i>) [NOTES: (Finlay)]

>  <b>LAING & BLACKWELL</b> <i>Drimys axillaris [(The Pepper-tree)] </i>... A decoction of the leaves is often used by bushmen as a medicine, and has earned the name of "Maori Painkiller.' ... Called by the settlers the Pepper Tree. Maori name <i>Horopito.</i> [The plant is closely related to the South American Winter's Bark.] <i>(1907,Plants of NZ,172,1940 p. 180 ed.</i>)

>  <b>MACLEAN</b> Jacky made a poultice of horopito leaves, 'Maori pain killer', which he steamed over the fire and applied to Bannister's knee. <i>(1994,Tararua : the Story of a Mountain Range,69</i>)

>  According to "Plants of New Zealand," [sic] a decoction of the leaves of the pepper tree is often used by bushmen as a medicine, and has earned the name of "Maori Painkiller." [sic] <i>(Sep 19, 1906,Otago Witness,73</i>) [NOTES: Punctuation as given]

 <i>n.</i>
