>  Mouri path. <i>(1841,NZ Journal,2/35/122</i>)

### Maori peach
 
 <i>n.</i> a wild peach [?get Atkinson's ref. planting peach-stone in RA Paps 1/ near end ?1861 ASA Journal]

>  She has been travelling the Whanganui River collecting plant material that Maori gardeners have saved since the last century, including the small, tender-fleshed peaches called Maori peaches which were probably a legacy from early whalers and missionaries. <i>(Oct 2, 1999,Dominion,23</i>)

>  Do you know the Korako peach? ... The Korako (meaning white) is the old Maori peach, as it is called, the big white honey-sweet fruit the natives received from the missionaries ... <i>(Apr 10, 1920,Quick March,37</i>)

>  ... from the `Sixties' to the early `Nineties' of the last century the peach flourished exceedingly in the warmer areas of the North Island ... Trees were first raised from stones brought to New Zealand by missionaries and early settlers; they were subsequently grown so widely by the Maori that they became known as the `Maori' peach. None survives today; all having been destroyed during the late `Nineties' mainly by two diseases ... <i>(1946,NZ Geographer,247</i>) [NOTES: Cunningham Plant Diseases]

### Maori picnic
 
 <i>n.</i> ? a picnic of stolen fruit;? free fruit; ? slacking instead of working

>  <b>GLOVER</b> While we were picking the berry fruits we were expected to whistle all the time ...the whistling was not quite as disconnected as would lead any earnest adult picker to suppose that we were making a Maori picnic of it under the blackberry bush ... <i>(1981,Hot Water Sailor,14</i>)

### Maori pig
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>HYDE</b> Maori pigs are the only inhabitants, little grey freshwater lobsters are easy to catch, and the big shining rainbows simply ask to be lifted from the water. <i>(1934,Journalese,125</i>)

>  Droves of Maori pigs began to devastate the crops. <i>(Dec 21, 1900,Auckland Weekly News,Suppl. 3</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a wild pig [ originally kept domestically by Maori. ]

>  All those who are able to do so should fence in their land and fence out the Maori pigs and cattle before stocking their farms. If the white men openly slay the Maori pigs ... <i>(Nov 1880,Hawera Star,10</i>)

>  Complaints reach us of considerable damage being done to the Mawhitiwhiti Road by Maori pigs rooting up the foundation. <i>(Jun 30, 1888,Egmont Star,8</i>)

### Maori Pioneer Battalion
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <i>Te Hokowhitu A Tu</i> is the story of the Maori Pioneer Battalion in World War I... The title comes from the name given to the first contingent of "pioneers" who embarked from Wellington in 1915 bound for Egypt and Malta... In 1992, Major Wally Fraser found the lost colour of the Maori Pioneer Battalion in the Museum of New Zealand. <i>(1995,Mana,No. 10 : 83</i>)

>  The arrangement refer to places in France and Flanders where the Pioneer Maori Battalion fought in World War I. <i>(Jun 9, 2001,Listener (New Zealand Listener),54</i>)

 <i>n.</i>

>  His Worship investigated and came forth with the positive verdict of Haki Haki or Maori pox.<i>Ibid.</i> 16 The disease was not chicken pock or small pock or Maori pock - but just plain kapok <i>(Aug 2, 1913,NZ Observer,4</i>)

>  The black marks on the child were caused by the Maori pock. <i>(Nov 13, 1890,Otago Witness,24</i>)

 <i>n.</i>

 <i>n.</i> a brumby; a broken-in wild-horse (of King Country district)

 <i>n.</i>

 <i>n.</i>

We preferred to reserve our appetites for lunch ... . <i>(1908,My Simple Life in NZ,71</i>)

 <i>n.</i> an earth oven, hangi, copper Maori

### Maori potato
 
 <i>n.</i> any of the varieties of small purple or reddish potatoes, possibly from stock introduced in late 18th or early 19th century

>  ...it's unclear exactly when the group of dark purple types known collectively as Maori potatoes, taewa or peru peru (apparently because they were introduced by Peruvian sailors) first arrived here. <i>(Feb 2004,Sunday (Sunday Star Times Magazine),15</i>)

>  Icon Restaurant offers baby puaua from Stewart Island occasionally, as well as certain types of seaweed to put under their oysters, and a variety of purple Maori potato known as moi moi. <i>(Jun 1, 1999,Evening Post,11</i>) [NOTES: check spelling puaua]

>  There will be contrasts in texture, such as crisp saffron wafers alongside tender nuggets of scallop, and there will be contrasts in flavour like sweet balanced with sour as in his farmed venison on a brussels sprout puree with Maori potato ravioli. <i>(Aug 15, 2005,Internet - see Notes,00</i>) [NOTES: cuisine.co.nz/index.cfm?pageID=33528]

>  American whalers brought what are now called Maori potatoes and one called 'Nga oto outi' with deep eyes and purple skin was dug for me. <i>(Aug 1991,NZ Gardener,30</i>)

>  <b>ASKEW</b> [Auckland] They have darkish coloured, thin skins, and when cooked are mealy, and have a good flavour. <i>(1857,Voyage to Australia & NZ,347</i>)

>  <b>RICHARDS</b> <i>THELYMITRA ... longifolia </i>... A ground orchid having a fleshy leaf ... Common thelymitra, Maori Potato. MAI KUKU (claw). <i>(1952,Chatham Islands,23</i>)

>  <b>MASTERS</b> Jeff and his companion ran out of food, and for 6 weeks after their arrival at Herbertville, had to live on shell fish and Maori potatoes. Captain Cook had left seed potatoes with the Maoris, hence the Maori potatoes. [1856] <i>(1959,Tales of the Mails,134</i>)

>  <b>HILLIARD</b> Maoris speak] `You got any Maori potatoes this year?' `How you mean Maori potatoes?' `You can tell a real Maori potato by you cut it and it's got a design right through.' <i>(1969,Green River,13</i>)

>  <i>Maori potato [=] microtis: sun orchid</i> [s] <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,46</i>)

>  This long length of chipolata shape, coiled itself, snake-like, around a mound of purple Maori potato, made into a "Moi Moi Salad." <i>(Jul 14, 2001,Dominion,6</i>)

>  The first permanent European introduction of real consequence to the Bay of Islands was a type of potato, brought by Marion du Fresne's expedition of 1772 and called by the New Zealanders uwhi - the name of one of their own yams. The uwhi potato and other early introduced varieties - known collectively today as `Maori potatoes' although each has its own distinctive name - are still found growing in some home gardens, more especially in the rural areas of the north. <i>(1971,NZ Heritage,1/253</i>)

>  <b>BURTON</b> These early varieties of Irish potato came to be known as `Maori potatoes' and are still found in country areas of Northland. <i>(1982,Two Hundred Years of NZ Food & Cookery,14</i>)

>  It is always a great pity our so called Maori potatoes, with their ...purplish-red skins and flecks of purple through the flesh, have been allowed to become virtually extinct. <i>(Apr 27, 1983,Evening Post,33</i>)

>  <b>A.D. THOMSON</b> By chance in 1984 I received a boole from the International Potato Center at Lima, Peru ... and the cover shows Andean potato cultivars - some very similar to the `Urenika' type. I have speculated that perhaps this similarity provides some support for the notion that lines of the maori potato came to Aotearoa direct from South America and not via Europe. <i>(Jun 1985,Letter</i>)

>  The Maori Potato The ancient potato cultivars commonly referred to as Maori potatoes may be descended from tubers given to the Maori by early explorers, or they may have come direct from South America with the early colonisation of the Pacific. Doug Yen ... now at the Bishop Museum appears to have been the first to collect and study these early potatoes (<i>The Potato Journal summer 1961/61:</i> 2-5) and he refers to the introductions by de Surville in 1769 and 1772 and by Cook in 1773. Yen also refers to the possible introduction direct from South America, since Callao in Peru and Acapulco in Mexico were provisioning ports for whaling vessels. This year I grew `Urenaka' (Yen refers to `Urenika') ... and I understand this cultivar came from the East Coast. It is certainly an unusual potato with purplish stems and tubers. ... Yen ... notes that they resemble the storied (Iodine) potato of Ireland, used as a talisman against illness. ... Audrey [Eagle] said that when she gave some [similar] tubers to a Maori lady she exclaimed `ah, rokekuri'. <i>(1983,Botany Division (DSIR) Newsletter,No. 85/11</i>) [NOTES: (A.D. Thomson)]

>  INCIDENCE OF VIRUS DISEASES IN `MAORI' POTATOES [heading] The `Maori' potato is one of our earliest introduced plants and there is documented evidence that James Cook in 1769 gave potato tubers to the Maori people ... <i>(1987,Triennial Report Botany Division,1982-1984/68</i>)

>  Even the Maori potato clearings barely scarred the primal beauty of forests. <i>(Jul 22, 1922,Auckland Weekly News,18</i>)

>  While Red's lamb chops were passing over into whatever a lamb becomes when it has a birthday, sheliked it as it pranced among crushed Maori potatoes and a field of rocket. <i>(Jun 21, 2003,Dominion Post,F6</i>)

>  Maori are working with Massey University students planting about 25 varieties of Maori potatoes (taewa) as part of continuing research into seed and growth characteristics. <i>(Nov 9, 2000,Dominion,11</i>)

>  Maori potatoes selling now... The commercialisation of the Maori potato, or taewa, has been a long-term project for researcher Nick Roskruge. <i>(Nov 2003,Massey,No. 15/8</i>)

### Maori privet
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) hangehange, a privet-like species [<i>Geniostoma ligustrifolium.</i>]

>  In the southern part of Hawera district, early bush flows of nectar can be expected from five finger, Maori privet, and kowhai from September onward. Usually there is a light flow from Maori privet (Geniostoma ligustrifolium) in this area, and this surplus is taken from the bush hives in early November. <i>(May 15, 1956,NZ Journal of Agriculture,427</i>)

### Maori problem
 
 <i>n.</i> [cf. Maori difficulty; Native problem/difficulty]

>  So indeed there would seem to be a Maori problem from every aspect. <i>(Mar 3, 1937,Tomorrow,3(9) : 276</i>)

### Maori prophetess
 
 <i>n.</i>

Her panacea for all ills is to be liberal doses of brandy, with the result that there has been an enormous run on the hotel stock. <i>(May 14, 1906,Taranaki Herald,5</i>)

>  Mr. Hakuere M.H.R., has visited the Maori prophetess, and reports that everything is quiet. <i>(Mar 25, 1885,Wairarapa Daily,2</i>)

### Maori PT
 
 <i>n.</i> resting, loafing, lotus-eating

>  I suppose you strained yourself doing Maori PT. <i>(Jul 5, 1943,NZEF Times,III/106 : 6</i>)

>  I recall once (while lying quietly on a bunk participating in what was knownn as 'Maori PT) watching a mouse stealthily and carefully descending down [sic] a length of wire to access the bag of suspended flour. <i>(2007,Internet - see Notes,2</i>) [NOTES: www.nzdeercullers.org.nz/literature.aspx]

>  <b>TURNER</b> In New Zealand the word Maori sometimes enters into slang..in a term like Maori P.T. (From the army PT = physical training) to mean lying down and doing nothing, what the Australian calls spinebashing. <i>(1966,English Language in Aust. & NZ, The,135</i>)

>  <b>THE SARGE</b> No fatigues, no guards, no P.T. (Physical drill, Pakeha type, as opposed to Maori P.T., flat out on one's back.. preferably asleep). <i>(1950,Excuse my Feet!,16</i>)

>  <b>Bernard Gadd</b> 'We got to shift. Let's move,' said the cop. 'No time for Maori P.T.,' and he laughed, though in a friendly sort of way. <i>(1981,Where to Go?,12</i>)

>  <b>GOLDSMITH</b> Banks' next misdemeanour was his use of the term 'Maori PT' (Maori physical training) in Parliament... The Speaker, Peter Tapsell, came to Banks' defence, pointing out that both Maori and pakeha working in his shearing shed regularly used the term and that Banks' use of it had not harmed race relations. <i>(1997,John Banks : A Biography,229</i>)

>  Three or four years ago they were at a very low ebb, with the Moutoa Gardens occupation proving highly divisive.. and the John Banks "Maori PT" and John Carter "Hone" incidents still fresh in the mind. <i>(Mar 27, 1999,Listener (New Zealand Listener),17</i>)

>  I suppose you strained yourself doing Maori P.T. <i>(Jul 5, 1943,2NZEF Times,6</i>) [NOTES: 1943 July 5 2NZEFT JNZ 6]

>  <b>JACKSON</b> Maori Physical Training, or Maori P.T. ... meant no more and no less strenuous exercise than lying on the flat of his back on his bed. <i>(1943,Passage to Tobruk,105</i>)

>  <i>Maori</i> P.T. carried out in a recumbent position. <i>(1946,Johnny Enzed in the Middle East,Glossary</i>)

>  `Maori P.T.' was a very popular exercise. <i>(1946,Tramper,18(8)/8</i>)

>  <b>SARGE</b> <i>Maori PT </i>(Kiwi-ism) P.T. that entails lying on a soft bed, preferably sleeping or resting, when there is work to be done. <i>(1950,Excuse my Feet!</i>) [NOTES: (Glossary)]

>  <b>MASON</b> used among trampers 'Always, I think, used by those who had been in the Army.' (see also 1946 Tramper 18(8):8.) [check] <i>(1950,Personal Communication</i>)

>  I would include among New Zealand English words: <i>Maori P.T. </i> <i>(May 6, 1952,VUC First Year English Class</i>)

>  <b>SLATTER</b> Yes she was. A good worker. Come on pakeha she would say. Get up from there. None of that Maori P.T. <i>(1959,Gun in My Hand, A,43</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> `Doing a bit of Maori P.T., eh, Jim? Come on. We need your help to win this game of poker.' <i>(1965,Outcasts,149</i>)

>  <b>TURNER</b> In New Zealand the word <i>Maori</i> sometimes enters into slang with contemptuous connotation but there is a good-natured tolerance, even tinged with envy for an admirable adjustment to life's problems, in a term like <i>Maori P.T.</i> (from the army P T = physical training) to mean lying down and doing nothing, what the Australian calls <i>spinebashing. </i> <i>(1966,English Language in Aust. & NZ, The,135</i>)

>  <i>Maori P.T</i>.: a rest, a sleep <i>(1980,Lynfield College, Auckland</i>) [NOTES: Goldie Brown Collection]

>  The complaint to the [Race Relations Conciliator] was in one sense rightly made because `Maori time' belongs to a whole genre of derogatory sayings which include such negative ones as `Maori day off', "Maori PT', Maori car', etc. <i>(Sep 24, 1983,Listener (New Zealand Listener),59</i>) [NOTES: [Ranginui Walker]]

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> We march and train (up at 5.30am, Maori PT also known as siesta from 12.30 to 4.30, for August ending [in Egypt] is still hot) gundrill and manoeuvres. <i>(1983,Down from Marble Mountain,262</i>)

>  <i>Maori P.T.</i>: lying down <i>(Feb 1981,Avondale College Slang Words in Use</i>) [NOTES: Goldie Brown Collection]

>  Brown and white there were no differences between the colours then [in WW2]. The happy members of the 28th Battalion referred to themselves as `horis' (I've never known what the word meant, but now to use it is as big an insult as for a white to be called a Pakeha), and spoke of `Maori time' (some time) and `Maori PT' (lying on one's bedroll) and we followed their lead, not knowing that they, and we, were using such appallingly insulting terms to the Maori race that a special Race Relations Conciliator would have to be installed to quell them. <i>(Jul 1, 1988,Dominion,10</i>) [NOTES: (John C. Mcab)]

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>Maori PT</i> taking a rest; jokey, offensive. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,72</i>)

>  `We also have sport in our camp. We have all kinds of sport, such as hockey, cricket, Maori PT, practicing the lying load, etc., etc. <i>(1943,Flak</i>) [NOTES: 1943 `Flak' Magazine of 26th Composite Anti Aircraft Regiment in Iris Latham The WAAC Story (1986) 140]

>  Why is the term "Scotsman's grandstand" acceptable when "Maori PT" is not? <i>(Feb 19, 1995,Sunday Star Times,C4</i>)

>  <b>JACKSON</b> Maori physical training, or Maori P.T. ... meant no more or less strenuous exercise than lying on the flat of his back on his bed ... <i>(1943,Passage to Tobruk,105</i>)

### Maori quail
 
 <i>n.</i> = koreke

>  <i>koreke,</i> a bird (Coturnix novae zealandiae), Maori quail, now extinct, its place being taken by the imported Caliornia and Tasmania quail, now very plentiful. <i>(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/152</i>) [NOTES: Andersen Maori Words in English]

### Maori Queen
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>MILLER</b> The Maori Queen, Dame Te Atairangikaahu, lives at Waahi Marae in Huntly. Her ceremonial headquarters are at Turangawaewae Marae in Ngaruawahia. <i>(1986,Getting around NZ's North Island,59</i>)

>  <b>POPE, Diana & Jeremy</b> Ngaruawahia hosts the much venerated marae of the Maori Queen with its elaborately carved buildings. <i>(1986,Getting around NZ's North Island,41</i>)

>  <b>HANNAM, G.& McINTOSH,L.</b> By the riverbank at Ngaruawahia travellers can sometimes see great ceremonial canoes moored by Turangawaewae Marae, a splendid complex of buildings and home of the Maori queen. <i>(1988,Travelling NZ,160</i>) [NOTES: 2nd edition]

>  Prayers were presented by the Archbishop of New Zealand..; the Maori Queen, Dame te Atairangikaahu.. and the Bishop of Auckland. <i>(Feb 7, 1990,NZ Herald,Section I/2</i>)

>  <b>COWAN</b> The slayers of Timoti ... intended to lay the heart before Tepaea, or Tiaho, the Maori Queen, but she disapproved of their actions, so the trophy was not presented to her. <i>(1922,NZ Wars, The,2/470,1983 ed.</i>)

>  Do you know the Korako peach? ... The Korako (meaning white) is the old Maori peach, as it is called, the big white honey-sweet fruit the natives received from the missionaries ... <i>(Apr 10, 1920,Quick March,37</i>)

>  <b>KING</b> [Caption] Here he [sic. Archbishop Reeves] is flanked at Turangawaewae marae by the Maori Queen, Dame Te Atairangikaahu, and her husband Whatumoana Paki. <i>(1983,Maori: A Photographic & Social History,268</i>)

>  [Caption] The Archbishop of Canterbury, Dr. Robert Runcie, admires an elaboratelycarved ceremonial steering paddle being presented to him by the Maori Queen, Dame Te Atairangikahahu,.. yesterday. <i>(Apr 22, 1983,Evening Post,1</i>)

### Maori question
 
 <i>adj.</i> (See [cf Maori difficulty](../C#cf-maori-difficulty),[problem](../P#problem))

>  <b>MACLEAN</b> However, every well-wisher of the Maori race must hail with satisfaction a spirit of enquiry among the Europeans, as to the Maori question, which must lead to a more full appreciation and just recognition of their social and political rights. <i>(Nov 3, 1860,Letter</i>) [NOTES: 1860 Nov 3 Maclean to F.E. Maning in 1940 Cowan Sir Donald Maclean 77]

>  ... but anything more treacherous than the Home Govt. [sic] in luring on the Colony to such ... expenditure for the final settlement of this Maori question, ... I never heard of. <i>(Aug 5, 1864,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,2/117,1860 ed.</i>) [NOTES: (Jane M. Atkinson)]

>  ... the history of New Zealand since 1840 can be summarized under three heads: (1) English colonization involving land questions which interlocked with (2) the Maori question; ... <i>(1936,Handbook NZ Flora (List of Names),10</i>) [NOTES: (J.Rutherford)]

>  <b>103</b> To draw attention of the nursing world to this aspect [sc. health] of the Maori question, I have put together ... a few notes on my yet short experience among my people. <i>(Jul 1910,Kai Tiaki</i>)

>  <b>KELLY</b> [Verse title] `The Maori Question' - `Debit and credit' <i>(1902,Heather and Fern,91</i>)

### Maori quota
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Is it because the selectors are determined to have a "Maori quota", without regard to requirements for South Africa? <i>(Sep 28, 1947,NZ Observer,27</i>)

### Maori rabbit
 
 <i>n.</i> kiore, native rat

>  <b>CAPLES</b> [He was] driven back by want of provisions which compelled him to feed on `Maori rabbits' (Anglice, rats) <i>(1862,Diary</i>) [NOTES: 1862 Caples [a miner] Exploring Hollyford Valley in 1863 AJ. D/6/7 (Pyke Gfds Repts)]

>  <b>CAPLES</b> He then retreated up the Hollyford, ravenously hungry, to eat every `Maori rabbit' (rat) he could catch. <i>(Mar 9, 1863,Diary</i>) [NOTES: c1863 Mar 9 P.Q.Caples Diary in 1968 Hall-Jones Early Fiordland 78]

>  Driven back by the want of provisions, which compelled him to feed on `Maori rabbits' (<i>Anglice,</i> rats), Caples made a second attempt, and again succeeded in crossing the range near the head of the Greenstone River. <i>(1863,Report</i>) [NOTES: 1863 Report in 1887 Pyke History of the Early Gold Discoveries in Otago (1962) 151]

>  ... but for the aboriginal rodent, the Kiore, we may now look in vain. In the early day of the gold rush, they were not uncommon in the interior, used to be caught and eaten by the diggers under the name of Maori rabbits. <i>(1872,TrNZI,4/140</i>) [NOTES: (Thompson)]

>  <b>PYKE</b> <i>(1887,Early History of Otago,148</i>) [NOTES: [quotes or paraphrases the 1862 report above.]]

### Maori race
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  In these and many other ways the efforts of the Government have been successfully directed to the improvement and civilization of the Maori race. <i>(Oct 13, 1858,Memorandum by Native Secretary</i>) [NOTES: 1858 Oct 13 Memorandum by Native Secretary in 1949 Rutherford ed. Select Documents 105.4]

### Maori rat
 
 <i>n.</i> the rat introduced by the Maori, also called kiore (See [also bush rat](../A#also-bush-rat))

>  <b>ROBERTS</b> ... I think it was the Norwegian rat, which had destroyed the Maori rat and overrun the country. It also caught quail, ground larks, grass birds, and anything else that came its way. <i>(Jun 1856,Diary</i>) [NOTES: 1856 June W.H.S. Roberts Diary in 1947 Beattie Early Runholding 31]

>  <b>HURSTHOUSE</b> The Pakeha-Rat ate up the Maori-Rat. <i>(1865,England's NZ War,40</i>)

>  <i>(1871,TrNZI,3/3</i>) [NOTES: (Buller) [check]]

>  Dr Buller believed the so-called Maori rat, which lived in trees, was really identical with the common <i>Mus rattus </i>of Europe. <i>(1884,TrNZI,16/553</i>)

>  I believe this is the true Maori Rat - the <i>Mus maorium</i> of Professor Hutton - the Kiore Maori - the rat with whose presence we now are so largely favoured. <i>(1884,TrNZI,17/206</i>) [NOTES: (Hector)]

>  The tradition at that time [1855] among the Canterbury settlers was that the Maori rat was of a red colour. <i>(1890,TrNZI,23/196</i>) [NOTES: (White)]

>  The so-called Maori rat (<i>Mus Maorium,</i> Hutton). <i>(1896,TrNZI,28/4</i>) [NOTES: (Kirk)]

>  <b>COWAN</b> ... how the Maori rat, a cleanly animal that fattened on fallen berries, was caught ... <i>(1916,Bush Explorers,26</i>)

>  Some people have expressed abhorrence of the idea of eating rats, but my Maori friends were careful to explain that the Maori rat was an altogether different creature from the filthy-eating European rat. <i>(1921,TrNZI,20/56</i>) [NOTES: (Beattie)]

>  <i>Sceloglaux albifacies</i> Gray. Laughing-owl. Mr. Clouston reports this species from one South Island locality. No other first-hand or reliable information is available. The undoubted diminution in the numbers of the laughing-owl is usually ascribed to the practical extinction of the `Maori' rat. <i>(1923,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,6/84</i>) [NOTES: Myers Endemic Birds]

>  <b>REISCHEK</b> ... and was told later that sailors had wantonly destroyed these remains, which comprised human skeletons, and bones of Maori rats, seal, ocydromus, and kiwi. <i>(1930,Yesterdays in Maoriland,62,1933 ed.</i>)

>  <b>McLINTOCK</b> The Maori or native rat (<i>Rattus exulaus</i> Peale) was known to the Maoris as kiore. <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,3/49</i>)

>  <b>OGILVIE</b> Other pioneers in the Opihi district [of Canterbury] refer to the abundance of native pigeons, wood hens, native trout, fresh water crayfish, eels and Maori rats - which were small, grey, fluffy, vegetarian and evidently succulent. <i>(1971,Moonshine County,131</i>)

>  <b>PAYTON</b> I was much astonished to see a rat at that altitude... Had he been a kiore, or Maori rat, one could have given him a pathetic biography at once. <i>(1888,Round about NZ,159</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> kiore

>  <b>W.H.S. ROBERTS</b> ... I think it was the Norwegian rat, which had destroyed the Maori rat and overrun the country. It also caught quail, ground larks, grass birds, and anything else that came its way. <i>(Jun 1856,Diary,31</i>) [NOTES: 1856 June Diary in 1947 Beattie Early Runholding 31]

>  <b>HURSTHOUSE</b> The Pakeha-Rat ate up the Maori-Rat. <i>(1865,England's NZ War,40</i>)

>  <i>(1871,TrNZI,3/3</i>) [NOTES: (Buller) [check]]

>  Dr Buller believed the so-called Maori rat, which lived in trees, was really identical with the common <i>Mus rattus</i> of Europe. <i>(1884,TrNZI,16/553</i>)

>  I believe this is the true Maori Rat - the <i>Mus maorium</i> of Professor Hutton - the Kiore Maori - the rat with whose presence we now are so largely favoured. <i>(1884,TrNZI,17/206</i>) [NOTES: (Hector)]

>  The tradition at that time [1855] among the Canterbury settlers was that the Maori rat was of a red colour. <i>(1890,TrNZI,23/196</i>) [NOTES: (White)]

>  The kiore maori, or native rat, is said in Maori tradition to have been imported ... from Polynesia ... Many persons stoutly maintain that the old Maori rat is still with us ... <i>(1909,TrNZI,41/242</i>)

>  The so-called Maori rat (Mus Maorium, Hutton). <i>(1896,TrNZI,28/4</i>) [NOTES: (Kirk)]

>  <b>COWAN</b> ... how the Maori rat, a cleanly animal that fattened on fallen berries, was caught ... <i>(1916,Bush Explorers,26</i>)

>  Some people have expressed abhorrence of the idea of eating rats, but my Maori friends were careful to explain that the Maori rat was an altogether different creature from the filthy-eating European rat. <i>(1921,TrNZI,20.56</i>) [NOTES: (Beattie)]

>  The undoubted diminution in the numbers of the laughing-owl is usually ascribed to the practical extinction of the `Maori' rat. <i>(1923,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,6/84</i>) [NOTES: Myers Endemic Birds]

>  <b>REISCHEK</b> [He] was told later that sailors had wantonly destroyed these remains, which comprised human skeletons, and bones of Maori rats, seal, ocydromus, and kiwi. <i>(1930,Yesterdays in Maoriland,62,1933 ed.</i>)

>  <b>McLINTOCK</b> The Maori or native rat (Rattus exulans Peale) was known to the Maoris as kiore. <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,3/49</i>)

>  <b>OGILVIE</b> Other pioneers in the Opihi district [of Canterbury] refer to the abundance of native pigeons, wood hens, native trout, fresh water crayfish, eels and Maori rats - which were small, grey, fluffy, vegetarian and evidently succulent. <i>(1971,Moonshine County,131</i>)

### Maori remedy
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  I applied Maori remedies, consisting of tutu and hot stones. <i>(Feb 27, 1900,Taranaki Herald,2</i>)

### Maori renaissance
 
 <i>n.</i> a term describing the revival of Maori culture which occurred in the final decades of the 20th century.

>  <b>Michael King</b> By the late 1970s there was talk of a 'Maori renaissance' and ample evidence that a movement of this kind was gathering momentum. <i>(2003,Penguin History of New Zealand,415</i>)

### Maori reserve
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  It is erected on a Maori reserve, the lease of which has 11 years to run. <i>(Jan 5, 1856,Nelson Examiner and New Zealand Chronicle,2</i>)

>  MEETING. Maori Reserve Gold Mining Company - Swan Hotel, at half-past 2 p.m. <i>(Oct 15, 1866,West Coast Times,5</i>)

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> A statement in print that `Wairakinui is a Maori Reserve at the Waiau' greatly intrigued an old friend of mine, as he said Wairakinui was the name of a remote ancestor of his, but he could not understand how his name came to be over at the Waiau <i>(1944,Maori Place-Names of Otago,51</i>)

>  <b>ADAMS</b> On the north side of the [Lyttelton] harbour ... is situated the Maori Reserve, a beautiful ... valley which was retained by the natives ... <i>(1853,Spring in the Canterbury Settlement, A,49</i>)

>  The peninsula ... is a Maori Reserve, but is unoccupied except by ... an old whaler. <i>(1872,Appendix Proc. Otago Prov. Council,39</i>) [NOTES: (Black Stewart Island)]

### Maori road
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  In my opinion the Govt. ought to insist that as all European roads are open & free to maoris and Europeans, so all maori roads should be free to all. <i>(1960,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,I/502</i>) [NOTES: 1859 quote]

### Maori roast
 
 <i>n.</i> (offensive) a meal of fish and chips or pie and a beer [ca 1982 (Herb Wilford, Hawkes Bay); 1984 17 M E109 Pakuranga Coll. 22B Maori roast: fish in chips]

>  This delicacy of the English-speaking world is referred to in other circles as `shark and taties', `Maori roast', or `greasies' - understanding all depends on knowing the convention. Once the convention is established, it is just a matter of stating one's preference for two meat patties and a scoop, over a stuffed frankfurter. <i>(Aug 1986,Metro,194</i>) [NOTES: [quoting Imogen de la Bere, NZ Listener ?date]]

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>Maori roast </i>a pie and a jug; offensive. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,72</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>Maori roast</i> fish and chips; possibly offensive. <i>(1989,Dinkum Kiwi Dictionary, The,66</i>)

### Maori roll
 
 <i>n.</i> the roll of electors of a Maori electoral district

>  They all know I'm on the Maori roll but they won't stick their necks out and say who they're going to vote for. <i>(Nov 1999,North & South,86</i>)

>  This election promises to be the easiest yet for Maori roll voters to make their voices heard after law changes allowing them to vote quickly in any polling booth. <i>(Jul 17, 2002,Dominion Post,A9</i>)

### Maori rugby
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>ZAVOS</b> "Maori Rugby" is a term, favourable admittedly in its connotations, which is used to sum up a style which lacks distinction and discipline. <i>(1979,After the Final Whistle,193</i>)

### Maori sandal
 
 <i>n.</i> (usu. pl.) a makeshift sandal of ? palm-leaves or flax for bush-travelling; paraerae

>  <b>HAAST</b> The next day, ... the coast becoming very rocky, we exchanged our boots for Maori sandals (pereiras), which allowed better foothold on the smooth boulders. <i>(1861,Report on Topographical Exploration of Nelson,48</i>)

>  <b>DOBSON</b> When working on the beaches and in the river beds we wore on our feet Maori sandals. <i>(1930,Reminiscences,157</i>)

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> He would walk down to Dunedin wearing pararas (Maori sandals) and carrying his boots ... <i>(1939,First White Boy Born in Otago,178</i>)

>  <b>VON HAAST</b> On the beaches and in river-beds they [Haast and Dobson c1862] wore Maori sandals. <i>(1948,Julius Von Haast,201</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a primitive footcasing made of woven flax [used by Maori, pioneers and explorers.]

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> He would walk down to Dunedin wearing pararas (Maori sandals) and carrying his boots. <i>(1939,First White Boy Born in Otago,178</i>)

>  <b>PASCOE</b> Locke's boots had not been as satisfactory in Harper's opinion as Maori sandals made from flax. <i>(1959,Great Days in NZ Exploration,75</i>)

### Maori sandalwood
 
 <i>n.</i> maire

>  <b>LAING & BLACKWELL</b> <i>Mida salicifolia (Maori Sandalwood)</i>. A small tree, with variable leaves. <i>(1940,Plants of NZ,153</i>)

### Maori scare
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  They have told of `Maori scares,' when they had to flee for their lives from hostile natives, and of other `Maori scares,' which proved to be merely neighbourly visits ... and the wonderful joy of letters from Home (always spelt with a capital H) ... <i>(1940,Tales of Pioneer Women,vii,1988 ed.</i>)

### Maori school
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Life moves with a certain zest in a Maori school. <i>(Dec 17, 1935,Christchurch Star-Sun,12</i>)

>  <b>GRACE</b> <i>First</i>: that Maori Schools in or near to colonial towns are absolutely foolish. <i>(Apr 29, 1853,Letter</i>) [NOTES: 1853 Apr 29 T.S.Grace letter in Grace Pioneer Missionary (1928) 24]

>  Nurse Lewis ... has been giving a series of lectures ... to the pupils of a Maori school, ... <i>(Oct 1912,Kai Tiaki,99</i>)

>  <b>REISCHEK</b> On January 7, I went to inspect the Maori school there. <i>(1930,Yesterdays in Maoriland,75,1933 ed.</i>)

>  <b>HINTZ</b> The story is told of a new teacher at a Maori shcool in the Taupo district. <i>(1964,Trout at Taupo,220</i>)

>  Maori Schools</i> ... The Maori state primary schools (called `native schools' until 1947) were established after 1867 usually in remote, rural, and predominantly Maori communities. The official policy was one of assimilation. The schools provided an English-type education and special instruction in English for Maori children, the majority of whom still spoke Maori as their first language. <i>(Jun 29, 1988,Dominion,10</i>)

>  It is 30 years since the state-funded system of Native Schools (later called Maori Schools) ceased to exist. <i>(Jan 31, 1993,Sunday Times (NZ),15</i>)

### Maori scone
 
 <i>n.</i> = fried scone

>  Mum baked real white bread, Pakeha bread, and you know those Maori scones fried golden brown all over? <i>(Jun 1966,Arena,65/4</i>)

>  <b>FINLAYSON</b> [A Maori speaks:] Mum baked the bread and Maori scones there. Somehow the bread she bake taste <i>good</i>. Look at the ready-cut there for the kids' school lunch tomorrow. <i>(1966,Brown Man's Burden,119,1973 ed.</i>)

### Maori season
 
 <i>n.</i> a notional open season for Maori trout poachers

>  <b>HINTZ</b> But they are resourceful [220] poachers when they need a fish for the pot. When winter comes, the `Maori season' begins and, the law and the activities of rangers notwithstanding, they consider themselves entitled to take trout when they want them and by the simplest and most effective means - net, spear or pitchfork. <i>(1964,Trout at Taupo,210</i>)

### Maori seat
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>WALKER</b> After the wars the government introduced the Maori Representation Act, 1867, to establish the four Maori seats... Initially, it was intended that the Maori seats be held by Pakehas. <i>(Jun 15, 1985,Listener (New Zealand Listener),79</i>)

>  The terms main roll and supplementary roll are not British English; nor naturally are general and Maori rolls and electoral districts. (DNZE. has Maori seats.) <i>(Jan 1999,NZWords,2</i>)

>  The capturing of Maori seats by the Maori Party has undoubtedly shaken the political landscape. <i>(2008,Mana,85/72</i>)

### Maori settlement
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  A Maori settlement is being removed down to Kopua, near the confiscated boundary. <i>(Jun 5, 1876,North Otago Times,2</i>)

>  There was once a large Maori settlement on the spot where the discovery was made. <i>(Jun 27, 1884,Hawera & Normanby Star,2</i>)

>  THE NATIVE SETTLEMENT AT ROTORUA. <i>(Jul 25, 1906,Otago Witness,42</i>) [NOTES: Photo Caption]

>  It is reported that the natives at a Maori settlement on the Waikato river below Mercer have, during the last few days, been attacked with an epidemic which has resulted in the death of 17 of them. <i>(Jun 5, 1890,Evening Post,2</i>)

>  There is a Maori settlement adjoining. <i>(Apr 9, 1887,Auckland Weekly News,12</i>)

>  The same unhealthy huts, with the absence of all domestic convenience, still characterize the Maori settlements, as before the immigration of the Europeans. <i>(Dec 28, 1855,Daily Southern Cross,16</i>)

>  Those who have never been to see any of the more flourishing Maori settlements can have but little idea of the gay parties which they turn out all over the island for the purpose of celebrating Christmas. <i>(Feb 1, 1861,Daily Southern Cross,4</i>)

### Maori shoe
 
 <i>n.</i> = Maori sandal

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> The narrator says the second line should read `Old Jack Hughes had Maori shoes', according to the way he heard it as a child. These Maori shoes were flax or cabbage-tree sandals and many a white man wore them. <i>(1939,First White Boy Born in Otago,32</i>)

### Maori shout
 
 <i>n.</i> a treat in which one pays for one's own drink [1953 Feb 19 (Wgton Hotel) `to have a Maori', or `a Maori shout] (See [Parnell shout](../P#parnell-shout),[Yankee shout](../Y#yankee-shout))

### Maori showband
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Most of that time he spent overseas, touring Europe with Maori showbands, working the club circuit in Asia, playing Vegas... The showband era thrived, says Ratana, because there was nothing else offering then. "There was no TV, so you had to entertain people." <i>(Jan 14, 1991,Listener (New Zealand Listener),25</i>)

>  In the 1960s and 70s, Maori showbands criss-crossed the globe, their names emblazoned in lights above marquees from Las Vegas to London, Hawaii to Hamburg... What was distinctive about Maori showbands, apart from their Maoriness, was the versatility. Members had to be able to mime, dance and do comedy skits as well as sing and play several instruments... But the audience still gets what she describes as the "pure showband" routine, which means frequent instrument and costume changes and lost of comedy (Shirley Bassey and the Bee Gees are favoured spoof subjects) in between the straight songs. <i>(Feb 15, 2003,Dominion Post,F3</i>)

### Maori sickness
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [mate Maori](../M#mate-maori))

>  <b>BEAGLEHOLE</b> .. he thinks of sickness, particularly that kind of sickness which he calls Maori sickness, as very often caused by spirits as a punishment for the violation of tapu or custom. <i>Ibid.</i> 219 The sign most often taken to indicate this sort of sickness is that the sickness does not appear to be yielding readily to ordinary Maori or to pakeha methods of treatment. It is then considered to be a Maori sickness - a `mate Maori' as the phrase is commonly used - and for sure, safe, and quick results, treatment should be referred to a Maori expert, a tohunga, either male or female, who will use a combination of religious prayer, seance-possession, and magic to restore the sick person to health. The inner bark [of <i>M. fulgens</i>] was also boiled and the liquid was drunk for `Maori sickness'. <i>(1946,Some Modern Maoris,205</i>)

>  The pa wasted away after Te Kere's death about the turn of the century, affected by, among other thing, Maori sickness (mate Maori). <i>(Mar 31, 1984,Listener (New Zealand Listener),19</i>)

>  .. week to the psychiatric hospital to diagnose and treat `Maori sickness,' break tapu and sometimes hold Church services in Maori. <i>(Jan 30, 1853,Press, The,32</i>)

>  If a Maori is genuinely disturbed, in Maori terms, the answer again is to restore their mauri (life force), as for physical illness. This is particularly true for <i>mate</i> Maori, Maori sickness, which arises from spiritual and social causes, not physical ones (Pakehas would call it psychosomatic). <i>(May 1987,Metro,100</i>)

>  "Maori sickness was pretty strong in the time of our fathers and grandfathers. It is still happening if things are not done in the right manner." In 2000, Tariana Turia, who was then associate Maori Affairs minister [sic], questioned whether psychologists had the appropriate training to deal with issues such as Maori sickness (mate Maori). <i>(Mar 10, 2007,Dominion Post,A1</i>)

### Maori sidestep
 
 <i>n.</i> (offensive) running straight into an opponent in rugby [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  <b>ZAVOS</b> A "Maori sidestep" is another term frequently used by rugby men.. to describe the tactic of putting the shoulder down and charging through and over an opponent rather than actually side-stepping him. <i>(1979,After the Final Whistle,193</i>)

>  <i>Maori side-step</i>: direct charge <i>(Feb 1981,Avondale College Slang Words in Use</i>) [NOTES: (Goldie Brown Collection)]

>  The term `Maori sidestep' was made during the instant re-play of Stone's first-half try, which ended with a powerful, barging dive at the line. Mr Wallace complained that commentator Grant Nisbett described it as `the famous Maori sidestep' while others attribute the remark to Grahame Thorne. Mr Wallace said: `To me that comment means Maoris do not take the peaceful way, or sidestep, they bully their way through anything.' <i>(Aug 21, 1984,Dominion,3</i>)

>  (Similar expressions include <i>...Maori sidestep</i> = a head-on charge in rugby, a <i>Maori job/outfit/etc.</i> = something rough or disorganised - compare the use of <i>bush</i> in similar formations.) <i>(1985,NZ English (Gordon & Deverson),42</i>) [NOTES: Gordon and Deverson]

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>Maori sidestep</i> headlong knees-threshing barge through a tackle; doubly offensive. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,72</i>)

### Maori skirt
 
 <i>n.</i> piupiu

>  Australian lingerie designer Mrs Jean Griffin, has developed a 'Maori Princess' style for the coming season. Nightgowns will be calf-length and feature hundreds of tiny knife-edge pleats to give the swishing effect of a Maori skirt. <i>(Aug 1957,Te Ao Hou,19: 59</i>)

>  <b>WINTER</b> Mr. H. Bromley, mounted on a Maori pony. <i>(1939,King Country, The,41</i>)

>  <b>MIHAKA</b> I kind of suspected that [sc. ignorance of things Maori] when you referred to the so-called Maori skirt as consisting of beads, ... <i>(1984,Whakapohane,46</i>)

### Maori slabcake
 
 <i>n.</i> chocolate [1987 p.c. D.McGill]

### Maori spade
 
 <i>n.</i> a Maori digging implement; a ko

>  ... warriors armed with seven long spears having a rest for the foot on them like a mauri spade. <i>(Apr 20, 1842,Journal of Ensign Best,354,1966 ed.</i>)

### Maori spear
 
 <i>n.</i> taiaha

>  <b>HUNT</b> Saying this, [Rauparaha] took down a Maori spear, highly ornamented with feathers, and placed it in my hand. <i>(1866,Chatham Islands,17</i>)

### Maori station
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Plenty of timber upon the hills on the west side of the bay from the maori station at Raupaki. <i>(Dec 16, 1848,Torlesse Papers,41,1958 ed.</i>)

### Maori stones
 
 <i>n.</i> [>1954 R. Mason notes: `Not in OED cf <i>Maoris' sand</i> seems to be common idea of heavy in both uses as magnetite is a heavy mineral. Also `Maori, white' OED suppl. (and Morris)]

>  Small concretions popularly known as `Maori stones' on account of their high specific gravity, are frequently met with in the concentrates derived from [Humphrey's Gully] gravels [Hokitika] <i>(1908,NZ Geol. Survey Bulletin,No. 1. (NS) 89</i>) [NOTES: (Bell and Fraser)]

### Maori strum
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  He scratches out the simple introduction, the "Maori strum" that he had taken from Te Awamutu to the world. <i>(Jun 22, 1996,Listener (New Zealand Listener),26</i>)

>  <b>Geoff Chun on Split Enz</b> Way out of the blue, Phil Judd turns up with a song that has the first Maori strum played by a white boy. <i>(1996,TV1,00</i>)

>  Such defiantly non-digital instruments as cellos, trumpets and steel guitars ornament what is, at its core, nothing more than a bro singing to the Maori strum. <i>(Nov 30, 1996,Listener (New Zealand Listener),42</i>)

>  ...very different from the Maori strum, of which I am quite proud. <i>(Jul 23, 2004,Radio NZ / National Radio</i>) [NOTES: Manu Taylor , Nine to Noon]

>  If Maori mythology is ever updated, perhaps it could make reference to Maui wrestling the guitar from Elvis and inventing the legendary "Maori strum". <i>(Jun 24, 2000,Listener (New Zealand Listener),36</i>)

>  There's a strum on the guitar that is particularly New Zealand. Jinga jick, jinga jick... Don't Dream It's Over was a classic Maori strum, and there's been a lot of them. <i>(Oct 1, 1995,Sunday Star Times,D2</i>)

### Maori summer
 
 <i>n.</i> ? a humorous coinage: cf. Indian Summer

>  <b>HULME</b> ... the weather has held fine and windless; ("Maori summer', he says. `In the middle of winter.' `When better to get a bit of brown in.") <i>(1983,Bone People, The,251</i>)

### Maori swing
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>LOCKE</b> The boys swung round and round the pole, as with the Maori swing or morere. It was known as the Giant's Stride. <i>(1984,Kauri & The Willow, The,79</i>)

>  <b>ANGAS</b> The Maori or native swing is an amusement amongst the Taupo people which is obsolete upon the coast. A pole, generally the trunk of a <i>kaikatea</i> pine, is erected in the centre of an open space adjoining the village; flax ropes are suspended from the top, and, holding on to these, the natives swing round and round, in a manner similar to that which is practised in gymnasia and at country fairs in Europe. <i>(1847,Savage Life,2/118</i>)

### Maori tackle
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  -interview with George Nepia - referring to Welsh game in 1924 `I'd have to do a Maori tackle - eh - I'd bang on to two or three of them without the ball.' <i>(Aug 30, 1986,TV1</i>)

### Maori tea
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  I believe it would prove a great boon ... warning the natives of the dangers of inordinate use of tobacco, sleeping on the ground, and drinking Maori tea. <i>(1903,TrNZI,35/179</i>)

### Maori time
 
 <i>n.</i> a relaxed and flexible view of time as a regulator of people's activities, reputedly a Maori characteristic (compare Pakeha time).

>  The phrase "Maori time" has not been abroad much of late, but recent events have highlighted a linguistic gap that it will fill very neatly. Maori time is a period of 12 days, or the length of time that elapses between a major disaster and the government's getting around to doing something about it when that disaster affects a predominantly Maori community, say, in the Far North. <i>(Feb 20, 1999,Listener (New Zealand Listener),61</i>)

>  <b>McGill</b> "Kia ora," said Rangi Rangihau, grinning. "...Sorry we're late, my friends. You know how it is. Maori time, eh?" <i>(1995,Gold in the Creek,36</i>)

>  Cue all those jibes about "Maori time" and the new ones about the "grievance industry" and the Treaty "gravy train". <i>(Mar 22, 2003,Listener (New Zealand Listener),22</i>)

>  <b>CHAVASSE</b> Now, boss, we've been on Maori time for an hour or two. We'd better get on to pakeha time. What did you come here for. <i>(1990,Integrity,53</i>)

### Maori tobacco
 
 <i>n.</i> mullein

>  <b>DON</b> Some slopes are golden with the `Maori Onion', others bright with the `Maori Tobacco'. <i>(1907,Chinese Mission Work,22</i>)

>  Farmers and others who wish to obtain a satisfactory return will bear this in mind: the Havannah leaf is used for the outside wrapper of cigars, and other varieties are used for the bulk of the trade, and do well enough for making up the rolls. What is known as the Maori tobacco is used for this purpose. <i>(Jul 26, 1884,Bay of Plenty Times,2</i>)

>  There are Maori carved pipes, Maori tobacco (rourou), pawa shells, eel baskets, haka balls, Maori gods, and Maori devils, (fern beaters); in fact, the collection is too large for use to enumerate everything we saw. <i>(Sep 19, 1892,Taranaki Herald,2</i>)

### Maori township
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  It [the new bridge] will cross the Waipa at Whatiwhatihoe, and literally joins together the European and Maori townships, bringing Finch's hotel in a line with the native township, a facility which, judging by last night's proceedings, will be largely taken advantage of. <i>(May 1, 1882,Hawke's Bay Herald,3</i>)

>  Manutuke, a Maori township about fifteen miles from Gisborne, suffered a heavy loss within the past few days in the deaths of two tribal leaders, the second occurring under unusual circumstances. <i>(Apr 12, 1932,Evening Post,10</i>)

>  Some curiosity may possibly be felt as to the fate of the scheme in which he assisted - that of forming a Maori township; and on this point we are able to supply a little information derived from Karaitiana himself. <i>(May 15, 1858,Hawke's Bay Herald,3</i>)

>  The Maori township lands comprise areas which, mainly at the opening of the present century, were carved out of tracts of Maori land with the idea that they would be likely centres of population. <i>(1955,NZ Parliamentary Debates,307/2947</i>)

### Maori track
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>Ihimaera</b> ...although the old Maori tracks are still there, many of them have barbed-wire fences across them, necessitating a detour... <i>(2007,Ask the Posts,273</i>)

>  What if I never struck that Maori track? <i>(Jan 26, 1900,Auckland Weekly News,Suppl. 3</i>)

>  One occasion ... I travelled ... to Mangamuka by the old Maori track crossing the western shoulder of Maungataniwha. <i>(1911,TrNZI,43/605</i>)

>  <b>MACLEAN</b> On the eastern side the long rugged ridge that extends from the top of Cone to the summit of Mount Hector remained unexplored, although it seems certain that it was an early Maori track. <i>(1994,Tararua : the Story of a Mountain Range,155</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a well-defined track used by Maori before and during early European colonisation.

>  <b>SCOTT</b> The track through the bush was originally a Maori track used by war parties. <i>(1866,Letter,10</i>) [NOTES: in McGregor (1940) Pioneer Trails of Hawke's Bay (1975)]

>  <b>STUDHOLME</b> The Maori track which led through it to the lake had numerous turns and twists in it to avoid the clumps of snowgrass, manuka and cabbage-trees. <i>(1940,Te Waimate,67</i>)

>  <b>EASY</b> Our children had a three mile walk night and morning along an old Maori track which had to be constantly cleared. <i>(1991,Looking Backwards,29</i>)

>  <b>E.M.L STUDHOLME</b> The Maoris' track leads upwards to the Pa. <i>(1860,Diary,29</i>) [NOTES: in Studholme (1940) Te Waimate]

>  Mr Woon travelled over his district in all weathers, along the old Maori tracks, under the cliffs, across the swollen rivers and deep swamps, the discomforts of Maori kaingas, his hermit life etc. <i>(1882,Hawera & Normanby Star,Supplement 1</i>)

>  <b>ANDERSON</b> I can still remember riding over Maori tracks. <i>(1962,Life's Roundabout, A,168</i>)

>  <b>KERR</b> One might follow a Maori track and be led only into an eel trap in a swamp. <i>(1976,From the Beginning: chronicles of a county,44</i>)

### Maori treatment
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) the burning of the tops of root-crops after frost-bite in order to save a crop.

>  Where potatoes are frost-bitten it would be easy enough to mow off the tops and earth them over, which would have the same effect as the Maori treatment, and save the crop. <i>(Oct 1890,NZ Farmer,410</i>)

### Maori trout
 
 <i>n.</i> whitebait

>  They [whitebait] are koaro, also known as mountain trout; banded kokopu; giant kokopu; and short-jaw kokopu - which some would call Maori trout, says Bob McDowell, scientist for the National Institute of Water and Atmospheric Research. <i>(Sep 2005,Air New Zealand inflight magazine,67</i>) [NOTES: -]

>  In the back waters of the lake they were also able to catch a number of eels and kaka bulls, or Maori trout, and subsequently they caught alive several kiwis, which they brought with them on their return. <i>(Nov 29, 1866,Grey River Argus,2</i>)

>  The North Canterbury Society also wrote on the Kaiapoi Maori trout fishing, and it was resolved to assist them to the extent of five per cent on last year's revenue. <i>(May 18, 1891,Marlborough Express,3</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> [kokopu] (See [native trout](../N#native-trout),[New Zealand trout](../N#new-zealand-trout),[et al](../E#et-al))

>  In the back waters of the lake they were also able to catch a number of eels and kaka bulls, or Maori trout, and subsequently they caught alive several kiwis, which they brought with them on their return. <i>(Nov 29, 1866,Grey River Argus,2</i>)

>  "The fish you forwarded is a kokopu or Maori trout " <i>(Mar 6, 1914,Hawera & Normanby Star,4</i>)

### Maori turnip
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  For vegetables there was plenty of pora, the Maori turnip, and puwha (rauriki) or sowthistle. <i>(1988,Tales of Pioneer Women,207</i>) [NOTES: Writing of 1940]

>  Mr. Goodson, an old settler, who had lived on the Coast a long time, told Mr. Laird that the ground in the vicinity of the plaintiff's land was impregnated with the seeds of the Maori turnip or cabbage. <i>(Dec 11, 1883,Hawera & Normanby Star,2</i>)

### Maori vegetable marrow
 
 <i>n.</i> kumikumi

>  The kumikumi, or Maori vegetable marrow crops, have been exceptionally heavy in the Tauranga district this season. <i>(Jul 28, 1916,Dannevirke Evening News,4</i>)

### Maori village
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [cf. kaik](../K#kaik))

>  <b>HAAST</b> I use the habitual expression Maori pah, but a description of this settlement would give a very poor idea of such a Maori village, consisting as it did of three miserable low huts, in which a very old couple ... were living. <i>(1879,Geology of the Provinces of Canterbury & Westland,98</i>)

>  ... to appoint from among the Maoris of any Maori kainga, village, or pa a Committee. . . who shall be called the Village Committee (Komiti Marae) ... <i>Ibid.</i> 417 Maori Social Welfare and Advancements Act 1945 repeats the above Act and repeats in Section 21 ... Maori Villages: A Tribal Committee may ... define the boundaries of any kainga, village, or pa. <i>(1900,NZ Statutes,1900, 64 Vict., No.48, pp. 252-60,170 ed.</i>)

>  In pursuance of the recommendations made by the Royal Commission which reported in 1926 upon the advisability of establishing model Maori villages at Whakarewarewa and Ohinemutu at Rotorua, the Government has instructed Mr. R. B. Hammond, until lately the Director of Town Planning, who was a member of the Commission, to prepare a scheme for a new village adjacent to the inhabited area at Whakarewarewa. <i>(Dec 28, 1928,Evening Post,6</i>)

>  <b>HOCKEN</b> Otago - strictly Otakou - was appropriated to the whole block, but really relates to a small district within the Heads, where there was a Maori village and a whaling station. <i>(1898,Contributions to the Early History of New Zealand,80</i>)

>  <b>HINTZ</b> At the little Maori village of Waihi near by hot springs bubble away at boiling point. <i>(1964,Trout at Taupo,30</i>)

>  (Black Stewart Island) We crossed the neck behind the Maori village ... <i>(1872,Proceedings Otago Provincial Council,39</i>) [NOTES: the Proceedings of the Otago Provincial Council 39]

### Maori village settlement
 
 <i>n.</i> [scheme to establish villages espressly for Maori]

>  Among the Bills to be introduced by the Government is one for the establishment of a Maori village settlement under the perpetual lease system. A small rental will be charged, and stringent conditions as to occupancy will be enforced. The right of transfer will be limited to other Natives, and all precautions will be taken to prevent a transfer to Europeans. The system will be applied especially to the South Island Natives, many of whom are said to be almost homeless. The Government will buy the land necessary for the settlement. <i>(Jun 3, 1944,Star, The,3</i>)

>  In accordance with an intention formed some time ago, the Hon. the Native Minister has drafted a Bill,whereby his scheme for the formation of Maori village settlements will be carried out. <i>(Jun 8, 1891,Poverty Bay Herald,3</i>)

>  They Say - That <i>The Bulletin</i> has commenced to talk now about 'the success of the Maori Village Settlement system, as compared with the failure of the schemes adopted in neww South Wales and Victoria.' Next please. <i>(Dec 1, 1894,Observer (Auckland),6</i>)

>  The Government, assuming the Maori village settlement successful, would have every incentive to encourage the natives to build their school and runanga house, to lay off a cemetery or recreatioin ground, but these aids would come in when the settlement should have advanced a little. <i>(May 29, 1891,Thames Star,4</i>)

 <i>n.</i> <i>Muehlenbeckia australis</i> [1924 CCN. (Andersen)]

### Maori War medal
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  There were Crimean decorations there and New Zealand Crosses, as well as the more plentiful Maori War medal. <i>(Aug 10, 1912,NZ Free Lance,4</i>)

### Maori War(s)
 
 <i>n.</i> a now disfavoured Pakeha-oriented term for the New Zealand Wars. [-] (See [New Zealand War(s)](../N#new-zealand-war(s)),[Land Wars](../L#land-wars),[Anglo-Maori Wars](../A#anglo-maori-wars))

>  <b>No. 34/9</b> Most people have heard of the great battles of what used to be called the "Maori Wars", such as Rangiriri and Orakau, which were both fought in the Waikato campaign... But in the end, the British army was just too strong for Maori in what is now generally known as the "New Zealand Wars." <i>(Feb 2002,NZ Memories</i>)

>  <b>GUTHRIE</b> Down in the arena, half a dozen veterans of the forgotten Maori wars ... tilted their old beards up towards the sky ... <i>(1935,Little Country, The,411,1937 ed.</i>)

>  <b>BULLER</b> The Maori war./ A lecture/ Delivered at the rooms of the Young men's Christian Association, on/ Friday Evening, June 25 1869. <i>(1869,Maori War, The</i>)

>  <b>BULLER</b> The Maori War. [Chapter heading] <i>(1878,Forty Years in NZ,416</i>)

>  <b>MEREDITH</b> There is a good story told of an incident which occurred near Wanganui during the Maori War. <i>(1875,Adventuring in Maoriland in the Seventies,416</i>)

>  <b>VON HAAST</b> ... I suppose you will see all about the Maori War and the Pelorus gold-fields in the newspapers. <i>(May 14, 1864,Julius Von Haast,83,1935 ed.</i>)

>  <b>R PICKMERE</b> ... and some hen-hearted people leaving in fright of the Maori war. <i>(1860,Letter</i>) [NOTES: 1860 R.Pickmere in 1940 Matthews Matthews of Kaitaia 209]

>  During the Maori war in the interior under Sir George Whitmore my original MS. map ... was ... copied for them. <i>(1895,TrNZI,27/382</i>)

>  <b>GRACE</b> This sketch of the Maori War is not intended to have any merit except spontaneity. <i>(1899,Sketch of the NZ War,i</i>)

>  THE MOUNTED RIFLEMAN [title] New Zealanders in these regions are popularly referred to, in their Homeland, as `The Mounteds"; and as being indicative of their pecularities in method of warfare, the term has its value. In origin, the Mounted Riflemen date back to the 'sixties, when that fine soldier, Major Sir Harry Atkinson, formed the settler-riflemen into a mounted force of a nature demanded by the topographical and tactical conditions of the fiercely-contested Maori wars. <i>(Aug 15, 1918,Kia-ora Coo-ee,Second Series 2/16,1981 ed.</i>)

>  This was the last time Te Kooti was seen over a gunsight, and it was the final engagement in the Maori wars; ... <i>(1922,NZ Wars, The,2/462,1983 ed.</i>)

>  Ho! ave yer seen mi EN-ER-Y? .../ E'es gone hand jined the Mowrie war;/ ... <i>(Jun 17, 1865,Punch in Canterbury,47</i>)

>  <b>COWAN</b> The South Island was free from Maori wars ... Here in the Greenstone Land, the chief element of adventure and romance was supplied by the successive `rushes' to the great alluvial goldfields in Otago and on the West Coast. <i>(1926,Travel in NZ,(Vol II South Island) viii</i>) [NOTES: [?adjectival here]]

>  <b>BATHGATE</b> There was a good deal of discontent in the Old Country about the Maori War ... [On Home Government's decision to withdraw the troops]. It was no uncommon thing to hear talk of cutting the painter. One honourable member of the Upper House said: `If a flag and a governor is all they can send us we can do without either.' <i>(1925,Random Recollections,18</i>)

>  He does not remember seeing any carving done either. And he is well over 70. Did the Maori wars of a century ago knock all this art out in the district? <i>(Jan 5, 1929,Na To Hoa Aroha,1/173</i>) [NOTES: (Ngata)]

>  <b>PINFOLD</b> Hauhauism may be regarded as the aftermath of the Maori War. <i>(1930,Fifty Years In Maoriland,102</i>)

>  So, although the Maori wars had greatly retarded the advancement of the North Island, by 1870 the South Island had made amazing strides. <i>(1930,NZ Memories,150</i>)

>  After the Maori wars, King Tawhiao had left Kuiti and settled in Hikurangi, ... <i>Ibid.</i> 156 During our tramp he told me much about his people and the Maori wars. <i>(1930,Yesterdays in Maoriland,113,1933 ed.</i>)

>  <b>COAD</b> He had his ups and downs, of course - there was the Maori War, for instance - but his Scotch grit carried him through the hard times. <i>(1931,Such Is Life,32</i>)

>  <b>LEE</b> New Zealand had Maori wars in which the Native Race, in its resistance to the encroachments of the white, alternated between blood-curdling ferocity and chivalry. <i>(1934,Children of the Poor,121,1949 ed.</i>)

>  <b>PARTRIDGE</b> [The] following [was] employed by those who served in the [Great] War ..<i> ti-tree oneself,</i> to take shelter from artillery, the ti-tree being very common in N.Z., where during the Maori War, the natives had an exasperating habit of retreating to `the bush' ... <i>(1936,Slang Today,286</i>)

>  <b>BABBAGE</b> An Historical Retrospect: The Maori Wars, 1860-1864. [heading] <i>Ibid.</i> 69 The Maori Wars had arisen over the struggle of the natives to preserve the integrity of their lands. <i>(1937,Hauhauism,9</i>)

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> When volunteers were called for to go to the Maori War Tom was one who responded ... <i>(1939,First White Boy Born in Otago,62</i>)

>  <b>COWAN</b> The military position ... was the most ... anxious in the whole history of the Maori Wars. <i>(1940,Sir Donald Maclean,100</i>)

>  The Maori War was in progress at this time [1864], and father joined the Fourth Waikato Militia Regiment ... <i>(1940,Tales of Pioneer Women,37,1988 ed.</i>)

>  <b>SUTHERLAND</b> One of the best known collections of such heads was ... obtained by a British General engaged with Imperial troops in the Maori wars. since the Maori wars <i>(1941,Numismatic History of NZ,34</i>)

>  <b>BURTON</b> There has been a deepseated inferiority among the Maori people ever since the conclusion of the Maori wars; ... <i>(1945,In Prison,123</i>)

>  <b>FINLAYSON</b> `They give it to him when he came out here to the Maori Wars. Every soldier got a razor,' he says. <i>(1948,Tidal Creek,34,1979 ed.</i>)

>  Owing to the Maori Wars, the collapse of the alluvial-gold boom in the South Island and the unstable economy of the Colony, dependent as it was on the overseas price of wool, the primary export, unemployment became serious in the 1860's. <i>(1950,Growth and Development of Social Security,29</i>)

>  <b>ROGERS</b> He ought to hate this <i>pakeha</i>, as his father hated all <i>pakehas</i> - yes, and killed them in the Maori Wars. <i>(1960,Long White Cloud, The,87</i>)

>  The Maoris kept what was then the best land - until the Europeans confiscated it during the Maori Wars. <i>(1961,Distance Looks Our Way,31</i>) [NOTES: (Sinclair)]

>  <b>OLIVER</b> A small piece of fertile land at Waitara ... precipitated that protracted frontier engagement called the Maori Wars. <i>(1963,Story of New Zealand, The,87</i>)

>  <b>KING</b> It was these [1810-1839 musket] wars and not the ones that followed that were the true `Maori Wars' - those in which Maori fought Maori. used as standard in <i>Encyclopedia of NZ </i> <i>(1981,New Zealanders at War,21</i>)

>  <b>McLINTOCK</b> Recent research appears to confirm that the `Great Fleet' of Maori tradition is a myth coined by European Maori-phils in the generation after the Maori Wars. <i>Ibid.</i> 3/331-332 SUTHERLAND, Donald (1839-1919). Backwoodsman. ... Like so many more of his kind he became embroiled in the Maori Wars, [332] and when fighting was finished he left the Armed Constabulary and took a job ... on a Government steamer. <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,2/88</i>)

>  <b>SEDDON</b> In front of the procession marched `Taranaki Bill,' a veteran of the Maori War, carrying a large flag on a long pole. <i>(1968,Seddons, The,25</i>)

>  <b>THOMAS</b> Many of the settlers had fought ... in the bloody campaigns of the Maori Wars. <i>(1970,Way Up North,2</i>)

>  There should be no place in New Zealand for the expression `the Maori wars'. They could possibly be called `the land wars' but "te riri Pakeha' (white man's anger) would be more accurate. <i>(Sep 24, 1983,Listener (New Zealand Listener),10</i>)

>  Tregear had fought in the Maori Wars ... <i>(Feb 1953,Here & Now,14</i>) [NOTES: (Roth)]

>  <b>MACLEOD</b> Maori rights and wrongs were keenly debated in the Mackay home all through the Maori wars, which were the years of Jessie's childhood. <i>(1955,Voice on the Wind,16</i>)

>  <b>SHADBOLT</b> And one day he let slip that his great-grandfather was one of the old chiefs who fought the pakeha in the Maori wars [sic]. <i>(1959,New Zealanders, The,66,1986 ed.</i>) [NOTES: (The Strangers)]

>  <b>MIDDLETON</b> I think it was the first real excitement there had been in the area since the Maori Wars. <i>(1959,Stone, The,76</i>) [NOTES: (Corporal's Story)]

>  <b>REED</b> Captain Gilbert Mair and Major William G. Mair, Maori War heroes. <i>(1961,North Cape to Bluff,82</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> ... the monuments [sic] to the Maori Wars dead lifted its slim grey column. <i>(1976,Pacific Star,136</i>)

### Maori Warden
 
 <i>n.</i> a semi-official, uniformed volunteer who patrols public places, bars, etc., in ways helpful to Maori people and others.

>  <b>HUTT</b> Maori Wardens were appointed in 1949 as a result of the Liquor Licensing Bill 1948. Wardens were administered through the Department of Maori Affairs and nominated by tribal executives. Wardens had power over liquor and liqor-related disturbances within tribal districts, with powers over Maori pub patrons and over Pakeha at hui or if there was sly-grogging... In many ways the Maori wardens were following in the tradition of the Ringatu pirihimana, the Ratana katipa and the Kingite watene. <i>(1999,Maori & Alcohol,73</i>) [NOTES: See FLERAS <i>A Descriptive Analysis of Maori Wardens in the Historical and Contemporary Context of New Zealand Society</i>", Ph.D, VUW, 1980, esp. pp.269-275]

>  <b>REED</b> This is to certify that during his stay in Te Kao he did NOT disturb the peace. K. Wiki, No. 19, Maori District Warden, Te Kao. <i>(1961,North Cape to Bluff,27</i>)

>  Sharon Rio Tauhore, 31, a trainee maori warden of Wellington, had earlier pleaded guilty to a charge of assault with intent to injure. <i>(Jul 2, 1986,Evening Post,10</i>)

>  You put through a call to your brother, Maori warden south ... <i>(1987,Metro Fiction,10</i>) [NOTES: (Keogh, Chiaoscuro)]

>  First there are those New Zealand words [in Collins English Dictionary, 2nd ed.] ... for example, ... <i>solo mother, walk socks</i> (but not <i>walk shorts</i>), <i>block, kiwi</i> (as in Golden Kiwi), <i>mate's rates, ... Maori Warden. </i> <i>(Feb 27, 1988,Listener (New Zealand Listener),65</i>)

>  Maori Warden Eb Hauraki says he hasn't been called out once ... in the past six months. <i>(Jun 5, 1988,Sunday Star (Auckland),A9</i>)

 <i>n.</i>

### Maori way
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>Stella Morice</b> His uncle, Hori, had no children but he had room in his whare and plenty of food, so Wiri was given to his uncle. That is the Maori way. <i>(1944,Book of Wiremu, The,1</i>)

>  When the ship finally docked at Penang on 23 November 1959 the High Commissioner for New Zealand, Mr C. M. Bennett, was thrilled to be welcomed aboard in the traditional Maori way... <i>(Jun 1961,Te Ao Hou,35: 24</i>)

>  A prominent Maori teacher is performing a daily tapu ceremony to influence a burglar to return ... goods stolen from his Pakuranga home ... He says placing the curse is `the Maori way' of dealing with such matters, as the police hold little hope of recovering his belongings. <i>(Apr 12, 1987,Sunday Star (Auckland),A3</i>)
