### Maori wedding
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>OWEN & PERKINS</b> In those days [c1890s] they had what was known as the `Maori wedding', which was not legal to the Pakeha, but was a binding thing among the Maori people themselves. A young couple would decide that they wanted to become man and wife, and they would have to tell their parents they wanted this. Well, at night-time everyone would congregate in the meetinghouse [the matter would be discussed, the boy and girl would be asked whether they wanted to marry, and they would take each other in front of all their people]. <i>(1986,Speaking for Ourselves,109</i>) [NOTES: (Florence Harsant, Taupo)]

### Maori weed
 
 <i>n.</i> a brumby, Maori pony

>  The horse looked very sad, and I had my doubts about it reaching the settlement, but one can never tell what these Maori "weeds" are capable of. <i>(1920,Kai Tiaki,XIII/176</i>)

>  <b>SCRYMGEOUR</b> Once at a horse sale at Palmerston North, Mr. Bob Waller was selling a draft of what might be termed `Maori Weeds', or rather stunted, but nice condition nags, from the Sandy Country towards Foxton. <i>(1960,Memories of Maoriland,165</i>)

>  Undoubtedly there was good blood among these horses, though the great majority would come into the somewhat elastic classification of `Maori weed.' In actual fact, there was no reproach in the term, Born and reared on the inhospitable plains, a horse developed a stamina that no finely-bred animal possessed. Well broken in and handled properly, a `weed' would get through more hard toiling in a day than the average equine would put behind him in a week. Looks might be against him, but for downright hard work in the high hill country he had the goods, as they say, and with something to spare. <i>(Aug 31, 1963,Dominion,7</i>) [NOTES: [reference to ca 1910, Kaingaroa Plains]]

>  It takes Bill and I two hours to catch that old roany Maori weed we used for packing tucker. <i>(Jul 5, 1918,Chronicles NZEF,IV/47 : 251</i>)

>  They say... That a lot of "Maori weeds" and other horseflesh are wide-eyed with surprise at the extra feeds they are getting. Japan wants re-mounts. <i>(Feb 6, 1904,NZ Observer,7</i>)

### Maori wheat
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>MASTERS</b> The only sheep lost on the journey were 4 [sic] that had been [134] poisoned through eating Maori wheat. [1856] <i>(1959,Tales of the Mails,133</i>)

### Maori wife
 
 <i>n.</i> a (bushman's) Maori mistress

>  <b>MILLER</b> Unable to bear the solitary life, not a few acquired 'Maori wives', which was 'the conventional name fo a Maori mistress.' <i>(1958,Early Victorian NZ,141</i>) [NOTES: 1847 Nov 24 H.S. Chapman letters to His Father [TS, ATB] in 1958 Miller Early Victorian NZ 141]

### Maori-bashing
 
 <i>n.</i> (informal)

>  Maori-bashing doesn't seem to work as well for Act as it once did. <i>(Dec 17, 2000,Sunday Star Times,C3</i>)

>  It is sad to see a knight of the realm indulging in Maori-bashing in a manner that borders on racism, especially for electoral gain. <i>(Sep 3, 1990,Listener (New Zealand Listener),98</i>)

>  Some of those tags are on display in <i>North & South Letters</i> where Atareta Poananga attacks the magazine for what she sees as "an endless record of Maori-bashing." <i>(Feb 1989,North & South,4</i>)

>  There are points in politics for Maori-bashing, but Act didn't even score them. <i>(Nov 25, 2000,Listener (New Zealand Listener),14</i>)

>  Present beneficiaries will label criticism "Maori-bashing." <i>(Feb 6, 2002,Dominion,12</i>)

>  Please tell your leader what those laws are, before Election Day, so he may have an opportunity to balance his Maori bashing policies. <i>(Sep 6, 2005,Whakatane Beacon,2</i>)

>  Thinly disguised Maori-bashing lurked in columns and editorials. <i>(Feb 22, 1997,Listener (New Zealand Listener),94</i>)

>  [Hdg] Tuku's trials: Indignation or old-fashioned Maori bashing? <i>(May 4, 1997,Listener (New Zealand Listener),7</i>)

>  They only made the checks because they were Maori-bashing... take it from me, if a senior Maori figure says something's Maori-bashing, then the correct response in this country is not to make a logical argument, but to feel a surge of liberal guilt. <i>(May 18, 2002,Listener (New Zealand Listener),14</i>)

>  The thrust of her talk? How the mainsteam media is a propaganda machine perpetuating Maori-bashing stereotypes. <i>(Jul 16, 2005,Dominion Post,B4</i>)

### Maori-bred
 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  <b>MALONE</b> An impromptu race of Maori-bred horses, ridden by Maories, added much to the amusement of the day. <i>(1854,Three Years Cruise,118</i>)

>  <b>COWAN</b> There were nine of us who fell in ... at Harry Tanner's little bush store and accommodation-house, and each ... picked out a horse from the little mob, of Maori-bred animals that the packer brought along ... <i>(1916,Bush Explorers,2</i>)

>  <b>HARGREAVES</b> No doubt much of the pork exported [from Taranaki c1850] had been derived from Maori-bred pigs, and it was possible that some of the flour was from ... Maori-owned flour mills to the south of the settlement. <i>(Apr 1963,NZ Geographer,58</i>)

### Maori-Chinese
 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  <b>BEAGLEHOLE</b> We may sum up the local attitudes to the Chinese by [323] saying that the pakeha is relatively indifferent to their presence but disapproves of Maori-Chinese intermixture. <i>(1946,Some Modern Maoris,322</i>)

### Maori-fed
 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  <b>CAMPBELL</b> For the pork of those days was not as these days, for it was Maori-fed - in other words, the pigs were free to roam and get fat how and where they could, or remain lean. <i>(1881,Poenamo,3/3/152</i>)

### Maori-grown, Maori-built
 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  <i>Mauri</i> grown potatoes <i>(1843,Letters from Settlers,6</i>)

>  Six maori built houses. <i>(1843,Letters from Settlers,54</i>) [NOTES: (J.Philps)]

### Maori-isation
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>ODELL</b> On one map it [67] appears as Koeti Stream which looks like a Maori-ization of `Goat.' <i>(1935,Handbook Arthur Pass National Park,66</i>)

### Maori-ise
 
 <i>v.</i> make Maori (See [Maorify](../M#maorify),[Maoricise](../M#maoricise))

>  He shifts into galvanising-leader mode, exhorting membership drives, slamming recent government publicity "about-turns" on Maori policy, dismissing the planned 2005 hui tamata (Maori economic summit) as little more than a government publicity stunt, stressing the need to validate Maori knowledge and "take New Zealand with us. We have to do as we preach; we have to help them get over the whakama [embarassment] of saying 'Papatoetoe' [correctly] in front of their friends ... We will Maori-ise New Zealand. ..." <i>(Dec 2004,North & South,66</i>)

### Maori-ised
 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  In writing a Maori-ised English word there is no attempt to spell it English fashion; ... <i>(1893,TrNZI,25/410</i>)

### Maori-ish
 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  The sort of Maori-ish character I do works because they can identify it. <i>(Sep 1986,Metro,35</i>)

### Maori-ism
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>CAMPBELL</b> Was our day to have been spent in vain? Saints of Maori-ism forbid! <i>(1881,Poenamo,2/7/119</i>)

### Maori-language
 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  Maori-language primary schools must eventually lead to demands for Maori-language secondary schools and tertiary education. <i>(Aug 29, 1988,Press, The,20</i>)

### Maori-led
 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  Congratulations to Mr Norman Jones, MP ... on his open protests against Maori-led racism in this country. <i>(Dec 29, 1986,Dominion,12</i>)

### Maori-like
 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  <b>GUDGEON</b> Maori-like, ever suspicious, they expressed a belief that his house had been built opposite the mouth of the river .... to guide man-o-war boats to surprise them at night. <i>(1879,Reminiscences of the War in NZ,42</i>)

>  <i>(Jul 1920,Chambers Edinburgh Journal,452/2</i>) [NOTES: [OEDS]]

>  <i>(Jul 1920,Chambers Edinburgh Journal,452/2</i>) [NOTES: [OEDS]]

### Maori-marrying
 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  <b>SENIOR</b> In this bay [sc Poverty] the whalers - the rollicking, hard-drinking, Maori-marrying fellows from Botany Bay, who ... invented a mixed language known as whalers' Maori - had important stations. <i>(1880,Travel & Trout,236</i>)

### Maori-only
 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  Secondly, the commission sought a Maori-speaking secretary. ... Maori-only advertisements did not preclude non-Maori from applying for the job because there were many non-Maori people who did have adequate Maori language skills ... <i>(Jan 19, 1989,Evening Post,2</i>)

### Maori-owned
 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  Sales of timber from Maori-owned forests are not subject to any operational restrictions beyond regular felling and prevention of fire. <i>(1936,Agricultural Organization in NZ,575</i>)

>  ... what was a growing evil common to sheep owners throughout the North Island at this time [c1850], namely the frequent killings (especially of lambs) by half-wild Maori-owned dogs. <i>(Apr 1963,NZ Geographer,56</i>) [NOTES: (Hargreaves)]

>  And the government's stance ... is understood to have nudged others into ... reviewing forest service leases of Maori-owned land. <i>(Jan 30, 1987,National Business Review,1</i>)

>  <b>MIKAERE</b> Parliament introduced legislation to limit the number of Maori-owned dogs, by imposing steep registration fees, or dog taxes <i>(1988,Te Maiharoa,82</i>)

### Maori-Pakeha
 
 <i>adj.</i> of or relating to Maori and Pakeha together

>  <b>SUTHERLAND</b> Every phase of early the Maori-Pakeha history of New Zealand has been associated with barter; ... <i>(1941,Numismatic History of NZ,1</i>)

>  <b>BEAGLEHOLE</b> The inquiry into the Maori-paheka intermarriages is disheartening. <i>Ibid.</i> xxii <i>pakeha</i> any person of non-Maori ancestry, used synonymously with `white man' in the context of Maori-pakeha social relations. <i>(1946,Some Modern Maoris,xiv</i>)

>  <i>Pakeha:</i> `... But I believe it's a long way from being typical of maori-pakeha relationships.' <i>(Jun 1953,Here & Now,28</i>)

>  <b>MASON</b> ISOBEL: How Maori are you? ... Half? Three-quarters? SMITHSON: I'm not sure. I think, half. You see: I was a piece of flotsam, thrown up on the beach of Maori-pakeha relations. Adopted at an early age. Brought up pakeha. <i>(1974,Swan Song,41</i>)

>  <b>GOLDBLATT</b> The treaty of Waitangi set the tone of Maori-Pakeha relations ... <i>(1957,Democracy at Ease,27</i>)

### Maori-phil
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>McLINTOCK</b> Recent research appears to confirm that the `Great Fleet' of Maori tradition is a myth coined by European Maori-phils in the generation after the Maori Wars. <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,2/88</i>)

### Maori-phile
 
 <i>adj.</i> (See [cf. philo-Maori noun](../P#philo-maori-noun))

>  For this we owe a great debt to Coates. The danger is that some day they will accuse him of being ultra Maori-phile. <i>(Oct 9, 1928,Na To Hoa Aroha,1/140</i>) [NOTES: (Ngata)]

>  <b>MARAIS</b> `Native policy had a great deal to do with this disastrous state of affairs. It was a `Maori-phile' policy administered by the aid of missionaries according to the principles of the Aborigines Committee of 1837. <i>(1927,Colonisation of NZ,IX</i>)

### Maori-speaking
 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  Secondly, the commission sought a Maori-speaking secretary. ... Maori-only advertisements did not preclude non-Maori from applying for the job because there were many non-Maori people who did have adequate Maori language skills ... <i>(Jan 19, 1989,Evening Post,2</i>)

### Maoria
 
 <i>n.</i> things Maori

>  <b>JOHNSTONE</b> A sketch of the Manners and customs of the aboriginal inhabitants of New Zealand. [title]... Perhaps the principal reason which has `tapued' Maoria to the writer of fiction is the difficulty of depicting a Maori heroine. <i>(1874,Maoria</i>)

### Maorian
 
 <i>adj.</i> New Zealand, coined by Bret Harte in his tale prophesying the San Francisco earthquake `[Macaulay's] Maorian philosopher' [check]

>  <i>maorian adj, usu cap [Maori + -an]</i> : NEW ZEALAND <i>(1961,Merriam-Webster Third International,1379</i>)



 
 <i>adj.</i> (Geology) [Geol]

>  Rakaia Terrane may have been docked in the Middle Jurassic, coincident with the temporary cessation of volcanism in the Median Tectonic Zone, Kirwan's Dolerite emplacement, a phase of metamorphism through the region, minnor uplift of the Murihiku Basin to give its shallower marine and non-marine Temaikan sequence in both New Zealand and New Calaedonia, and the change from Maorian provinciality to a strong Tethyan influence in benthic fauna. <i>(Jul 1998,Geological Society of NZ Newsletter,116/21</i>)

### Maoriana
 
 <i>n.</i> an indigenous subset of Kiwiana

>  Broad fuses Maoriana with pulp-sci and tin toys. His work explores New Zealand history. <i>(Aug 6, 2003,Capital Times,11</i>)

>  <b>WESTERWOOD</b> [title] <i>(1918,Maoriana</i>)

>  "I'm mixing Kiwiana with Maoriana," says Bryant, of his works merging the Maori palette of red, white and black with the Union Jack's red, white and blue. <i>(Aug 27, 2000,Sunday Star Times,F3</i>)

### Maoricise
 
 <i>v.</i> give a Maori form to (esp. a word) (See [Maorify](../M#maorify))

>  <b>ROBERTS</b> It is only the English word Maoricised. <i>(1913,Place Names...of Otago and Southland,5</i>)

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> A European told me this last word [`paaka' for a division of land] was the word `block' Maoricised, but an ancient meaning of paka was a fence or enclosure. <i>(1945,Maori Place-Names of Canterbury,66</i>)

### Maoridom
 
 <i>n.</i> the Maori people and their world, the Maori race

>  "If we can set the model here, then the model can be extrapolated throughout Maoridom." <i>(Jun 6, 2006,Dominion Post,A8</i>)

>  The 12th Regiment of Foot, the 14th, the 18th Royal Irish, the 40th, the 65th York and Lancashire, the 70th, the Royal Artillery and the Engineers - at one time or another all stepped out down this road on their controversial missions into the heart of Maoridom. <i>(Jan 2003,NZ Geographic,No. 61/59</i>)

>  'Maoridom' is an entity I can't really begin to deal with. All I <i>can</i> being to deal with is my own Maori group - the people of Taranaki. <i>(Jun 15, 1985,Listener (New Zealand Listener),20</i>)

>  Nor is it realistic to try to use television to force-feed New Zealanders with Maoridom. <i>(Aug 31, 1985,Listener (New Zealand Listener),11</i>)

>  In Maoridom, I am young. <i>(May 24, 1997,Listener (New Zealand Listener),26</i>)

>  Labour is out to trash the part of Bolger that so wants to be Maoridom's Great Bwana that he will grant it anything, in order to get more credit than the judiciary for Maori advancement. <i>(Feb 22, 1997,Listener (New Zealand Listener),16</i>)

>  "No. I get bloody annoyed when something happens to a Maori and they say this affects Maoridom." <i>(Jul 23, 2000,Evening Post,23</i>)

>  Yet I am afraid it is not possible to believe that anything but a sharp physical lesson will go home to young Maoridom. <i>(May 12, 1860,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,1/534</i>) [NOTES: (J.C. Richmond)]

>  Child abuse is the stain upon Maoridom. <i>(Jul 29, 2007,Sunday Star Times,A10</i>)

>  <b>BAGNALL & PETERSEN</b> The native teacher, though he be a mere slave, became a power in Maoridom and challenged the authority of chief and <i> tohunaga. </i> <i>(1948,Willam Colenso,51</i>)

>  Mr Sim [M.P.] continued ... `South Island Maoris also had the highest arrears in rents ... on those houses of any in Maoridom. <i>(Sep 16, 1953,Evening Post</i>)

>  <b>PHILLIPPS</b> <i>(1955,Maori Carving,8</i>)

>  They are a new breed, leaving their Maoridom behind them <i>(Mar 29, 1958,Press, The,8</i>)

>  The whole face of Maoridom is changing <i>(Jul 26, 1958,Evening Post,24</i>)

>  Through Maoridom today blow warm winds of hope,... <i>(Nov 1961,NZ Monthly Review,9</i>)

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> I am the taiaha left among people/ who dance and twirl poi/ in gaudy halls/ of plastic maoridom. <i>(1979,Eyes of the Ruru,13</i>) [NOTES: (Taiaha Haka Poem)]

>  <b>458</b> <i>Maoridom n</i>. the Maori world; Maori culture. <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.]]

>  Hori Brennan ... Ngati Tuwharetoa in the land of Ngai Tahu and recipient of an OBE for his services to Maoridom ... The collection of unfinished projects among which he has encamped represents not only almost 10 million dollars of public money, but a very deep schism in Maoridom locally and the dismal end of a great dream. <i>(Mar 5, 1988,Listener (New Zealand Listener),16</i>)

>  Pakehadom, as controllers of media, choose and create the so-called spokespersons for Maoridom. <i>(Oct 23, 1988,Sunday Star (Auckland),A12</i>)

>  Gleamed all their muskets bare, / Frightening the children there, / Heroes to do and dare, / Charging a village, while / Maoridom wondered. <i>(1886,Canterbury Rhymes,116</i>) [NOTES: Maoridom]

>  <b>CAMPBELL</b> ... Waipeha was deep in the past history of Maoridom. <i>Ibid.</i> Bk3/2/135 For the influx of Pakehas, consequent upon the colonisation of Maoridom, killed his monopoly with the aborigines. <i>(1881,Poenamo,Bk2/7/114</i>)

>  <b>HAY</b> We pass along the streets of this British city in the very heart of recent Maori-dom. <i>(1882,Brighter Britain,19</i>)

>  ... the word `Devil,' <i>Rewera</i>, and the name `Satan' ... have been naturalised in Maoridom ... <i>(1893,TrNZI,25/411</i>)

>  Some Aspects of maoridom [title] <i>(1901,NZ Illustrated Magazine,4/785</i>)

>  This belief it was that provided much employment for the priestly experts, shamanistic mediums, angurers, and that maturgists of Maoridom. <i>(1921,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,4/367</i>) [NOTES: (Best)]

>  <b>COWAN</b> It was a kind of advanced frontier post, beyond which the chiefs of old Maoridom held undisputed rule. <i>Ibid.</i> ) 2/179 A<i> tohunga</i> schooled in the lore of ancient Maoridom,he revived the practices of the cannibal era ... <i>(1922,NZ Wars, The,2/37,1983 ed.</i>)

>  After a night spent in the usual declaration by the ope [ed fn: Visiting group] that it wished to return the next day and to have ashes deposited in their last resting-place before departure and the usual pupuri [ed fn: Invitation to remain] by the tangata whenua the casket was borne to the vault. Three Taranaki poi parties, six persons representing three Hahi [ed fn: Churches], a gathering representative of the Crown, Parliament (all three parties in the Lower House and the Acting-Leader of the Upper), the Press and Pakeha public and all sections of Maoridom, gathered together all the elements that constitute the New Zealand community of to-day, ... <i>(Sep 20, 1930,Na To Hoa Aroha,2/51,1987 ed.</i>) [NOTES: (Ngata)]

>  Paki, my Tuhoe son-in-law, ... asked me to sing over one or two Maori songs when I was at home last week. I did so and found him crooning the old monotones with understanding and a close approach to the ancient music. He has a full set of Nga Moteatea and in Wi Waititi finds an excellent teacher. And there may be others like minded to carry on some of the concrete forms of Maoridom. <i>(May 15, 1931,Na To Hoa Aroha,2/149,1987 ed.</i>) [NOTES: (Ngata)]

>  The work in this will [88] be representative of all Maoridom. The first collections from cream cheques came to a little under [pounds]140 ... <i>(Jun 11, 1933,Na To Hoa Aroha,3/87</i>) [NOTES: (Ngata)]

>  <b>VON TEMPSKY</b> In the great centres of Maoridom, the few whites residing there, were looked upon as useful tribute - paying slaves; ... .. that feat would have lifted the cause of Maoridom above all doubt in the excitable Maoris' imagination. <i>(1864,Memoranda,4</i>)

>  <b>BAINES</b> It is the grand unwritten creed in all `Maoridom' that nothing is wrong unless found out ... <i>(1874,Edward Crewe,87</i>)

>  Representing Maoridom in parliament ... <i>(Feb 4, 1870,Evening Post,2</i>)

>  <b>BAINES</b> ... all `Maoridom' <i>(1874,Edward Crewe,87</i>)

>  ... the place had that semi-civilized appearance which indicated Maoridom. <i>(May 5, 1879,North Otago Times,2</i>) [NOTES: 1879 May 5 North Otago Times 2 in 1988 Mikaere Te Maiharoa 78]

### Maories
 
 <i>n.</i> Maories (See [Maori](../M#maori))

### Maorification
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  There's a new generation of Kiwis, under 25, who've been exposed to the Maorification of the education system. <i>(Aug 2007,North & South,85</i>)

>  This is, neatly, both a suck-up to the Maori voter, and a further affront to the average pensioner, who wrestles with any Maorification of the language. <i>(Oct 10, 1998,Listener (New Zealand Listener),18</i>)

### Maorified
 
 <i>adj.</i> (See [also Maorify](../A#also-maorify))

>  <b>BULLER</b> They took the name of Pikopo. This no doubt was maorified from the Latin word <i>episcopos</i>. <i>(1878,Forty Years in NZ,295</i>)

>  <b>COWAN</b> He had taken a Scriptural name, Zerubbabel (maorified into Horopapera). <i>(1922,NZ Wars, The,II : 4</i>) [NOTES: 1955 edition]

>  I am a white New Zealander, not a pakeha.. and I am bewildered and angered by incessant demands and efforts to Maorify everything. <i>(Dec 24, 1988,Dominion,8</i>)

>  <b>IRONSIDE</b> In conversing with [Maoris] I was often puzzled with these Maorified-English expressions that fell so glibly from their lips. <i>(1840,Journal</i>) [NOTES: 1840-41 Ironsides's Journal in 1933 MacDonald Pages from Past 130]

>  <b>MANING</b> At last the war-dance ended; and then my tribe (I find I am already beginning to get Maorified,) ... endeavoured to out-do even their amiable friends' exhibition. <i>(1863,Old New Zealand,(iii) 52</i>)

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> <i>Rauna</i> i katoa is a half-caste word, the English word for round being here Maorified, and thus makes a more emphatic expression than any of their own <i>(1868,Past and Present of NZ,134</i>)

>  <b>COWAN</b> A maorified version of the Benediction was chanted with one voice ... <i>(1922,NZ Wars, The,2/12,1983 ed.</i>)

>  <b>COWAN</b> At the principal villages in that rugged region ... the Hauhau poles, the sacred niu, were set up and round them marched the half-crazed people ... chanting their newly learned hymns ... the prayers of the <i>`Kororia,</i>' Maorified English phrases and scraps of English and Roman Catholic church services. <i>(1940,Sir Donald Maclean,85</i>)

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> Another reason was that he was a `Maorified' <i>pakeha,</i> and therefore one on whom you could play a joke without offence. <i>(1948,Taina,24</i>)

>  <b>PARTRIDGE</b> <i>By Christchurch, hooya.</i> ? Juvenile c.p.: NZ: 20C. B1941. Euphemistic and Maorified form of `By crickey, who are you?' <i>(1949,Dictionary of Slang Addenda,1006</i>)

### Maorify
 
 <i>v.</i> make Maori, esp. by transliteration (cf. MAORICISE)

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> (fn)<i> Rauna katoa</i> is a half-caste word, the English word round being here Maorified, and thus makes a more emphatic expression than any of their own. <i>(1868,Past and Present of NZ,134</i>)

>  <b>BULLER</b> They took the name of Pikopo. This no doubt was maorified from the Latin word <i>episcopos</i>; but, unfortunately for them, it had the ominous meaning in the Maori tongue, - <i>creeping in the dark. </i> <i>(1878,Forty Years in NZ,295</i>)

>  These words are ludicrously Maorified from the owls' common note of <i>ko&#773;u. ko&#773;u.</i>! ... by a kind of onomatopeia ... <i>(1889,TrNZI,21/205</i>)

>  <b>COWAN</b> He had taken a Scriptural name, Zerubbabel (maorified into Horopapera). <i>(1922,NZ Wars, The,2/4,1983 ed.</i>)

>  ... Hirawanu Tapu (properly Tchap' Tche'-har-r, Maorified into Tapu Te hara; and baptismally, Hirawanu Tapu) ... <i>(1928,BP Bishop Memoirs,Vol. IX No. 5 /358</i>) [NOTES: 1928 Baucke Manuscript in Skinner and Baucke The Morioris (Bishop Museum Memoirs Vol. IX No. 5 pp. 343-382) 358]

>  <i>To Maorify</i>, to infuse with or influence by Maori elements. 1878, J. Buller <i>Forty Years in New Zealand</i>. <i>(1945,Journal of the Polynesian Society,54/.231</i>) [NOTES: (S.J. Baker)]

>  <b>MIHAKA</b> Mr Mihaka I think I used the name Wiremu. ... [sic] My Grandfather came from Durham; hence the William. I took his name. Here and there they Maorify it. <i>(1984,Whakapohane,103</i>)

>  Pei Te Hurinui tells us that `although she [Puhiwahine] became quite proficient in English ... [sic] there was always a tendency on her part to `maorify' English words, ...' <i>(Mar 1983,Landfall,145/10</i>)

>  I am a white New Zealander, not a Pakeha, ... and I am bewildered and angered by incessant demands and efforts to Maorify everything. <i>(Dec 24, 1988,Dominion,8</i>)

### Maorihead
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) niggerhead or Carex secta, a tussocky swamp plant.

>  <b>ELWELL</b> 'Niggerheads', you know, are what you in New Zealand call 'Maori-heads'. <i>(1878,Boy Colonists,67</i>)

>  <b>ROBERTS</b> Maoriheads, a kind of grass tree about three feet high, abounded in the swamps, and in some we saw toitoi grass and raupo. <i>(1856,Diary,18</i>) [NOTES: in Beattie (1947) Early Runholding]

>  <b>MCGILL</b> maorihead aka niggerhead or Carex secta, a tussocky plant in swamps that looks, when burned off, like a blackened head. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,72</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> usu. <i>Carex</i> spp (esp. <i>C. secta</i> and <i>C. virgata</i>) (See [niggerhead](../N#niggerhead))

>  <b>W H S ROBERTS</b> Writing of the Waimea Plain in 1856: - `The ground was well grassed, but the speargrass was too plentiful. The pasturage was very good. There was a good deal of scrub in the bends of the Mataura known as `wire plant' (Olearia virgata). There were also manuka and koromiko. Maori-heads, a kind of grass tree about three feet high, abounded in the swamps, and in some we saw toitoi grass and raupo. We did not pass any cabbage trees to-day ... we proceeded slowly through tomatakuru scrub, speargrass, Spaniard, snowgrass, and other native grasses ... we camped in a bend with good feed at the Elbow and a swamp on one side. Maori cabbage abounded ...' <i>(1856,Diary</i>) [NOTES: 1856 W.H.S. Roberts Diary in 1947 Beattie Early Runholding 18]

>  Beneath the sheltering leaves of a large Maori-head (Carex virgata) <i>(1869,TrNZI,73</i>)

>  <b>THOMSON</b> ... Stilted tuft-grass called `Maori heads' by the settlers - trunk fibrous, roots three to five feet high with a tuft of grass on its head. <i>(1857,Southern Districts of NZ,323</i>) [NOTES: also in database: The stilted tufts called Morari [sic] heads .... indicate quagmire]

>  <b>TUAPEKA</b> To the village succeeded a swamp full of `Maori heads', which plant I can only liken to our own Victorian grass tree, except that it is a greener and thicker tuft. <i>(Sep 27, 1861,NZ Goldfields,24,1976 ed.</i>)

>  The [teal's] nest is made of grass, thickly lined with down, sometimes close to the edge of a swampy creek, or beneath the sheltering leaves of a large `Maori-head' (<i>Carex virgata</i>). <i>(1870,TrNZI,2/73</i>) [NOTES: (Potts Birds)]

>  ... the tops of the Maori-heads ... <i>(1871,TrNZI,3/108</i>) [NOTES: Potts]

>  <b>ELWELL</b> [Australia speaks] `Niggerheads', you know, are what you in New Zealand call `Maori-heads'. <i>(1878,Boy Colonists</i>)

>  And here the tussock grass grows in `Maori heads' above the soft treacherous mud. <i>(1883,Boy Colonists,15/486</i>) [NOTES: (Black)]

>  <b>D'AVIGDOR</b> The native New Zealand or tussock grass is a coarse, rough plant, and the ground which it covers is often dotted with huge tufts of a darker colour, called `Maori heads'. <i>(1888,Antipodean Notes,69</i>)

>  <b>PAULINE</b> Maori-head is the name given to a kind of grass which grows in tufts ... often several feet long. It is always found on swampy ground. When the swamp is drained the Maoriheads disappear. <i>(1889,Wild West Coast,31</i>)

>  <b>MCH</b> <i>(1892,Camp-Life in Fiordland,34</i>) [NOTES: [Morris]]

>  <b>MOFFAT</b> ... prowl around amongst many swampy lagoons back from the sea ... Then get a tussock of grass (known as Maori head) cut as much out of the middle as would go over your head, crawl into the swamp, [to catch ducks]. <i>(1896,Adventures by Sea and Land,55,1979 ed.</i>)

>  <b>ADAMS</b> <i>Carex virgata </i> <i>(1897,Counterfeit Seal</i>) [NOTES: (And)]

>  <b>MORRIS</b> <i>Maori-head, n.</i> a swamp tussock, so called from a fancied resemblance to the head of a Maori. (compare <i>Black-boy.</i>) It is not a grass, but a sedge (<i>carex</i>). <i>(1898,Austral English,285</i>)

>  <b>POT</b> <i>(1892,Out in the Open,169</i>)

>  <b>HOCKEN</b> Trending N. and N.E., a widespread level lies below, covered with flax and cabbage trees, toe-toe grass, and Maori-heads, intersected by sinuous, sluggish watercourses. Into this flat, the matau-kareao, descends a great semicircle of forest-clad spurs, ... <i>(1898,Contributions to the Early History of New Zealand,79</i>)

>  <i>Haumata</i> was the name of what are now called Maori-heads. ... [Williams 40 <i>Haumata ... Snow-grass</i>.] <i>(1921,TrNZI,52/72</i>) [NOTES: (Beattie)]

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> She rapidly crossed by jumping from one Maorihead to another and being complimented on her agility and skill, laughingly exclaimed, `A rush bush never deceived an Irishman.' <i>Ibid.</i>60 Of tussocks, swamp-hens, Maori-heads,/ And mud and bullock drays. <i>(1956,Pioneer Recollections,44</i>) [NOTES: 1947-1948 Beattie Pioneer Recollections (1956) 44]

>  <i>Maori-head:</i> swamp sedge. <i>(1947,Reeds' School Dictionary: NZ Suppl.,175</i>)

>  <b>EVEAS</b> The Maori Heads in the lagoon had assumed their peculiarly ghastly look. <i>(1874,Over the Hills,206</i>)

>  <b>PASCOE</b> Down where the drunken Woman's Creek/ Flows past the Cranky Man/ There sitting on a Moarie head/ I first espied my Nan <i>(1957,Mr Explorer Douglas,148</i>) [NOTES: 1957 (?d) C.Douglas Report to G.J.Roberts `Mr Explorer Douglas' ed J.D.Pascoe 148]

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>maorihead</i> aka `niggerhead' or <i>Carex secta</i>, a tussocky plant in swamps that looks, when burned off, like a blackened head. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,72</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>THOMSON</b> There were flax bushes and Maori-heads and thick clumps of rushes. <i>(1905,Bush Boys of NZ,181</i>)

>  Then it was one huge swamp.. covered with flax and "Maori heads." <i>(Dec 21, 1897,Otago Witness,7</i>)

### Maorihood
 
 <i>n.</i> [[is this really a reference to <i>Maoritanga</i>]]

>  <b>SOLJAK</b> [Sir James Carroll's] watchword, `Hold fast to your Maorihood,' inspired one of the most remarkable racial revivals in history. <i>(1946,New Zealand (various authors/titles),44</i>)

### Maoriland
 
 <i>n.</i> New Zealand (very frequent in `Truth' newspaper from 1905 on)

>  Casualness is asserted to be a Maoriland characteristic. <i>(Aug 12, 1905,NZ Truth,8/7/5</i>)

>  <b>WHITE</b> Maoriland hedgerows are calculated to disappoint a new-comer, for they deserve no praise whatever. <i>(1905,My NZ Garden,110</i>)

>  <b>BAUCKE</b> [The Maori woman] simply invited her skin-bleached new sister to enter and honour her whare, an incline on a mat inscribed with the legend, `Maoriland Hospice.' <i>(1905,Where the White Man Treads,245</i>)

>  <b>REISCHEK</b> The magnificence, the stately luxury of the plant world of the Maoriland bush filled me with constant wonder and delight. <i>(1930,Yesterdays in Maoriland,228,1933 ed.</i>)

>  [Piet] dived into his sack and produced a bottle of `dop'. `What is it you say when you drink in your country?' `Kia Ora!' explained a Maoriland trooper. Piet reverentially said `Kia Ora!' and sucked the mouth of the bottle. <i>(1930,NZ Short Stories,145</i>) [NOTES: Claude Jewell]

>  <b>CAMPBELL</b> ... joints of cold roast hogget mutton and a bottle ... of ... apple cider. It was a Maoriland man's picnic. <i>(1955,By Reef and Range,22</i>)

>  ... it was ... from the deck of a small trading schooner ... that I first cast eyes on Maori land. It <i>was</i> Maori land then; but, alas! what is it now? Success to you, O King of Waikato. May your<i> mana</i> never be less! <i>Ibid.</i> (vii) 105 There were in the old times two great institutions, which reigned with iron rod in Maori land - the <i>Tapu</i> and the <i>Muru</i>. Pakehas who knew no better called the <i>muru</i> simply `robbery', because the word <i>Muru</i>, in its common signification, means to plunder. But I speak of the regular legalized and established system of plundering as penalty for offences, which in a rough way resembled our law by which a man is obliged to pay `damages'. <i>(1863,Old New Zealand,(i)3</i>)

>  In the opinion of many Maoriland democrats, the old system of life membership was preferable. <i>(Feb 6, 1904,NZ Observer,3</i>)

>  The Maoriland youth has a liking to call his boss Bill. <i>(Aug 6, 1904,NZ Observer,3</i>)

>  The four Maoriland Parliamentarians have had a good time during the last two months, and have greatly enjoyed their stay in the old Dart. <i>(Aug 30, 1916,Chronicles NZEF,I/1 : 1</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> (informal) New Zealand, orig. journalistic or poetic, but later taken up by Sydney <i>Bulletin</i> writers and editors.

>  <i>Maoriland</i> is the earliest of these [informal nicknames], recorded in <i>AND</i> from 1859 and <i>DNZE</i> from 1865; this was a common journalistic term in Australia in the 1880s and later (popularised in particular by the Sydney <i>Bulletin</i>, but it has long since had its day. <i>(Oct 2000,NZWords,4 : 2</i>)

>  The Duchess of Dillwater and the Dishonourable Percy Prig will.. see nothing but mutton and eggs, and bonedust from Maoriland. <i>(Aug 15, 1923,Aussie (NZ Section),I</i>)

>  Maoriland is no longer the land of the artistically lost. <i>(May 28, 1928,NZ Free Lance,11</i>)

>  <b>MORRIS</b> <i>Maoriland, n</i>. a modern name for New Zealand. It is hardly earlier than 1884. If the word, or anything like it, such as <i>Maoria</i>, was used earlier, it meant `the Maori parts of New Zealand.' It is now used for the whole. <i>(1898,Austral English</i>)

>  The Sydney Bulletin in writing of New Zealand, styles it Maoriland; Queensland, it dubs Bananaland; West Australia is designated Westralia ... <i>(Aug 10, 1894,Argus and Newtown Chronicle,3</i>)

>  <b>LAWSON</b> <i>(1896,When the Billy Boils,23</i>)

>  <b>ST JOHN</b> [Title]: `Pakeha Rambles through Maori Lands.' <i>(1873,Pakeha Rambles Through Maori Lands</i>)

>  <b>JOHNSTONE</b> Maoria: a sketch of the Manners and Customs of the Aboriginal Inhabitants of New Zealand.' <i>(1874,Maoria</i>)

>  [etc: very frequent `Truth' word] ... since their return to Maoriland. <i>(Jun 24, 1905,NZ Truth,1/1/1</i>)

>  Have we not drawn our sins of advertisement to a head by tamely adopting the foreign label of `Maoriland' for our country? As a foreign label it is melodious and in offensive ... As adopted and ratified by ourselves, it is as appropos and dignified as a sale-board at the front gate. <i>(1907,Red Funnel,4/33</i>)

>  <b>DOWNES</b> ... who, like the author, have lived all their days in Maoriland ... <i>(1909,TrNZI,42/75</i>)

>  <b>PINFOLD</b> Some of the names given to it [sc New Zealand] are very suggestive and often throw light upon its position, character and destiny. It has been called the Britain of the South; the Wonderland of the Pacific; and God's Own Country. Maoriland is another name by which it is known and indicates the native race ... <i>(1930,Fifty Years In Maoriland,13</i>)

>  <b>HYDE</b> The Deans' bush is the only unspoiled haven for the trees and birds of Maoriland anywhere near Christchurch ... <i>(1934,Journalese,109</i>)

>  <b>HYDE</b> Sometimes the school journal called New Zealand `Maoriland' or `Ao-te-aroa'. There, again, you hardly ever saw a Maori, and if you did, it was in town. <i>(1938,Godwits Fly, The,32,1970 ed.</i>)

>  Maui our Ancestor fished up this Aotearoa of ours from the dark waters of Kiwa. Today it is our Rangatira Savage who is fishing up this Maoriland of ours from the gloom and slime of depression ... <i>(1938,Ephemera</i>) [NOTES: 1938 Labour pamphlet for Northern Maori in 1986 Gustafson Cradle to Grave [facing 232]]

>  <b>BAKER</b> Hitherto it has been commonly supposed that [<i>Maoriland</i>] was originated by the <i>Bulletin,</i> Sydney, after 1880. The <i>Bulletin </i>certainly uses the initials M.L. to signify New Zealand, and may possibly have used the derivative<i> Maorilander</i> for a New Zealander, originally, although this is to be doubted. <i>(1941,NZ Slang,31</i>)

>  <b>STEVENSON</b> Apparently some time during the early part of the 19th century, the continuity of this ancient form of life was finally broken and Moaland became Maoriland. <i>(1947,Maori and Pakeha in North Otago,156</i>)

>  <i>Maoriland ... n</i>. modern name for N.Z.; whence <i>Maorilander</i>, whites born in N.Z. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),13</i>)

>  <b>SCRYMGEOUR</b> [Title] Memories of Maoriland <i>(1960,Memories of Maoriland</i>)

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> One of the last of the bellringers to parade the streets of Maoriland could often be heard [at Reefton c1900], ringing out his sad or glad, or indifferent tidings, ... <i>(1961,Friends in Chains,45</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> When we flew home we sang songs ...Our green and brown Maoriland below looked wonderfully bright, as we came in over the coast. <i>(1976,Pacific Star,129</i>)

>  `Always something new from Maori-land! Our New Zealand friends are kindly obliging far demagogues in office will actually go.' <i>(Jul 22, 1896,Argus, The,4/8</i>)

>  <b>CARTER</b> In the first expedition I ever undertook in Maori-land I was accompanied by two gentleman. <i>(1866,Life in NZ,428</i>)

>  <b>BAINES</b> [I] would like again, if ever I got back to Maoriland, to have another turn in my old boat. <i>(1874,Edward Crewe,286</i>)

>  <b>HOOD</b> [Nervous] persons, when building their houses in Maoriland, recollect that it will be prudent to construct them of wood. <i>(1875,Chowbokiana,73</i>)

>  [title] Maoriland: an Illustrated Handbook <i>(1884,Maoriland</i>)

>  <b>BUTLER</b> [title] Glimpses of Maoriland <i>(1886,Glimpses of Maori Land</i>)

>  <b>BRACKEN</b> [title] Musings in Maoriland (Poems) <i>(1890,Musings in Maoriland</i>)

>  We colonists, having adopted Maoriland as our country .... <i>(1893,TrNZI,25/398</i>)

>  That author has, with his camera, visited many outlying parts of Maoriland with great enterprise and success. <i>(1898,TrNZI,30/540</i>) [NOTES: (Hector)]

>  <b>WAITE</b> Smoking the ration cigarette after tea, the New Zealander ... would see the sheep ... cropping the sweet green grass of Maoriland - when `Whizz! crash!' would come the Turkish gunners' evening hate. <i>(1919,New Zealanders at Gallipoli,184</i>)

>  <i>New Zealand - or What?</i> [title] As New Zealand the world knows us, for better or for worse, and the title will stick, and good luck to it! As for Zealandia, Maoriland, and the like, we may safety leave them to the poets. <i>(Jun 10, 1922,Quick March,7</i>)

>  The jolly folk of Maoriland to me are very dear - (And they will be jollier still when lasting peace is here)! <i>(Feb 27, 1920,Hawke's Bay Herald,6</i>)

>  <b>NALDER</b> You [sc. G.R. Bradford] were the first to fall / Of those of Maoriland. <i>(1902,Battle-smoke Ballads,23</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> This use may be a narrowing of 1, but it would seem to refer to the Maori parts of New Zealand, and not to the whole country or to pre-European New Zealand as a `land of Maori' (Best's examples may be his personal use: or the expression may be mainly `pakeha-maori')

>  <b>ANNA ROBINA</b> <i>(1886,Glimpses of Maori Land</i>) [NOTES: [title NZNB no.837 1/149]]

>  In Maoriland seeds were planted at the full of the moon, .. <i>(1903,TrNZI</i>) [NOTES: (Best)]

>  Scenes of violent abduction were by no means rare in Maoriland. <i>Ibid.</i> 42 The term tanmen ..... is applied to the custom of betrothal of young people which obtained in Maoriland. <i>(1904,TrNZI,36/41</i>) [NOTES: (Best)]

>  On the tops I saw no trace of the presence of any animal alien to primitive Maoriland <i>(1908,TrNZI,40/291</i>)

>  Some person chopping grubs (huhu) from a decayed tree, a common practice in Maoriland <i>(1908,TrNZI,41/278</i>) [NOTES: Best]

>  ... Kai-Tahu, who belonged to the conquering strain, whose achievements in Maoriland were analogous to the Norman Conquest in New Zealand. <i>Ibid.</i> 74 Some of the old people were not pleased with the introduction of vermin to Maoriland. <i>(1921,TrNZI,52/48</i>) [NOTES: (Beattie)]

>  Moa bones found in a cave while excavating for the foundations of a new Auckland hospital have been identified as the remains of the ancient birds of Maoriland <i>(1958,Better Business,21(219):75 7/1958</i>)

>  <b>REED</b> It must be understood that no definite date can be fixed for any Maoriland event that took place before the coming of the white man. The New Zealanders had, of course, no written language until the missionaries showed them ... how the sound of the written word could be represented by written signs. <i>(1947,Story of Canterbury, The,23</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> a Maori district, or specifically the King Country [[see Payton Knocking About NZ 107]]

>  <b>KENNEDY</b> Suddenly, as if to signalise our arrival at the precincts of Maori-Land, there shot across the heavans a most brilliant meteor. <i>(1877,Colonial Travel,252</i>)

>  <b>REISCHEK</b> A fortunate accident made it possible for me to enter the King Country, the heart of Maoriland. <i>Ibid.</i> 139 `... Avoid all sale and lease of land. Permit no European to cross the border of this, our last free Maoriland. We want no roads or schools from them. Let them do with their own land what they will.' The boundaries of a free and independent Maoriland were fixed as follows: ... <i>Ibid.</i> 149 When I arrived in Alexandra, the boundary township of Maoriland, the chiefs told me to my dismay that I might not cross the border. <i>(1930,Yesterdays in Maoriland,96</i>)

>  <b>RINGLAND</b> You are away from the beaten track of travel, and far into the heart of Maoriland [specifically the King Country] <i>(1888,In Southern Seas,96</i>)

### Maorilander
 
 <i>n.</i> a New Zealand-born white; a New Zealander (Probably orig. Australian; or used to distinguish New Zealanders from Australians)

>  Many of us tired Maorilanders.. seek the distractions of.. the South Sea Islands. <i>(Aug 4, 1899,Auckland Weekly News,Suppl. 4</i>)

>  <b>BRACKEN</b> A recent arrival from New Zealand walking along Collins St, Melbourne, a short time since encountered another Maorilander. <i>(1893,Lays and Lyrics,5</i>)

>  <b>MORRIS</b> <i>Maorilander, n</i>. modern name for a white man born in New Zealand. <i>(1898,Austral English,286</i>)

>  The average Maorilander is strong, fast, and has more stamina than his Australian brother. <i>(1900,NZ Illustrated Magazine,3/236</i>)

>  <b>WALKER</b> [NZ soldiers in Boer War, distinct from `you Cornstalks' a N.S.W. sentry speaks] I've got a bit [of a newspaper account] .... about the Maorilander too. <i>(1902,Zealandia's Guerdon,223</i>)

>  At the present gait of going the Maorilander of the future will have an oval-shaped head [like a football]. <i>(Jun 24, 1905,NZ Truth,1/1/1</i>)

>  <b>PICARD</b> [I] may some day have to give Matilda another ride through this spot they call God's own country. Think its [sic] Dick Seddon's mostly. Gets a big hearing with the Maorilanders. <i>(1906,Some Ups and Downs in NZ&A,8</i>)

>  There have been frequent mentions in `Truth' of the inconceivable dislike manifested for Australians by Maorilanders. <i>(Sep 15, 1906,NZ Truth,65/5/6</i>)

>  <b>RUTHERFORD</b> We ate our Christmas dinner in company with a small party of other Maorilanders ... <i>(1912,Impressions of a NZ Pastoralist on Tour,139</i>)

>  <b>'ANZAC'</b> Men who had seen service with the Maorilanders in South Africa <i>(1916,On the Anzac Trail,2</i>)

>  An inspiring little tale of ... a big Maorilander, himself badly wounded, ... <i>(Aug 1, 1918,Quick March,23</i>)

>  <b>SCRYMGEOUR</b> ... for two years the youthful Maorilanders walked to school and back, ... <i>(1960,Memories of Maoriland,26</i>)

>  <i>maorilander n</i> -s <i>cap [Maoriland</i> New Zealand (fr.<i> Maori + land) + -er</i>] : a native or inhabitant of New Zealand <i>(1898,Merriam-Webster Third International,1379</i>)

>  `Norman is a pushing young Maorilander who apparently has the Britisher by the right ear.' <i>(Apr 9, 1896,Melbourne Punch,233/2</i>)

>  The 1884 team and other New Zealand teams through to the 1930s were invariably described as "Maorilanders". <i>(Jan 1992,Metro,79</i>)

>  The latter, on the other hand, asserted that he was giving the Maorilander every chance and the referee took the same view ... <i>(May 2, 1885,Otago Witness,21</i>)

>  Looking at the condition of "Marvellous Melbourne", it wouldn't surprise us to see each returning Maorilander bring a brace of Australians back with him. <i>(Feb 17, 1892,Tuapeka Times,2</i>)

>  The wiry little Maorilander set to his task with a will, and he covered the six miles and had over a half-mile to spare in the time alloted to him. <i>(May 4, 1886,Wanganui Herald,2</i>)

>  Until New Zealand began to settle down into national conditions the Americans were, perhaps, the greatest people in creation, but now Brother Jonathan must surely give way to the Maorilander. <i>(Nov 30, 1889,North Otago Times,1</i>)

### Maoriness
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>JAMES MARTIN</b> `Let it not be said that the Maoriness of a man unfits him to be a Bishop! <i>(1877,Letter</i>) [NOTES: 1877 James Martin letter in T.S.Grace Journal in Grace Pioneer Missionary (1928) 258]

>  <b>HILLIARD</b> What, after all, was wrong? It was his Pakehaness. ... What is wrong with the Negroes is their Negroness. What is wrong with the Chinese is their Chineseness. What is wrong with me is my Maoriness. <i>(1969,Green River,73</i>)

>  <b>HULME</b> I think he was ashamed, secretly ashamed, of my Nana and her Maoriness. <i>(1983,Bone People, The,238</i>)

>  Another of the worries that I have about MTS is that the 'Maoriness' of it all isn't as front-of-the-mind as it should be. <i>(Jun 8, 2002,Listener (New Zealand Listener),27</i>)

>  The overarching theme of the play is straightforward enough..; how the Europeanised Maori Battalion veteran.. rediscovered his Maoriness. <i>(Apr 22, 2000,Listener (New Zealand Listener),37</i>)

>  "The Maori seats are an opportunity to endorse my Maoriness". <i>(May 9, 2006,NZ Herald,A6</i>)

>  It was really getting a Maori Education Foundation grant at the University of Canterbury that awoke in me a consciousness of my Maoriness. <i>(Jul 29, 1992,Listener (New Zealand Listener),56</i>)

>  Its important the work doesn't move so far out it loses its Maoriness, its identity. <i>(Jun 7, 2001,City Voice,4</i>)

>  While regular visits home for important events such as tangihana continued, a sense of "Maoriness" also evolved. <i>(Nov 30, 1999,Evening Post,24</i>)

>  The Maoriness of the North Island is what is best about it -to this Southerner, at least. <i>(1973,Islands,6/357</i>)

>  <b>Michael King</b> This [Kingitanga] movement... arose in part from the fact that the presence of Europeans had helped to create a sense of 'Maoriness'. <i>(2003,Penguin History of New Zealand,191</i>)

>  I believe in the importance of early childhood experience. I think they formed a bottom layer of Maoriness in him, if you see what I mean. <i>(Jun 17, 2000,Listener (New Zealand Listener),43</i>)

### Maorioke
 
 <i>n.</i> Maori Television song contest

>  The only Whakatane person to reach the final of <i>Maorioke</i> will have her chance to shine on Sunday as she vies for top place. <i>(Sep 21, 2005,Whakatane Beacon,11</i>)

>  These are the qualities that helped Tamehana win Aotearoa's first Maorioke competition. <i>(2005,Te Mana  Taiohi,20,Spring ed.</i>)

>  Maorioke first went to air on Maori Television in 2003. <i>(Jul 2006,Pu Kaea,10</i>)

### Maoriphobia
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Baby No-Eyes is an oral history, a case study, a social document, a novel torn from today's headlines. It's about Maoriphobia - racism lurking within the recesses of institutions. <i>(Nov 28, 1998,Listener (New Zealand Listener),40</i>)

### Maoris in Common
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) a scheme involving Maori farmers in joint management.

>  <b>TAIT</b> Before the turn of the century, the property was farmed by Maoris under a scheme known as 'Maoris in Common.' <i>(1961,Farms & Stations of NZ Vol III,159</i>)

### Maorisation
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>McCRAE</b> But <i>b</i> finds no place in the vocabulary, and <i>buka buka</i> [u-macrons] was evidently regarded as a Maorization of the word `book' though <i>pukapuka</i> is genuine Maori. <i>(1820,Journal,29</i>) [NOTES: 1928 J.C. Andersen in 1820 McCrae Journal 29]

### Maorise
 
 <i>v.</i>

>  <b>THOMSON</b> All the English week-days have been Maorised except Sunday, which is known as the Ratapu, or sacred day. <i>(1859,Story of New Zealand, The,1/199</i>)

### Maoristan
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  If it were possible or desirable for Maori people.. to .. live on a reservation, "homeland" or "Bantustan", they could, of course, run their own schools, hospitals, police and courts... The "Maoristan" would have to be subsidised by the general taxpayer, reducing the people who lived there to colourful pets. <i>(Sep 24, 1995,Sunday Star Times,C3</i>)

### Maoritanga
 
 <i>n.</i> a general name for traditions, practices and beliefs which are Maori, as distinct from Pakeha. [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  <b>DIEFFENBACH</b> Maoritanga - native custom, natural use <i>(1843,Travels in NZ,2/370</i>)

>  There is no doubt that more than the spirit which pervades our people on development work ..., what impressed the Tokerau delegates last year when they visited those districts was the `maoritanga' of the tribes inhabiting that part of the Eastern Maori electorate. It was not a maoritanga of the lips as among the Waikato, but was manifested in persistent cultural traits, carvings, plaiting, marae ceremonial, poetry and song, speech and so forth. <i>(Jun 11, 1933,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,3/85</i>) [NOTES: (Ngata)]

>  <b>ZIMMERMAN</b> Immediately she came in, however, something within her made her incline to the <i>maoritanga</i> of her companions, and I believe it was at her request that the girls began to speak only Maori in their free hours and at meals, ... <i>(1946,Where the People Sing,59</i>)

>  <b>CRUISE</b> Only less [9] astonishing than the number of chiefs who went overseas, even to England, during this time was the contrast between their general bearing while away and the resumption of their Maoritanga on their return. <i>(1957,Journal</i>) [NOTES: 1957 Bagnall Introduction to 1823 Cruise Journal (1957) 8-9]

>  <i>maoritanga n -s usu cap</i> [Maori] 1 : the traditions and ideals and culture of the Maori people 2 : Maori nationalism <i>(1961,Merriam-Webster Third International,1379</i>)

>  <b>CAMERON</b> To a certain extent, in fact, <i>Maoritanga</i> is not a matter of inheritance; it is a matter of <i>he manawa</i>, of `how one feels.' <i>Ibid.</i> 75 But when Sir James Carroll and other leaders of the Young Maori Party wanted a term to denote the way of life of their people, they took the completely-assimilated modern meaning of the word `Maori' - which, thanks to the <i>pakeha</i>, transcended tribal boundaries - and coined a new meaning for `Maoritanga.' The meaning of the term is debated and discussed, analyzed and defined, used and abused with great frequency in the present upsurge of interest in Maori culture and traditional ways of thinking, feeling, and acting. A second Maori renaissance seems to be under way, aided by this all-embracing term and strengthened by the success of the first one in the early decades of this century. <i>(1965,New Zealand (various authors/titles),94</i>)

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> [King Tawhiao] withdrew across the Puniu River to bear the standard of <i>Maoritanga</i> is isolation from missionaries, ... . <i>(1963,Ratana,4,1972 ed.</i>)

>  <b>BAXTER</b> You might stay well if you learnt Maoritanga/ <i>(1979,Collected Poems,556,1972 ed.</i>)

>  <i>(Dec 13, 1968,Listener (New Zealand Listener),6/1</i>)

>  <b>REED</b> (gloss) maoritanga: cultural heritage of the Maori. <i>(1977,Treasury of Maori Exploration,268</i>)

>  <b>MARSH</b> On my first visit to New Zealand I met one of your people who told me about Maoritanga. <i>(1980,Photo-Finish,97</i>)

>  <b>HULME</b> 64 Maoritanga = Maori culture, Maoriness <i>(1983,Bone People, The,465</i>)

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Suttonia <i>australis</i>, A.Rich. ... Nat. names, `Mapau', `Sipau', and `Matipo', Middle Island, <i>Lyall.</i> `Tipau', north of the Thames river, and `Mapua', south of that river, <i>Colenso.</i> ... A small leafy tree, very closely resembling <i>Pittosporum undulatum</i> in appearance. <i>(1853,Flora NZ,I Flowering Plants 172</i>)

>  For the two women their Maoritanga even spelt the end of their marriages to Pakeha. <i>(May 17, 1986,Listener (New Zealand Listener),22</i>)

>  The keynote to Maoritanga is the fact that the Maori race has distinct racial personality, and what we have come to term Maoritanga is the expression of that distinctive racial personality in language, poetry and art, customs and usage, rite and ceremony, work and play. <i>(1954,Te Ao Hou,8: 10</i>) [NOTES: Winter 1954 number]

>  Ngata? told me that the word was coined by Sir James Carroll just after the First World War. [Bill Parker confirmed this in private communication - but check again]. <i>(Dec 7, 1983,Radio NZ / National Radio</i>) [NOTES: 1983 Dec 7 2YA Wellington Wiremu Parker reviewing Michael King's book.]

>  <i>Maoritanga n. </i>being Maori; Maori traditions and culture. [M] <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,458</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 299]]

>  <b>MACRAE</b> `I'm not interested in any maoritanga shit,' Lillian said ... <i>(1988,Awful Childhoods,88</i>)

>  <b>FRAME</b> Then they get restless, they want to be somewhere else, they've had Maoritanga for the time being, thank you, and yet if you happen to be among a group of people at a function you hear the same people holding forth about having 'been on the marae' and how wonderful it is. <i>(1988,Carpathians, The,84</i>)

### mapau
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>TUAPEKA</b> Here [at Taeri] was the tall and graceful black pine; the manukau, a shrub like the [Victorian] tea-tree scrub, only more greenly leaved; a few broad leaved grass-trees; the mappo, the kolu, the red pine, and others too numerous for me to remember; but their leaves were ... delightful to one accustomed to the dingy foliage of Victorian forests. <i>(Sep 27, 1861,NZ Goldfields,23,1976 ed.</i>)

>  <b>BARKER</b> The mappo ... matapo <i>(1874,Station Life in NZ,94</i>)

>  The few clumps of trees consist of pohutukawa, mapou, manuka, rewarewa, akeake, whau or corkwood, pukapuka, and a few puriri, which, however, is of little value, being very scattered and ruined by fire. <i>(1884,TrNZI,17/419</i>) [NOTES: (Gold-Smith)]

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> The Maori went to the bush and cut down a [31] mapou - not matapo, which is neither Maori nor English - a great, heavy green trunk and carried it to Suisted's house ... <i>(1939,First White Boy Born in Otago,30</i>)

>  MAPOU/ <i>Myrsine australis</i>/Tree up to 6m. Bark and leaf stalks red-brown. <i>(1982,Field Guide to Common NZ Trees & Shrubs,26</i>)

>  <b>HOLMES</b> <i>Myrsine Coxii</i> (Mapou) - a small swamp-forest tree found ... on b. Pittosporum spp. (usu. with colour epithet) <i>(1984,Chatham Islands,93</i>)

>  There are several varieties of Pittosporum (<i>Mapau</i>) in Otago. <i>(1868,Province of Otago in NZ, The,34</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> often loosely called matipo: a group of shrubs or small trees, of roughly similar general appearance, used mainly as hedge-plants or ornamentals. a) red matipo, having reddish bark. b) loosely used for some Pittosporum species, such as black mapau or matipo; white mapau, tarata. [Maori mapau[a1-macron], mapou[a1-macron] red matipo]. 1. <i>Myrsine australis</i> (formerly <i>Suttonia australis, Myrsine Urvillei, Rapanea Urvillei</i>) (red) matipo, red mapau, =maple (See [also mapauriki](../A#also-mapauriki),[maple](../M#maple))

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Mapau, <i>Col[enso]. Myrsine australis. </i> <i>(1867,Handbook NZ Flora (List of Names),766</i>)

>  <b>MORRIS</b> <i>Mapau, n</i>. a Maori name for several New Zealand trees; called also<i> Mapou</i>, and frequently corrupted by settlers into <i>Maple</i>, by the law of Hobson-Jobson. The name is applied to the following. The Mapau - <i>Myrsine urvillei</i>, De C., <i>N.O. Myrsineaem</i>; sometimes called <i>Red Mapau</i>. Black M. - <i>Pittosporum tenuifolium</i>, Banks and Sol., <i>N.O. Pittosporeae; </i>Maori name <i>Tawhiri</i>. White M. - <i>Carpodetus serratus,</i> Forst., <i>N.O. Saxifrageae; Pittosporum eugenoides,</i> A. Cunn.; Maori name, <i>Tarata</i> (q.v.); called also the <i>Hedge-laurel</i> (q.v.), <i>Lemon-wood,</i> and <i>New Zealand Oak</i>. See <i>Oak</i>. The first of these (<i>Myrsine urvillei</i>) is, according to Colenso, the only tree to which the Maoris themselves give the name <i>Mapau.</i> The others are only so called by the settlers. <i>(1898,Austral English,286</i>)

>  <b>LAING & BLACKWELL</b> <i>[Suttonia australis (The Red Matipo)<i>] ... Native name <i>Mapau, Mapou</i>, or <i>Matipo</i>. The name Matipo is generally applied by Europeans to <i>Pittosporum tenuifolium</i>, but this is apparently the plant to which the Maoris attached it. This tree bears some resemblance to <i>Pittosporum tenuifolium</i>, but can be readily distinguished by the reddish colour of the leaves. [<i>Rapanea Urvillei</i>, 1907] <i>(1907,Plants of NZ,332,1940 p. 353 ed.</i>)

>  <b>LAING & BLACKWELL</b> <i>[Suttonia australis (The Red Matipo)</i>] ... Native name <i>Mapau, Mapou</i>, or <i>Matipo</i>. The name Matipo is generally applied by Europeans to <i>Pittosporum tenuifolium</i>, but this is apparently the plant to which the Maoris attached it. This tree bears some resemblance to <i>Pittosporum tenuifolium</i>, but can be readily distinguished by the reddish colour of the leaves. [<i>Rapanea Urvillei</i>, 1907] <i>(1907,Plants of NZ,332,1940 p. 353 ed.</i>)

>  <i>Mapau n</i>. a small tree of several kinds, sometimes corrupted into `maple' [Maori] <i>(1929,Whitcombe's Etym. Dict. Aust-NZ Suppl.,7</i>)

>  <i>mapau, matipo n. </i>several varieties of New Zealand trees [Maori corrupted to maple]. <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Whitcombe's),620</i>)

>  <i>mapau, matipo ... n. </i>varieties of N.Z. trees [Maori corr. of<i> maple</i>]. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),13</i>)

>  <b>MOORE & IRWIN</b> <i>MYRSINE AUSTRALIS, mapau</i>. A tall shrub widespread ... recognized by its dull red stems ... <i>(1978,Oxford Book of NZ Plants,116</i>)

>  The only true Matipo has red stems and is also named Mapou (Myrsine australis). <i>(Sep 19, 1975,Evening Post,2/8</i>)

>  <i>mapau n.</i> small NZ tree with reddish twigs and small dark berries. [M] <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,458</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.]]

>  Red Mapau (Myrsine Urvillei) common at Dunedin. Wood red ... <i>Ibid.</i> 39 Mapau Pittosporum tenuifolium, Banks and Sol. <i>Ibid.</i> 46 Mapau. Myrsine Urvillei, A. D.C. <i>(1868,TrNZI,1/33/37</i>) [NOTES: Buchanan Botany Otago]

>  The Tipau, or Mapau, (<i>Myrsine australis</i>,) is a small leafy tree ... more plentiful at the north. <i>(1869,TrNZI,1/III/48</i>) [NOTES: (Colenso Botany North Isd)]

>  <b>KIRK</b> MAPAU.- (<i>Myrsine australis</i>.) <i>(1875,Durability NZ Timbers,20</i>)

>  <b>HAY</b> The Mapau (<i>Myrsine Urvillei</i>) affords good material for fencing. <i>(1882,Brighter Britain,2/198</i>)

>  Amongst smaller trees and shrubs the most prominent are the horoeka(<i>Panax crassifolia</i>).. the mapau (<i>Myrsine australis</i>), the pokako (<i>Eloeocarpus hookerianus</i>), also <i>Carpodetus serratus, Myrsine divaricata</i>, both of which are abundant. <i>(1884,TrNZI,17/216</i>) [NOTES: (Kirk)]

>  <b>KIRK</b> [Native name] Mapua .. [Settlers' name] Mapua .. [Family] Myrsineae <i>(1902,Settler's Handbook of NZ, The,122</i>) [NOTES: 1886 T.Kirk in 1902 Settler's Handbook of NZ 122]

>  <b>BATHGATE</b> <i>(1889,Colonial Experiences</i>)

>  Mapau .. Myrsine Urvillei .. Wood pale red colour; ... and, the grain being prettily marked, it is suitable for cabinetmaking <i>(1890,PWD Catalogue of Timbers,12</i>)

>  <b>1/37</b> <i>(1868,TrNZI</i>) [NOTES: `Essay on Botany of Otago,' p. 37: [quotations also 1883 Hector, 1889 Kirk]]

>  <b>CHEESEMAN</b> <i>M[yrsine] Urvillei,</i> D.C. ... <i>Mapau; Tipau </i> <i>(1906,Manual NZ Flora,432</i>)

>  <i>Ma&#769;.pa-u</i> (ma.&#769;.pa.-u; <i>colloq.</i> m&adot.&#769;.pou), <i> n</i>. [Maori] <i>Ma&#769;.po-u</i>(ma.&#769;.p&omacr.-u;<i> colloq</i>. m&adot.&#769;.pu), <i> (Bot.)</i> A New Zealand tree<i> Myrsine Urvillei</i>), of the order <i>Myrsineae</i>; called also <i>matapou, matapo, matipo,</i> and <i>maple. [Local, N. Z.] Black mapau, </i>name applied incorrectly by New Zealand settlers to the tree <i>Pittosporum tenuifolium</i>, of the order <i>Pittosporeae. - White mapau, (a) </i>The tarata <i>(Pittosporum eugenoides);</i> -called also <i>turpentine tree. (b) </i>The saxifragaceous tree piripiri <i>(Carpodetus serratus); called often mapou.</i> See PIRIPIRI. <i>(1898,Merriam-Webster Third International,2026</i>)

### maple
 
 <i>n.</i> a folk alteration of mapau, mapou, esp. common in southern South Island: (possibly from exudations of gum (see 1769) as well as spelling and pronunciation: final -ou would approach a variant dark -l final) [AND 1889]

>  <b>KEENE</b> The maples - red, white and black - were there, and ribbonwood or lace bark; ... <i>(1967,Tang of The Bush,34</i>) [NOTES: [Otago]]

>  Maple trees, totara, miki miki, broadleaf, lancewood, all entangled by a dense undergrowth of ferns and parasitic creepers. <i>(Jun 6, 1885,Adelaide Observer,41</i>) [NOTES: 1885 June 6 The Adelaide Observer 41 in 1987 Hill Richard Henry of Resolution Island 88]

>  <i>Myrsine australis </i>, maple <i>(1877,TrNZI</i>) [NOTES: (Andersen).]

>  The perversity with which the true `beeches' have become popularly known as `birches,' and different kinds of `mapau' as `maples - a popular name which includes small trees of widely distinct Natural Order, is enough to fill with despair any botanist who desires to foster a system of popular names that will be free from misleading and inaccurate suggestions. <i>(1891,NZ Journal of Science,1/50</i>)

>  <b>MORRIS</b> <i>Maple, n</i>. In New Zealand, a common settlers' corruption for any tree called <i>Mapau</i> (q.v.); in Australia, applied to <i>Villaresia moorei,</i> F. v. M., N.O. <i>Olacinea,</i> called also the <i>Scrub Silky Oak</i>. See <i>Oak. </i> <i>(1898,Austral English,287</i>)

>  <b>PARTRIDGE</b> <i>Maple</i>. `In New Zealand, a common settlers' corruption for any tree called <i>Mapau'</i>: C. 19-20. Morris. <i>(1937,Dictionary of Slang,509</i>)

>  <i>(1877,TrNZI</i>) [NOTES: (Andersen)]

>  The tree which we cut for fireing was something like Maple and yielded a whitish Gum, 2. there was another sort of a deep yallow which we imagined might prove usefull in dying. [f@n2] <i>Tarata, Pittosporum eugenioides</i>; early colonists called it maple, though its leaf is quite different; now generally known as lemonwood. <i>(Oct 29, 1769,Cook's Journals,1/186</i>)

>  goi, rata, bokako, birch, maple <i>(1875,Official Handbook of NZ,107</i>)

>  <b>LAING & BLACKWELL</b> [<i>Pittosporum eugenioides</i>] it is often cultivated for its beauty, and is sometimes - though not so often as <i>P. [195] tenuifolium</i> - used to form an ornamental hedge. It is known by a variety of names amongst the colonists, such as <i>Mapau, White Mapau</i>, and even<i> Maple</i>, and <i>Lemon-tree.</i> The Maori name seems to have been <i>Tarata,</i> but it is also called the <i>Mapau. </i> <i>(1907,Plants of NZ,194,1940, p. 203 ed.</i>)

### mapou
 
 <i>n.</i> = mapau, matipo (<i>Myrsine divaricata</i>) [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  <b>BARKER</b> I can give you no idea of the variety among the shrubs: the koromika, like an Alpine rose, a compact ball of foliage; the lancewood, a tall, slender stem, ... with a few long leaves at the top, turned downwards like the barb of a spear, and looking exactly like a lance stuck into the ground; the varieties of matapo ... each leaf a study, with its beautiful tracery of black veins on a yellow-green ground; the mappo, the gohi, and many others ... <i>(1866,Station Life in NZ,94,1870 ed.</i>)

>  <i>Ma&#769;.pa-u</i> (ma.&#769;.pa.-u; <i>colloq</i>. m&adot.&#769;.pou), n. [Maori] <i>Ma&#769;.po-u</i> (ma.&#769;.p&omacr.-u; <i>colloq</i>. m&adot.&#769;.pu), (<i>Bot.</i>) A New Zealand tree <i>Myrsine Urvillei</i>), of the order <i>Myrsineae</i>; called also <i>matapou, matapo, matipo</i>, and <i>maple. [Local, N. Z.] Black mapau</i>, name applied incorrectly by New Zealand settlers to the tree <i>Pittosporum tenuifolium</i>, of the order <i>Pittosporeae. - White mapau, (a) </i>The tarata (<i>Pittosporum eugenoides</i>); -called also <i>turpentine tree. (b) </i>The saxifragaceous tree piripiri (<i>Carpodetus serratus); called often mapou</i>. See PIRIPIRI. <i>(1898,Merriam-Webster Third International,2026</i>)

>  In the understorey, there is excellent regeneration of tawa, matai and mapou. <i>(Mar 2009,Open Space,75/24</i>)

>  About five minutes in, you'll see a side track off to the right, which leads under a low, almost pure canopy of mapou to another lookout. <i>(Jan 5, 2004,Dominion Post,C3</i>)

>  Another native that tolerates very dry, hostile conditions is the weeping mapou or matipo (Myrsine divaricata). <i>(Sep 16, 2001,Sunday Star Times,D8</i>)
