### marae
 
 <i>n.</i> [attrib. uses]

>  There is no doubt that more than the spirit which pervades our people on development work ..., what impressed the Tokerau delegates last year when they visited those districts was the `maoritanga' of the tribes inhabiting that part of the Eastern Maori electorate. It was not a maoritanga of the lips as among the Waikato, but was manifested in persistent cultural traits, carvings, plaiting, marae ceremonial, poetry and song, speech and so forth. <i>(Jun 11, 1933,Na To Hoa Aroha,3/85</i>) [NOTES: (Ngata)]

>  <b>FULLER</b> STEAMED MARAE PUDDING <i>for 260 </i> 6 kilos (13 lbs) flour ... 3 doz. eggs ... Grease with butter 1 doz large fruit or milk-powder tins, ... <i>(1978,Maori Food and Cookery,29</i>)

>  It was hoped that the hour's bus ride into town each day would be a broadening experience for a sheltered `marae' kid ... <i>(Dec 1986,Landfall,160/492</i>)

>  MARAE 2000 Contact 25 May 1 Aurora says the partners want to convert their country marae background into commercial success by emulating the "marae experience" that requires the host to "feed and water" visitors. REWENA BREAD 2000 Contact 25 May 1 The partners plant to ply the hungry with an eclectic assortment of dishes - seafood mornay, raw fish, lambs fry, whitebait fritters and rewena bread. <i>(May 25, 2000,Contact (Wgtn),1</i>)

>  "Some sit for six hours," Gilgen says. "If I'm here until 10 at night, so be it." He chuckles. "It's marae time." <i>(Jun 7, 1997,Listener (New Zealand Listener),22</i>)

>  His assistant, Tuariki John Delamere, is best known not for his fiscal stewardship, but for his marae chauvinism. <i>(Mar 29, 1997,Listener (New Zealand Listener),16</i>)

>  <b>POROPORO RUGBY AND SPORTS CLUB "Marae Touch" <i>(Sep 29, 2006,Whakatane Beacon,18</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> early examples in Cook and Monkhouse may be using the Tahitian sense, mistakenly applied by the informant

>  <b>MONKHOUSE</b> The north head forms a peninsula ending in a high bluff on the summit of which is one of the fortified Villages of this country, which we now took to be a <i>Marai</i> or Burial ground from an opinion of our friend Tupia; ... <i>(Oct 10, 1769,Journal</i>) [NOTES: 1769 Oct 10 W.B. Monkhouse in 1955 Cook Journal I-571]



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  We have before now observed on several parts of the Coast small Villages inclosed with Pallisades, and Works of this kind built on eminences and ridges of hills, but Tupia hath all along told us that they were Mories or places of Worship, but I rather think that they are places of retreat or Stronghold. [cf gloss; `fortified village' Nov 4 1/193] <i>(Nov 2, 1769,Cook's Journals,1/191,1955 ed.</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> the courtyard of a Maori meeting-house, the centre of tribal life. [Maori]; enclosed space in front of a house; courtyard; village common (So Williams) Maori, marae pron ['marae], [ma'rae]. [often now used of the whole complex of courtyard, meeting-house, and ancillary buildings and grounds. Also in modern use as urban marae, a marae complex designed to serve Maori townspeople of mixed iwi affiliation who have moved away from their tribal areas. Maori meeting-houses as such are post-European: to be distinguished from carved houses : meeting-place the marae; but the Maori marae must be distinguished from the larger ritualistic Tahitian marae]

>  <b>HULME</b> 238 Marae = a place for gathering, to learn to mourn, teach, welcome and rejoice <i>(1983,Bone People, The,467</i>)

>  But in its day it was one of the largest marae on the river, with around 1000 inhabitants. <i>(Mar 31, 1984,Listener (New Zealand Listener),19</i>)

>  But in its day it was one of the largest marae on the river, with around 1000 inhabitants. <i>(Mar 31, 1984,Listener (New Zealand Listener),19</i>)

>  <b>HOWE</b> Marae - marae are not just the buildings on a piece of land, though we can refer to particular marae. Marae need people and their customs for them to be anything meaningful. The front of the marae is called the atea (from `Te mare nui atea o Tu matauenga' - the big marae of Tu, the god of war). Marae are places where the Maori may assert his/her uniqueness and culture, and are used for tangi, feasts, meetings, weddings and many other reasons. <i>(1985,Towards Taha Maori,21</i>)

>  <b>GRA</b> And don't cook. You come down the marae after and have a kai down there you hear me ... [sic] <i>(1985,Landfall,156/453</i>) [NOTES: Grace (Waimarie)]

>  <b>IHIMAERA</b> The marae as we know it today is taken to mean a complex comprising a meeting house, courtyard and dining hall, although this is a relatively modern innovation. Originally the marae was the earthen courtyard in the village, and it was situated immediately outside the chief's house. <i>(1986,Matriarch, The,116</i>)

>  Paul Temm feels essentially unchanged by his experience on the tribunal, sitting at marae around the country, listening to the elders vent their frustrations in Maori, hongi-ing with both tangata whenua and manuhiri (visitors), chatting over tea and kai. <i>(Jun 21, 1986,Evening Post</i>) [NOTES: [pl marae]]

>  The gate across the road into the Nga Hau e Wha Christchurch National marae is chained and double- padlocked. Behind the palisade fence the weeds are knee high. The whare nui is boarded up ... None of its carvings and tukutuku are in place and the carvings are still tapu. The whare wananga beside it, meant to house the kohanga reo, lies uncompleted, torn building paper flapping. <i>(Mar 5, 1988,Listener (New Zealand Listener),16</i>)

>  <i>marae n. N.Z</i>. a traditional Maori tribal meeting place, originally one in the open air, now frequently a purpose-built building. [< Maori] <i>(1986,Collins Concise English Dictionary</i>) [NOTES: [1986 NZPOD 458; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 299]]

>  <b>LEE & KENDALL</b> Mar|ae, <i>s</i>. A court-yard. <i>(1820,Grammar & Vocabulary of the Language of NZ,176</i>)

>  <b>LEE & KENDALL</b> Mar|ae, <i>s</i>. A court-yard. <i>(1820,Grammar & Vocabulary of the Language of NZ,176</i>)

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> The young chief ... led him to the <i>marae</i>, and there surrounded by all the inhabitants of the pa, he related all the circumstances of the case, and then demanded whether he ought to be killed; ... <i>(1868,Past and Present of NZ,12</i>)

>  <b>BULLER</b> [The Skua] came into the possession of the Hon. Wi Parata, who kept it in [sic] his marae until it became quite tame. <i>(1888,Birds of New Zealand,2/64</i>)

>  <b>TREGEAR</b> A very sacred spot for centuries was the temple and courtyard (marae) at Tarapora in the Kaipara Harbour. <i>(1904,Maori Race, The,203</i>)

>  <b>BAUCKE</b> ... according to the sensible code of [Maori] justice, every order and tribal regulation had to be decided in meeting-house convened, and proclaimed in the marae (village green) to the whole people there assembled before the penalties could be exacted. <i>(1905,Where the White Man Treads,276</i>)

>  I circled the narrow half dozen streets of Hatepe looking for a marae until Robert Tutehekengarangi Morehu came to my rescue and explained that the tennis pavilion doubles as a marae. <i>(Jul 2004,AA Directions,26</i>)

>  <b>COWAN</b> The Maori word <i>marae</i> was particularly appropriate. The <i>marae</i> , or village assembly ground, the square among the houses, was the gathering-place of tribe or <i>hapu</i>. <i>(1940,Sir Donald Maclean,146</i>)

>  <b>MANNERING</b> In the pa the various hapus or tribes were drawn up in Companies, a large open space being left in the Marae (or open square) for the speech-makers. <i>(1943,Eighty Years,73</i>)

>  <b>ZIMMERMAN</b> ... she told me that when you go to a strange <i>pa</i>, and have your first meal on the <i>(1946,Where the People Sing,60</i>)

>  <i>marae</i> , enclosed space in front of a house; open space among the houses of a village, the favoured place for oratory: from his Mexican experience Elsdon Best favoured the descriptive plaza. (Definition incomplete: used in O.E.D., only as above in New-zealand.) <i>(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/145</i>) [NOTES: Andersen Maori Words in English]

>  <b>BEAGLEHOLE</b> <i>marae</i> open green space before the tribal meeting-house <i>Ibid.</i> 275 No woman may speak at a marae council. (The Arawa have the same rule. They say: `It is a bad omen to hear a hen crow!') <i>(1946,Some Modern Maoris,xxii</i>)

>  <i>marae</i> : communal meeting place in kainga. <i>(1947,Reeds' School Dictionary: NZ Suppl.,174</i>)

>  <b>BUCK</b> <i>(1948,Coming of the Maori, The,375, 483</i>)

>  The [Maori] labourers on the market gardens of Pukekohe and Patumahoe have no link with the land or with one another; they have come from many different localities to a district which has not had a <i>marae</i> since the wars of the 1860's. <i>(Oct 1952,NZ Geographer,122</i>) [NOTES: (Metge)]

>  Flowers, flowers at the<i> pa</i>, frothing about Hinewake. ... Flowers lapping further and further out upon the <i>marae</i> as the visitors arrive; adding and adding. <i>(Aug 1953,Here & Now,21</i>)

>  <b>PHILLIPPS</b> <i>(1955,Maori Carving,30</i>)

>  <b>McLINTOCK</b> In the country proper the Maori has retained his traditional mode of life which is symbolised in the <i>marae</i> . In a literal sense the term means the open courtyard in front of the communal meeting house; today ... it embraces all aspects of community life - community buildings, tribal gatherings, church activities, and recreation. <i>(1959,Descriptive Atlas,72</i>)

>  <b>SINCLAIR</b> Among the Maoris the <i>marae</i> , a word which elsewhere in Polynesia meant temple, was the plaza which lay before the meeting-house and was the focus of village life. Most important of all, the <i>marae</i> was the place where men of influence made their speeches. <i>Ibid.</i> 302 [Maori glossary] <i>marae</i> Space in front of meeting house; plaza <i>(1959,History of New Zealand,24</i>)

>  <i>marae n,</i> pl <i>marae</i> or <i>maraes</i> [Tahitian & Maori] 1 : a Polynesian temple enclosure used for worship or sacrifice or other religious ceremonies 2 : a square or similar open area before a Maori tribal or family meetinghouse used for formal reception of guests or other formal functions <i>(1961,Merriam-Webster Third International,1379</i>)

>  <b>SCHOLEFIELD</b> The Native sentiment which opposed the advance of the white pioneers was reinforced by periodical meetings of chiefs and debates on the marae. <i>(1960,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,1/67</i>)

>  <b>HILLIARD</b> SOME MAORI WORDS ... <i>marae</i> traditional meeting-place <i>(1963,Piece of Land, A,192</i>)

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> During the christmas<i>hui</i> of 1924, when about two thousand Morehu were gathered on the <i>marae</i> , Ratana made a last effort to cooperate with the recognised churches. <i>(1963,Ratana,45</i>)

>  <b>KEENE</b> <i>marae</i> : enclosed ground used as a meeting place by a tribe. <i>(1963,O Te Raki,192</i>)

>  <b>FRANCES</b> ... The marae had trembled beneath pounding feet as warriors leapt and thrust with their plumed tewhatewhas in the peruperu, ... <i>(1964,Johnny Rapana,3</i>)

>  <b>BRATHWAITE</b> MARAE Level or slightly sloping, bare patch of ground in pa or kainga which serves as gathering place. A sort of forum. <i>(1967,Evil Day,380</i>)

>  ~ n. assembly place of any Maori settlement [Maori] <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Whitcombe's),620</i>)

>  <i>marae ... n</i>. assembly place of any Maori settlement [Maori]. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),13</i>)

>  <b>MASON</b> WERIHE. Here, sir, the guest is king. Welcome to our marae. <i>(1969,Awatea,52,1978 ed.</i>)

>  <b>BAXTER</b> These are the holy objects of the town marae. The mattresses, the chairs, the sofa, the table, the kai, the cigarettes, have all been shared. <i>(1972,Islands,1/27</i>) [NOTES: Elegy for Boyle Crescent]

>  The marae serves as a museum, a meeting spot and a place where the dead are mourned and the living celebrated... "Our marae can dish up a meal that will meet the Heart Foundation's standards, but also the best 'boil-up' too. <i>(Aug 29, 2004,Sunday  Star Times: Escape,12</i>)

>  <b>BAKER</b> That night they were all installed on the high-school marae, bags at the feet of their mattresses, and their supply of food on the pantry shelves next to the main kitchen. <i>(2000,Kokopu Dreams,36</i>)

>  <b>GOLDSMITH</b> McCready was given to blunt language and word pictures; when discussing the new definition of a 'Maori' in the Maori Affairs Amendment Bill, 1974, he produced the line, 'It now appears that anyone who rides past a marae on a pushbike can claim to be a Maori.' <i>(1997,John Banks : A Biography,64</i>)

>  ..a large square, or marai <i>(Oct 23, 1880,NZ Mail,9</i>)

>  And as for not saying Grace before the state dinner, we only do this before kai on the marae these days. <i>(Mar 9, 2002,Listener (New Zealand Listener),16</i>)

>  <b>NGATA</b> <i>(1930,Na To Hoa Aroha,11</i>) [NOTES: Cit: "Some tribes have the marae instinct" comes from p. 80, letter of 17Nov. 1930]

>  [=the whole marae-complex] For these units the Department supplied stock, fencing-materials, fertilizers, seeds, cow-sheds ...<i>Ibid.</i> 51. Now two large dams built in the hills [above Ruatoki] supply every house, marae, and cow-shed. <i>Ibid.</i> 51-52 [the area in front of meeting house] On a large [52] grassed <i>marae</i> behind the store are two meeting-houses. You may be lucky enough to see a <i>hui</i> in progress. Perhaps a welcome home to one of the many Ruatoki boys who are serving with the Maori Battalion. <i>(1948,Our Own Country,50</i>)

>  <b>HILLIARD</b> [a Maori speaks] Not too many Maori live in the pa these days boy. You pakeha saw to that. Do you know the difference between pa and marae? <i>(1974,Maori Woman,167</i>)

>  <b>BINNEY et al.</b> Marae - the complex of meeting-house and open space for speeches and ceremony, to which, in modern times, a dining-hall has usually been added. In original usage, the term applied specifically to the open space. <i>(1979,Mihaia,202 ed.</i>)

>  <b>SORENSON</b> [pl -s; cf hui] But as Anne Salmond has pointed out, at hui on maraes throughout the country, the Great Fleet lives on: ... <i>(1979,Maori Origins and Migrations,86</i>)

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> My name is Tu the freezing worker./ Ngati D.B. is my tribe./ The pub is my Marae./ My fist is my taiaha. <i>(1979,Eyes of the Ruru,15</i>) [NOTES: (Sad Joke on a Marae)]

>  marae: central meeting ground of a village, with its meeting-house and dining-hall, where meetings and public ceremonies are held and visitors entertained. <i>(1973,Brown Man's Burden,141</i>)

>  <b>LELAND</b> ~ I'm told this used to refer specifically to the open (ceremonial) ground in front of a Maori meetinghouse. In common usage today, it refers not only to this ground but the meeting house itself and any auxiliary space or buildings. <i>(1980,Personal Kiwi-Yankee Dictionary, A,63</i>)

>  <i>(1982,Collins Concise English Dictionary,688</i>)

>  <b>BAXTER</b> marae <i>tribal meeting-ground </i> <i>(1982,Selected Poems,188</i>)

>  <b>O'BISO</b> There is a large Maori meeting house in the great hall of the [Wellington] museum. On Monday morning there was a row of chairs in front of it (an area called the marae) for the museum director and about five Maori men. <i>(Nov 9, 1982,First Light,41</i>) [NOTES: 1982 Nov 9 in O'Biso First Light (1987) 41]

>  <b>GORST</b> ... we walked up, ... to the `Mare', the wide open space in the centre of the town, where a great assembly of Waikato Maories was waiting for us. <i>(1908,New Zealand Revisited,303</i>)

>  <b>BAUGHAN</b> <i>Marae (mah-ri)</i>: The courtyard. <i>(1908,Shingle-Short,188</i>)

>  The feature of his visit was the display afforded in the ceremonial receptions at fourteen maraes ... <i>(Jun 11, 1933,Na To Hoa Aroha,3/85</i>)

>  <b>COWAN</b> Presently the people were out on the <i>marae</i> for the morning meal. <i>(1935,Hero Stories of NZ,182</i>)

>  <b>LAMBERT</b> The Maoris held a council on the <i>marae</i> (village green) ... <i>(1936,Pioneering Reminiscences of Old Wairoa,38</i>)

>  <b>BUICK</b> ... to assemble upon their improvised <i>marae</i> and there give lusty welcome ... <i>(1937,Moa-Hunters of NZ,250</i>)

>  <b>FINLAYSON</b> The open marae where the sun beats down relentlessly is now deserted. <i>Afterwards there were pois and there were hakas, ... and there was to be a korero on politics and the land question ... </i> <i>(1938,Brown Man's Burden,13,1973 ed.</i>) [NOTES: `Christmas Day' see also p. 9]

>  <b>FRAME</b> Then they get restless, they want to be somewhere else, they've had Maoritanga for the time being, thank you, and yet if you happen to be among a group of people at a function you hear the same people holding forth about having 'been on the marae' and how wonderful it is. <i>(1988,Carpathians, The,84</i>)

>  "One of the mums at kohanga was telling me that her seven year old has become really obsessed with her appearance." <i>(2003,Huia 5: Contemp. Maori Fiction,19</i>)

### marae atea
 
 <i>n.</i> courtyard of a marae [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  On most Marae, the Whaikorero usually takes place outside on the marae atea (courtyard), and not inside tthe meeting house. <i>(Feb 4, 2006,NZ Herald,Canvas, p21</i>)

>  <b>Grace</b> She was dressed in a tight fawn jacket and a small skirt, suitable for sitting behind a desk in a quiet office but not for sitting on old seats of rigged-up beach logs out on a marae atea. <i>(2001,Dogside Story,190</i>)

>  He maintained he was entitled to shoot at the flag on the marae atea (the courtyard in front of the meeting house). <i>(Jun 14, 2006,NZ Herald,A8</i>)

### marae-based
 
 <i>adj.</i> [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  The woman who led a protest in the Porirua District Court on Wednesday demanding marae-based justice for her brother says there is growing support ... for the idea. <i>(May 6, 1989,Evening Post,1</i>)

>  Another kaumatua, Terry Putaka, said he had seen marae-based justice operate in Taranaki in the late 1950s. <i>(Jul 20, 1989,Dominion,3</i>)

>  Marae-based Kaumatua Support Worker (40hrs) <i>(Jul 15, 2006,NZ Herald,H15</i>)

>  Members of many other marae committees throughout New Zealand share the vision of Whakaruruhau, of young people being trained and employed to grow trees and other plants from marae-based nurseries to ensure an enduring supply of material for traditional uses. <i>(Nov 22, 1988,Dominion,10</i>)

### maral deer
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Jee Hansen's maral deer are a damn nuisance at feeding out time, he says.... When he tows a trailer-load of hay into the paddock, the marals will jump on to it... The marals are still a relatively rare sight in New Zealand. They are a type of red deer originating from the Caucasus mountains in Russia and are specially prized for their calm temperament. The New Zealand marals all stem from one hind imported 15 years ago. <i>(Dec 20, 2002,Dominion Post,C6</i>)

### marama
 
 <i>n.</i> the moon

>  <b>FORSTER</b> A Comparative Table of the Various Languages in the Isles of the South-Sea ... [English] <i>The Moon</i> ... [New-Zeeland] Marama [a1-macron, a2-macron grave, a3-macron] <i>(1778,Observations Made During a Voyage,284</i>)

>  <b>POLACK</b> The traditional inventions relative to the sun (<i>Ra</i>[acute]) and moon (<i>Morama</i>[a1-acute]) are innumerable; <i>Rona</i> is the `man [245] in the moon,' whose lodgings on earth have been vacated previous to the decease of <i>Mawi</i>[a-acute]. <i>(1840,Manners & Customs of the NZers,1/244</i>)

### maramataka
 
 <i>n.</i> a Maori moon calendar. [ORIGIN: Maori, noun. maramataka = almanac; calendar. http://translator.kedri.info/translate.php]

>  Maramataka, had a certain generic consistency throughout the country, but the wananga had so far recorded 44 versions used by Maori. <i>(Aug 19, 2005,Whakatane Beacon,8</i>)

### Maramatanga
 
 <i>adj.</i> a Maori religious movement

>  It provides a perfect metaphor for both the subject and the persona dramatis of this absorbing, well-researched book focusing on the Maramatanga movement, a Maori belief system that grew out of a desire to incorporate the ancient tohunga ways with the teachings of the New Testament... Based around the Ruapehu area and stretching as far as the Whanganui River area, the Maramatanga is a unique series of beliefs. Although the majority of its practitioners belong to the Roman Cathlic branch of Christianity, the teaching of Rikiriki, who was both a prophet and a healer, are designed to being together the old and the new, rather than the outright rejection of the old ways preferred by most Protestant missionaries. <i>(Oct 5, 2002,Listener (New Zealand Listener),60</i>)

>  <i>Maramatanga</i> literally means <i>enlightenment</i> or <i> understanding</i>. The people of the Maramatanga belong to a prophetic and religious movement who by faith, story, waiata and ritual have sustained their identity and autonomy in the face of an overwhelming Pakeha society. <i>(Nov 2002,Wel-com (Wellington),16</i>)

>  The Maramatanga movement, a prophetic movement based around the teachings of prophetess Mere Rikiriki, grew up around the Wanganui river early last century. <i>(Nov 16, 2002,Dominion Post,F8</i>)

### marangai
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>SELWYN</b> When we looked out on the morrow lo! a Marangai (North Easter) a violent gale had sprung up, in which no vessel ever put out to sea. <i>(1961,Reminiscences,38</i>) [NOTES: 1850 in 1961]

### marare
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [marari](../M#marari))

### marari
 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Coridodax pullus</i>

>  CORIDODAX PULLUS (Forster). <i>Butterfish; Marari</i>. Sold as `greenbone' or `kelp-salmon' in Dunedin. An outside fish secured in the vicinity of kelp around the northern coasts during most months of the year. ... Distribution: New Zealand coasts. <i>(Jun 1921,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,4/117</i>) [NOTES: Phillipps Notes on the Edible Fishes]

>  <b>PHILLIPPS</b> <i>Coridodax pullus</i> (Forster). BUTTERFISH, SOUTHERN KELP-FISH; MARARI. <i>(1927,Bibliography of NZ Fishes,40,1971 ed.</i>)

>  A similar net ... was used ... in pools where conger eel and <i>marari </i>(kelp-fish) abound, hence its name kupeng tchitok (pole net). <i>Ibid.</i> 378 Plainly ... I see old Kirapu gather his <i>kupeng' tchitok'</i> (pole net) ... to net <i>mararii</i> (kelp-fish) and <i>koiro</i> (conger eel) and I go with him. <i>(1928,Bishop Museum Memoirs,Vol. IX No. 5 /360</i>)

>  <b>POWELL</b> BUTTERFISH (<i>Coridodax pullus</i>), Marari of the Maoris, is a kelp fish found throughout New Zealand, but most commonly in the South. <i>(1947,Native Animals of NZ,69</i>)

>  <b>GRAHAM</b> GREENBONE, BUTTERFISH, KELP SALMON, or KELPIE (MARARI) <i>Coridodax pullus</i> (Bloch and Schneider) <i>(1956,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,261</i>)

>  <b>PARROTT</b> The Butterfish [Corridodax pullus] <i>was known to the Maoris as Marari, and was captured by using basket traps close inshore. They may be taken all the year round by the angler and are said to take a shrimp bait readily, if it is rapidly dragged through the water. </i> <i>(1957,Sea Anglers' Fishes of NZ,130</i>)

>  <b>MORELAND</b> BUTTERFISH [<i>Coridodax pullus</i>] ... The term greenbone is widely used in southern waters referring to the bone colour. The Maori names marari, and rari are seldom used. <i>(1967,Marine Fishes,28</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> [mairari here ?misspelling or mistranscription nf Williams] Mainly a vegetarian the Butterfish will take small sea animals and shellfish on occasions. Kelp-fish and Kelpie are names sometimes used as are the Maori names `mairari' and `rari'. No relation to his Australia namesake. <i>(1981,Fisherman's Bible,33</i>)

>  <b>DOOGUE & MORELAND</b> BUTTERFISH ... <i>Other names</i>: <i>Coridodax pullus</i>; greenbone; marari, rari (Maori). <i>(1966,Sea Anglers' Guide,256</i>)

>  <b>AYLING</b> <i>BUTTERFISH </i>(Greenbone, marari)<i> Odax pullus </i> (<i>Coridodax pullus, Odax vittatus </i>) ... Small individuals have a markedly different colour pattern from that of the adults and until recently were thought to belong to a separate species named the kelpfish, [266] <i>Odax vittatus.</i> These young fish are yellow-brown with a line of white dashes running down each side ... [Butterfish] are an excellent eating fish, with delicate tasty flesh that prompted the name butterfish ... <i>(1982,Collins Guide,265</i>)

>  <b>DIEFFENBACH</b> 58. <i>Odax pullus.</i> - ... Named `Mararee' by the inhabitants of Queen Charlotte's Sound. <i>(1843,Travels in NZ,2/218</i>) [NOTES: 1842 Gray's Fauna (Richardson's Fish) in 1843 Dieffenbach Travels in NZ 2/218]

>  <b>HECTOR</b> 71. BUTTER FISH. ... The Marare of the natives, known as the Kelp Fish among the fishermen and the Butter Fish in the market (<i>Coridodax pullus</i>), is the fish most commonly sold in Wellington during the winter ... <i>(1872,Edible Fishes,114</i>)

>  Marari (or marare in other provinces) - the butter-fish, or kelp fish. <i>(1880,South Island Maori Lists</i>) [NOTES: c1880 South Island Maori Lists `Wahi Mahika Kai' in 1945 Beattie Maori Place-names of Canterbury 61]

>  <b>SHERRIN</b> BUTTER FISH (<i>Coridodax pullus</i>). ... [14] Known by many as the kelp-fish, and by the fishermen in some places as the butter-fish. Dr. Knox, in the <i>Transactions of the New Zealand Institute</i>, gives the following interesting details of the marare of the Natives : `On the 2nd August, 1870, a large quantity of the kelp-fish were offered for sale in and about Wellington, and though by no means prepossessing in external appearance, being of a dingy-black colour, and covered with a slimy mucus, I found that the fish frequent Cook Strait, more especially off and around the Mana.' <i>(1886,Handbook Fishes of NZ,13</i>)

>  <i>marari n.</i> = BUTTERFISH. [M] <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,458</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.]]

>  In New Zealand waters there were two species of closely allied fish with similar feeding habits, locally known as butter-fish (<i>Coridodax pullus</i> and <i>Odax vittatus</i>). ... The New Zealand butter-fish was called `marare'. <i>(1898,TrNZI,30/556</i>) [NOTES: spg marare]

>  [Maori or Settlers' Name] Spotties, Marare .. [Scientific Name] <i>Labrichthys bothryocosmus </i> <i>(1878,TrNZI,10/326</i>) [NOTES: (P. Thomson, Otago)]



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> spottie

>  [Native or Common Name] Marare, Butterfish ... [Scientific Name] <i>Labrichthys bothrycosmus</i> <i>(1877,TrNZI,9/485</i>) [NOTES: (P. Thomson, Otago)]

### maratea
 
 <i>n.</i> a fish

>  <b>LEE & KENDALL</b> Mara t|ea; A certain fish; <i>(1820,Grammar & Vocabulary of the Language of NZ,176</i>)

>  CHIRONEMUS SPECTABILIS (Hutton). <i>Maratea; Nanua</i>. A fish common in the Auckland market. ... Distribution: North Island coasts (rare south of Bay of Plenty). <i>(Jun 1921,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,4/116</i>) [NOTES: Phillipps Notes on the Edible Fishes]

### marble
 
 <i>n.</i> (marble names in citations below)

>  <b>SCANLAN</b> `Reverend Mother talks of when she used to give you marbles for being a good little boy - pee-wees and agates, and stonies and glassies and a big tor. And once she tried to play with you, and you said she funked because her knuckle was inside the ring.' <i>(1933,Tides of Youth,153</i>)

>  <b>SUTTON-SMITH</b> Marbles had a language of its own. There were names for the marbles, the games, and all the various aspects of the play. There were terms for marbles such as `taw' or `shooter', referring to the marbles used to fire with; `dubs', `dates', `stakes', `dukes', or `changers', referring to the marbles put down to be fired at. Then there were the terms referring to particular kinds of marbles: for example, agates, aggies, aggotties, American alleys, blood alleys, bomb-squashers, bonies, bonsers, bottlies, bull's eyes, bum-squashers, cat's eyes, chinkies, connies, duckies, eggies, glassies, Greeks, milkies, molly-bars, mulley-bars [mullibars: large marbles], peelies, peewees, piss-wees, pretties, Scotties, Scottish alleys, smokies, stinkies, stonies, stripies, tom-bobblers, white alleys, and in more recent years ball-bearings, chippies, plastics, and woodies. <i>(1972,Folkgames of Children,174</i>)

### marble orchard
 
 <i>n.</i> (from US) a cemetery [[like boneyard a Baxter borrowing from ? US folksong]]

>  <b>BAXTER</b> Between five and six the night before , Horse had had the pleasure of sinking fifteen handles at the Green Island pub. Ghandi, the bald rollsman, had held up the first beer to the light ... and had said. `the old boys up in the marble orchard would give their eyeteeth for this.' <i>(1985,Horse,14</i>) [NOTES: (Mills of God) c1962]

>  <b>BAXTER</b> Swagger Joe and my dry father/ In the marble orchard lie <i>(1979,Collected Poems</i>) [NOTES: (1953-61)]

### marble trout
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [trout](../T#trout),[marble-trout](../M#marble-trout),[marbletrout](../M#marbletrout))

>  <b>MORELAND</b> MARBLEFISH [<i>Aplodactylus meandratus</i>] ... Another name is marbletrout, while the Maori keke is still in use in some northern areas. <i>(1967,Marine Fishes,28</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> MARBLEFISH ... Other names commonly used in the southern waters where it is found are Marbletrout and Granitetrout. <i>(1981,Fisherman's Bible,161</i>)

>  <b>PAUL</b> <i>Marblefish Aplodactylus arctidens</i> Also called marble trout, granite trout, Maori chief. <i>(1986,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,111</i>)

>  <b>PAUL et al.</b> [index to common names 283-293] trout, marble 1554 <i>(1987,Fishes: A Bibliography</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> [marble trout, marbletrout, granite trout, granitetrout]

### marble wood
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <i>marble wood n.</i> tree with mottled timber. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),13</i>)

### marble-leaf
 
 <i>n.</i> putaputaweta [also marbleleaf] (See [putaputaweta](../P#putaputaweta),[weta](../W#weta))

>  Sub-canopy species include coprosma..mountain totara and putaputaweta (marbleleaf). <i>(1990,Tramping in South Island Forest Parks,177</i>)

>  <b>MARTIN</b> Sole representative of the endemic and monotypic genus <i>Carpodetus</i> is PUTAPUTAWETA or MARBLE-LEAF, ... . <i>(1961,Flora NZ,180,1929 ed.</i>)

>  <b>MOORE & IRWIN</b> <i>CARPODETUS SERRATUS, putaputaweta, marble-leaf.</i> ... It is one of the species grouped by foresters under the slightly opprobrious term `scrub hardwoods'. <i>(1978,Oxford Book of NZ Plants,78</i>)

>  PUTAPUTAWETA/ (Marble Leaf)/ <i>Carpodetus serratus/</i> ... Leaves have a marbled effect. <i>(1982,Field Guide to Common NZ Trees & Shrubs,14</i>)

### marble-top
 
 <i>n.</i> an old fashioned aerated-water bottle with a patent marble stopper

### marbled skink
 
 <i>n.</i> (Environment) two similar skink species resident in islands north and east of Coromandel. Distinguished by marbled pattern on undersides [<i>Oligosoma oliveri</i> aka pre 2009 <i>Cyclodina oliveri</i> aka Oliver's skink and also <i>Cyclodina pachysomaticum</i>]

>  Superficially similar to the marbled skink, <i>Cyclodina oliveri</i>, [this] is genetically distinct from other populations. <i>(May 2002,Forest & Bird,No. 304/18</i>)

### marblefish
 
 <i>n.</i> a seaweed-eating fish, greenish-brown marbled with creamy spots, found also in southern Australia. (See [also granite trout](../A#also-granite-trout),[Maori chief](../M#maori-chief))

>  APLODACTYLUS MEANDRATUS (Richardson). <i>Marble-fish; Keke</i>. Secured in deep water in the vicinity of kelp, and is sometimes taken in mullet-nets. <i>(Jun 1921,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,4/115</i>) [NOTES: Phillipps Notes on the Edible Fishes]

>  APLODACTYLUS MEANDRATUS. <i>Marble-fish.</i> Generally known as `Maori chief' in Auckland, where quantities were received from line fishermen during March, May, and June. ... During the winter of 1921 June specimens were received in Wellington from the Marlborough Sounds and sold as `Maori chief.' <i>(May 1922,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,5/92</i>) [NOTES: Phillipps and Hodgkinson Further Notes on Edible Fishes]

>  <b>PHILLIPPS</b> <i>Haplodactylus meandratus</i> Richardson. MARBLEFISH; KEKE. <i>(1927,Bibliography of NZ Fishes,37,1971 ed.</i>)

>  <b>PHILLIPPS</b> <i>Haplodactylus meandratus</i> Richardson. MARBLEFISH; KEKE. <i>(1927,Bibliography of NZ Fishes,37,1971 ed.</i>)

>  <i>Haplodactylus meandratus </i>(Richardson). Marble-fish (granite trout). <i>(1938,TrRSNZ,68/411</i>) [NOTES: Graham Fishes of Otago Harbour]

>  <b>GRAHAM</b> GRANITE TROUT or MARBLE FISH (KEKE) <i>Dactylosargus arctidens maeandratus</i> Richardson The common names of this fish are derived from the resembalance of the skin to marble or granite. The ground colour is black, mottled or waved with slaty-grey. At one time this fish was sold in Auckland and Wellington as Maori Chief but I do not know why this should be, as the true Maori Chief is quite different and not at all like the Granite Trout. <i>(1956,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,248</i>)

>  <b>GRAHAM</b> Marblefish <i>Dactylosargus arctidens</i> (Richardson) (<i>Syn</i>. Sciaena meandrites and maeandratus <i>Richardson. Haplodactylus donaldii </i>Haast, and <i>H. schauinslandii</i> Steindachner) <i>(1956,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,407</i>) [NOTES: 1955 Whitley Namelist in 1956 Graham NZ Fishes 407]

>  <b>PARROTT</b> Marble Fish Haplodactylus meandratus Granite Trout <i>(1957,Sea Anglers' Fishes of NZ,169</i>)

>  <b>DOOGUE & MORELAND</b> MARBLEFISH ... <i>Other names: Aplodactylus meandratus</i>; granite trout; keke (Maori). <i>(1966,Sea Anglers' Guide,246</i>)

>  <b>McLINTOCK</b> marblefish .. .. keke .. .. <i>Aplodactylus meandratus </i> <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,3/705-712</i>)

>  <b>MORELAND</b> MARBLEFISH [<i>Aplodactylus meandratus</i>] ... Another name is marbletrout, while the Maori keke is still in use in some northern areas. <i>(1967,Marine Fishes,28</i>)

>  <b>NATUSCH</b> Family APLODACTYLIDAE. The marble-fish or keke, <i>Haplodactylus meandratus</i>, grows to thirty inches, and is found throughout New Zealand. <i>(1967,Animals of NZ, The,222</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> MARBLEFISH A cool-water denizen who grows up to about 30 inches (76.2cm) the ... <i>(1981,Fisherman's Bible,161</i>)

>  <b>AYLING</b> <i>MARBLEFISH</i> (Keke) <i>Aplodactylus arctidens</i> ... The body colour ... is a uniform olive brown, finely mottled with white on the body and fins. <i>(1982,Collins Guide,240,1984 ed.</i>)

>  <i>marble fish</i> a mottled greenish-brown NZ fish. <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,458</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.]]

>  [species name] marblefish .. [scientific name] <i>Aplodactylus arctidens</i> .. [other name(s)] granite trout, maori chief, morepork <i>(Jul 23, 1986,Codes for Species in NZ Waters</i>)

>  <b>PAUL</b> <i>Marblefish Aplodactylus arctidens </i>Also called marble trout, granite trout, Maori chief. <i>(1986,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,111</i>)

>  <b>PAUL et al.</b> [index to common names 283-293] marblefish 1554-65 MARBLEFISHES 1554-65 <i>(1987,Fishes: A Bibliography,176</i>)

>  <b>PARROTT</b> THE MARBLE FISH <i>Haplodactylus meandratus</i> (Richardson)<i> ... Maori name: KEKE ... The Marble Fish, or Granite trout as it is sometimes called, derives its name from the colour, which is black, mottled with slaty grey, resembling the markings of granite or marble. </i> <i>(1957,Sea Anglers' Fishes of NZ,112</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <b>SORENSON</b> <i>COD, ROCK </i>(a) Scientific name: <i>Lotella rhacinus</i>. (b) Suggested commercial name[s]: Rock Cod; Kelp Cod. (c) Other common names: Parrotfish; Soldiers, Kelp, Kelpies, Moreporks, Marblefish, Maori Chief, Wrasse, Bourbon, Guffy, Cloudy Bay Cod, Rasp. <i>(1970,Nomenclature of NZ Fish,18</i>)



 
<b>3</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <b>AYLING</b> <i>NOTCH-HEAD MARBLEFISH Aplodactylus etheridgi </i> <i>(1982,Collins Guide,241,1984 ed.</i>)

### marbles
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) dags.

### marching
 
 <i>n.</i> women's formation marching as a competitive sport [It seems the trail leads back to Aucklandabout 1924 at earliest, first real exhibition probably 1926; competitions between various factories in the clothing trade. The restrictions on uniforms mentioned below were brought in to prevent big firms outspending the smallones by providing splendiferous uniforms. Organisations outside the rag trade do not seem to have been allowed in officially into Interhouse Marching until about 1954. Despite the evidence below that it started in Auckland under Jean Stevenson, Bateman's Encyclopedia says marching started in Wellington and spread to Auckland later. It must be wrong. The plot thickens. Passing through London Bookshop today I spotted Gordon Ell's new book on NZ Trivia and Kiwiana and he says Marching Girls started in Dunedin in the 1920s. have I found any early Auckland ones, nothing before 1941 in Wellington. guess is that one or other or both of Jean Stevenson and Kathleen Montgomery started them in Auckland and through the YWCA spread them rapidly throughout the main centres.]

>  New Zealanders should be proud of marching. It is the most major sport invented in this country. <i>(May 10, 2003,Listener (New Zealand Listener),37</i>)

>  [Advt] Spectacular Interhouse Marching Drill... Pretty girls by the hundred! Massed drill movement and marching to the music of the First Battalion, Wellington Regiment. <i>(Mar 23, 1940,Dominion,5</i>)

>  [Caption] The Governor-General.. as he passed between the lines of the guard of honour from the girls' marching teams at Athletic Park on Saturday. <i>Ibid.</i> 9 One of the highlights of the display was the spectacular marching evolutions of the girls' teams. <i>(Mar 31, 1941,Dominion,5</i>)

>  <b>2</b> [Advt] 200 Girls of the Interhouse Association in Spectacular Marching and Ball Games. <i>(Mar 21, 1942,Dominion</i>)

>  [Hdg] Athletics and Interhouse Competitions... With the Wellington Pipe Band at their head, 21 girls' interhouse teams.. made a fine showing as they marched past at the sports meeting conducted at the Basin Reserve on Saturday. <i>(Mar 23, 1942,Dominion,8</i>)

>  The A.P. Smith trophy for the championship marching [was won] by the Wellington Woollen Company, the Sutherland Cup for the runners-up in the marching contest going to Fashions Ltd. <i>(Mar 23, 1943,Dominion Infotech Weekly,2</i>)

>  Before the ceremony the Air Force Band gave a short programme on the bandstand and girls' marching teams gave an exhibition. <i>(Mar 13, 1944,Dominion,6</i>)

>  [Hdg] Girls' Marching... A cold drizzle.. marred the Interhouse Girls' Marching Championship and Athletic Meeting at the Basin Reserve on Saturday... A midgets' team from Wanganui showed how well girls under the age of 11 can march. <i>(Mar 20, 1944,Dominion,6</i>)

>  Charlie Stone, a Wellington businessman, and Sergeant Major.. G. O'Leary... were responsible for getting the Wellington Interhouse Girls' Marching Association formed, though the Wellington and Kiwi Amateur Athletic Clubs, with O'Leary one of the leading lights, were the first to stage outdoor marching displays in Wellington. <i>(Nov 24, 1961,Listener (New Zealand Listener),36</i>)

>  Girls' marching as a sport or competition was started in Auckland by Miss Jean Stevenson, General Secretary of the YWCA in Auckland in the early 1920s. The idea was to give some recreation to girls who did not favour the robust sports of hockey or basketball... In due course, marching competitions came to Wellington... You can tell a marching girl by the way she carries herself - and by the absence of round shoulders... The Wellington Inter-house Marching Association was formed with rules governing costume to allow the participants to take part in running and ball-relay events as well as marching. <i>(Apr 15, 1966,Listener (New Zealand Listener),14</i>)

>  <b>CONEY</b> The highlights.. [in Auckland, in 1926] were the massed drill display of 400 girls coached by Kathleen Montgomery, and the grand march-past... This marching would develop in skill and complexity from year to year and eventually lead to the formation of a marching association in Auckland. <i>(1986,Every Girl,179</i>)

>  [Advt] Wellington Easter Show... Grand Marching Competition. <i>Ibid.</i> 6 The Inter-house girls' marching competition, which is on a bigger scale than last year, will be one of the main attractions tonight. <i>(Mar 24, 1934,Dominion,4</i>)

>  Bright costumes and attractive marching were seen at the Easter Show Stadium on Saturday evening, when the first performance for the massed trades display was given. The competing teams, of which there were ten, comprised eight girls, or, in one case, boys and a leader, all of whom were dressed by the firms they represent. <i>(Mar 26, 1934,Dominion,16</i>)

>  At the end of last season, there were 132 marching teams in New Zealand and 1346 marchers. At marching's peak, in the 1970s, there were up to 368 teams and more than 4000 marchers... <i>(Mar 16, 2002,Dominion Weekend Magazine,3</i>)

>  Despite scurrilous suggestions that marching was an Australian invention, NZ Marching asserts that the sport was introduced to Australia when New Zealand's Grenadier Guards visited there in 1953. It was not until 1977 that trans-Tasman rivals competed in the first inter-dominion championships. <i>(Mar 16, 2002,Dominion Weekend Magazine,3</i>)

### marching girl
 
 <i>n.</i> [ca1963 March J. Winchester (Wellington) `a New Zealand invention.']

>  ... not for a long time have I observed such an odd symptom of our <i>malaise </i>as the business of `marching girls'. This cult has not only established itself firmly in New Zealand: it is even being exported. ... Enquiries I have made as to the origin of this astonishing sport, or prank, ... have so far been fruitless. It has the earmarks of being an American invention, but I cannot be at all sure about this [suggests Air Force training grounds]. <i>(Jul 1952,Here & Now,9</i>) [NOTES: (A.R.D.Fairburn)]

>  <b>BRIDGES & DOWNS</b> The first record of 'Marching Girls' is in the official history of the visit of the Duke and Duchess of Cornwall and York in 1901. During the Dunedin celebrations, about 500 girls marched past as a celebration of 'girls' drill'. <i>(2000,No 8 Wire: The Best of Kiwi Ingenuity,132</i>)

>  Part of the answer may also be found in an innocent exhibitionism; marching girls are really modelled on the drum majorettes who strut and prance at the head of American processions. <i>(Mar 21, 1958,Listener (New Zealand Listener),10</i>)

>  [Caption] Marching girls in a drill display at Basin Reserve, Wellington, in 1936... It is not easy to win a marching medal these days, so when you next see a marching girl with a display of medals that would make a Field Marshal envious you will realise that the walking jingle represents many hours of hard practice. <i>(Nov 24, 1961,Listener (New Zealand Listener),36</i>)

>  You can tell a marching girl by the way she carries herself - and by the absence of round shoulders. <i>(Apr 15, 1966,Listener (New Zealand Listener),14</i>)

>  The psychological explanation of the 'marching girls' cult is probably complex. <i>(Jul 1952,Here & Now,2(10) : 9</i>)

>  <b>MASON</b> "Tell me," I asked, "why playing in an orchestra is better than marching girls?" She turned over lazily. "Because they are engaged in making something bigger than themselves, and they are more potent together than they are alone." <i>(1986,Every Kind of Weather,249</i>)

>  <b>MASON</b> The marching girl display paraded something like suppressed fury, for all its skill and panache. <i>(1986,Every Kind of Weather,250</i>)

>  Fiona Samuel, The Marching Girls writer, has always thought marching teams are ""neat".... The Marching Girls, which is as much about the lives of a group of New Zealand women as it is about marching, fitted the bill exactly. Even if they are called marching "girls". <i>(Oct 24, 1987,Listener (New Zealand Listener),21</i>)

>  Marching girls have featured on postage stamps, been the subject of a television drama series, and even had a punk band named after them. <i>(Mar 16, 2002,Dominion Weekend Magazine,3</i>)

>  <i>(Dec 10, 1954,Listener (New Zealand Listener)</i>) [NOTES: 1954 Dec 10 Listener D. Glover in 1961 Reid Kiwi Laughs 182-183]

>  <i>(Nov 24, 1961,Listener (New Zealand Listener),36</i>)

>  Nor have we invented any games that I know of - or did we breed the first marching girls? <i>(1961,Distance Looks Our Way,36</i>) [NOTES: (Sinclair)]

>  <b>TURNER</b> In sport ... <i>marching girls</i> appear to have been a New Zealand idea, though already on July 1952 when A.R.D. Fairburn wrote about them in the periodical <i>Here and Now</i> (Vol. 2, no. 10, p. 9), the origin of the idea could not easily be traced. <i>(1966,English Language in Aust. & NZ, The,176</i>)

>  <b>LELAND</b> <i>(1980,Personal Kiwi-Yankee Dictionary, A,63</i>)

>  <i>marching girl NZ & Austral.</i> girl trained to march in formation. <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,459</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 299]]

>  I always write about real life, though sometimes indirectly - whereas something as direct and visible as a team of marching girls never quite manages to seem like real life to me. <i>(May 22, 1982,Listener (New Zealand Listener),87</i>)

### marginal country
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a class of land of questionable economic potential. (See [marginal land](../M#marginal-land))

>  <b>HINDMARSH</b> Fertiliser application, accepted elsewhere as so beneficial on marginal country, was still cried down as uneconomic on the big hills of the East Coast. <i>(1997,Come Be a Pioneer,44</i>)

>  In the South Island a lot of country is marginal and slowly reverting to native scrub <i>(Nov 18, 1999,Taranaki Daily News,18</i>)

>  It didn't take him long to realise that just raising animals for sale to the big distributers was a hard way to make a living and that it was even harder in such marginal country. <i>(Jul 2011,Air New Zealand inflight magazine,79</i>)

>  <b>HERD</b> Technical terms produce their own difficulties [for translators]. The translators fail to appreciate what is meant by `marginal country' (not border districts) or by `blade-shearing'; they ... <i>(1973,Islands,5/328</i>)

>  (glossary) Marginal country On the border of farm and forest <i>(1983,Land of the Mist,106</i>)

### marginal farmer
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) farmer whose land is considered to have questionable agricultural or pastoral potential.

>  <b>GASCOIGNE</b> And just when the planes were organised to operate, the wool boom of 1950 came and the marginal farmer, as never before, had the money to pay for his manure and the planes to spread it. <i>(1965,From the Top of a Hill,45</i>)

### marginal land
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a class of land of questionable economic potential. (See [marginal country](../M#marginal-country))

>  Huge areas of New Zealand's hill country and marginal lands have been seriously depleted. <i>(Oct 11, 1951,NZ Farmer,41</i>)

>  Aerial topdressing will play a significant part..in constant battle to bring in marginal land. <i>(Feb 1966,NZ Journal of Agriculture,13</i>)

>  <b>WRIGHT</b> Because this expansion into marginal land was coupled with farming techniques - such as the 'burn and graze' method of running sheep - that led to the rapid degradation of the land, it was not long before farming became impossible in some areas ... <i>(1985,Farming in the Mohaka State Forest,50</i>)

>  Richard and Jill Woodd farm 400 ha of marginal land 11 km from Whangamomona, Taranaki. <i>(Sep 26, 1985,NZ Farmer,18</i>)

>  <b>ELLMERS</b> The distress of losing their stock, and the financial loss, were heavy burdens for these farmers struggling with the development of blocks of marginal back-country land ... <i>(1993,Schoolhouse Under the Sky,46</i>)

>  <b>SLATTER</b> There were the scrublands of blackened second growth, the pastures of danthonia and browntop ... the marginal land of bracken, hebe and flax, the reproachful columns of burnt-over bush ... <i>(1968,Pagan Game, The,20</i>)

>  Marginal Lands Board [heading] This organisation is supervised by the Department of Lands and Survey. It was established by the Marginal Lands Act 1950 to help farmers to restore, maintain, and [52] increase the production of marginal land. <i>(1974,NZ Agriculture,51</i>)

 <i>n.</i>

>  Marginal Lands Board [heading] This organisation is supervised by the Department of Lands and Survey. It was established by the Marginal Lands Act 1950 to help farmers to restore, maintain, and [52] increase the production of marginal land. <i>(1974,NZ Agriculture,51</i>)

### marigold
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <i>marigold</i> [s] <i>Calendula officinalis </i>L. ... marigold, bur [=] beggars' ticks: cobblers' pegs</i> [s] ... marigold, corn</i> [s]<i> Chrysanthemum segetum</i> L. ... <i>marigold, Mexican</i> [s] <i>Tagetes minuta</i> L. <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,47</i>)

Lyalli</i>] growing ... side by side with the yellow Mountain Lily (<i>R. Godleyanus</i>), and interspersed with Alpine marygolds (<i>Senecios</i>) and the little star-like <i>Montias</i>. <i>(1889,Art Album NZ Flora,6</i>)

### marine farm
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) an oyster or mussel farm.

>  An Opotiki marine farm that will be the largest in New Zealand has just been given the go-ahead by Environmental BOP. [This will] .. more than double the area covered by current New Zealand marine farms. <i>(May 8, 2002,RD1.com,Rural News</i>)

### marine national park
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Planning is under way to create New Zealand's first marine national park off Northland's east coast. . . . The regional council's manager of planning and policy, Glenn Mortimer is recommending to today's council meeting that the marine national park concept should be supported in principle. <i>(Oct 19, 2005,NZ Herald,A8</i>)

### marine reserve
 
 <i>n.</i> an area of sea and foreshore set aside for preservation in their natural state as a habitat of marine life for scientific study [See Marine Reserves Act 1971]

>  He was shocked there were only three marine reserves along the east coast. <i>(Dec 20, 2001,Dominion,2</i>)

### Marion
 
 (=Tribe of Marion) <i>n.</i> Early nickname for `Frenchman'; in pl. `The French'. cf WIWI (from Marion du Fresne, a French Captain killed, with his crew, in a Maori revenge attack, 182?) esp. the TRIBE OF MARION, trans. from Maori

>  <b>BUICK</b> We have heard that the tribe of Marion is at hand coming to take away our land ...[fn. The French were called by the natives `the tribe of marion' after Captain Marion du Fresne, who met his untimely death at their hands in 1772.] <i>(1936,Treaty of Waitangi,11</i>) [NOTES: 1831 Nov 16 Petition of Chiefs to King William in 1936 Buick Treaty of Waitangi 11]

>  <b>LANG</b> One of the natives ..... perceived that M. Lacoste was not an Englishman, and asked Mr. Mair `if he were not a Marion' to which Mr Mair replied in the affirmative. Marion ['s] ... name has ever since [ca1800] been the synonym for `Frenchman' in New Zealand. <i>(1839,New Zealand (various authors/titles),16</i>)

>  <b>POLACK</b> The French are known only, to this day, to the New Zealanders as `Te heveh no <i>Marion </i>(its), or the tribe of Marion. <i>(1840,Manners & Customs of the NZers,2/121</i>)

### Marist
 
 <i>n.</i> of the Roman Catholic orders of the Society of Mary, their members, school or old pupils' sporting clubs.

>  of the Roman Catholic Marist order, its schools.. or school old boys' sporting clubs. <i>(1979,Heinemann NZ Dictionary,662</i>)

### Marist Brothers
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  When he'd gone to Marist Brothers Sandy's mother had bought his school uniform at George Courts <i>(1987,Metro Fiction</i>) [NOTES: (Brown Beachcombers)]

### marjoram
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <i>marjoram, wild</i> [s]<i> Origanum vulgare</i> L. <i>Ibid.</i> 86 <i>wild marjoram</i> [s] <i>Origanum vulgare </i>L. <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,47</i>)

### mark
 
 <i>v.</i>

>  <b>BOOTH</b> A good hand can cut and mark two thousand lambs per day... <i>(1912,Five Years in NZ,26</i>)

>  <i>Mark.</i>- To <i>ear-m.</i> Now a frequent euphemism for cut and tail (q.v.). (C.) <i>(Nov 4, 1933,Press, The,15</i>) [NOTES: Acland Glossary]

>  <b>459</b> <i>mark ... NZ & Austral</i>. ear-mark (lamb, calf, etc.), castrate (lamb). <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.]]



 
 <i>v.</i> to castrate (and often dock the tail of) a lamb, or to castrate a calf.

>  <b>T.C. KIERNAN</b> 48 sheep shorn, 3 lambs marked. <i>(Jan 28, 1879,Diary</i>) [NOTES: 1879 Jan 28 T.C. Kiernan Diary in 1921 Guthrie-Smith Tutira 125]

>  <b>FERGUSON</b> But by-and-bye the overseer/ [168] Rode out to learn the shepherds' cheer./ It now was time to mark the lambs,/ <i>(1883,Castle Gay,167</i>)

>  Marking lambs all day from home paddocks. Most miserable result. <i>(Oct 7, 1886,Chudleigh Diary,348,1950 ed.</i>)

>  We have marked nearly 1,300 Merino lambs, ... <i>(Dec 17, 1903,Letter</i>) [NOTES: 1903 Dec 17 letter in 1978 Hayes Toss Of a Coin 78]

>  I commenced marking lambs today. <i>(Sep 28, 1906,Chudleigh Diary,436,1950 ed.</i>)

>  I commenced marking lambs today. <i>(Sep 28, 1906,Chudleigh Diary,436,1950 ed.</i>)

>  <b>PERRY</b> [caption] Temporary yards for marking lambs. <i>(1922,Sheep Farming in NZ,79</i>)

>  <i>(1950,NZ Journal of Agriculture,463</i>) [NOTES: also June 516/2]

>  <b>McLEOD</b> ...800 lambs to catch and mark,... <i>(1951,High Country,19</i>)

>  <b>TOMLINSON</b> We mustered ... several other blocks, marking about 9,000 lambs, and we did another 3,000 at shearing time. ... When we finished the lamb marking we returned to Tarndale ... <i>(1968,Remembered Trails,41</i>)

>  <b>GRUNDY</b> The cattle are sorted out, and the bulls are marked. ... A few good specimens are sent back unmarked for breeding purposes. <i>(1968,Who'd Marry a Doctor?,66</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> Calf marking is another major job that is done is in Bush Gully yards. ... I was interested to find that all the calves - marked in March and fairly sizable - are done in the bail ... . The marking cradle was tried but did not find favour. <i>(1973,Big Country,65,1977 ed.</i>)

>  <b>MEREDITH</b> A little further up the [East] coast the Maoris have a flock of very "scabby' sheep, which roam over a large area of land. These sheep are all long-tailed, and have never been marked, except on their ears. <i>(1875,Adventuring in Maoriland in the Seventies,145,1935 ed.</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>FITTON</b> Several marks of Adelaide [wool] have been sold which equal any we have seen from this colony; and we have a general improvement ... in condition in which the wool is brought to market. <i>(1856,New Zealand (various authors/titles),232</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>v.</i> (Rural) to castrate, earmark and tail lambs and calves [originally S.I.]

>  <b>CHUDLEIGH</b> I commenced marking lambs today. <i>(Sep 28, 1906,Diary,438</i>) [NOTES: (1950)]

>  <b>MEREDITH</b> These sheep are all long-tailed, and have never been marked, except on their ears. <i>(1875,Adventuring in Maoriland in the Seventies,145</i>) [NOTES: (1935)]

>  <b>DEANS</b> The hardest day I can recall was about 1901 when the lambs from 3,000 Merino ewes running on the Bush Gully block had to be marked in one day. <i>(1964,Pioneers on Port Cooper Plains,114</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <b>HYDE</b> After sundown, still slouching in the streets, he felt the muzzle of a gun stuck into his back ... and a man said softly: `You shout for the boys, or you'll be put on the mark.' [?pay up, or take the consequences] <i>(1938,Nor the Years Condemn,214</i>)

### marker bull
 
 <i>n.</i> a bull (usually vasectomised) fitted with a marking harness to find oestrous cows

>  Marker Bull: a bull (usually vasectomised) fitted with a marking harness to find oestrous cows. <i>(1982,MAF Agricultural Glossary,19</i>)
